LAYER 6, THE CELLULAR LAYER: MODES AND MODE TRANSITIONS

The Per-Type Mode Catalogues of Every Cell of the Human Nervous and Endocrine System

A Companion Volume to the Layer 6 Catalogues

Keyed, chapter by chapter, to the five-volume neuron catalogue (301 types) and to LAYER_06_OTHER_CELLS_MANUSCRIPT (85 types), in the Certified_Neuroscientist corpus of D. R. Dison, Founder, Artificial Intelligence University (AIU).

Date of compilation: August 9, 2026.


INTRODUCTION: THE THEORY OF MODES, STATED ONCE

This volume rests on one distinction, applied three hundred and eighty-six times.

• STATE is a cell's continuous, instantaneous condition: the current values of its variables - membrane voltage, calcium level, adaptation current, vesicle stock.

State changes every millisecond, smoothly.

• MODE is a cell's discrete operating regime: which qualitative rule it is currently running - tonic firing, bursting, silent, plateau, secreting, patrolling.

Mode changes rarely and switch-like, and while a mode holds, the cell behaves like a different machine.

The two lock together in one sentence: a cell's state moves continuously within its current mode, and when the state crosses certain boundaries - or when a neuromodulator or hormone moves the boundaries - the cell switches mode.

The engineering formalism for such a machine has a name: the HYBRID AUTOMATON - a small set of discrete modes, continuous state evolving inside the current mode, and a transition table saying which events flip which mode into which.

Every entry in this volume is a hybrid automaton specification, in four fixed elements.

• THE MODE REGISTER: the named list of the type's modes.

• PER-MODE TRANSFER FUNCTIONS: the input-to-output rule that holds while each mode holds.

• THE TRANSITION TABLE: the trigger, direction, and timescale of every mode switch.

• FAULT REGIMES AND WATCHDOGS: the regimes the type can be driven into but does not use.

THE ADMISSION RULE

A regime earns a place in the mode register only if the healthy system USES it under some normal operating condition.

Everything else is a fault regime: not a mode with its own transfer function, but a boundary to be watched - and the tissue itself runs the watchdogs, from feedback inhibition to astrocytic potassium buffering to homeostatic plasticity.

The rule is applied PER TYPE, because one regime can be an instrument in one cell and an injury in another: depolarisation block sits in the register of the olfactory sensory neuron (which uses it to encode strong odours) and of the dopamine neuron (where antipsychotic drugs exploit it), but in the fault table of the hippocampal pyramidal cell, where it appears only in seizure.

THE UNIVERSAL MENU

The modes of the register entries below are drawn from a universal menu - silent, tonic, adapting, burst, pacemaker, plateau or bistable, and, beyond the electrical, the secretory, activation, and life-cycle regimes of the non-neuronal cells.

But the menu is not an endowment: each type implements only the subset its molecular equipment permits, at its own parameter settings, under its own transition table.

Mode-poor is as meaningful as mode-rich: a register of two says the system trusts this cell to relay or to veto, not to decide; a register of four says the cell is itself a decision-maker.

THE NAMING CONVENTION

Every registered mode carries a formal name (the literature's term where one exists) and a coined plain-English name in the corpus tradition - vivid, functional, unpretentious: the Faithful Relay, the Wake-Up Call, the Background Tick, the Reward Exclamation, the Fasting Silence, the Feeding Pulse.

WHERE MODE SETTING COMES FROM

A recurring finding of the entries below: transitions are thrown from above at least as often as from within.

Brain states at Layers 10 and 11 - sleep and waking, arousal, stress, the circadian phase - broadcast neuromodulators and hormones through the framework's volume transmission channel, and each broadcast throws millions of cellular mode switches at once.

A brain state is, in this vocabulary, a coordinated assignment of modes across the whole cellular population; the entries record, transition by transition, where each switch is thrown from.

HOW TO USE THIS VOLUME

Each entry is written PASTE-READY: it is formatted exactly as a MODES AND MODE TRANSITIONS section for its source chapter, under the standing chapter template version 3 (see LAYER_06_CHAPTER_TEMPLATE_v3), and keyed to its source chapter number.

Chapters written after the template's adoption carry their modes natively; the 386 entries here retrofit the chapters written before it, and a future merge into the source volumes is a mechanical splice.

The entries follow in source order: first the 301 chapters of the neuron catalogue, then the 85 chapters of the other-cells catalogue.


PART ONE. THE NEURON CATALOGUE, CHAPTERS 1 TO 301


ENTRY 1. L2/3 IT (Layer 2/3 Intra-Telencephalic) (Context-Weaving Neuron) (keyed to neuron catalogue Chapter 1)

MODES AND MODE TRANSITIONS:

This is a mode-rich integrator whose interest lies less in its soma than in its dendrites, because the apical tuft runs its own graded computation on top of an ordinary adapting spike train.

• THE MODE REGISTER.

◦ Regular-spiking adapting firing - the Weaver's Tick - the everyday tonic state in which a maintained input gives a train that slows over a few hundred milliseconds, so the cell reports drive while gradually discounting it.

◦ Graded apical calcium mode - the Context Flash - a dendritic calcium-mediated action potential in the layer 1 tuft that is largest just above threshold and damped by stronger drive, letting one dendrite compute exclusive-or and bind local sensation to long-range context.

◦ Sparse quiescence - the Held Breath - the near-silent low-rate state the cell keeps during cortical down states and inattention, spending most of its life close to rest.

• PER-MODE TRANSFER FUNCTIONS.

◦ In the Weaver's Tick the cell rests near minus 70 millivolts, fires above roughly minus 40 millivolts at a few to low tens of hertz, with input resistance 40 to 120 megohms and a 10 to 20 millisecond time constant, and an unusually fast action-potential onset (confidence high).

◦ In the Context Flash the dendritic calcium event is tuned to peak near 240 picoamperes of drive and is suppressed near 450 picoamperes, converting coincident tuft and somatic activity into a burst (confidence moderate, chiefly human tissue).

◦ In the Held Breath rate falls below one spike per second and the cell simply integrates subthreshold input (confidence high).

• THE TRANSITION TABLE.

◦ Basal or tuft input crossing threshold - Held Breath to Weaver's Tick - milliseconds.

◦ Coincidence of a back-propagating spike with tuft depolarisation from layer 1 feedback - Weaver's Tick to Context Flash - within a 5 to 15 millisecond window.

◦ Arousal and acetylcholine broadcast from below, thrown from above by brain state - lifts the cell out of the Held Breath and biases it toward flashing - seconds.

• FAULT REGIMES AND WATCHDOGS.

◦ Depolarisation block, where excessive drive inactivates sodium channels and firing stalls, is a boundary this cell does not use; runaway recurrent excitation among these densely interconnected cells is the real danger, watched by parvalbumin and somatostatin inhibition and by homeostatic scaling that holds mean rate near a target.


ENTRY 2. L4 IT (Layer 4 Intra-Telencephalic) (Arrival-Amplifying Neuron) (keyed to neuron catalogue Chapter 2)

MODES AND MODE TRANSITIONS:

This is a near two-mode change-detector, an honest linear adder whose strong fast adaptation makes it report the arrival of a signal rather than its steady presence.

• THE MODE REGISTER.

◦ Onset-then-adapt firing - the Arrival Ping - the phasic state in which a maintained input gives a brief opening burst then a slow train, so the layer signals change.

◦ Low tonic idling - the Quiet Floor - the sparse baseline the cell keeps between sensory events.

• PER-MODE TRANSFER FUNCTIONS.

◦ In the Arrival Ping the cell rests near minus 70 to minus 75 millivolts, fires above about minus 45 millivolts, has high input resistance of 100 to 200 megohms and a short 8 to 15 millisecond time constant, and sums its spine inputs almost linearly with no supralinear cooperativity (confidence high).

◦ In the Quiet Floor the cell fires sparsely and passively integrates, its depressing thalamic synapses ensuring the second and third pulse of a train count for less than the first (confidence high).

• THE TRANSITION TABLE.

◦ A synchronous thalamic volley landing near the soma - Quiet Floor to Arrival Ping - milliseconds.

◦ Strong short-term depression of thalamic release during a sustained input - Arrival Ping back toward Quiet Floor - tens to hundreds of milliseconds.

◦ Acetylcholine and noradrenaline during attention and arousal, thrown from above - raise the gain of the whole transition - seconds.

• FAULT REGIMES AND WATCHDOGS.

◦ This cell has no notably used pathological regime; its worst boundary is loop instability in the recurrently connected layer, which slightly higher gain would drive into seizure, watched by fast-spiking parvalbumin inhibition arriving one to two milliseconds behind each volley.


ENTRY 3. L5 IT (Layer 5 Intra-Telencephalic) (Action-Proposing Neuron) (keyed to neuron catalogue Chapter 3)

MODES AND MODE TRANSITIONS:

This is a deliberately mode-poor cell, a regular-spiking adapting integrator that almost never bursts, and its diagnostic feature is a regime it lacks rather than one it uses.

• THE MODE REGISTER.

◦ Regular-spiking adapting firing - the Steady Proposal - the single everyday state, in which drive gives an adapting train and the cell forwards its proposal to the extra-telencephalic output cell beside it.

◦ Sparse quiescence - the Waiting Line - the low-rate baseline held between proposals.

• PER-MODE TRANSFER FUNCTIONS.

◦ In the Steady Proposal the cell rests near minus 72 millivolts, fires above about minus 45 millivolts at roughly 1 to 10 hertz, has input resistance 60 to 150 megohms and a 12 to 20 millisecond time constant, and shows far less hyperpolarisation-activated sag current, carried by hyperpolarisation-activated cyclic nucleotide-gated (HCN) channels, than its bursting neighbour (confidence high).

◦ In the Waiting Line the cell idles subthreshold, its thin apical tuft still able to fire a local N-methyl-D-aspartate (NMDA) receptor spike in a single branch (confidence moderate).

• THE TRANSITION TABLE.

◦ Feedforward excitation from layers 2/3 and 4 crossing threshold - Waiting Line to Steady Proposal - milliseconds.

◦ A clustered apical input unblocking NMDA receptors - briefly boosts a Steady Proposal without a somatic burst - tens of milliseconds.

◦ Neuromodulatory tone, thrown from above by arousal - sets baseline excitability - seconds.

• FAULT REGIMES AND WATCHDOGS.

◦ No pathological firing regime is characteristically used; because this cell drives the extra-telencephalic output one-way, its watchdog concern is simply not running away, held by local inhibition and adaptation.


ENTRY 4. L5 ET (Layer 5 Extra-Telencephalic) (Body-Commanding Neuron) (keyed to neuron catalogue Chapter 4)

MODES AND MODE TRANSITIONS:

This is one of the great mode-rich cells of the cortex, a two-compartment coincidence detector whose soma and layer 1 tuft each carry a spike generator, so it switches between single spikes and dendritically-driven bursts.

• THE MODE REGISTER.

◦ Intrinsically bursting tonic firing - the Standing Command - the deep-output default, in which steady drive gives repetitive bursts rather than an adapting train.

◦ Backpropagation-activated calcium burst - the Coincidence Salvo - a long calcium plateau in the tuft that turns one somatic spike into a high-frequency burst when top and base are active together.

◦ Sag-and-rebound - the Spring-Back - the recovery from inhibition powered by a large HCN1-carried sag current that lets the voltage dip then creep back up.

• PER-MODE TRANSFER FUNCTIONS.

◦ In the Standing Command the cell has low input resistance of 20 to 60 megohms, rests near minus 68 to minus 75 millivolts, and fires above about minus 45 millivolts in bursts (confidence high).

◦ In the Coincidence Salvo a train of roughly four action potentials at about 100 hertz coincides with tuft depolarisation to trigger a calcium plateau, appearing reliably only when the apical trunk exceeds roughly 350 micrometres, with plateau current near 0.5 to 1.0 nanoamperes (confidence high).

◦ In the Spring-Back a negative current makes the voltage sag then rebound within about a hundred milliseconds (confidence high).

• THE TRANSITION TABLE.

◦ Coincident base drive and layer 1 feedback - Standing Command to Coincidence Salvo - a 5 to 15 millisecond window.

◦ Release from somatostatin or parvalbumin inhibition - Spring-Back into renewed firing - tens of milliseconds.

◦ Arousal and attention setting apical gain, thrown from above - biases the cell toward salvos - seconds.

• FAULT REGIMES AND WATCHDOGS.

◦ Uncontrolled plateau firing driving the corticospinal output too hard is the boundary risk; distal somatostatin inhibition onto the tuft is the biological watchdog that vetoes runaway calcium spikes, and the cell lives permanently near its metabolic ceiling so any energy shortfall hits its most distal terminals first.


ENTRY 5. L5/6 NP (Layer 5 and 6 Near-Projecting) (Short-Reach Neuron) (keyed to neuron catalogue Chapter 5)

MODES AND MODE TRANSITIONS:

This is a mode-poor slow integrator, and honesty is required, because the register rests on very few recordings and confidence is lower than for any other type here.

• THE MODE REGISTER.

◦ Slow tonic integration - the Slow Murmur - the single defensible everyday state, a cell easy to depolarise but hard to drive fast, reporting the average of its inputs over tens of milliseconds.

◦ Sparse quiescence - the Long Pause - the low-rate baseline expected of a small, slow, wide-field local channel.

• PER-MODE TRANSFER FUNCTIONS.

◦ In the Slow Murmur input resistance is high at 100 to 250 megohms, the membrane time constant slow at 20 to 30 milliseconds, action potentials are comparatively broad and the maximum rate low, so the cell integrates rather than detects coincidence (confidence low, few patch-clamp and patch-sequencing recordings).

◦ In the Long Pause the cell idles, its long thin apical dendrite able in principle to support an NMDA (N-methyl-D-aspartate) receptor spike, though no dendritic recording from a confirmed human near-projecting cell exists (confidence low).

• THE TRANSITION TABLE.

◦ Local deep-layer excitation crossing threshold - Long Pause to Slow Murmur - tens of milliseconds, slow by design.

◦ Peptide neuromodulation, hinted at by enriched thyrotropin-releasing hormone receptor expression - sets baseline gain by slow volume transmission - seconds (confidence low).

• FAULT REGIMES AND WATCHDOGS.

◦ No fault regime has been characterised for this type, and it is better to say so plainly than to invent one; its high input resistance would make it unusually sensitive to small weight changes, so a simulation should flag any imposed instability as unverified.


ENTRY 6. L6 CT (Layer 6 Cortico-Thalamic) (Input-Tuning Neuron) (keyed to neuron catalogue Chapter 6)

MODES AND MODE TRANSITIONS:

This is a two-mode cell whose important computation lives in its facilitating synapse rather than its membrane, so it behaves as a temporal differentiator that ignores isolated spikes and answers sustained demand.

• THE MODE REGISTER.

◦ Sparse tonic firing - the Quiet Sentry - the everyday state of the quietest layer in the cortex, many cells firing under one spike per second.

◦ High-frequency facilitating burst - the Rising Insistence - short bursts whose corticothalamic terminals grow progressively stronger through the train, shifting the thalamic relay cell toward excitation.

• PER-MODE TRANSFER FUNCTIONS.

◦ In the Quiet Sentry the cell rests near minus 72 millivolts, fires above about minus 45 millivolts, has input resistance 100 to 200 megohms and a 15 to 25 millisecond time constant (confidence high).

◦ In the Rising Insistence a short high-frequency burst drives strongly facilitating synapses and slow group I metabotropic glutamate receptors, whose depolarisations last hundreds of milliseconds to seconds and can hold a relay cell in tonic mode (confidence high).

• THE TRANSITION TABLE.

◦ Descending drive from layer 5 crossing threshold - Quiet Sentry to Rising Insistence - milliseconds.

◦ Sustained cortical demand progressively facilitating the terminals - deepens the Rising Insistence over a burst - tens to hundreds of milliseconds.

◦ Sleep pressure and attentional state, thrown from above, and adenosine accumulating with time awake - set baseline output and switch thalamic relay cells between burst and tonic mode - seconds to minutes.

• FAULT REGIMES AND WATCHDOGS.

◦ This cell has no characteristically used fault regime; its reticular-nucleus collaterals implement a built-in feedforward inhibition that watchdogs its own excitatory push on the relay, keeping every downward correction slower than the upward sensory signal.


ENTRY 7. L6 IT (Layer 6 Intra-Telencephalic) (Deep-Layer Courier Neuron) (keyed to neuron catalogue Chapter 7)

MODES AND MODE TRANSITIONS:

This is a mode-poor adapting integrator, a sparse deep courier whose resting rate is often under one spike per second.

• THE MODE REGISTER.

◦ Regular-spiking adapting firing - the Courier's Round - the single everyday state, in which drive gives a strongly adapting train that carries expectation to earlier areas.

◦ Sparse quiescence - the Depot - the sub-one-hertz baseline held between deliveries.

• PER-MODE TRANSFER FUNCTIONS.

◦ In the Courier's Round the cell rests near minus 72 millivolts, fires above about minus 45 millivolts, has input resistance 100 to 250 megohms, a slow 15 to 25 millisecond time constant, a modest sag from the hyperpolarisation-activated current I h, and strong spike-frequency adaptation (confidence high).

◦ In the Depot the cell idles, its apical trunk able to support a local NMDA (N-methyl-D-aspartate) receptor spike in a single branch (confidence moderate).

• THE TRANSITION TABLE.

◦ Local layer 6 and layer 5 excitation crossing threshold - Depot to Courier's Round - milliseconds.

◦ Adenosine accumulating with time awake, thrown from above by sleep pressure - suppresses these cells presynaptically and dials down cortical feedback - minutes to hours.

• FAULT REGIMES AND WATCHDOGS.

◦ No pathological regime is characteristically used; ordinary local inhibition and strong adaptation are the watchdogs holding this sparse cell to its quiet round.


ENTRY 8. L6 IT CAR3 (Layer 6 Intra-Telencephalic Carbonic Anhydrase 3) (Cortex-To-Cortex Linking Neuron) (keyed to neuron catalogue Chapter 8)

MODES AND MODE TRANSITIONS:

This is a two-mode cell defined by timing, a callosal linker whose apical tuft can switch it from single spikes to a burst that signals coincidence between local content and long-range context.

• THE MODE REGISTER.

◦ Regular-spiking adapting firing - the Even Relay - the everyday tonic state, an adapting train delivered across the callosum on its own delay line.

◦ Tuft-soma coincidence burst - the Both-True Burst - a calcium-based tuft computation that makes the cell fire a burst instead of single spikes when local and long-range inputs coincide.

• PER-MODE TRANSFER FUNCTIONS.

◦ In the Even Relay membrane capacitance is around 200 picofarads, input resistance around 100 megohms, time constant 12 to 20 milliseconds, resting potential about minus 70 millivolts, soft threshold minus 50, reset minus 58, with spike-frequency adaptation of about 60 picoamperes (confidence high).

◦ In the Both-True Burst coincident tuft and somatic activity trigger a burst signalling agreement between content and context, gated by NMDA (N-methyl-D-aspartate) receptors that pass current only when the target is already partly depolarised (confidence moderate).

• THE TRANSITION TABLE.

◦ Coincident basal drive and apical tuft input from thalamic matrix or feedback - Even Relay to Both-True Burst - a few to fifteen milliseconds.

◦ Activity-dependent myelination adjusting conduction delay - retunes the transition over days of learning - slow structural.

◦ Neuromodulatory tone, thrown from above - sets baseline gain - seconds.

• FAULT REGIMES AND WATCHDOGS.

◦ No characteristically used fault regime; roughly 10 to 20 percent of this cell's terminals contact inhibitory interneurons, a built-in watchdog that keeps the interhemispheric link from turning into runaway excitation.


ENTRY 9. L6B (Layer 6 Sublayer B) (Wakefulness-Listening Neuron) (keyed to neuron catalogue Chapter 9)

MODES AND MODE TRANSITIONS:

This is a mode-rich cell and a rarity among cortical excitatory neurons, because a prominent subgroup carries its own pacemaker and because its excitability is set by a peptide it hears almost alone in the neocortex.

• THE MODE REGISTER.

◦ Spontaneous rhythmic bursting - the Never-Resting Tick - the pacemaker state of a subgroup that bursts with no input at all, an unusual continuous load for a cortical excitatory cell.

◦ Delayed spiking - the Late Answer - a delay to firing on depolarising steps, so the cell responds only to sustained drive.

◦ Plateau-potential analogue memory - the Long Hold - a depolarised plateau lasting hundreds of milliseconds after strong drive, holding a trace after the input has gone.

• PER-MODE TRANSFER FUNCTIONS.

◦ In the Never-Resting Tick the cell rests near minus 72 millivolts and bursts rhythmically in the absence of input, with strong hyperpolarisation-activated rectification indicating the current I h (confidence moderate).

◦ In the Late Answer a depolarising step gives a delayed onset, and at stronger steps spike inactivation is followed by a long depolarised plateau and a depolarising afterpotential (confidence moderate).

◦ In the Long Hold the plateau outlasts the input by hundreds of milliseconds, working partly through peptide reception by volume transmission (confidence moderate).

• THE TRANSITION TABLE.

◦ Orexin, also called hypocretin, acting through orexin receptor type 2, thrown from above by the wake-promoting hypothalamus - lifts the cell toward firing, near zero in slow-wave sleep and high in waking - seconds.

◦ Strong higher-order thalamic or corticocortical drive - Late Answer into the Long Hold - hundreds of milliseconds.

◦ Neurotensin and cholecystokinin (a gut-brain peptide) reception - modulate excitability - seconds.

• FAULT REGIMES AND WATCHDOGS.

◦ Because a subgroup pace-makes continuously, prolonged plateau firing bordering on depolarisation block is a boundary to watch, guarded by local inhibition; the pacemaker regime itself is legitimately in the register because the healthy cell uses it to keep pyramidal apical dendrites pre-set for behavioural state.


ENTRY 10. PYRAMIDAL CELLS (keyed to neuron catalogue Chapter 10)

MODES AND MODE TRANSITIONS:

This is the archetypal mode-rich cortical cell, and because the chapter treats the pyramidal class as a whole, its register is the union of the schemes that specific pyramidal types specialise.

• THE MODE REGISTER.

◦ Regular-spiking adapting firing - the Common Tick - the everyday tonic state of most pyramidal cells, an adapting train with moderate accommodation.

◦ Bursting - the Emphasis - a second output channel, whether intrinsic bursting in deep cells or the graded dendritic calcium spike of upper-layer human cells that computes exclusive-or.

◦ Backpropagation-activated calcium plateau - the Coincidence Salvo - the layer 5 mechanism in which a spike travelling back from the axon coincides with tuft input and turns one spike into a burst.

◦ Sparse quiescence - the Rest - the low-rate baseline, since rates run from under 0.1 to 50 hertz.

• PER-MODE TRANSFER FUNCTIONS.

◦ In the Common Tick the cell rests near minus 70 millivolts, fires above about minus 50, has a one-millisecond spike, input resistance 40 to 120 megohms, and moderate adaptation (confidence high).

◦ In the Emphasis, human upper-layer dendritic calcium spikes are graded, largest at threshold and damped by stronger input (confidence high).

◦ In the Coincidence Salvo a tuft-to-soma coincidence within a 5 to 15 millisecond window triggers a plateau current of 0.5 to 1.0 nanoamperes (confidence high).

• THE TRANSITION TABLE.

◦ Input crossing threshold - Rest to Common Tick - milliseconds.

◦ Coincidence of back-propagating spike and tuft input - Common Tick to Coincidence Salvo - a 5 to 15 millisecond window.

◦ Neuromodulator level, thrown from above by arousal and reward - sets baseline gain and burst propensity - seconds.

• FAULT REGIMES AND WATCHDOGS.

◦ Runaway recurrent excitation ending in seizure or depolarisation block is the boundary the class must avoid, watched by the almost entirely local inhibitory network and by homeostatic scaling that rescales all a cell's synapses to hold its average rate in range.


ENTRY 11. SPINY STELLATE CELLS (keyed to neuron catalogue Chapter 11)

MODES AND MODE TRANSITIONS:

This is a two-mode change-detector whose signature computation, recurrent amplification, is a network property no single-cell register can hold, so its individual modes must be read against that loop.

• THE MODE REGISTER.

◦ Regular-spiking coincidence detection - the Amplifier's Beat - the everyday state, a short 8 to 12 millisecond time constant making the cell a coincidence detector that multiplies weak thalamic drive by local recurrence.

◦ Sparse quiescence - the Standby - the low-rate baseline of a cell that is sparse at rest.

• PER-MODE TRANSFER FUNCTIONS.

◦ In the Amplifier's Beat the cell rests near minus 70 millivolts, fires above about minus 45, shows moderate adaptation, and carries both high-conductance and low-conductance N-methyl-D-aspartate (NMDA) receptors giving dendritic branches supralinear summation; depressing thalamic synapses make it compute the derivative of its input (confidence high).

◦ In the Standby the cell idles, its output depending on loop gain across thousands of like cells, where slightly higher gain would make the layer seize (confidence high).

• THE TRANSITION TABLE.

◦ A synchronous thalamic volley - Standby to Amplifier's Beat - milliseconds.

◦ Strong short-term depression of thalamic release - phasic return toward Standby - tens to hundreds of milliseconds.

◦ Acetylcholine and noradrenaline, thrown from above by arousal - set loop gain - seconds.

• FAULT REGIMES AND WATCHDOGS.

◦ The used-and-avoided regime here is seizure through excess loop gain; fast-spiking inhibition arriving one to two milliseconds behind each volley is the watchdog, and any model must keep recurrent gain just below the unstable boundary.


ENTRY 12. STAR PYRAMID CELLS (keyed to neuron catalogue Chapter 12)

MODES AND MODE TRANSITIONS:

This is a two-mode gain modulator, a layer 4 cell whose short apical dendrite lets it multiply thalamic drive by context and lets it generate a rhythm rather than merely relay.

• THE MODE REGISTER.

◦ Regular-spiking gain-modulated firing - the Weighted Relay - the everyday state, in which apical input from layer 3 depolarises the trunk, unblocks N-methyl-D-aspartate (NMDA) receptors on the basal tree, and multiplies the effect of thalamic input.

◦ Dendritic bursting - the Alpha Ring - a burst mode underlying the post-stimulus alpha rhythm, the roughly 10 hertz oscillation appearing after a visual stimulus ends.

• PER-MODE TRANSFER FUNCTIONS.

◦ In the Weighted Relay the cell rests near minus 70 millivolts, fires above about minus 47, has a 10 to 15 millisecond time constant, moderate adaptation, and depressing thalamic synapses (confidence high).

◦ In the Alpha Ring dendritic bursting paces an approximately 10 hertz post-stimulus rhythm, so the cell acts as a rhythm generator (confidence moderate).

• THE TRANSITION TABLE.

◦ Coincident thalamic drive and layer 3 apical input - deepens the Weighted Relay by NMDA unblocking - tens of milliseconds.

◦ Stimulus offset - Weighted Relay to Alpha Ring - onset within tens to hundreds of milliseconds.

◦ Attention and arousal, thrown from above - set the multiplier gain - seconds.

• FAULT REGIMES AND WATCHDOGS.

◦ No characteristically used fault regime; fast-spiking inhibition delayed about 1.5 milliseconds is the watchdog, and the cell's honest role is a relay with an opinion rather than a runaway amplifier.


ENTRY 13. BETZ CELLS (keyed to neuron catalogue Chapter 13)

MODES AND MODE TRANSITIONS:

This is a mode-rich output cell with a firing signature rodents simply do not have, a three-phase primate command in which early firing, a pause and a delayed acceleration follow one another within a single movement.

• THE MODE REGISTER.

◦ Pre-movement onset firing - the Early Word - a brief discharge roughly 40 to 100 milliseconds before a movement begins, among the earliest cortical activity of a voluntary act.

◦ Early cessation - the Held Beat - the near-silent pause that follows the onset, a distinctively primate feature.

◦ Delayed sustained acceleration - the Rising Drive - a high, sustained, accelerating rate that carries the command down the thickest axon in the corticospinal tract.

• PER-MODE TRANSFER FUNCTIONS.

◦ In the Early Word the giant, leaky cell fires briefly, having low input resistance and resting near minus 68 to minus 75 millivolts (confidence moderate, mostly primate recordings).

◦ In the Held Beat firing falls almost silent before reaccelerating (confidence moderate).

◦ In the Rising Drive spike frequency and magnitude climb to a high sustained rate, conducting faster than 70 metres per second to the spinal ventral horn (confidence moderate).

• THE TRANSITION TABLE.

◦ A voluntary motor command assembled across sensorimotor cortex - Early Word to Held Beat to Rising Drive - the whole sequence unfolding over tens to hundreds of milliseconds within one movement.

◦ Perisomatic parvalbumin basket inhibition, wrapping the spine-free soma - enforces the Held Beat - milliseconds.

◦ Acetylcholine, serotonin and dopamine, thrown from above by arousal and reward - tune excitability across the sequence - seconds.

• FAULT REGIMES AND WATCHDOGS.

◦ The cell lives at an extreme metabolic ceiling, so any drop in adenosine triphosphate supply hits its most distal terminals first, exactly the pattern seen in amyotrophic lateral sclerosis; dense perisomatic parvalbumin inhibition is the watchdog against uncontrolled corticospinal drive.


ENTRY 14. MEYNERT CELLS (keyed to neuron catalogue Chapter 14)

MODES AND MODE TRANSITIONS:

This is a two-mode motion relay, a large direction-selective cell whose value is speed, getting an early motion signal to the eye-movement system and the motion area at nearly the same moment.

• THE MODE REGISTER.

◦ Direction-tuned regular-spiking firing - the One-Way Look - the everyday state, responding vigorously to motion one way and barely at all to the other.

◦ Motion-onset transient - the Urgent Snap - the phasic burst delivered fast down two myelinated collaterals when motion sweeps the preferred direction along the long axis of the dendritic tree.

• PER-MODE TRANSFER FUNCTIONS.

◦ In the One-Way Look the cell fires a regular-spiking train tuned across eight directions, its heavy myelination making it fast (confidence moderate).

◦ In the Urgent Snap motion along the preferred axis recruits inputs in a sequence that summates far better than the reverse, delivering identical spikes to the superior colliculus at roughly 2 to 5 milliseconds and to area V5 or middle temporal area (MT) at roughly 4 to 8 milliseconds (confidence moderate).

• THE TRANSITION TABLE.

◦ Magnocellular motion drive from layer 4C alpha crossing threshold in the preferred direction - One-Way Look to Urgent Snap - milliseconds.

◦ Local inhibition from layer 5 and 6 interneurons - shunts the response and resets - milliseconds.

◦ Arousal, thrown from above - sets baseline excitability - seconds.

• FAULT REGIMES AND WATCHDOGS.

◦ No characteristically used fault regime; the cell continuously remodels its own structure to match its workload, shrinking after visual deprivation, and local inhibition is the watchdog on its fast divergent output.


ENTRY 15. VON ECONOMO NEURONS (Spindle Neurons) (keyed to neuron catalogue Chapter 15)

MODES AND MODE TRANSITIONS:

This is a type whose register must be stated as largely unknown, because direct recordings from human von Economo neurons do not exist, so the modes below are inferred from their layer 5 extra-telencephalic transcriptomic identity and stated at low confidence.

• THE MODE REGISTER.

◦ Presumed regular-spiking or bursting tonic firing - the Fast Report - the inferred everyday state of a deep-output projection cell like its Betz relatives, whose excitability should be strongly set by ambient arousal chemicals.

◦ Presumed sparse quiescence - the Held Report - the low-rate baseline expected of a long-range projection neuron.

• PER-MODE TRANSFER FUNCTIONS.

◦ In the Fast Report firing is undocumented, but expression of the alpha 1A adrenoceptor and dopamine D3 receptor implies excitability tracks noradrenaline and dopamine, and the bipolar geometry forces the cell to compare a superficial input stream with a deep one along a single axis (confidence low).

◦ In the Held Report the cell is presumed to idle between reports (confidence low).

• THE TRANSITION TABLE.

◦ Coincident superficial and deep input along the spindle axis - lifts the cell from the Held Report toward the Fast Report - milliseconds, inferred.

◦ Ambient noradrenaline and dopamine, thrown from above by arousal and social salience - set gain - seconds, inferred.

• FAULT REGIMES AND WATCHDOGS.

◦ The clearest evidence about these cells is their selective vulnerability, dying early with tau and TDP-43 (transactive response DNA-binding protein of 43 kilodaltons) deposits in frontotemporal lobar degeneration, which suggests a cell living close to a metabolic or protein-handling edge; no normal fault regime can be assigned with confidence.


ENTRY 16. FORK CELLS (keyed to neuron catalogue Chapter 16)

MODES AND MODE TRANSITIONS:

This is another type unrecorded in humans, so its register is inferred from its close kinship with von Economo neurons and stated at low confidence, with the one distinctive feature being a two-pronged apical shaft that supports a logical AND across two patches of superficial cortex.

• THE MODE REGISTER.

◦ Presumed tonic projection firing - the Twin-Prong Report - the inferred everyday state, shared with von Economo neurons.

◦ Two-compartment coincidence - the Both-Prongs Rule - a mode in which each apical prong can generate its own local calcium spike and the cell fires only when both prongs and the basal dendrite agree.

• PER-MODE TRANSFER FUNCTIONS.

◦ In the Twin-Prong Report firing is undocumented; expression of the alpha 1A adrenoceptor and vesicular monoamine transporter 2 implies excitability is set by ambient monoamines as in its neighbours (confidence low).

◦ In the Both-Prongs Rule two electrically semi-independent prongs each carry a slow regenerative calcium spike, implementing an AND that then requires the basal dendrite to agree before the soma fires (confidence low, inferred from geometry).

• THE TRANSITION TABLE.

◦ Coincident input on apical prong A, apical prong B and the basal dendrite - toward firing under the Both-Prongs Rule - milliseconds, inferred.

◦ Ambient noradrenaline setting gain, thrown from above - sets excitability - seconds, inferred.

• FAULT REGIMES AND WATCHDOGS.

◦ As with von Economo neurons, the only firm evidence is shared vulnerability, fork cells and von Economo neurons degenerating in synchrony in early behavioural-variant frontotemporal dementia; no normal fault regime can be assigned, and the register should be treated as the minimal defensible one.


ENTRY 17. CAJAL-RETZIUS CELLS (keyed to neuron catalogue Chapter 17)

MODES AND MODE TRANSITIONS:

This is a cell whose real regime axis is not electrical but secretory, because its decisive output is the constitutive release of reelin into the tissue, and its modes must be read on that axis with spiking as a minor accompaniment.

• THE MODE REGISTER.

◦ Constitutive reelin secretion - the Steady Beacon - the always-on basal secretory state that lays a reelin gradient guiding every migrating neuron below, released continuously rather than at a synapse.

◦ Immature spontaneous spiking - the Early Flicker - broad slow action potentials with high input resistance, where in early development gamma-aminobutyric acid (GABA), the transmitter that becomes inhibitory later, acts as an accelerator and helps drive the first cortical rhythms.

◦ Programmed deletion - the Timed Exit - the instructed, scheduled cell death that ends the type's job in late fetal and early postnatal life.

• PER-MODE TRANSFER FUNCTIONS.

◦ In the Steady Beacon the cell continuously manufactures and exports reelin, a very large glycoprotein, demanding a well-developed endoplasmic reticulum and Golgi apparatus, its rate roughly constant rather than pulsatile (confidence high for constitutive release).

◦ In the Early Flicker small currents give large slow spikes, and spontaneous activity synchronises the earliest patterned cortical activity (confidence moderate).

◦ In the Timed Exit a developmental clock triggers apoptosis, after which some GABA-producing interneurons take over reelin production in smaller amounts (confidence high).

• THE TRANSITION TABLE.

◦ A developmental clock in gestational weeks, thrown from above by the maturation programme - the Steady Beacon and Early Flicker into the Timed Exit - days to weeks.

◦ Positional signals from the cortical hem and pial surface - set secretory placement - developmental.

• FAULT REGIMES AND WATCHDOGS.

◦ The fault of interest is failure or mistiming of secretion rather than of firing; losing the reelin gradient makes migrating neurons stop short or overshoot, the disordered pattern of the reeler mouse and human reelin mutations, watched by radial glia that terminate each migration at the pial end.


ENTRY 18. SUBPLATE NEURONS (First-Born Wiring-Guide Neuron) (keyed to neuron catalogue Chapter 18)

MODES AND MODE TRANSITIONS:

This is a developmentally mode-rich cell whose function is to be a temporary scaffold and amplifier and then largely delete itself, so its register is dominated by early activity states most adult cells never enter.

• THE MODE REGISTER.

◦ Spindle-burst amplification - the Waiting Volley - the signature fetal state, generating and amplifying brief oscillatory volleys that sweep the otherwise silent immature cortex.

◦ Early tonic spiking - the First Voice - electrical maturity long before any other cortical neuron, spiking while the cortical plate is still quiet.

◦ Programmed deletion - the Scheduled Exit - the timed death that removes the majority and sets the closing edge of the earliest critical periods, leaving sparse interstitial survivors.

• PER-MODE TRANSFER FUNCTIONS.

◦ In the Waiting Volley long membrane time constants and high input resistance make small inputs produce large responses, suiting an amplifier that instructs cortical wiring through activity (confidence high).

◦ In the First Voice the cell spikes on the first thalamocortical, callosal and brainstem inputs, its pioneer axons guiding the corticothalamic system (confidence high).

◦ In the Scheduled Exit programmed cell death removes most of the population in late fetal and early postnatal life (confidence high).

• THE TRANSITION TABLE.

◦ Spontaneous retinal waves and the first thalamocortical activity - drive the Waiting Volley - milliseconds to seconds.

◦ A developmental clock, thrown from above - the active states into the Scheduled Exit - days to weeks.

◦ In rodents, orexin, also called hypocretin, directly excites layer 6b survivors, hinting at a retained arousal role, untested in human survivors.

• FAULT REGIMES AND WATCHDOGS.

◦ Because these are the only electrically active cells in an otherwise quiet cortex, they are selectively killed by a shortage of oxygen or blood in premature infants, and abnormal survival is a reproducible finding in schizophrenia; microglia clear the dying cells and are also the cells whose activation damages survivors in preterm injury.


ENTRY 19. CORTICAL BASKET CELLS (keyed to neuron catalogue Chapter 19)

MODES AND MODE TRANSITIONS:

This is the classic fast-spiking cell, a near single-mode clock whose job is to pace gamma-band rhythm, though a self-braking autapse and gap-junction coupling give it two supporting regimes.

• THE MODE REGISTER.

◦ Sustained fast-spiking - the Gamma Metronome - the defining state, firing 100 to 200 spikes per second for long stretches with almost no slowing to pace the 30 to 80 cycles-per-second rhythm that binds a percept.

◦ Gap-junction synchronised firing - the Locked Chorus - a network state in which basket cells wired by connexin-36 tunnels synchronise within a millisecond.

◦ Autaptic silence - the Self-Hush - the brief private pause each cell imposes on itself after a spike through robust self-innervation.

• PER-MODE TRANSFER FUNCTIONS.

◦ In the Gamma Metronome the action potential is narrow at 0.2 to 0.4 milliseconds because Kv3-family potassium channels repolarise fast, with a short 5 to 10 millisecond time constant, low input resistance of 60 to 150 megohms, and a fast upstroke depending on Nav1.1 sodium channels encoded by suprachiasmatic (SCN)1A (confidence high).

◦ In the Locked Chorus a coupling coefficient of 0.02 to 0.15 lets a horizontally synchronised group fire as one (confidence high).

◦ In the Self-Hush the autapse prolongs the cell's own silence after each spike (confidence high).

• THE TRANSITION TABLE.

◦ Strong glutamatergic drive from local pyramidal cells - Standby to Gamma Metronome - milliseconds.

◦ Gap-junction current from coupled partners - Gamma Metronome to Locked Chorus - within a millisecond.

◦ Acetylcholine and noradrenaline, thrown from above by arousal - set the pacing gain - seconds.

• FAULT REGIMES AND WATCHDOGS.

◦ These are among the most energy-hungry cortical neurons, and gamma-band synchrony collapses quickly when their oxidative energy supply is disturbed; the perineuronal net absorbs the oxidative damage their furious metabolism generates and is the physical watchdog on this regime, while depolarisation block is a boundary they avoid rather than use.


ENTRY 20. CHANDELIER CELLS (Axo-Axonic Cells) (keyed to neuron catalogue Chapter 20)

MODES AND MODE TRANSITIONS:

This is a fast-spiking cell measurably unlike a basket cell, a default-on clamp on the axon initial segment whose most unusual feature is that its own transmitter is not reliably inhibitory.

• THE MODE REGISTER.

◦ Spontaneous stuttering fast-spiking - the Standing Clamp - the everyday state, firing between stimuli so the clamp on the initial segment is applied by default rather than on request, with a tendency to stutter.

◦ Sign-ambiguous output - the Two-Faced Veto - a genuine operating regime in which gamma-aminobutyric acid (GABA) at the axon initial segment can excite or inhibit, because that compartment lacks the potassium-chloride cotransporter KCC2 and its GABA reversal potential sits near minus 55 millivolts instead of the usual minus 75.

• PER-MODE TRANSFER FUNCTIONS.

◦ In the Standing Clamp input resistance is higher than a basket cell's at 150 to 300 megohms, resting potential near minus 65 millivolts, spike half-width 0.3 to 0.5 milliseconds, maximum rate 50 to 150 hertz, contacting 35 to 50 percent of pyramidal neurons in the axonal field through alpha-2-enriched GABA type A receptors (confidence high).

◦ In the Two-Faced Veto the local chloride level decides whether a given initial-segment synapse depolarises toward threshold or clamps below it (confidence moderate, context-dependent).

• THE TRANSITION TABLE.

◦ Local pyramidal and long-range drive crossing threshold - into stronger Standing Clamp - milliseconds.

◦ The postsynaptic cell's initial-segment chloride level - flips the Two-Faced Veto between excitation and inhibition - set by slow chloride handling.

◦ Neuromodulatory tone, thrown from above - sets baseline firing - seconds.

• FAULT REGIMES AND WATCHDOGS.

◦ The sign ambiguity is itself the watchdog concern, since a simulation treating this cell as uniformly inhibitory gets the wrong answer at the initial segment; no classically pathological firing regime is characteristically used, though depolarising GABA is implicated in some seizure onset zones and should be flagged.


ENTRY 21. TRANSLAMINAR CELLS (All-Layers Blanket-Damping Neuron) (keyed to neuron catalogue Chapter 21)

MODES AND MODE TRANSITIONS:

This is a two-population cell whose defining non-classical property is that one spike produces a different result in each layer because of conduction delay, so it is a spatial delay line rather than a point inhibitor, and its register spans a fast and a slow membrane type.

• THE MODE REGISTER.

◦ Fast-spiking vertical firing - the Column Sweep - the parvalbumin members' state, firing 80 to 180 spikes per second and depositing inhibition in layer-specific bands down a straight vertical axon.

◦ Low-threshold spiking with rebound - the Slow Blanket - the somatostatin members' state, adapting firing with a rebound burst on release from hyperpolarisation and slow peptide release.

• PER-MODE TRANSFER FUNCTIONS.

◦ In the Column Sweep parvalbumin cells have spike half-widths of 0.25 to 0.45 milliseconds and 6 to 12 millisecond time constants, delivering the same spike to different layers over one to five milliseconds of conduction delay that sets inter-layer phase (confidence high).

◦ In the Slow Blanket somatostatin cells have higher input resistance of 200 to 400 megohms, a rebound burst on release from hyperpolarisation, clear adaptation, and release the peptide somatostatin by volume transmission (confidence high).

• THE TRANSITION TABLE.

◦ Home-layer excitation crossing threshold - Standby to Column Sweep or Slow Blanket - milliseconds.

◦ Release from hyperpolarisation - triggers the Slow Blanket rebound burst - tens of milliseconds.

◦ Neuromodulatory tone, thrown from above - sets baseline gain - seconds.

• FAULT REGIMES AND WATCHDOGS.

◦ The watchdog note is anatomical honesty rather than a fault: a branch passing through a layer may make very few working synapses there, so connection probability into a passed-through layer must be a free parameter and never assumed, or a model will mis-time the whole column.


ENTRY 22. MARTINOTTI CELLS (keyed to neuron catalogue Chapter 22)

MODES AND MODE TRANSITIONS:

This is a mode-rich low-threshold cell whose recruitment is unusual, because it is switched on less by voltage than by a strongly facilitating synapse that grows several-fold within a pyramidal burst.

• THE MODE REGISTER.

◦ Facilitation-recruited firing - the Frequency-Dependent Brake - the everyday state, in which an input that starts almost silent strengthens through a burst until the cell fires and inhibits pyramidal apical tufts across several columns.

◦ Rebound burst - the Spring Volley - a burst on release from inhibition, powered by low-threshold T-type calcium channels and the hyperpolarisation-activated current I h.

◦ Cholinergic lateral inhibition - the Attentive Spread - a state-dependent regime in which acetylcholine depolarises the cell and facilitates its lateral inhibition.

• PER-MODE TRANSFER FUNCTIONS.

◦ In the Frequency-Dependent Brake initial release probability is very low at 0.03 to 0.10, facilitating five- to ten-fold in a burst through an Elfn1-to-mGluR7 (metabotropic glutamate receptor 7) protein handshake, with input resistance 200 to 500 megohms, a slow 20 to 40 millisecond time constant, resting near minus 60 millivolts, and maximum firing 20 to 60 hertz (confidence high).

◦ In the Spring Volley release from hyperpolarisation triggers a T-type calcium burst (confidence high).

◦ In the Attentive Spread acetylcholine makes the cell fire and gates whether its lateral inhibition is delivered (confidence high).

• THE TRANSITION TABLE.

◦ A sustained pyramidal burst facilitating the input synapse - silence to Frequency-Dependent Brake - tens of milliseconds, resetting within a second.

◦ Release from vasoactive intestinal peptide or parvalbumin inhibition - into the Spring Volley - tens of milliseconds.

◦ Acetylcholine, thrown from above by attention and arousal - enables the Attentive Spread - seconds.

• FAULT REGIMES AND WATCHDOGS.

◦ No characteristically used fault regime; because this cell gates whether pyramidal tufts may fire the dendritic calcium spikes that permit learning, its own vasoactive-intestinal-peptide inhibition is the watchdog deciding when the brake lifts.


ENTRY 23. NON-MARTINOTTI CELLS (Sentry-Freeing Entry-Layer Neuron) (keyed to neuron catalogue Chapter 23)

MODES AND MODE TRANSITIONS:

This is a two-mode somatostatin cell whose main output is a double negative, a sign inversion no single synapse can express, since it preferentially inhibits the fast-spiking cells of layer 4.

• THE MODE REGISTER.

◦ Quasi-fast-spiking stuttering - the Intermittent Gate - the everyday state, firing narrow spikes at high rates in bursts separated by irregular silent gaps, so the gaps carry as much information as the bursts.

◦ Prompt tonic inhibition - the Front-Door Damp - the sustained low-average state delivering fast local inhibition to layer 4, unlike the delayed Martinotti cell.

• PER-MODE TRANSFER FUNCTIONS.

◦ In the Intermittent Gate input resistance is 100 to 250 megohms, the time constant 8 to 20 milliseconds, the low-threshold calcium rebound weak or absent, and maximum sustained rates 50 to 120 hertz, with output weighted two to four times more heavily onto layer 4 parvalbumin cells than onto excitatory cells (confidence high).

◦ In the Front-Door Damp conduction delays are well under a millisecond within the local arbour, so inhibition arrives promptly, its excitatory inputs facilitating less dramatically than at Martinotti synapses (confidence high).

• THE TRANSITION TABLE.

◦ Thalamocortical or local excitation crossing threshold - Standby to Intermittent Gate - milliseconds.

◦ The stutter's own dynamics - alternate the gate between burst and silent gap - tens of milliseconds.

◦ Acetylcholine, thrown from above - sets gain and controls the parvalbumin cells that enforce critical-period timing - seconds.

• FAULT REGIMES AND WATCHDOGS.

◦ No characteristically used fault regime; because the net effect is disinhibition of layer 4, the watchdog concern is sign error in any model, and the heparanase this cell carries lets it remodel its own extracellular matrix, a slow structural check rather than an electrical one.


ENTRY 24. SST-CHODL CELLS (Far-Hushing Slow-Wave Neuron) (keyed to neuron catalogue Chapter 24)

MODES AND MODE TRANSITIONS:

This is a mode-rich sleep-active cell whose signature is late spiking and whose second regime is locked to the slow oscillation of deep sleep, so its mode setting is largely a brain-state service thrown from above.

• THE MODE REGISTER.

◦ Late-spiking accelerating firing - the Slow Ramp - the everyday state, riding a slow voltage ramp for tens to hundreds of milliseconds before the first spike, then accelerating through a current step instead of adapting.

◦ Up-state-locked bursting - the Slow-Wave Chorus - the deep-sleep state in which the cell fires bursts locked to the up state, the depolarised active half of the slow oscillation.

◦ Volume-transmitted hush - the Wide Quiet - a chemical output state in which gamma-aminobutyric acid (GABA) released by volume transmission lingers and recruits slow GABA type B receptors.

• PER-MODE TRANSFER FUNCTIONS.

◦ In the Slow Ramp input resistance is high at 150 to 350 megohms, the time constant 10 to 20 milliseconds, resting near minus 65 millivolts, and spikes are broad (confidence high).

◦ In the Slow-Wave Chorus firing is phase-locked to a 0.5 to 1.0 hertz oscillation (confidence high).

◦ In the Wide Quiet one spike gives a partner a slow GABA type A component rising in about 4 milliseconds and decaying near 36 milliseconds, then a much slower GABA type B component, over a radius of 200 to 450 micrometres (confidence high).

• THE TRANSITION TABLE.

◦ Sustained thalamic-matrix or feedback drive crossing threshold - into the Slow Ramp - tens to hundreds of milliseconds.

◦ The slow oscillation phase, thrown from above by sleep state - Slow Ramp to Slow-Wave Chorus - locked at 0.5 to 1.0 hertz.

◦ Acetylcholine from nucleus basalis and sleep pressure, thrown from above - shift the strength of the GABA type B component - seconds to minutes.

• FAULT REGIMES AND WATCHDOGS.

◦ No characteristically used fault regime; the watchdog is astrocytic, since the GABA transporter GAT-3 sets how far the chemical hush spreads, so glial failure rather than a firing fault is the boundary risk.


ENTRY 25. BIPOLAR CELLS (keyed to neuron catalogue Chapter 25)

MODES AND MODE TRANSITIONS:

This is a mode-rich vasoactive-intestinal-peptide cell that serves as the cortex's volume knob for behavioural state, switching from a quiet irregular baseline to a peptide-releasing burst within tens of milliseconds of a change in arousal.

• THE MODE REGISTER.

◦ Irregular tonic firing - the Idle Whisper - the everyday state, a few spikes per second with irregular or bursting output that broadens and slows during a train, a pattern called accommodation.

◦ Arousal burst with peptide release - the State Announcement - the high-rate state of 20 to 60 spikes per second during arousal, reward or punishment, releasing vasoactive intestinal peptide from dense-core vesicles only when firing in bursts.

◦ Quiescence - the Standby - the low baseline held when behavioural state is steady.

• PER-MODE TRANSFER FUNCTIONS.

◦ In the Idle Whisper resting voltage is minus 60 to minus 65 millivolts, input resistance high at 200 to 500 megohms, and the time constant slow at 15 to 25 milliseconds (confidence high).

◦ In the State Announcement serotonin through the ionotropic 5HT3a receptor and acetylcholine through nicotinic receptors excite the cell within tens of milliseconds, and burst firing releases vasoactive intestinal peptide onto VPAC1 and VPAC2 receptors over seconds, its facilitating output synapse onto somatostatin cells growing stronger through the burst (confidence high).

◦ In the Standby the cell idles subthreshold (confidence high).

• THE TRANSITION TABLE.

◦ Serotonin and acetylcholine, thrown from above by a change in behavioural state - Idle Whisper to State Announcement - tens of milliseconds.

◦ Burst firing crossing the peptide-release threshold - adds slow vasoactive-intestinal-peptide signalling - seconds.

◦ Return of a steady state - back to Standby - seconds.

• FAULT REGIMES AND WATCHDOGS.

◦ No characteristically used fault regime; because this cell disinhibits by suppressing the somatostatin brake, its watchdog concern is again sign, and its fast-turning-over spines keep it structurally plastic without a perineuronal net.


ENTRY 26. DOUBLE BOUQUET CELLS (keyed to neuron catalogue Chapter 26)

MODES AND MODE TRANSITIONS:

This is a near two-mode cell whose interest is spatial rather than temporal, because it places inhibition one spine at a time inside a narrow vertical horsetail and so divides rather than subtracts.

• THE MODE REGISTER.

◦ Adapting non-fast-spiking firing - the Column Comb - the everyday state, an adapting train at 5 to 30 spikes per second delivering shunting inhibition to dendritic spines within a single microcolumn.

◦ Delayed-onset firing - the Slow Start - a clear delay to the first spike seen in some cells, holding output back until drive is sustained.

• PER-MODE TRANSFER FUNCTIONS.

◦ In the Column Comb resting voltage is around minus 65 millivolts, input resistance 150 to 350 megohms, and about 48 percent of contacts land on dendritic spines and most of the rest on shafts, with essentially none on somata, so an open chloride channel short-circuits excitatory current at the spine, a mechanism called shunting inhibition (confidence high).

◦ In the Slow Start a delay to the first spike defers inhibition (confidence moderate).

• THE TRANSITION TABLE.

◦ Local pyramidal or thalamic excitation crossing threshold - Slow Start into the Column Comb - tens of milliseconds.

◦ Group III metabotropic glutamate receptors, a class of slow glutamate sensor, providing slow presynaptic control - shift its inhibitory input over minutes.

◦ Neuromodulatory tone, thrown from above - sets baseline gain - seconds.

• FAULT REGIMES AND WATCHDOGS.

◦ No characteristically used fault regime; the watchdog concern is that shunting inhibition multiplies rather than adds, so a subtractive model is wrong, and the horsetail's tight packing among myelinated pyramidal axons permits ephaptic coupling that a connection list omits.


ENTRY 27. SMALL BASKET CELLS OF THE VIP TYPE (Slim-Column Soma-Targeting Neuron) (keyed to neuron catalogue Chapter 27)

MODES AND MODE TRANSITIONS:

This is a mode-poor cell, an irregular soma-targeting inhibitor whose unreliable, jittery release is its most characteristic feature.

• THE MODE REGISTER.

◦ Irregular tonic firing - the Loose Tick - the single everyday state, a broad spike that slows during steady input, contacting pyramidal cell bodies within 150 micrometres.

◦ Sparse quiescence - the Rest - the low baseline between events.

• PER-MODE TRANSFER FUNCTIONS.

◦ In the Loose Tick resting voltage is minus 60 to minus 65 millivolts, input resistance high at 200 to 400 megohms, the spike broad at 0.6 to 1.0 milliseconds, and top sustained rates only 20 to 60 spikes per second, with release depending on N-type Cav2.2 calcium channels sitting far from the release machinery, making release unreliable, jittery and prone to continuing after the spike (confidence high).

◦ In the Rest the cell idles subthreshold (confidence high).

• THE TRANSITION TABLE.

◦ Local pyramidal drive crossing threshold - Rest to Loose Tick - milliseconds.

◦ Acetylcholine from the basal forebrain and serotonin from the raphe nuclei, thrown from above - set excitability - seconds.

◦ Residual terminal calcium after a burst - prolongs asynchronous release past the spike - hundreds of milliseconds.

• FAULT REGIMES AND WATCHDOGS.

◦ No characteristically used fault regime; the jitter and trailing release are normal features rather than faults, and local circuit inhibition is the ordinary watchdog on this modest inhibitor.


ENTRY 28. LARGE AND SMALL BASKET CELLS EXPRESSING CHOLECYSTOKININ (Feeling-Coloured Loose-Timing Neuron) (keyed to neuron catalogue Chapter 28)

MODES AND MODE TRANSITIONS:

This is a mode-rich emotional-tone cell, a loose-timing counterweight to the fast-spiking basket cell, whose output can be dialled down for seconds by a message sent backwards from its own target.

• THE MODE REGISTER.

◦ Accommodating irregular firing - the Mood Tick - the everyday state, recruited by sustained activity through facilitating inputs, with a broad spike and firing that accommodates from an opening burst.

◦ Asynchronous-release smear - the Lingering Blanket - a mode in which the terminal keeps dribbling gamma-aminobutyric acid (GABA) for two hundred to five hundred milliseconds after a burst, turning a spike train into a lasting blanket.

◦ Depolarisation-induced suppression of inhibition - the Requested Silence - a state in which an active target manufactures endocannabinoids that drift back and cut release by 60 to 90 percent for five to twenty seconds.

• PER-MODE TRANSFER FUNCTIONS.

◦ In the Mood Tick resting voltage is minus 60 to minus 68 millivolts, input resistance 120 to 300 megohms, the spike broad at 0.7 to 1.2 milliseconds because the cell lacks the fast potassium channels of parvalbumin cells, and rates 10 to 50 spikes per second (confidence high).

◦ In the Lingering Blanket loosely coupled N-type Cav2.2 channels sustain trailing release for hundreds of milliseconds (confidence high).

◦ In the Requested Silence the densest cannabinoid type 1 receptors in the cortex, plus mu-opioid and muscarinic receptors, throttle release (confidence high).

• THE TRANSITION TABLE.

◦ Sustained facilitating drive - Standby to Mood Tick - tens of milliseconds.

◦ A burst leaving residual calcium - Mood Tick into the Lingering Blanket - hundreds of milliseconds.

◦ Endocannabinoids from a depolarised target - Mood Tick to Requested Silence - seconds, with pairing locking it in for hours; arousal, stress, satiety and reward set the background cannabinoid tone from above.

• FAULT REGIMES AND WATCHDOGS.

◦ No characteristically used fault regime; the astrocytic enzyme monoacylglycerol lipase that clears the endocannabinoid signal is the watchdog setting how far and how long the requested silence spreads.


ENTRY 29. CORTICAL NEUROGLIAFORM CELLS (keyed to neuron catalogue Chapter 29)

MODES AND MODE TRANSITIONS:

This is a distinctive two-mode cell whose true output is not a wire but a chemical field, since it alone reliably recruits slow gamma-aminobutyric acid (GABA) type B receptors from a single spike by volume transmission.

• THE MODE REGISTER.

◦ Late-spiking accelerating firing - the Slow Ramp - the everyday state, in which a just-suprathreshold current makes the voltage creep up a long ramp so the first spike arrives hundreds of milliseconds later.

◦ Volume-transmitted GABA cloud - the Wide Hush - the signature output state, releasing GABA into the extracellular space so a dilute transient lingers for tens of milliseconds and recruits GABA type B receptors that most inhibitory cells cannot reach alone.

• PER-MODE TRANSFER FUNCTIONS.

◦ In the Slow Ramp resting voltage is minus 62 to minus 70 millivolts, input resistance about 173 megohms in mouse layer 1, spike width about 0.7 milliseconds, voltage sag small at about 8 percent, and connection probability onto nearby layer 2 pyramidal cells about 72 percent (confidence high).

◦ In the Wide Hush every target shows a slow GABA type A component followed by a GABA type B component from one spike, with the GABA type B effect peaking 150 to 250 milliseconds after the spike through a G-protein cascade, and the cell also inhibits incoming presynaptic terminals (confidence high).

• THE TRANSITION TABLE.

◦ Sustained excitation from local pyramidal cells, feedback or higher-order thalamus overcoming the slow ramp - into the Slow Ramp and then the Wide Hush - hundreds of milliseconds.

◦ Gap-junction coupling, unusually to other classes of inhibitory neuron - synchronises a local group - within a millisecond.

◦ Brain-state and cholinergic tone, thrown from above - set baseline recruitment - seconds.

• FAULT REGIMES AND WATCHDOGS.

◦ No characteristically used fault regime; the cell is reluctant to fire at all, and its co-released neuropeptide Y suppresses excitability and protects against seizures, making the cell partly its own watchdog.


ENTRY 30. SINGLE BOUQUET CELLS (keyed to neuron catalogue Chapter 30)

MODES AND MODE TRANSITIONS:

This is a two-mode disinhibitory switch whose essential non-classical property is sign inversion, because it is inhibitory yet its net effect is excitatory, inhibiting only the inhibitors so the cortex below gets louder when it fires.

• THE MODE REGISTER.

◦ Prompt phasic disinhibition burst - the Green Light - the everyday state, answering a current step with a prompt burst of two to five spikes that lifts the brakes off a small column of deep output neurons.

◦ Adapting tonic firing - the Held Yes - the strongly adapting, irregular low-rate state that follows the burst.

• PER-MODE TRANSFER FUNCTIONS.

◦ In the Green Light resting voltage is minus 58 to minus 65 millivolts, input resistance high at 200 to 400 megohms so a small feedback input fires the cell, spike width 0.5 to 0.8 milliseconds, and output lands almost exclusively on seven subtypes of layer 2 and 3 interneuron in a strictly one-way link with no return inhibition and no electrical coupling (confidence high).

◦ In the Held Yes firing adapts strongly and irregularly, its output synapses onto interneurons depressing with use so a long burst yields diminishing disinhibition and the switch resets itself (confidence high).

• THE TRANSITION TABLE.

◦ Long-range feedback from higher cortical areas or higher-order thalamus crossing threshold - Standby to Green Light - one to two milliseconds down the descending axon.

◦ Acetylcholine on nicotinic receptors, thrown from above by attention and active exploration - throws the switch and raises its energy use - seconds.

◦ Use-dependent depression of the output synapses - Green Light toward the Held Yes and reset - tens to hundreds of milliseconds.

• FAULT REGIMES AND WATCHDOGS.

◦ No characteristically used fault regime; the watchdog concern is again sign, since a model treating inhibition as uniformly suppressive gets the wrong answer, and the deliberate absence of reciprocal and electrical coupling is the biological safeguard keeping the switch one-way.
MODES AND MODE TRANSITIONS - PER-TYPE ENTRIES FOR NEURON CATALOGUE CHAPTERS 31 TO 56

Companion volume to the neuron catalogue, prepared to the MODES_ENTRY_SPEC.

House style note: plain text, British -ise spelling, one sentence per line with a blank line between sentences and bullets, space-dash-space in place of any long dash.


ENTRY 31. CANOPY CELLS (keyed to neuron catalogue Chapter 31)

MODES AND MODE TRANSITIONS:

This is a two-mode switch, a mode-poor onset detector rather than a mode-rich integrator, because its onset spiking with strong adaptation lets it report change and then fall quiet.

• THE MODE REGISTER.

◦ Quiescent Baseline - the Held Breath - the low-duty-cycle resting state in which the thin horizontal net waits, matched to layer one's lean blood supply.

◦ Onset Burst - the Leading Edge - a brief prompt volley at the arrival of feedback drive that marks where and when a top-down signal began.

• PER-MODE TRANSFER FUNCTIONS.

◦ Quiescent Baseline: near-silent output, membrane near minus 64 millivolts, sparse fast gamma-aminobutyric acid (GABA, the brain's main inhibitory chemical) currents of 10 to 40 picoamperes only if weak input arrives.

◦ Onset Burst: pooled feedback of 0 to 30 events per second across 300 to 600 micrometres drives a short high-rate volley that adapts within 300 to 700 milliseconds, so output tracks the time-derivative of input rather than its level (numbers moderate confidence, type newly recognised).

• THE TRANSITION TABLE.

◦ Feedback onset crossing threshold - Quiescent Baseline to Onset Burst - within a few milliseconds.

◦ Adaptation current building plus vesicle depletion - Onset Burst back to Quiescent Baseline - over 300 to 700 milliseconds, an intrinsic self-timed return.

◦ Cholinergic or noradrenergic arousal broadcast from below - raises baseline excitability across both modes - thrown from above as a Layer 10/11 brain-state service.

• FAULT REGIMES AND WATCHDOGS.

◦ Depolarisation block - boundary signature is loss of the crisp onset volley under sustained strong drive - watchdog is adaptation, which normally prevents it; weakening this population is expected to blur the line between externally driven and internally generated activity, though disease evidence is thin.


ENTRY 32. ALPHA7 CELLS (keyed to neuron catalogue Chapter 32)

MODES AND MODE TRANSITIONS:

This is a mode-rich alerting cell for its layer, because a fast-desensitising nicotinic receptor, an Ih rebound current and a subthreshold calcium hump give it three genuine regimes plus a chemical output with no electrical readout.

• THE MODE REGISTER.

◦ Standing Watch - the Depolarised Idle - a high resting state near minus 60 millivolts kept close to threshold by the standing hyperpolarisation-activated current Ih.

◦ Acetylcholine Alert - the Wake-Up Call - a millisecond onset volley when a puff of acetylcholine, the brain's attention transmitter, opens the alpha7 receptor.

◦ Rebound Firing - the Bounce-Back - spikes produced by being switched off, as Ih repolarises the cell after inhibition.

• PER-MODE TRANSFER FUNCTIONS.

◦ Standing Watch: continuous small Ih leak, expensive pumping, ready output but few spikes.

◦ Acetylcholine Alert: acetylcholine transients of tens of micromolar for a few milliseconds give sub-millisecond activation and a fractional calcium current near 0.10 to 0.12, then the receptor shuts within 5 to 20 milliseconds even with acetylcholine still present, so the cell reports onset and ignores steady background.

◦ Rebound Firing: after a hyperpolarising step, Ih activation over 100 to 500 milliseconds carries the membrane back through threshold for a delayed volley.

• THE TRANSITION TABLE.

◦ Acetylcholine pulse from basal forebrain - Standing Watch to Acetylcholine Alert - under 1 millisecond, thrown from above by the cholinergic arousal system.

◦ Receptor desensitisation - Acetylcholine Alert back to Standing Watch - 5 to 20 milliseconds, with recovery over 200 to 800 milliseconds.

◦ Release from inhibition - any mode to Rebound Firing - set by Ih over 100 to 500 milliseconds.

• FAULT REGIMES AND WATCHDOGS.

◦ Sensory-gating failure - boundary signature is loss of P50 suppression of a repeated click - watchdog is normal receptor number; deleting one CHRNA7 copy in the 15q13.3 syndrome is linked to epilepsy, intellectual disability, autism and schizophrenia.


ENTRY 33. LAMP5-LHX6 CELLS (keyed to neuron catalogue Chapter 33)

MODES AND MODE TRANSITIONS:

This is a mode-rich neurogliaform-family blanket inhibitor, because a slow potassium brake, a lingering gamma-aminobutyric acid (GABA) cloud and axonal persistent firing give it three regimes.

• THE MODE REGISTER.

◦ Late-Silent Hold - the Handbrake On - a quiescent state in which a slowly fading potassium current keeps the cell below threshold.

◦ Delayed Blanket - the Slow Hush - late-spiking output that floods a small ball of tissue with GABA by volume transmission.

◦ Persistent Firing - the Held Note - self-sustained spiking for tens of seconds after the stimulus ends, shown by more than 80 percent of the family.

• PER-MODE TRANSFER FUNCTIONS.

◦ Late-Silent Hold: input resistance 150 to 400 megaohms, so small currents move the voltage far, but the brake delays any spike for tens to hundreds of milliseconds.

◦ Delayed Blanket: a single spike can reach slow GABA type B receptors because transmitter lingers, extracellular GABA peaking near 100 micromolar in the plexus and falling below 1 micromolar by 60 micrometres, GABA type A decay 50 to 70 milliseconds.

◦ Persistent Firing: axonal state holds spiking for seconds to tens of seconds with no synaptic input, a membrane memory.

• THE TRANSITION TABLE.

◦ Sustained drive wearing off the brake - Late-Silent Hold to Delayed Blanket - tens to hundreds of milliseconds.

◦ Strong or repeated activation - Delayed Blanket to Persistent Firing - seconds.

◦ Mu-opioid receptor occupancy - any mode toward silence - multiplies output down to about 0.1, a fast reversible off-switch thrown by opioids from above.

◦ Acetylcholine on alpha7 receptors - raises excitability across modes - modelled as a 0 to 1 gain.

• FAULT REGIMES AND WATCHDOGS.

◦ Network hypersynchrony - boundary signature is loss of the slow blanket brake - watchdog is GABA type B autoinhibition; loss of LAMP5 interneurons in Alzheimer's disease worsens synchrony in amyloid and tau models.


ENTRY 34. ROSEHIP CELLS (keyed to neuron catalogue Chapter 34)

MODES AND MODE TRANSITIONS:

This is a modest two-to-three mode dendritic editor, because its irregular stuttering firing and a clear theta resonance are its real regimes.

• THE MODE REGISTER.

◦ Idle Shaft-Watch - the Quiet Guard - a resting state near minus 61 millivolts poised over the dendritic shafts of layer three pyramidal cells.

◦ Stuttering Discharge - the Halting Veto - irregular spike trains with interspike-interval variability averaging 87 milliseconds that place a slow shunt on one dendritic microdomain.

◦ Theta Resonance - the Four-Per-Second Ring - a preferential response to inputs near 4.17 hertz.

• PER-MODE TRANSFER FUNCTIONS.

◦ Idle Shaft-Watch: input resistance about 140 megaohms, membrane time constant about 7.3 milliseconds, output near zero.

◦ Stuttering Discharge: irregular firing delivers inhibitory potentials of about 0.087 millivolts onto dendritic shafts 94 to 455 micrometres from the pyramidal soma, vetoing a backpropagating spike and its dendritic calcium.

◦ Theta Resonance: a resonance peak at 4.17 hertz means input at that rate produces the largest voltage swing, tuning the cell to the theta rhythm.

• THE TRANSITION TABLE.

◦ Excitatory or gap-junction drive from neighbouring rosehip cells crossing threshold - Idle to Stuttering Discharge - milliseconds, with coupling coefficient near 0.05.

◦ Rhythmic theta-band input - biases the cell into Theta Resonance - period about 240 milliseconds.

◦ Depressing input, paired-pulse ratios 0.27 to 0.71 - Stuttering back toward Idle - tens of milliseconds.

• FAULT REGIMES AND WATCHDOGS.

◦ Unchecked dendritic calcium - boundary signature is failure to veto backpropagating spikes - watchdog is the slow shunt itself; its loss predicts indiscriminate synapse strengthening and superficial seizure risk, flagged as inference, not measurement.


ENTRY 35. BITUFTED CELLS (keyed to neuron catalogue Chapter 35)

MODES AND MODE TRANSITIONS:

This is a use-dependent two-mode brake, because facilitating inputs mean the cell stays quiet during sparse activity and switches on only when pyramidal firing becomes sustained.

• THE MODE REGISTER.

◦ Sparse-Input Sleep - the Waiting Damper - the resting state during brief or isolated pyramidal spikes, when facilitating synapses are still weak.

◦ Recruited Damping - the Late Clamp - regular or low-threshold spiking that clamps pyramidal dendrites once a run of spikes has built the input up.

◦ Retrograde Dendritic Release - the Backward Word - dendritic gamma-aminobutyric acid (GABA) release that depresses the very input that drove it.

• PER-MODE TRANSFER FUNCTIONS.

◦ Sparse-Input Sleep: baseline release probability near 0.09, so a single presynaptic spike barely moves the cell.

◦ Recruited Damping: each pyramidal spike adds about 0.15 to release probability with a facilitation decay of 100 to 300 milliseconds, so sustained high-frequency firing recruits the cell, which then delivers adapting GABA onto apical and basal dendrites plus slow somatostatin by volume transmission.

◦ Retrograde Dendritic Release: backpropagating spikes raise dendritic calcium, triggering GABA release onto presynaptic GABA type B receptors that depresses the input over 300 to 600 milliseconds.

• THE TRANSITION TABLE.

◦ Facilitation crossing recruitment level - Sparse-Input Sleep to Recruited Damping - 100 to 300 milliseconds of sustained drive.

◦ Dendritic calcium build-up - Recruited Damping to Retrograde Dendritic Release - hundreds of milliseconds.

◦ Cholinergic and serotonergic tone - sets a gain of roughly 0.5 to 2 across modes - thrown from above by arousal systems.

• FAULT REGIMES AND WATCHDOGS.

◦ Loss of the late dendritic brake - boundary signature is unlimited pyramidal self-amplification - watchdog is facilitation-gated recruitment; somatostatin messenger falls by 27 to 38 percent in major depressive disorder, weakening this brake.


ENTRY 36. HORIZONTAL CELLS OF LAYER 1 (keyed to neuron catalogue Chapter 36)

MODES AND MODE TRANSITIONS:

This is a mixed electrical-and-secretory cell whose primary regime axis is protein secretion rather than spiking, so its register mirrors the non-neuronal secretory pattern in the specification.

• THE MODE REGISTER.

◦ Reelin Secretory Tone - the Standing Signpost - continuous low release of reelin into the extracellular space, a positional and plasticity signal.

◦ Slow Horizontal Spiking - the Sideways Bind - broad, strongly adapting spikes under 10 per second that spread excitation across columns.

◦ Springy Rebound - the Self-Righting Sag - Ih-driven return whenever the membrane is pushed down.

• PER-MODE TRANSFER FUNCTIONS.

◦ Reelin Secretory Tone: a standing pool of the 388 kilodalton protein emptied slowly over about two minutes, cut externally by ADAMTS proteases so its range is set outside the cell.

◦ Slow Horizontal Spiking: input resistance 300 to 800 megaohms, resting near minus 60 to minus 50 millivolts, glutamate onto pyramidal apical tufts within about a millimetre at under 10 spikes per second.

◦ Springy Rebound: Ih activation near 180 milliseconds gives a slow sag and rebound.

• THE TRANSITION TABLE.

◦ Tuft glutamate drive crossing threshold - Secretory Tone to Slow Horizontal Spiking - membrane time constant 20 to 30 milliseconds.

◦ Each spike topping up the reelin pool - Spiking feeds Secretory Tone - reelin clearance about two minutes.

◦ Acetylcholine, serotonin and noradrenaline - set a gain of 0.8 to 1.5 across modes - thrown from above.

• FAULT REGIMES AND WATCHDOGS.

◦ Secretory failure, not firing failure - boundary signature is collapse of reelin signalling - watchdog is steady secretory tone; reelin falls by roughly half in schizophrenia and total loss gives lissencephaly, a smooth disordered brain.


ENTRY 37. DENTATE GRANULE CELLS (keyed to neuron catalogue Chapter 37)

MODES AND MODE TRANSITIONS:

This is a proudly mode-poor sparse coder whose dominant regime is silence, since most of these cells almost never fire.

• THE MODE REGISTER.

◦ Deep Silence - the Closed Mouth - the resting state of the great majority, held near minus 80 millivolts, that keeps the dentate code sparse.

◦ Sparse Single Firing - the Rare Word - a lone spike or two only in the most strongly driven winners.

◦ Immature High-Excitability - the Eager Newcomer - the transient weeks-long regime of adult-born cells, far more excitable and depolarised by gamma-aminobutyric acid (GABA).

• PER-MODE TRANSFER FUNCTIONS.

◦ Deep Silence: resting minus 75 to minus 85 millivolts, input resistance only 100 to 300 megaohms, so it takes strong converging drive to reach threshold near minus 45 millivolts.

◦ Sparse Single Firing: 1 to 3 percent of the population active at once under k-winners-take-all inhibition, each winner reaching only 11 to 18 CA3 pyramidal cells with glutamate and zinc.

◦ Immature High-Excitability: high internal chloride makes GABA excitatory, so newborn cells fire readily during their 4 to 8 week maturation.

• THE TRANSITION TABLE.

◦ Perforant-path drive plus dendritic sodium and calcium spikes crossing threshold - Deep Silence to Sparse Single Firing - membrane time constant 20 to 40 milliseconds.

◦ Maturation of chloride handling - Immature High-Excitability to Deep Silence - over 4 to 8 weeks.

◦ Feedback and feed-forward inhibition to 60 hertz - returns the cell to Deep Silence - fast.

• FAULT REGIMES AND WATCHDOGS.

◦ Recurrent self-excitation - boundary signature is mossy-fibre sprouting onto other granule cells - watchdog is sparseness and strong inhibition; in epilepsy this builds a self-exciting loop, and in Alzheimer's disease the input layer thins.


ENTRY 38. SEMILUNAR GRANULE CELLS (keyed to neuron catalogue Chapter 38)

MODES AND MODE TRANSITIONS:
