This is a mixed-population motif, not a single cell: dopamine, gamma-aminobutyric acid and glutamate cells whose interaction, especially local disinhibition, is the real mode machine and must be modelled as at least a two-cell circuit.

• THE MODE REGISTER.

◦ Dopamine Tonic - the Wanting Idle - slow broad-spike firing at 1 to 8 hertz setting baseline motivation.

◦ Dopamine Burst - the Reward Exclamation - a 2 to 6 spike burst at 15 to 30 hertz releasing disproportionate dopamine.

◦ Gamma-aminobutyric acid Tonic Restraint - the Standing Guard - constant 10 to 40 hertz inhibition the dopamine cells must overcome.

• PER-MODE TRANSFER FUNCTIONS.

◦ In the Wanting Idle, dopamine cells fire regularly or irregularly with an unreliable hyperpolarisation-activated sag.

◦ In the Reward Exclamation, glutamate on N-methyl-D-aspartate receptors drives the burst, released when the Standing Guard falls.

◦ In the Standing Guard, fast narrow-spiking gamma-aminobutyric acid cells carrying mu-opioid receptors hold dopamine cells down.

• THE TRANSITION TABLE.

◦ Opioid or other disinhibition silencing gamma-aminobutyric acid cells - Standing Guard down, dopamine cells to Reward Exclamation - milliseconds to seconds; this is how morphine raises accumbens dopamine.

◦ Excitatory reward drive (prefrontal, orexin, pedunculopontine) - Wanting Idle to Reward Exclamation - milliseconds.

◦ Chronic stress - reshapes mesocortical firing into a burst-heavy pattern - days to weeks; thrown by the stress axis.

• FAULT REGIMES AND WATCHDOGS.

◦ Losing the gamma-aminobutyric acid guard makes every stimulus over-significant (the leading circuit account of psychosis); a single drug exposure strengthens glutamate synapses onto dopamine cells for days by inserting calcium-permeable alpha-amino-3-hydroxy-5-methyl-4-isoxazolepropionic acid (AMPA) receptors, and D2 autoreceptor self-inhibition plus retrograde endocannabinoid signalling are the local watchdogs.


ENTRY 80. NUCLEUS ACCUMBENS MEDIUM SPINY NEURONS (Motivation-To-Action Converting Neuron) (keyed to neuron catalogue Chapter 80)

MODES AND MODE TRANSITIONS:

This is the limbic two-state switch: a deeply hyperpolarised convergence detector that only fires when emotional, contextual and cognitive inputs agree, so its removal removes the brain's scepticism.

• THE MODE REGISTER.

◦ Down State - the Deaf Floor - a deep resting state near minus 80 to minus 90 millivolts clamped by Kir2 inward-rectifier channels.

◦ Up State - the Verdict Plateau - a plateau near minus 55 millivolts from which sparse firing becomes possible only on convergent input.

• PER-MODE TRANSFER FUNCTIONS.

◦ In the Deaf Floor, low input resistance makes ordinary inputs barely move the cell, which never fires spontaneously.

◦ In the Verdict Plateau, an A-type potassium current delays the first spike by tens to hundreds of milliseconds and firing stays sparse at 1 to 5 hertz; D1 cells co-release substance P and dysphoric dynorphin, D2 cells co-release pleasurable enkephalin, so mood follows the mode only during vigorous firing.

• THE TRANSITION TABLE.

◦ Convergent prefrontal, amygdala, hippocampal and thalamic glutamate - Deaf Floor to Verdict Plateau - tens to hundreds of milliseconds; agreement is required.

◦ Dopamine on spine necks and cholinergic pauses timed to reward - modulate the flip - tens of milliseconds.

◦ Loss of convergent drive - Verdict Plateau back to Deaf Floor - hundreds of milliseconds.

• FAULT REGIMES AND WATCHDOGS.

◦ This is where addiction physically lives: cocaine raises spine density, delta-FosB accumulates in D1 cells for weeks, and calcium-permeable alpha-amino-3-hydroxy-5-methyl-4-isoxazolepropionic acid (AMPA) receptors inserted during withdrawal drive craving that grows with time; the astrocytic cystine-glutamate exchanger setting extrasynaptic glutamate tone is the watchdog, and its collapse is a therapeutic target.


ENTRY 81. BOTH-PATHWAY QUIETING NEURON (keyed to neuron catalogue Chapter 81)

MODES AND MODE TRANSITIONS:

This is a proposed and unconfirmed (predicted) backward-reaching pallidal canceller, effectively a two-mode cell: near-silent until a stop cue throws a brief non-repeatable burst.

Figures are rodent-based and predicted, so confidence is low.

• THE MODE REGISTER.

◦ Restrained Silence - the Held Cancel - irregular firing under 2 hertz, restrained by a deep afterhyperpolarisation, while neighbours fire above 10 hertz.

◦ Cancel Burst - the Emergency Brake - a 50 to 100 hertz burst that quiets both striatal pathways at once.

• PER-MODE TRANSFER FUNCTIONS.

◦ In the Held Cancel a deep afterhyperpolarisation after each spike keeps the cell quiet and irregular.

◦ In the Emergency Brake, gamma-aminobutyric acid from a burst spills to extrasynaptic receptors (volume transmission) and co-released enkephalin damps glutamate release for seconds; terminals fade within the burst, so the cancel is brief and not repeatable.

• THE TRANSITION TABLE.

◦ A stop cue relayed through subthalamic glutamate and striatal input - Held Cancel to Emergency Brake - conduction about 1 to 3 milliseconds in rat.

◦ Terminal depression within the burst - Emergency Brake back to Held Cancel - the burst self-limits within its own duration.

◦ Dopamine loss - strengthens striatal input and makes the brake overbearing - days; Parkinson's-linked.

• FAULT REGIMES AND WATCHDOGS.

◦ The pathological direction is an overbearing brake in dopamine depletion that removes last-moment reversibility; striatal astrocytic gamma-aminobutyric acid clearance setting the spread of the signal is the watchdog, and no exotic glial fault is documented.


ENTRY 82. DASHED-HOPE DAMPING NEURON (keyed to neuron catalogue Chapter 82)

MODES AND MODE TRANSITIONS:

This is a proposed and unconfirmed (predicted) disappointment subtractor: a tonically firing inhibitory cell that holds a learned expectation alive in its own rate, so it is a persistent-activity memory rather than an instant transform.

Predicted, low confidence throughout.

• THE MODE REGISTER.

◦ Tonic Baseline - the Standing Subtractor - steady firing at tens of spikes per second, faster than its dopamine neighbours' 1 to 8 hertz.

◦ Expectation Ramp - the Rising Hope - a firing ramp that climbs after a promising cue and stays high through the wait.

• PER-MODE TRANSFER FUNCTIONS.

◦ In the Standing Subtractor the conductance lands on the dopamine cell body exactly where it subtracts from, rather than scales, excitation.

◦ In the Rising Hope, persistent activity keeps a value alive across seconds; when the reward news arrives the held level subtracts from the dopamine response, and endocannabinoids sent back by the dopamine cell tune the terminals.

• THE TRANSITION TABLE.

◦ A trusted reward-predicting cue - Standing Subtractor to Rising Hope - the ramp builds over the wait, seconds.

◦ Arrival of the outcome - the ramp is subtracted, then decays - the subtraction must arrive with the reward news, so slow local inhibition is a feature.

◦ Extinction - shrinks the ramp - trials to days; the expectation is learned.

• FAULT REGIMES AND WATCHDOGS.

◦ Remove it and the region becomes an optimist who cannot be disappointed, so learning adds but never prunes; chronic opioid or nicotine exposure reweights its inputs, a predicted route by which addiction distorts what counts as disappointment, and midbrain astrocytic gamma-aminobutyric acid tone is the watchdog on how much of its output counts.


ENTRY 83. THALAMOCORTICAL RELAY NEURONS, ALSO CALLED PROJECTION OR PRINCIPAL NEURONS (Relay-Station Gateway Neuron) (keyed to neuron catalogue Chapter 83)

MODES AND MODE TRANSITIONS:

This is one of the great two-mode switches of the brain: the same cell has two completely different ways of talking, and which one it uses is thrown almost entirely by brain state.

• THE MODE REGISTER.

◦ Tonic Mode - the Faithful Relay - evenly spaced spikes whose rate is a running report of input strength, used in waking to pass sensory detail to cortex.

◦ Burst Mode - the Wake-Up Call - a low-threshold calcium hump carrying two to seven tightly packed spikes, used in sleep and to flag a stimulus appearing after quiet.

• PER-MODE TRANSFER FUNCTIONS.

◦ In the Faithful Relay the cell rests at minus 65 to minus 75 millivolts, depolarised, and firing tracks driver input linearly.

◦ In the Wake-Up Call, about 100 milliseconds of hyperpolarisation de-inactivates CaV3.1 low-threshold calcium channels, whose release fires the burst, while a hyperpolarisation-activated current through HCN channels paces sleep rhythms.

• THE TRANSITION TABLE.

◦ Brainstem cholinergic and modulator tone in waking depolarising the cell - Wake-Up Call to Faithful Relay - set from above by the sleep-wake and arousal state.

◦ Hyperpolarisation of about 100 milliseconds by reticular or local inhibition - Faithful Relay to Wake-Up Call - about 100 milliseconds to arm.

◦ Cortical layer 6 feedback (five to ten times the driver synapse count) - adjusts gain within a mode - milliseconds to seconds.

• FAULT REGIMES AND WATCHDOGS.

◦ Excessive synchronised burst mode across the population underlies absence seizures and sleep spindles run amok; the reticular shell and local interneurons that impose hyperpolarisation are also the watchdogs that keep bursting rhythmic rather than epileptic.


ENTRY 84. CORE RELAY NEURONS (Pinpoint Sensory-Map Neuron) (keyed to neuron catalogue Chapter 84)

MODES AND MODE TRANSITIONS:

This is a precision relay specialisation of the thalamocortical two-mode switch, biased hard toward faithful tonic transmission in waking but keeping the burst as a change alarm.

• THE MODE REGISTER.

◦ Faithful Tonic - the Pinpoint Report - short-latency high-rate firing (above 200 spikes per second) with a sharp receptive field, a near-copy of a single retinal or touch fibre.

◦ Wake-Up Burst - the Something Appeared - a low-threshold burst signalling a stimulus arriving after quiet, detected by cortex far more reliably than single spikes.

• PER-MODE TRANSFER FUNCTIONS.

◦ In the Pinpoint Report, one or a few large depressing driver terminals dominate, losing about half their strength per spike and recovering over about a tenth of a second, so the cell reports change rather than steady level.

◦ In the Something Appeared, dendritic low-threshold calcium channels let a brief inhibitory pause arm a burst that acts as a cortical wake-up call.

• THE TRANSITION TABLE.

◦ Brainstem arousal holding the cell depolarised in waking - keeps it in the Pinpoint Report - set from above.

◦ A brief inhibitory pause after quiet - Pinpoint Report to Something Appeared - tens to about 100 milliseconds to arm.

◦ Steep driver-synapse depression - converts a maintained stimulus into an onset report within a mode - about 100 milliseconds recovery.

• FAULT REGIMES AND WATCHDOGS.

◦ Loss of these cells leaves a wash of brightness without form; there is no notable used-pathological firing mode beyond the shared thalamic burst, and the perineuronal nets around these parvalbumin cells stabilise connections and are the developmental watchdog closing the critical period.


ENTRY 85. MATRIX RELAY NEURONS (Wide-Spreading Synchrony Neuron) (keyed to neuron catalogue Chapter 85)

MODES AND MODE TRANSITIONS:

This is a broadcast device rather than a channel: a burst-prone state-setter whose message is a floodlight over cortical layer 1, so its modes are about setting cortical state, not reporting detail.

• THE MODE REGISTER.

◦ Low-Rate Tonic - the House Lights - lower average firing than core cells, adapting, carrying a diffuse state signal to apical tufts.

◦ State Burst - the Rousing Flood - a readily fired burst that converts a single cortical spike into a burst by triggering an apical calcium spike.

• PER-MODE TRANSFER FUNCTIONS.

◦ In the House Lights, glutamate is released with a modulator's profile, small facilitating responses that build in trains, spread over several milliseconds across the terminal field and permitting volume transmission from the astrocyte-rich, loosely wrapped layer 1.

◦ In the Rousing Flood, a burst at the apical tuft flips pyramidal cells into their own bursts, a mechanism central to theories of conscious perception, and matrix firing rises before core firing as a person wakes.

• THE TRANSITION TABLE.

◦ Brainstem, basal forebrain and pallidal state input - House Lights to State Burst - milliseconds to arm; a wake and arousal service from above.

◦ Central thalamic deep brain stimulation of this population - lifts cortical state - the clearest human evidence that these cells set state.

◦ Transition into deep sleep - shifts firing toward slow oscillations and alpha rhythm - state-thrown.

• FAULT REGIMES AND WATCHDOGS.

◦ Damage to the paramedian thalamus causes coma out of all proportion to tissue lost, the boundary signature of losing the house lights; there is no cell-intrinsic runaway fault, and the diffuse convergent wiring is what keeps its influence distributed rather than focal.


ENTRY 86. THALAMIC LOCAL INTERNEURONS (Local Sharpening Inhibitor Neuron) (keyed to neuron catalogue Chapter 86)

MODES AND MODE TRANSITIONS:

This cell breaks the integrate-and-fire model more thoroughly than almost any other: it has two thresholds, computing and releasing transmitter at dozens of semi-independent dendritic sites without firing a whole-cell spike.

• THE MODE REGISTER.

◦ Local Dendritic Release - the Point Editor - gamma-aminobutyric acid released at a single F2 dendritic terminal in response to local input, without depolarising the soma.

◦ Global Spiking - the Whole-Cell Veto - a conventional narrow fast spike that engages the cell's axonal output onto relay cell bodies.

• PER-MODE TRANSFER FUNCTIONS.

◦ In the Point Editor, electrically leaky thin dendrites let input at one F2 terminal raise local calcium and release gamma-aminobutyric acid there, glutamate on metabotropic receptors at the terminal directly commanding its own local inhibition, with slow prolonged inhibitory kinetics.

◦ In the Whole-Cell Veto the cell fires fast narrow spikes at high rate from a depolarised rest, its axon delivering fast brief inhibition more widely.

• THE TRANSITION TABLE.

◦ Local driver input at one F2 site - rest to Point Editor - milliseconds, confined to that dendrite.

◦ Summed input crossing the higher global threshold - Point Editor to Whole-Cell Veto - milliseconds.

◦ Brainstem acetylcholine in alert waking - reduces dendritic release, letting more detail through - minute by minute; an arousal service from above.

• FAULT REGIMES AND WATCHDOGS.

◦ Loss of these cells flattens contrast and blunts onsets, so a face arrives as undifferentiated luminance; there is no notable pathological firing mode, and the glial glomerular capsule sealing the three-cell computation is the watchdog keeping local inhibition where it belongs.


ENTRY 87. THALAMIC RETICULAR NUCLEUS NEURONS (Attention-Gating Shutter Neuron) (keyed to neuron catalogue Chapter 87)

MODES AND MODE TRANSITIONS:

This is a classic two-mode switch cell whose whole meaning lies in the choice between a steady tonic brake and a rhythmic bursting engine, because the same inhibitory sheet either trims sensory channels during waking or paces the sleep spindle during light sleep.

The mode is thrown almost entirely from above, by sleep-wake state and by cortical attention, so mode setting here is a Layer 10 and 11 service and not a local decision.

• THE MODE REGISTER.

◦ Tonic gating - the Waking Shutter - steady low-rate inhibition that dims ignored thalamic channels while attention illuminates one.

◦ Rhythmic bursting - the Spindle Metronome - an accelerating-then-decelerating calcium burst that generates the sleep spindle of light non-rapid-eye-movement sleep.

• PER-MODE TRANSFER FUNCTIONS.

◦ Waking Shutter: relatively depolarised membrane near minus 60 millivolts, single spikes at a few to a few tens of spikes per second, output scaling with layer 6 corticothalamic feedback of 0 to 30 spikes per second, gain aimed sector by sector rather than as a blanket.

◦ Spindle Metronome: hyperpolarised membrane licenses a low-threshold calcium spike through CaV3.3 channels, giving bursts of 5 to 15 spikes rising to an intra-burst rate of 300 to 500 spikes per second, riding an 11 to 15 cycles per second envelope of 0.5 to 3 seconds, terminated by SK2 calcium-activated potassium channels; numbers are firm for rodent and moderate confidence for human.

• THE TRANSITION TABLE.

◦ Falling arousal or drowsiness (state broadcast from above) - Waking Shutter to Spindle Metronome - seconds, as the membrane hyperpolarises and de-inactivates the calcium channel over 70 to 120 milliseconds.

◦ Cortical or brainstem arousal, acetylcholine and noradrenaline (thrown from above) - Spindle Metronome back to Waking Shutter - seconds.

◦ Slow gamma-aminobutyric acid (GABA)-B inhibition from neighbours recharging the relay partner - within-mode pacing of successive spindle cycles - period 70 to 90 milliseconds.

• FAULT REGIMES AND WATCHDOGS.

◦ Runaway synchrony: the boundary signature is the normal spindle tightening into a 3 per second spike-and-wave discharge, the electrical form of an absence seizure; the watchdog is graded GABA and thalamocortical feedback that normally caps synchrony.

◦ Spindle collapse: reduced spindle density is one of the most reproducible findings in schizophrenia and tracks impaired sleep-dependent memory; the watchdog is the perineuronal net that shields these fast-firing cells from oxidative stress.


ENTRY 88. HABENULAR NEURONS (Setback-Signalling Aversion Neuron) (keyed to neuron catalogue Chapter 88)

MODES AND MODE TRANSITIONS:

This is a two-mode integrator in which a steady background tick is punctuated by a hard disappointment burst, and the medial and lateral halves keep partly separate registers.

The burst, not the single spike, is the message that shuts down dopamine.

• THE MODE REGISTER.

◦ Tonic pacemaking - the Background Tick - a constant low tone reporting the ongoing state of expectation, strongest in the medial habenula.

◦ Disappointment bursting - the Setback Volley - a powerful NMDA-driven burst when a promised reward fails to arrive, the inverted reward-prediction error of the lateral habenula.

• PER-MODE TRANSFER FUNCTIONS.

◦ Background Tick: spontaneous pacemaking at 2 to 10 spikes per second with no input, firing rate rising above baseline as expected reward fails, gain multiplier around 22 spikes per second per unit of disappointment (soft, model-derived).

◦ Setback Volley: requires prior hyperpolarisation, then N-methyl-D-aspartate receptors plus low-threshold calcium channels drive a burst 20 to 100 milliseconds long with a downstream weight roughly 2 to 3 times a tonic spike, producing a derived dopamine-suppression fraction of 0 to 1.

• THE TRANSITION TABLE.

◦ Worse-than-expected outcome after prior hyperpolarisation (synaptic and astrocytic) - Background Tick to Setback Volley - one trial, tens of milliseconds.

◦ Astrocytic Kir4.1 potassium stripping raising local hyperpolarisation - biases the cell toward the Volley - seconds to minutes.

◦ Better-than-expected outcome - Setback Volley back to Background Tick, with the cell falling briefly silent - one trial.

• FAULT REGIMES AND WATCHDOGS.

◦ Pathological over-bursting: chronic stress strengthens excitatory drive and raises bursting, producing the anhedonia and behavioural despair of major depression; the watchdog is NMDA-receptor availability, and a single dose of ketamine blocking it lifts mood within hours.

◦ Weakened medial signalling removes the aversive brake on nicotine and increases smoking; no notable depolarisation-block regime is used by this type.


ENTRY 89. ZONA INCERTA NEURONS (Action-Releasing Gate Neuron) (keyed to neuron catalogue Chapter 89)

MODES AND MODE TRANSITIONS:

This is a mostly two-mode inhibitory gate that idles slowly and answers a release from inhibition with a rebound burst, so its job is to lift restraint on whole behaviours rather than to command them.

State and arousal set the level from above through brainstem acetylcholine.

• THE MODE REGISTER.

◦ Slow tonic gating - the Standing Permission - low-rate inhibition setting the intensity of touch, feeding, fear, pain and sleep.

◦ Rebound bursting - the Release Kick - a low-threshold calcium burst fired the moment cortical drive pauses, converting a gap in input into a surge of output.

• PER-MODE TRANSFER FUNCTIONS.

◦ Standing Permission: 1.5 spikes per second in slow-wave states rising to about 6 spikes per second when activated, rheobase 23 to 41 picoamps by cell class, peak reachable 42 to 105 spikes per second, output mostly gamma-aminobutyric acid with a glutamate minority and A13 dopamine by volume transmission.

◦ Release Kick: T-type calcium channels arm during hyperpolarisation over about 100 milliseconds and fire a short rebound burst on release, storing recent inhibition as a delayed excitation.

• THE TRANSITION TABLE.

◦ Sudden loss of cortical layer 5 excitation - Standing Permission to Release Kick - tens to hundreds of milliseconds.

◦ Brainstem acetylcholine rising with arousal (thrown from above) - suppresses the population and raises output selectivity - seconds.

◦ Sleep pressure recruiting the Lhx6 subgroup (state broadcast) - shifts the sheet toward sleep-related firing - minutes.

• FAULT REGIMES AND WATCHDOGS.

◦ Too little output: the boundary signature is early, large, unfiltered fingertip signals reaching posterior thalamus, the picture of central pain syndrome where touch becomes agony; the watchdog is the standing GABA brake on the posterior thalamus.

◦ Disordered output feeds tremor; deliberate stimulation of the caudal zona incerta cuts contralateral tremor by about 88 percent, so the fault regime is therapeutically exploitable.


ENTRY 90. MAGNOCELLULAR NEUROSECRETORY CELLS (Large-Bodied Blood-Releasing Neuron) (keyed to neuron catalogue Chapter 90)

MODES AND MODE TRANSITIONS:

This parent type is a mode-rich neurosecretory hybrid whose two daughter families, the oxytocin and vasopressin cells of Chapters 91 and 92, each own a distinctive burst; at the shared level the register is a slow secretory idle and a demand-driven high-output state.

Modes are set by body state, osmolality and reproductive demand rather than by fast synaptic wiring.

• THE MODE REGISTER.

◦ Basal secretion - the Idle Drip - slow steady firing converting resting body facts into a low background hormone level.

◦ Stimulated high-output firing - the Demand Surge - facilitated firing that empties hormone into blood when the body is salty, bleeding or nursing.

◦ Dendritic broadcast - the Local Whisper - peptide released from dendrites into the nucleus to prime and couple neighbours, a mode running in parallel with the others.

• PER-MODE TRANSFER FUNCTIONS.

◦ Idle Drip: 1 to 3 spikes per second, output climbing about 0.4 to 0.5 picograms per millilitre per milliosmole per kilogram above a threshold near 280.

◦ Demand Surge: a slow depolarising afterpotential summates so firing eases further firing, ceiling near 13 spikes per second, with strong frequency facilitation of release.

◦ Local Whisper: back-propagating spikes drive dendritic peptide release of 0 to 100 nanomolar, with dynorphin self-timing and volume transmission independent of axonal output.

• THE TRANSITION TABLE.

◦ Rising plasma osmolality or blood loss (hormone and interoceptive state) - Idle Drip to Demand Surge - seconds to minutes.

◦ Astrocyte retraction during dehydration, birth or suckling (structural, thrown by body state) - enables synchrony and the Local Whisper - hours, reversing over days.

◦ Return to normal body water - Demand Surge back to Idle Drip - minutes.

• FAULT REGIMES AND WATCHDOGS.

◦ Loss of 80 to 90 percent of the vasopressin cells gives arginine vasopressin deficiency with torrential dilute urine; the watchdog is the intrinsic osmosensor and the astrocytic taurine brake.

◦ Inappropriate excess gives the syndrome of inappropriate antidiuresis, dangerously diluting blood sodium.


ENTRY 91. OXYTOCIN NEURONS (Birthing-And-Nursing Burst Neuron) (keyed to neuron catalogue Chapter 91)

MODES AND MODE TRANSITIONS:

This is a great mode-rich secretory cell defined by a rare and spectacular synchronised burst, so it alternates a regular slow drip with a whole-population three-second thunderclap that forces milk out in waves.

The burst is built by dendritic hormone priming rather than by wiring, and reproductive state sets the register from above.

• THE MODE REGISTER.

◦ Slow regular firing - the Steady Drip - evenly spaced background firing setting baseline plasma oxytocin.

◦ Milk-ejection bursting - the Nursing Thunderclap - an explosive synchronised burst every few minutes that ejects milk and, in labour, drives contractions.

◦ Dendritic priming - the Shared Charge - oxytocin released into the glia-free dendritic zone that primes every neighbour toward the next joint burst.

• PER-MODE TRANSFER FUNCTIONS.

◦ Steady Drip: 1 to 3 spikes per second kept regular by a transient potassium current, plasma oxytocin 1 to 5 picograms per millilitre.

◦ Nursing Thunderclap: 40 to 80 spikes per second for 2 to 4 seconds with every oxytocin cell in both hemispheres joining within about a second, strong frequency facilitation lifting plasma to a 20 to 100 picograms per millilitre pulse; inter-burst interval 3 to 10 minutes.

◦ Shared Charge: dendritic oxytocin builds over 10 to 60 seconds to a threshold near 0.6 (normalised), then discharges the population.

• THE TRANSITION TABLE.

◦ Dendritic oxytocin crossing threshold during suckling (autocrine and paracrine) - Steady Drip to Nursing Thunderclap - the priming step takes tens of seconds, the ignition about one second.

◦ Post-burst refractoriness - Thunderclap back to Steady Drip - a refractory pause of the order of minutes before priming can rebuild.

◦ Late-pregnancy chloride switch making gamma-aminobutyric acid (GABA) excitatory (hormonal, thrown from above) - raises readiness for bursting - days.

• FAULT REGIMES AND WATCHDOGS.

◦ Burst failure: the boundary signature is failed milk ejection despite normal milk production, and weak or prolonged labour; the watchdog is astrocyte retraction, which must occur for synchrony.

◦ Reduced central oxytocin signalling is implicated in autism spectrum conditions and Prader-Willi syndrome on correlational human evidence, not a used regime.


ENTRY 92. VASOPRESSIN NEURONS, ALSO CALLED ANTIDIURETIC HORMONE NEURONS (Water-Sparing Kidney-Signal Neuron) (keyed to neuron catalogue Chapter 92)

MODES AND MODE TRANSITIONS:

This is a mode-rich self-sensing oscillator whose signature is a phasic burst pattern, a genuine relaxation oscillator built from a fast self-excitation and a slow opioid self-brake.

Osmolality sets whether the cell idles or paces, and the cell is its own sensor.

• THE MODE REGISTER.

◦ Slow irregular firing - the Hydrated Idle - low-rate output when blood is dilute.

◦ Phasic bursting - the Thirst Pulse - alternating bursts and silences that release far more hormone per spike and let terminals recover.

◦ Dendritic autoinhibition - the Self-Timer - dynorphin released from the cell's own dendrites that ends each burst.

• PER-MODE TRANSFER FUNCTIONS.

◦ Hydrated Idle: 1 to 3 spikes per second, plasma vasopressin 0.5 to 20 picograms per millilitre, sensitivity 0.4 to 0.5 picograms per millilitre per milliosmole.

◦ Thirst Pulse: bursts of 20 to 60 seconds at 5 to 13 spikes per second separated by 15 to 40 seconds of silence, plateau built by a slow depolarising afterpotential, onset above an osmotic threshold near 280 to 285 milliosmoles per kilogram.

◦ Self-Timer: dendritic dynorphin accumulates over tens of seconds and switches off the afterpotential, setting the silent interval.

• THE TRANSITION TABLE.

◦ Rising plasma osmolality sensed by the shortened TRPV1 channel (intrinsic) - Hydrated Idle to Thirst Pulse - seconds; faster the thirstier the animal.

◦ Dendritic dynorphin reaching its ceiling - burst to silence within a phasic cycle - tens of seconds.

◦ Haemorrhage raising A1 noradrenaline (interoceptive, thrown from brainstem) - drives the cell hard toward continuous high output - seconds to minutes.

• FAULT REGIMES AND WATCHDOGS.

◦ Loss of 80 to 90 percent of these cells gives arginine vasopressin deficiency with 15 to 20 litres of dilute urine daily; the watchdog is the astrocytic taurine osmometer feeding the same cell.

◦ Unregulated excess gives the syndrome of inappropriate antidiuresis with confusion and seizures; the phasic silence is the built-in guard against terminal fatigue.


ENTRY 93. PARVOCELLULAR NEUROSECRETORY CELLS (Pituitary-Steering Small Neuron) (keyed to neuron catalogue Chapter 93)

MODES AND MODE TRANSITIONS:

This parent type is a pulse generator rather than a relay, so its essential mode is not a firing rate but a population pulse whose frequency the pituitary decodes.

Modes are largely thrown from above by stress, feeding, circadian and reproductive axes, and gated mechanically by glia.

• THE MODE REGISTER.

◦ Restrained basal firing - the Held Idle - irregular low-rate firing under dense gamma-aminobutyric acid (GABA) restraint, giving a low baseline hormone drip.

◦ Synchronised pulsing - the Episodic Salvo - the group firing together in bursts lasting minutes, the frequency and amplitude of which carry the message.

◦ Glial gating - the Tanycyte Shutter - a mode in which output changes with no change in spikes, as tanycyte processes open or close access to portal blood.

• PER-MODE TRANSFER FUNCTIONS.

◦ Held Idle: 2 to 10 spikes per second, many cells carrying a low-threshold calcium spike allowing brief doublets and triplets, hormone undetectable in the general circulation.

◦ Episodic Salvo: pulses 5 to 120 minutes apart, portal washout time constant about 6 seconds so the pituitary sees a sharp square pulse rather than a smeared average.

◦ Tanycyte Shutter: physical engulfing or exposure of terminals over hours, modulating delivered hormone independent of firing.

• THE TRANSITION TABLE.

◦ Lifting of GABA restraint plus glutamate drive (synaptic and hormonal) - Held Idle to Episodic Salvo - the low-threshold spike gives rebound firing within tens of milliseconds; pulse cadence over minutes.

◦ Steroid and thyroid feedback entering the nucleus (hormonal, thrown from above) - reshapes pulse frequency over hours by gene transcription.

◦ Tanycyte advance or retreat under oestrogen, thyroid hormone or nutrition - throws the Tanycyte Shutter - hours.

• FAULT REGIMES AND WATCHDOGS.

◦ Loss of the population, as in stalk section or tumour, causes panhypopituitarism with high prolactin as the tell-tale sign.

◦ Loss of pulsing alone is itself a disease, since continuous rather than pulsatile gonadotropin-releasing hormone shuts fertility down completely; the watchdog is the intrinsic tendency of the network to synchronise into episodes.


ENTRY 94. CORTICOTROPIN-RELEASING HORMONE (CRH) NEURONS (Stress-Cascade Starting Neuron) (keyed to neuron catalogue Chapter 94)

MODES AND MODE TRANSITIONS:

This is a mode-rich delayed-feedback oscillator that idles in rhythmic mini-bursts, flips to continuous firing under threat, and paces the whole hypothalamic-pituitary-adrenal axis in roughly hourly pulses.

Stress and the daily cortisol rhythm set the register from above.

• THE MODE REGISTER.

◦ Rhythmic mini-bursting - the Capped Idle - brief rhythmic bursts that cap the average rate at rest.

◦ Continuous single spiking - the Alarm Ramp - the mode a stressor throws it into, removing the cap and letting rate climb.

◦ Ultradian pulsing - the Hourly Cortisol Beat - a population rhythm near one hour, each upstate producing one cortisol pulse.

• PER-MODE TRANSFER FUNCTIONS.

◦ Capped Idle: 0.5 to 5 spikes per second in rhythmic bursts, a low-threshold calcium current giving rebound firing when inhibition lifts.

◦ Alarm Ramp: continuous firing to 15 to 25 spikes per second, corticotropin-releasing hormone plus co-released vasopressin multiplying the pituitary signal severalfold.

◦ Hourly Cortisol Beat: ultradian period 50 to 90 minutes, set by a roughly 15-minute adrenal transport delay in the feedback loop rather than by any membrane property.

• THE TRANSITION TABLE.

◦ Threat, pain, low blood sugar or cytokines (synaptic and blood-borne) - Capped Idle to Alarm Ramp - seconds, with immediate early genes switching on within about 5 minutes.

◦ Delayed cortisol feedback returning about 12 to 20 minutes later (hormonal, thrown by the axis) - resets each ultradian upstate - tens of minutes.

◦ Fast membrane and retrograde endocannabinoid feedback from cortisol - trims excitatory drive - 1 to 10 minutes.

• FAULT REGIMES AND WATCHDOGS.

◦ Persistent overactivity is the best-documented biological finding in major depression, where counted cell numbers run about four times control, and it appears in primary hypertension; the watchdog is genomic negative feedback by cortisol.

◦ Underactivity gives secondary adrenal insufficiency after long steroid treatment.


ENTRY 95. THYROTROPIN-RELEASING HORMONE (TRH) NEURONS (Chemical-Pace Setting Neuron) (keyed to neuron catalogue Chapter 95)

MODES AND MODE TRANSITIONS:

This is a mode-poor set-point cell with a movable target, holding a steady irregular output that it raises or lowers to defend body-wide metabolic rate, and whose feedback signal is manufactured for it by glia.

Energy state and thyroid feedback set the register, largely from above.

• THE MODE REGISTER.

◦ Set-point firing - the Metabolic Hold - modest irregular firing defending a chosen level of thyroid hormone.

◦ Energy-lowered output - the Fasting Turndown - a deliberately suppressed state that lowers metabolic rate when body fat is scarce, even though thyroid hormone is low.

• PER-MODE TRANSFER FUNCTIONS.

◦ Metabolic Hold: 1 to 6 spikes per second, irregular rather than bursting, feedback steep enough to hold free thyroxine within about 30 percent of set point, delivered hormone divided down by the tanycyte degrading enzyme.

◦ Fasting Turndown: gene expression and delivered hormone fall when leptin drops, a low-threshold calcium current still permitting rebound spikes when inhibition lifts.

• THE TRANSITION TABLE.

◦ Falling leptin and rising agouti-related peptide drive from the arcuate nucleus (hormonal and synaptic, thrown from above) - Metabolic Hold to Fasting Turndown - hours to days.

◦ Cold signal as noradrenaline from brainstem - raises output within the Hold - minutes.

◦ Tanycyte-made active triiodothyronine reaching the nucleus (glial feedback) - resets the set point - 6 to 48 hours.

• FAULT REGIMES AND WATCHDOGS.

◦ Loss or suppression causes central hypothyroidism, where thyroid hormone is low but thyroid stimulating hormone is not raised, the signature that the fault is in the brain.

◦ In starvation and severe illness the cells suppress their own gene despite low thyroid hormone, producing non-thyroidal illness syndrome, which looks like a bug and is probably a survival feature; the watchdog is the leptin permission signal.


ENTRY 96. GONADOTROPIN-RELEASING HORMONE (GNRH) NEURONS (Fertility Rhythm-Setting Neuron) (keyed to neuron catalogue Chapter 96)

MODES AND MODE TRANSITIONS:

This is a mode-rich pulse-and-surge cell in which a near-silent baseline is broken by hourly bursts, and once per cycle switched into a single sustained surge, because the pituitary answers a beat and goes deaf to a steady signal.

The KNDy pulse generator of Chapter 106 and ovarian oestrogen set the register from above.

• THE MODE REGISTER.

◦ Near-silent baseline - the Waiting Quiet - sub-hertz firing between pulses.

◦ Pulsatile bursting - the Hourly Beat - a burst of tens of seconds delivering one gonadotropin-releasing hormone pulse.

◦ Surge firing - the Ovulation Command - a prolonged high-output state that triggers the luteinising hormone surge and ovulation.

• PER-MODE TRANSFER FUNCTIONS.

◦ Waiting Quiet: under 1 spike per second, membrane near minus 60 millivolts.

◦ Hourly Beat: burst firing 5 to 15 spikes per second for 20 to 60 seconds, calcium from internal stores setting burst length, up to about 100 picograms per millilitre per pulse into portal blood; interpulse interval 60 minutes follicular, 90 luteal.

◦ Ovulation Command: sustained firing driven by rising oestrogen switching feedback from negative to positive, with remodelled synapses and glial coverage.

• THE TRANSITION TABLE.

◦ Kisspeptin drive from KNDy neurons (synaptic, thrown from above) - Waiting Quiet to Hourly Beat - seconds to tens of seconds.

◦ Oestrogen rising past the surge threshold across days (hormonal, thrown from above) - Hourly Beat to Ovulation Command - hours, reversing after ovulation.

◦ GnRH acting back on its own terminals by volume transmission - shapes burst termination within a pulse - tens of seconds.

• FAULT REGIMES AND WATCHDOGS.

◦ Failed nasal migration causes Kallmann syndrome, absent puberty with absent smell.

◦ Continuous rather than pulsatile exposure desensitises the pituitary and shuts fertility down, exploited deliberately by leuprolide; the watchdog is the intrinsic burst-and-silence structure of the beat.


ENTRY 97. GROWTH HORMONE-RELEASING HORMONE (GHRH) NEURONS (Growth-Spurt Prompting Neuron) (keyed to neuron catalogue Chapter 97)

MODES AND MODE TRANSITIONS:

This is a two-mode rhythm generator that uses inhibition as its metronome, alternating a quiet state with a rebound burst thrown when somatostatin lifts, so growth hormone leaves in sharp pulses rather than a stream.

Sleep and the opposing somatostatin brake set the register from above.

• THE MODE REGISTER.

◦ Braked quiet - the Held Pause - the state while somatostatin holds the potassium channels open.

◦ Rebound bursting - the Growth Prompt - a burst fired on withdrawal of that inhibition, loudest in deep sleep.

• PER-MODE TRANSFER FUNCTIONS.

◦ Held Pause: resting near minus 57 millivolts, near-silent while somatostatin tone is high.

◦ Growth Prompt: rebound firing 5 to 20 spikes per second on release, releasing up to about 500 picograms per millilitre of hormone into portal blood, ghrelin raising drive directly; 6 to 12 growth-hormone pulses daily.

• THE TRANSITION TABLE.

◦ Withdrawal of periventricular somatostatin (synaptic, thrown from above) - Held Pause to Growth Prompt - tens of seconds.

◦ Onset of slow-wave sleep (state broadcast) - biases toward the Growth Prompt, the loudest pulse arriving 60 to 90 minutes after sleep onset.

◦ Growth hormone feedback and rising somatostatin - Growth Prompt back to Held Pause - transcription over hours, membrane over seconds.

• FAULT REGIMES AND WATCHDOGS.

◦ GHRH receptor mutations cause growth hormone deficiency and severe short stature; the watchdog is the reciprocal somatostatin brake that shapes each pulse.

◦ A tumour elsewhere secreting the hormone drives acromegaly, adult overgrowth, a fault of drive rather than of this cell's mode.


ENTRY 98. SOMATOSTATIN NEURONS (Pulse-Trough Curbing Neuron) (keyed to neuron catalogue Chapter 98)

MODES AND MODE TRANSITIONS:

This is a mode-poor inhibitor whose whole purpose is subtraction, holding a steady brake and lifting it once a night, so its two states are simply a higher and a lower level of the same tonic suppression.

Growth hormone feedback and sleep set the register from above.

• THE MODE REGISTER.

◦ Tonic braking - the Standing No - continuous firing that digs the troughs between growth hormone pulses and also restrains thyroid stimulating hormone.

◦ Nightly withdrawal - the Released Gap - the drop in firing an hour after sleep onset that lets a growth hormone pulse escape.

• PER-MODE TRANSFER FUNCTIONS.

◦ Standing No: 2 to 6 spikes per second at rest rising to about 12 with growth hormone, releasing somatostatin-14 and somatostatin-28 that open potassium channels on targets; short form clears in about 3 minutes, long form several times slower.

◦ Released Gap: firing falls during deep sleep, occupancy of target receptors drops, and the growth hormone tap opens.

• THE TRANSITION TABLE.

◦ Rising growth hormone (hormonal feedback) - raises firing within the Standing No - minutes to hours.

◦ Onset of deep sleep (state broadcast from above) - Standing No to Released Gap - minutes.

◦ Chronic sleep loss - blunts the nightly withdrawal over days.

• FAULT REGIMES AND WATCHDOGS.

◦ Too little brake permits continuous rather than pulsatile growth hormone output, which is why somatostatin analogues such as octreotide and lanreotide treat acromegaly.

◦ No distinct pathological firing regime is used by this type; its risk is loss of rhythm rather than a runaway state.


ENTRY 99. TUBEROINFUNDIBULAR DOPAMINE (TIDA) NEURONS (Milk-Hormone Curbing Neuron) (keyed to neuron catalogue Chapter 99)

MODES AND MODE TRANSITIONS:

This is a two-mode intrinsic oscillator, a clock rather than a relay, that swings slowly between bursts and silences and is switched to steady firing by prolactin.

The register is set by the prolactin it controls, a hormonal feedback from above.

• THE MODE REGISTER.

◦ Phasic oscillation - the Slow Swing - an intrinsic rhythmic alternation of bursts and silences that clamps prolactin down by default.

◦ Tonic firing - the Nursing Reset - a steady broadened-spike mode prolactin throws it into, resetting dopamine output during lactation.

• PER-MODE TRANSFER FUNCTIONS.

◦ Slow Swing: membrane oscillation near 0.05 cycles per second in rodent slices, within-burst firing 5 to 20 spikes per second, dopamine swept away by blood rather than recaptured.

◦ Nursing Reset: continuous firing with broadened spikes, tyrosine hydroxylase and dopamine output rising over tens of minutes through the prolactin-STAT5 pathway.

• THE TRANSITION TABLE.

◦ Rising prolactin (hormonal feedback, thrown from above) - Slow Swing to Nursing Reset - minutes, with enzyme upregulation over about 30 minutes.

◦ Oxytocin excitation and opioid inhibition - modulate burst timing within the Swing - seconds.

◦ Weaning and falling prolactin - Nursing Reset back to Slow Swing - days.

• FAULT REGIMES AND WATCHDOGS.

◦ Antipsychotic blockade of dopamine D2 receptors removes the brake and causes hyperprolactinaemia with milk secretion and infertility; the watchdog is the prolactin feedback loop itself.

◦ Prolactin-secreting tumours are treated with cabergoline, a dopamine agonist replacing the missing restraint.


ENTRY 100. AGOUTI-RELATED PEPTIDE AND NEUROPEPTIDE Y (AGRP/NPY) NEURONS OF THE ARCUATE NUCLEUS (Hunger-Driving Foraging Neuron) (keyed to neuron catalogue Chapter 100)

MODES AND MODE TRANSITIONS:

This is a mode-rich multiplexing sensor whose firing carries a slow motivational urge, so its register is a fasted drive state, a fed quiet, and a striking anticipatory shut-off at the mere sight of food.

Blood-borne hormones set the level, not descending state.

• THE MODE REGISTER.

◦ Fasted drive firing - the Hunger Push - elevated tonic firing generating the urge to forage and eat.

◦ Fed quiet - the Full Rest - low firing when leptin and insulin report adequate stores.

◦ Sensory shut-off - the Sight-of-Food Silence - a rapid collapse of firing on seeing or smelling food, before any calorie is absorbed.

• PER-MODE TRANSFER FUNCTIONS.

◦ Hunger Push: firing up to about 20 spikes per second when fasted, driving gamma-aminobutyric acid in milliseconds, neuropeptide Y over minutes, and agouti-related peptide over hours as an inverse agonist at the melanocortin 4 receptor.

◦ Full Rest: 1 to 6 spikes per second, leptin and insulin inhibitory.

◦ Sight-of-Food Silence: firing falls within 1 to 5 seconds of a food cue, a predictive drop independent of blood chemistry.

• THE TRANSITION TABLE.

◦ Rising ghrelin and falling leptin (blood-borne) - Full Rest to Hunger Push - minutes, with synapses rearranging within hours.

◦ Food cue reaching the cell (sensory) - Hunger Push to Sight-of-Food Silence - 1 to 5 seconds.

◦ Absorption of a meal raising leptin and insulin - back toward Full Rest - minutes to hours.

• FAULT REGIMES AND WATCHDOGS.

◦ Losing these neurons in adulthood causes lethal loss of appetite, an animal starving beside food; the watchdog is the leptin and insulin brake reporting energy stores.

◦ Over-activity drives the hunger of leptin deficiency and post-diet regain, and their suppression contributes to cancer cachexia.


ENTRY 101. PRO-OPIOMELANOCORTIN AND COCAINE- AND AMPHETAMINE-REGULATED TRANSCRIPT (POMC/CART) NEURONS OF THE ARCUATE NUCLEUS (Meal-Ending Fullness Neuron) (keyed to neuron catalogue Chapter 101)

MODES AND MODE TRANSITIONS:

This is a mode-poor fullness reporter that holds a steady low firing scaled to fuel stores, its main computation being chemical rather than electrical, since one messenger RNA is cut into a satiety hormone, a pigment hormone and an opioid.

Blood leptin and insulin set the register.

• THE MODE REGISTER.

◦ Steady fullness firing - the Enough Signal - firing that rises with leptin and insulin and releases alpha-melanocyte-stimulating hormone to end the meal.

◦ Fasted quiet - the Deprivation Hush - suppressed firing under gamma-aminobutyric acid from the neighbouring hunger neurons when fuel is low.

• PER-MODE TRANSFER FUNCTIONS.

◦ Enough Signal: 2 to 8 spikes per second held low by a slow after-hyperpolarisation, alpha-melanocyte-stimulating hormone onto melanocortin 3 and 4 receptors, beta-endorphin onto mu-opioid receptors opposing its own satiety message.

◦ Deprivation Hush: firing falls under arcuate GABA, satiety peptide pool clearing over about 2 minutes.

• THE TRANSITION TABLE.

◦ Rising leptin, insulin and serotonin (blood-borne and synaptic) - Deprivation Hush to Enough Signal - minutes, with leptin rewiring synapses over hours.

◦ GABA drive from agouti-related peptide neurons - pushes toward the Hush - milliseconds to minutes.

◦ Early-life leptin surges - set the innervation of the paraventricular nucleus for life (developmental).

• FAULT REGIMES AND WATCHDOGS.

◦ Mutations in the precursor gene, convertase 1, the leptin receptor or the melanocortin 4 receptor cause severe childhood obesity, treatable with setmelanotide; the watchdog is the counterbalancing hunger circuit.

◦ Diet-driven inflammation can destroy these glucose-sensing cells, a slow structural failure rather than a used mode.


ENTRY 102. MELANOCORTIN RECEPTOR 4-EXPRESSING NEURONS OF THE PARAVENTRICULAR NUCLEUS (Appetite-Halting Hub Neuron) (keyed to neuron catalogue Chapter 102)

MODES AND MODE TRANSITIONS:

This is a mode-poor ratio detector that fires slowly and continuously and reports the balance between two arcuate peptides, its excitability set by a hormone receptor closing a potassium channel.

The register is set by the fed-versus-fasted peptide tug of war from below.

• THE MODE REGISTER.

◦ Satiety firing - the Stop-Eating Hub - raised firing when alpha-melanocyte-stimulating hormone dominates, passing a stop signal to the parabrachial nucleus.

◦ Hunger-quieted firing - the Keep-Eating Lull - lowered firing when agouti-related peptide dominates and reopens the potassium channel.

• PER-MODE TRANSFER FUNCTIONS.

◦ Stop-Eating Hub: spontaneous firing lifted from a 0.5 to 5 spikes per second baseline as the Kir7.1 potassium channel closes, voltage drifting toward minus 37 millivolts, high input resistance of 0.5 to 1 gigaohm making the cell sensitive to weak nudges.

◦ Keep-Eating Lull: agouti-related peptide as an inverse agonist reopens Kir7.1, hyperpolarising the cell by up to about 25 millivolts and lowering satiety output toward 0.

• THE TRANSITION TABLE.

◦ Shift in the alpha-melanocyte-stimulating-hormone-to-agouti-related-peptide ratio (peptide occupancy) - between Hub and Lull - peptide occupancy over 1 to 30 seconds.

◦ Leptin remodelling arcuate input (hormonal, thrown from below) - reshapes the balance - 2 to 24 hours.

◦ Receptor trafficking to the surface by its accessory protein - sets maximum gain - hours to days.

• FAULT REGIMES AND WATCHDOGS.

◦ Loss-of-function mutation in the melanocortin 4 receptor gives severe early-onset obesity, the commonest single-gene cause at roughly 1 in 500; the watchdog is the accessory protein escorting the receptor to the surface.

◦ Excess melanocortin drive, as in cancer wasting, produces anorexia and loss of body mass.


ENTRY 103. OREXIN NEURONS, ALSO CALLED HYPOCRETIN NEURONS, OF THE LATERAL HYPOTHALAMUS (Sleep-Intrusion Blocking Neuron) (keyed to neuron catalogue Chapter 103)

MODES AND MODE TRANSITIONS:

This is a mode-rich arousal-stabilising sensor whose firing tracks behavioural state, so its register runs from active-wake firing through drowsy quiet to near-silence in dream sleep, all while it doubles as a glucose and carbon-dioxide detector.

State and sleep pressure set the register from above, but the cell also self-ignites.

• THE MODE REGISTER.

◦ Active-wake firing - the Wake Hold - firing that clamps every other arousal system in the on position.

◦ Quiet-wake and sleep quiet - the Drowsy Idle - reduced firing as arousal falls.

◦ rapid-eye-movement (REM) near-silence - the Dream Cutout - almost complete silence during rapid eye movement sleep, keeping dream paralysis from leaking into waking.

• PER-MODE TRANSFER FUNCTIONS.

◦ Wake Hold: 3 to 7 spikes per second, resting near minus 58 millivolts close to threshold, orexin plus co-released glutamate exciting locus coeruleus, tuberomammillary and raphe targets, with a slow dynorphin self-brake.

◦ Drowsy Idle: sparse firing as accumulated adenosine and preoptic gamma-aminobutyric acid (GABA) rise; glucose above about 5 millimoles per litre opens a background potassium channel and quietens the cell.

◦ Dream Cutout: firing near zero, orexin receptor 2 signalling withdrawn from downstream arousal nuclei.

• THE TRANSITION TABLE.

◦ Falling arousal, rising adenosine and preoptic inhibition (state broadcast from above) - Wake Hold to Drowsy Idle - seconds to minutes; sleep pressure accumulates over 12 to 16 hours.

◦ Entry into rapid eye movement sleep (pontine drive, thrown from above) - Drowsy Idle to Dream Cutout - seconds.

◦ A meal raising blood glucose - lowers firing within the Idle - minutes; hunger or rising carbon dioxide raises it.

• FAULT REGIMES AND WATCHDOGS.

◦ Immune destruction of 85 to 95 percent of the population produces narcolepsy type 1, defined by cerebrospinal fluid orexin-A below 110 picograms per millilitre, with cataplexy and sleep-onset paralysis; the watchdog is astrocytic lactate supply, without which the cells cannot fire.

◦ Overactivity contributes to insomnia and panic, countered clinically by orexin receptor blockers such as suvorexant.


ENTRY 104. MELANIN-CONCENTRATING HORMONE (MCH) NEURONS OF THE LATERAL HYPOTHALAMUS (Dream-Sleep Promoting Neuron) (keyed to neuron catalogue Chapter 104)

MODES AND MODE TRANSITIONS:

This is a two-mode state cell that runs the mirror image of its orexin neighbours, near-silent by day and blazing during dreams, so its register is a waking quiet and a rapid-eye-movement-sleep burst.

State sets the mode from above; glucose excites where it inhibits orexin.

• THE MODE REGISTER.

◦ Waking silence - the Daytime Off - near-total quiet during waking.

◦ REM firing - the Dream Engine - maximal firing during rapid eye movement sleep, promoting that stage and quietening hippocampal memory circuits.

• PER-MODE TRANSFER FUNCTIONS.

◦ Daytime Off: essentially silent, resting near minus 60 millivolts, held down by preoptic gamma-aminobutyric acid (GABA) and locus coeruleus noradrenaline, with a prominent A-type potassium current delaying any first spike.

◦ Dream Engine: 3 to 6 spikes per second, melanin-concentrating hormone released to cortex, hippocampus and even cerebrospinal fluid, inhibitory at melanin-concentrating hormone receptor 1, an active instruction to forget.

• THE TRANSITION TABLE.

◦ Entry into rapid eye movement sleep, pontine sublaterodorsal drive (state broadcast from above) - Daytime Off to Dream Engine - 1 to 10 minutes.

◦ Rising glucose (blood-borne) - raises firing, the opposite of its effect on orexin cells - minutes.

◦ Waking, noradrenaline and orexin input - Dream Engine back to Daytime Off - minutes.

• FAULT REGIMES AND WATCHDOGS.

◦ Loss or silencing shortens and fragments rapid eye movement sleep and produces leanness resistant to diet-induced obesity.

◦ Overactivity is associated with excessive rapid eye movement sleep and weight gain; specific degeneration occurs in Alzheimer's disease, a structural fault rather than a used regime.


ENTRY 105. HISTAMINERGIC NEURONS OF THE TUBEROMAMMILLARY NUCLEUS (Wake-Holding Ceiling Neuron) (keyed to neuron catalogue Chapter 105)

MODES AND MODE TRANSITIONS:

This is a two-mode slow pacemaker, the sole histamine source for the brain, that ticks steadily whenever alert and falls completely silent in dream sleep, with its own autoreceptor computing its output level.

Sleep-wake state throws the switch from above.

• THE MODE REGISTER.

◦ Waking pacemaking - the Vigilance Tick - regular slow firing that broadcasts histamine and holds alertness.

◦ Sleep silence - the Lights Out - the cessation of firing in slow-wave and, absolutely, in rapid eye movement sleep.

• PER-MODE TRANSFER FUNCTIONS.

◦ Vigilance Tick: intrinsic pacemaking 1 to 4 spikes per second even in isolation, resting near minus 50 to minus 55 millivolts, broad 1.2 to 2 millisecond spikes, histamine released by volume transmission and self-limited through H3 autoreceptors suppressing release by 50 to 80 percent.

◦ Lights Out: firing near zero, near-halved already in quiet waking, absent in rapid eye movement sleep.

• THE TRANSITION TABLE.

◦ Ventrolateral preoptic gamma-aminobutyric acid and galanin, the sleep switch (thrown from above) - Vigilance Tick to Lights Out - seconds to minutes.

◦ Orexin excitation and circadian drive (thrown from above) - restores the Tick on waking - seconds to minutes.

◦ Extracellular histamine rising on H3 autoreceptors - self-limits firing within the Tick - tens of seconds to minutes.

• FAULT REGIMES AND WATCHDOGS.

◦ Blocking H1 receptors, as first-generation antihistamines do, causes sedation and cognitive slowing, an externally imposed Lights Out.

◦ Loss of these neurons in multiple system atrophy and dementia with Lewy bodies contributes to daytime sleepiness; the H3 autoreceptor is the built-in gain watchdog, and the drug pitolisant lifts it therapeutically.


ENTRY 106. KISSPEPTIN, NEUROKININ B, AND DYNORPHIN (KNDY) NEURONS (Hormone-Pulse Generator Neuron) (keyed to neuron catalogue Chapter 106)

MODES AND MODE TRANSITIONS:

This is a great mode-rich network oscillator whose computation lives in its chemistry, a relaxation oscillator that charges silently and discharges in a synchronised burst, because neurokinin B is the accelerator and dynorphin the brake carried in the same cells.

Sex-steroid feedback sets the pulse frequency from the bloodstream.

• THE MODE REGISTER.

◦ Inter-pulse silence - the Charging Quiet - the recovery phase while dynorphin inhibition wears off.

◦ Synchronised bursting - the Fertility Beat - the whole population igniting together to fire one kisspeptin pulse onto gonadotropin-releasing hormone cells.

• PER-MODE TRANSFER FUNCTIONS.

◦ Charging Quiet: well under 1 spike per second, dynorphin acting on the kappa opioid receptor over 10 to 60 minutes and decaying to release the network.

◦ Fertility Beat: individual rates 5 to 20 spikes per second for 1 to several minutes, neurokinin B on the NK3 receptor plus fast glutamate building all-or-none synchrony, one pulse every 30 to 120 minutes; the period is set by peptide decay, not membrane properties.

• THE TRANSITION TABLE.

◦ Dynorphin inhibition decaying below threshold (peptide) - Charging Quiet to Fertility Beat - the burst ignites over seconds once the brake clears.

◦ Dynorphin release at the burst peak - Fertility Beat back to Charging Quiet - tens of seconds to minutes.

◦ Oestradiol or testosterone through nuclear receptors, and energy-state signals (hormonal, thrown from above) - reset overall pulse frequency - hours to days; menopausal oestrogen loss speeds and enlarges the beat over months.

• FAULT REGIMES AND WATCHDOGS.

◦ Loss-of-function mutations in TAC3, TACR3, KISS1 or KISS1R abolish the pulse and cause failure of puberty; the watchdog is the paired accelerator and brake that keep the burst self-limiting.

◦ After menopause the neurons hypertrophy and their runaway neurokinin B signalling onto the preoptic thermostat produces hot flushes, treated with NK3 blockers such as fezolinetant.


ENTRY 107. SUPRACHIASMATIC NUCLEUS NEURONS (Master Body-Clock Neuron) (keyed to neuron catalogue Chapter 107)

MODES AND MODE TRANSITIONS:

This is the clearest case in the brain of a cell that computes with gene expression, so its true state variable is clock-protein abundance and its firing is a two-level day-and-night readout downstream of that loop.

Light resets the phase; otherwise the mode is self-generated.

• THE MODE REGISTER.

◦ Subjective-day firing - the Daylight High - raised tonic firing broadcasting the daytime phase to the body.

◦ Subjective-night silence - the Nighttime Low - near-silence, many cells electrically quiet.

◦ Light-reset transient - the Phase Nudge - the gene-induction state triggered when light arrives in the subjective night.

• PER-MODE TRANSFER FUNCTIONS.

◦ Daylight High: 6 to 10 spikes per second, resting near minus 45 millivolts, driven by clock-gated potassium and persistent sodium currents.

◦ Nighttime Low: below 1 spike per second, resting near minus 60 millivolts.

◦ Phase Nudge: glutamate and pituitary adenylate cyclase-activating polypeptide from melanopsin retinal cells induce Period genes, shifting phase by up to about 1 to 2 hours per pulse.

• THE TRANSITION TABLE.

◦ One turn of the transcription-translation feedback loop (intrinsic) - Daylight High to Nighttime Low and back - about 24 hours, free-running period near 24.2 hours in humans.

◦ Light in the subjective night (sensory) - throws the Phase Nudge - immediate early gene induction over 15 to 60 minutes.

◦ Vasoactive intestinal peptide coupling and astrocytic glutamate - hold the population in step - continuous; loss of coupling scatters phase.

• FAULT REGIMES AND WATCHDOGS.

◦ Clock-gene mutations cause inherited sleep-timing disorders, PER2 giving familial advanced sleep phase and a CRY1 variant delayed sleep phase.

◦ Age and Alzheimer's disease reduce neuron number and flatten the rhythm, producing sundowning; shift work and jet lag desynchronise the nucleus from organ clocks, with the astrocyte clock acting as a partner watchdog.


ENTRY 108. PREAUTONOMIC NEURONS OF THE PARAVENTRICULAR NUCLEUS (Organ-Command Setting Neuron) (keyed to neuron catalogue Chapter 108)

MODES AND MODE TRANSITIONS:

This is a two-mode descending controller that idles slowly, rebounds into a burst when released from inhibition, and can slide over days into a chronically high-gain state, biasing brainstem and spinal control of the heart and vessels.

Interoceptive feedback sets the moment-to-moment level; chronic load resets the gain.

• THE MODE REGISTER.

◦ Slow tonic drive - the Coasting Set - low-rate firing that biases blood pressure, heart rate and salt handling.

◦ Rebound bursting - the Release Burst - a low-threshold calcium burst fired the instant inhibition lifts.

◦ High-gain overdrive - the Overload Gear - a used but pathological standing state of fast tonic firing under chronic cardiovascular load, admitted here because it is reached routinely in disease and is largely reversible.

• PER-MODE TRANSFER FUNCTIONS.

◦ Coasting Set: 0.5 to 5 spikes per second, resting minus 60 millivolts, high input resistance of 0.5 to 1.5 gigaohms, output glutamate plus oxytocin, vasopressin or corticotropin-releasing hormone onto sympathetic and vagal targets.

◦ Release Burst: T-type calcium channels de-inactivate over 100 to 300 milliseconds and fire a rebound burst on release from gamma-aminobutyric acid (GABA).

◦ Overload Gear: firing several times faster for the same input as the GABA and nitric oxide brakes weaken, spike output up to about 20 spikes per second.

• THE TRANSITION TABLE.

◦ Baroreceptor error and angiotensin II (interoceptive and blood-borne) - modulate within the Coasting Set - seconds.

◦ Sudden loss of perinuclear GABA - Coasting Set to Release Burst - tens to hundreds of milliseconds.

◦ Chronic heart failure or salt loading weakening the brakes (slow gain change) - into the Overload Gear - 1 to 14 days, reversible if the load is removed.

• FAULT REGIMES AND WATCHDOGS.

◦ In hypertension and heart failure the Overload Gear produces sustained sympathetic overdrive, salt retention and arrhythmia; the watchdogs are the GABA shell and neuronal nitric oxide, whose failure defines the regime.

◦ Loss of the vagal branch removes the heart-slowing arm and raises resting heart rate.


ENTRY 109. OREXIGENIC AND THERMOREGULATORY NEURONS OF THE PREOPTIC AREA, INCLUDING WARM-SENSITIVE AND COLD-SENSITIVE CELLS (Body-Heat Thermostat Neuron) (keyed to neuron catalogue Chapter 109)

MODES AND MODE TRANSITIONS:

This is a mode-poor analogue thermostat cell that reads temperature in the slope of a subthreshold voltage ramp and holds heat generation off by firing, so its main register is a graded pacemaking state whose set point can be moved chemically.

The set point is thrown from above during fever, pregnancy and acclimatisation.

• THE MODE REGISTER.

◦ Graded warm-sensitive pacemaking - the Standing Thermostat - continuous firing that rises with local warmth and tonically inhibits heat-generating pathways.

◦ Raised-set-point firing - the Fever Reset - a state in which prostaglandin silences the brake so the body defends a higher temperature.

• PER-MODE TRANSFER FUNCTIONS.

◦ Standing Thermostat: 2 to 10 spikes per second at 37 degrees Celsius, a depolarising prepotential whose slope sets rate, thermosensitivity 0.8 to 5 spikes per second per degree Celsius, tonic gamma-aminobutyric acid onto the dorsomedial hypothalamus and raphe pallidus; heat generation produced by disinhibition.

◦ Fever Reset: prostaglandin E2 at the EP3 receptor switches off the GABA brake, raising the defended set point by up to about 2.5 degrees Celsius, half-maximal near 10 nanomolar.

• THE TRANSITION TABLE.

◦ Local or blood temperature change (intrinsic and interoceptive) - grades firing within the Standing Thermostat - seconds to minutes.

◦ Prostaglandin E2 from vessel walls during infection (immune, thrown from above) - Standing Thermostat to Fever Reset - 30 seconds to 10 minutes.

◦ Progesterone or heat and cold acclimatisation (hormonal and adaptive) - shift the set point - days to two weeks.

• FAULT REGIMES AND WATCHDOGS.

◦ Runaway loss of the brake gives fever and, in extreme form, heat stroke or malignant hyperthermia; the watchdog is the reciprocal warm-and-cold effector balance around the set point.

◦ Loss of the cells gives poikilothermia, body temperature drifting with the environment, as after hypothalamic surgery or Wernicke encephalopathy; blunted thermosensitivity in old age is a major reason the elderly die in heat waves.


ENTRY 110. MAMMILLARY BODY NEURONS (Memory-Circuit Relay Neuron) (keyed to neuron catalogue Chapter 110)

MODES AND MODE TRANSITIONS:

This is a two-mode active relay that imposes a rhythm on what it passes, locking many of its spikes to the hippocampal theta rhythm and switching into rebound bursts, rather than copying its input passively.

Brain state and movement set the register.

• THE MODE REGISTER.

◦ Theta-locked relay firing - the Rhythmic Pass - firing phase-locked to the hippocampal theta rhythm that carries memory traffic to the anterior thalamus.

◦ Rebound bursting - the Held-Then-Fired Burst - a burst that follows release from inhibition and sharpens directional and speed signals.

• PER-MODE TRANSFER FUNCTIONS.

◦ Rhythmic Pass: a large proportion of spikes locked to the 4 to 12 hertz theta rhythm, firing tracking running speed and head-turning speed, output up the mammillothalamic tract.

◦ Held-Then-Fired Burst: post-inhibitory rebound firing that holds information across the seconds-long gap between seeing something and acting; burst parameters soft, moderate confidence.

• THE TRANSITION TABLE.

◦ Onset of hippocampal theta during exploration (state, thrown from above) - into the Rhythmic Pass - within a theta cycle, tens to a couple of hundred milliseconds.

◦ Release from fornix or local inhibition - Rhythmic Pass to Held-Then-Fired Burst - tens of milliseconds.

◦ Quiet immobility or slow-wave sleep - out of theta locking - seconds.

• FAULT REGIMES AND WATCHDOGS.

◦ Thiamine deficiency shrinks these cells and cuts their output tract, producing the lasting amnesia of Korsakoff syndrome; the watchdog is the dense shared vascular supply, whose failure defines the vulnerability.

◦ No distinct runaway firing regime is used by this type; its risk is loss and disconnection rather than an aberrant mode.


ENTRY 111. BLOOD-SAMPLING THIRST NEURON (keyed to neuron catalogue Chapter 111)

MODES AND MODE TRANSITIONS:

This is a mostly mode-poor graded sensor sitting outside the blood-brain barrier, converting blood saltiness into a continuously scaled thirst drive, with a fast predictive shut-off when swallowing begins.

Blood chemistry sets the level directly, with little descending state control.

• THE MODE REGISTER.

◦ Graded osmosensitive firing - the Thirst Meter - firing that climbs smoothly with plasma osmolality and angiotensin to generate felt thirst.

◦ Swallow-gated suppression - the Drinking Cutout - a rapid feedforward drop in firing triggered by the act of swallowing, before blood sodium has changed.

• PER-MODE TRANSFER FUNCTIONS.

◦ Thirst Meter: 1 to 3 spikes per second at 285 milliosmoles per kilogram rising to 10 to 20 at 305, intrinsic osmosensing through the TMEM63B channel, osmotic gain about 0.5 to 1 spike per second per milliosmole, angiotensin adding a second dimension; glutamatergic output recruiting even the ventral tegmental reward system.

◦ Drinking Cutout: firing suppressed within seconds of swallowing and decaying over about 5 seconds, a predictive brake; nitric oxide gives automatic self-limiting gain control.

• THE TRANSITION TABLE.

◦ Rising plasma osmolality or angiotensin II (blood-borne, glia-relayed) - raises the Thirst Meter - seconds; the primary sodium reading is taken by Nax-bearing glia and handed over as lactate.

◦ Onset of swallowing (oropharyngeal sensory) - Thirst Meter to Drinking Cutout - seconds.

◦ Pregnancy lowering the osmotic set point by 5 to 10 milliosmoles per kilogram (hormonal) - resets the meter - weeks.

• FAULT REGIMES AND WATCHDOGS.

◦ Destruction or autoimmune attack on Nax causes adipsic hypernatremia, chronically high blood sodium with no thirst at all; the watchdog is the glial sodium sensor whose loss defines the disease.

◦ Failure of the swallow brake produces overdrinking and dangerously low blood sodium.


ENTRY 112. PUSH-AND-PULL NOVELTY NEURON (keyed to neuron catalogue Chapter 112)

MODES AND MODE TRANSITIONS:

This is a mode-rich supramammillary cell whose modes live at the synapse, since it ships excitation and inhibition down the same axon and lets the ratio flip with firing rate, so a slow baseline becomes a net-excitatory novelty signal.

Arousal and novelty set the register.

• THE MODE REGISTER.

◦ Balanced baseline firing - the Familiar Hum - moderate firing in which co-released glutamate and gamma-aminobutyric acid roughly cancel.

◦ Novelty high-frequency firing - the New-World Alert - fast theta-locked firing that becomes net excitatory and flags a moment to the hippocampus as worth recording.

• PER-MODE TRANSFER FUNCTIONS.

◦ Familiar Hum: 5 to 25 spikes per second, glutamate-to-gamma-aminobutyric-acid release ratio near 1, theta phase-locked, resting near minus 58 millivolts.

◦ New-World Alert: 30 to 60 spikes per second on entering a novel environment, glutamate depression lagging behind, ratio rising above 2, so the terminal acts as a high-pass filter passing only strong novelty as excitation.

• THE TRANSITION TABLE.

◦ Novelty and salience input from the ventral tegmental area plus orexin arousal (synaptic, thrown from above) - Familiar Hum to New-World Alert - milliseconds to a theta cycle.

◦ Sustained firing driving frequency-dependent glutamate depression - flips the push-pull ratio - 200 to 800 milliseconds.

◦ Habituation to the environment (novelty adaptation) - back to the Familiar Hum - 30 seconds to 10 minutes.

• FAULT REGIMES AND WATCHDOGS.

◦ Excessive drive is implicated in the hyperarousal and intrusive memory formation of post-traumatic states.

◦ Degradation of the balance toward pure excitation is a plausible contributor to seizure spread from the dentate gyrus, since the gamma-aminobutyric acid component normally caps how hard the hippocampal input stage can be driven; that co-released inhibition is the built-in watchdog.


ENTRY 113. PURKINJE CELLS (keyed to neuron catalogue Chapter 113)

MODES AND MODE TRANSITIONS:

This is one of the great mode-rich cells of the brain, a self-driving pacemaker that fires two utterly different spike types and answers its single climbing fibre with a pause, so it carries information by modulating a continuous inhibitory beat.

Most modes are intrinsic, with neuromodulators setting gain from above.

• THE MODE REGISTER.

◦ Simple-spike pacemaking - the Steady Brake - continuous tonic firing that shapes the timing and size of movement by speeding and slowing.

◦ Complex spiking - the Error Flag - a rare climbing-fibre-triggered burst of spikelets marking that something went wrong.

◦ Post-complex-spike pause - the Teaching Gap - a brief silence after each complex spike that carries information downstream.

◦ Dendritic calcium spiking - the Branch Compute - local calcium spikes confined to single branches, letting one cell hold several semi-independent computations.

• PER-MODE TRANSFER FUNCTIONS.

◦ Steady Brake: 20 to 100 and up to about 200 spikes per second, driven by a resurgent Nav1.6 sodium current, zebrin identity setting baseline near 60 or 90 spikes per second, output inhibitory onto deep nuclear neurons at about 40 to 1 convergence.

◦ Error Flag: 1 to 2 per second, one big depolarisation carrying spikelets near 600 spikes per second from the single climbing fibre, all-or-nothing.

◦ Teaching Gap: a pause of 10 to 30 milliseconds following the complex spike.

◦ Branch Compute: P and Q type calcium spikes decaying over about 200 milliseconds, weak decremental back-propagation keeping branches semi-independent.

• THE TRANSITION TABLE.

◦ Climbing fibre event from the inferior olive (synaptic) - Steady Brake to Error Flag and Teaching Gap - milliseconds.

◦ Coincidence of parallel fibre input with a complex spike (the conjunction rule) - throws long-term depression of parallel fibre gain - induction window 100 to 200 milliseconds, consolidation minutes to hours.

◦ Noradrenaline and serotonin (neuromodulators, thrown from above) - set simple-spike gain - seconds.

• FAULT REGIMES AND WATCHDOGS.

◦ In spinocerebellar ataxias the cell first loses dendrites and regular firing and only later dies, so ataxia begins as dysfunction; the watchdog is the huge metabolic support and the Bergmann glial partner.

◦ In essential tremor density falls and axonal torpedoes appear; loss of the P and Q calcium channel causes episodic ataxia type 2, and autoantibodies cause paraneoplastic cerebellar degeneration.


ENTRY 114. CEREBELLAR GRANULE CELLS (keyed to neuron catalogue Chapter 114)

MODES AND MODE TRANSITIONS:

This is an honest two-mode coincidence detector, electrotonically so compact that it does no dendritic computation, so it is either silent or firing a brief high-frequency burst when enough of its four inputs coincide.

Its threshold is set from outside by a glial curtain of inhibition.

• THE MODE REGISTER.

◦ Sub-threshold silence - the Waiting Dark - the quiet resting state that keeps the granule code sparse.

◦ Coincidence bursting - the Timing Spark - a short high-frequency burst fired only when several mossy fibres arrive together, encoding a precise moment.

• PER-MODE TRANSFER FUNCTIONS.

◦ Waiting Dark: resting near minus 70 millivolts, very high input resistance, held below threshold by tonic inhibition from an ambient gamma-aminobutyric acid of 0.1 to 0.2 micromolar acting on alpha-6 receptors.

◦ Timing Spark: needs roughly two to four of four dendrites active at once, then fires up to several hundred spikes per second, delivered along the parallel fibre with a 0 to 10 millisecond conduction delay that converts distance into timing.

• THE TRANSITION TABLE.

◦ Coincident mossy fibre bursts overcoming the tonic curtain (synaptic) - Waiting Dark to Timing Spark - within a millisecond or two.

◦ Golgi cell phasic inhibition and a rise in ambient gamma-aminobutyric acid (glial-gated, thrown from the Golgi network) - raises the threshold back toward silence - tens of milliseconds.

◦ A Golgi cell pause opening a window of freedom - transiently eases entry into the Spark - hundreds of milliseconds.

• FAULT REGIMES AND WATCHDOGS.

◦ Loss of granule cells produces cerebellar hypoplasia and severe ataxia; uncontrolled proliferation of their precursors produces sonic hedgehog medulloblastoma.

◦ Alcohol and benzodiazepine-like drugs act on the alpha-6 receptor, deepening the tonic curtain so intoxication mimics cerebellar disease; that tonic inhibition is both the normal watchdog and the drug target.


ENTRY 115. GOLGI CELLS (keyed to neuron catalogue Chapter 115)

MODES AND MODE TRANSITIONS:

This is a mode-rich pacemaker and gain-control cell that keeps the granule code sparse, running a spontaneous rhythm, a driven high-rate mode and a paradoxical post-inhibition speed-up, while gap junctions make a group oscillate as one.

Much of its mode setting is intrinsic, with granule and modulatory input adjusting it.

• THE MODE REGISTER.

◦ Spontaneous pacemaking - the Standing Rhythm - autonomous firing that imposes a theta-band rhythm on the granular layer and sets tonic inhibition.

◦ Driven firing - the Sparseness Clamp - faster firing when mossy and granule input rises, cutting how many granule cells respond.

◦ Granule-driven pause - the Freedom Window - a silence of hundreds of milliseconds when strong granule input arrives, releasing granule cells.

◦ Post-hyperpolarisation speed-up - the Rebound Overdrive - a lasting rise in spontaneous rate after inhibition, so the cell remembers having been inhibited.

• PER-MODE TRANSFER FUNCTIONS.

◦ Standing Rhythm: 1 to 10 spikes per second, an h current pulling the membrane back after each dip; output mixed gamma-aminobutyric acid and glycine into about 40 glomeruli reaching roughly 2,000 granule cells, plus a tonic ambient level of 0.1 to 0.2 micromolar.

◦ Sparseness Clamp: 20 to 50 spikes per second when driven.

◦ Freedom Window: full silence for 100 to 500 milliseconds.

◦ Rebound Overdrive: a long-lasting rate increase after hyperpolarisation or metabotropic glutamate receptor 2 activation, developing over minutes.

• THE TRANSITION TABLE.

◦ Mossy and parallel fibre input rising (synaptic) - Standing Rhythm to Sparseness Clamp - milliseconds.

◦ Strong granule cell ascending input - into the Freedom Window - tens of milliseconds, lasting hundreds.

◦ A period of hyperpolarisation - into the Rebound Overdrive - the rate change persists for minutes; gap junction current keeps neighbours phase-locked continuously.

• FAULT REGIMES AND WATCHDOGS.

◦ Selective ablation causes severe ataxia within days from loss of granule-layer sparseness.

◦ Ethanol blocks the granule-driven silencing, over-inhibiting the granular layer and contributing to the ataxia of intoxication; the presynaptic gamma-aminobutyric acid type B autoreceptor is the built-in output watchdog.


ENTRY 116. LUGARO CELLS (keyed to neuron catalogue Chapter 116)

MODES AND MODE TRANSITIONS:

This is a two-mode chemically gated switch, a standing reserve that is functionally silent until serotonin arrives and then fires like a metronome, broadcasting a state rather than a message across a whole parasagittal strip.

The mode is thrown from above by raphe serotonin.

• THE MODE REGISTER.

◦ Serotonin-off silence - the Dormant Reserve - near-total silence that costs almost nothing to maintain.

◦ Serotonin-on firing - the Strip Switch - steady regular firing that inhibits Golgi, basket and stellate cells along a strip and changes the operating mode of that slab of cortex.

• PER-MODE TRANSFER FUNCTIONS.

◦ Dormant Reserve: about 0.06 spikes per second, roughly one spike every sixteen seconds, held below threshold and further suppressed by Purkinje axon collaterals.

◦ Strip Switch: about 15 spikes per second, remarkably regular, firing at its own rate rather than following inputs, releasing gamma-aminobutyric acid onto Purkinje cells and gamma-aminobutyric acid plus glycine onto Golgi cells; noradrenaline notably does not switch it on.

• THE TRANSITION TABLE.

◦ Serotonin flooding the cortex from the raphe by volume transmission (neuromodulator, thrown from above) - Dormant Reserve to Strip Switch - a jump of more than two hundredfold, over seconds as serotonin binds 5-hydroxytryptamine type 2 receptors.

◦ Serotonin clearing - Strip Switch back to Dormant Reserve - seconds to minutes.

◦ Prolonged serotonin exposure desensitising the receptor - weakens the switch (adaptation) - minutes.

• FAULT REGIMES AND WATCHDOGS.

◦ Plasticity here is poorly characterised, and it is honest to say so; no long-term potentiation or depression rule has been demonstrated at any of its synapses, so a model should treat plasticity as absent unless new data appear.

◦ No distinct pathological firing regime is documented for this type; its defining risk is failure of the serotonin switch, with Purkinje collateral input serving as the standing brake that holds it dormant.


ENTRY 117. GLOBULAR CELLS (Feedback-Loop Wake-Boosting Neuron) (keyed to neuron catalogue Chapter 117)

MODES AND MODE TRANSITIONS:

The globular cell is a two-mode switch that is normally held in the off position by its own cortex and lifted out of it only by an arousal chemical.

It is mode-poor because it has no intrinsic pacemaker of its own, so its state is set almost entirely from outside: by the Purkinje cells above it and by the neuromodulators that report behavioural state.

• THE MODE REGISTER.

◦ Silent held-down state - the Pinned Silence - the resting condition in which about six Purkinje cells clamp the cell below threshold and it emits no spikes at all.

◦ Noradrenaline-driven firing - the Wake-Up Lift - the aroused condition in which noradrenaline from the locus coeruleus depolarises the cell and it fires in the low teens of hertz, disinhibiting the cortex.

◦ Serotonin-driven trickle - the Faint Stir - a weak firing condition, admitted only tentatively, in which serotonin lifts the cell to a couple of hertz at most.

• PER-MODE TRANSFER FUNCTIONS.

◦ In the Pinned Silence, input is an inhibitory barrage of about 53 events per second at roughly 85 picoamperes and output is zero hertz; this holds whenever Purkinje output is strong.
