◦ In the Wake-Up Lift, a rising noradrenaline concentration (treated as a scalar from 0 to 1 acting on alpha-1 adrenergic receptors) maps to a firing rate that reaches a measured maximum of about 12.7 to 14.0 hertz; confidence is moderate because only six cells were tested.

◦ In the Faint Stir, serotonin takes the cell from 0 to about 2.1 plus or minus 0.8 hertz, a weak effect whose behavioural relevance is low-confidence.

• THE TRANSITION TABLE.

◦ Collapse of Purkinje output (a strong cortical pause) - from Pinned Silence towards firing - within a few milliseconds, because removing the 53 hertz barrage lets the cell rise to threshold.

◦ Arousal broadcast of noradrenaline, thrown from above by the locus coeruleus during waking and stress - from Pinned Silence to Wake-Up Lift - over hundreds of milliseconds as the modulator rises and falls in tissue.

◦ Serotonin broadcast from the raphe - from Pinned Silence to Faint Stir - over hundreds of milliseconds, weaker and easily overridden.

• FAULT REGIMES AND WATCHDOGS.

◦ Tonic runaway firing: any condition that abolishes Purkinje firing, such as severe spinocerebellar ataxia, releases the cell from its dominant inhibition and drives it continuously; this is not a mode the healthy cell uses and its watchdog is simply the presence of ongoing Purkinje output.

◦ Pharmacological drive through alpha-1 adrenergic receptors (some antihypertensives and antipsychotics) can shift the operating point artificially, which a model should treat as an external perturbation rather than a native regime.


ENTRY 118. CANDELABRUM CELLS (keyed to neuron catalogue Chapter 118)

MODES AND MODE TRANSITIONS:

The candelabrum cell is a mode-poor inhibitory interneuron of the Purkinje cell layer whose regime set is thinly documented, so the minimal defensible register is a near-silent baseline with modulator-driven firing.

It is best modelled as a slow, sparsely active restraint element rather than a pacemaker.

• THE MODE REGISTER.

◦ Quiescent low-rate baseline - the Standing Hush - a near-silent or very slow resting condition that is its default; confidence moderate.

◦ Modulator-lifted firing - the Chemical Nudge - a raised firing condition when serotonin or noradrenaline from the brainstem arrives, by analogy with its Lugaro and globular neighbours; confidence low because direct recordings are scarce.

• PER-MODE TRANSFER FUNCTIONS.

◦ In the Standing Hush, sparse synaptic input yields little or no output, with any firing likely under a few hertz; numbers are soft and should carry a low-confidence flag.

◦ In the Chemical Nudge, a rising monoamine concentration (scalar 0 to 1) maps to a modest firing rate of a few hertz, its glycinergic and GABAergic (gamma-aminobutyric acid using) output spreading inhibition through the Purkinje cell layer.

• THE TRANSITION TABLE.

◦ Monoamine arrival, thrown from above by arousal-state systems - from Standing Hush to Chemical Nudge - over hundreds of milliseconds.

◦ Withdrawal of that modulator - from Chemical Nudge back to Standing Hush - over a similar timescale.

• FAULT REGIMES AND WATCHDOGS.

◦ The literature is genuinely sparse for this type, so no distinctive pathological regime is established; the honest statement is that its fault behaviour is unknown and should be recorded as a known unknown rather than assumed benign.


ENTRY 119. UNIPOLAR BRUSH CELLS (UBCS) (keyed to neuron catalogue Chapter 119)

MODES AND MODE TRANSITIONS:

The unipolar brush cell (UBC) is a genuine mode-rich amplifier: a single mossy-fibre input onto its brush-like dendrite produces prolonged after-discharges, and the family splits into oppositely signed subtypes, so it is both an integrator and a two-way switch.

It exists to stretch a brief vestibular or mossy signal into a long internal echo.

• THE MODE REGISTER.

◦ ON response with long after-discharge - the Slow Echo - the excited condition in which glutamate spillover drives a firing burst that outlasts the input by hundreds of milliseconds to seconds.

◦ OFF response with pause and rebound - the Held Breath - the inhibited condition of the OFF subtype, in which input suppresses firing and release produces a rebound.

◦ Tonic baseline - the Idling Tick - a spontaneous slow regular firing between inputs, on the order of a few hertz.

• PER-MODE TRANSFER FUNCTIONS.

◦ In the Slow Echo, a single mossy-fibre burst maps to an amplified after-discharge climbing to tens of hertz and decaying over roughly 100 milliseconds to several seconds, mediated by slow alpha-amino-3-hydroxy-5-methyl-4-isoxazolepropionic acid (AMPA)-type and metabotropic glutamate currents; confidence moderate.

◦ In the Held Breath, input drives inhibitory metabotropic signalling and output falls to zero for the duration, then rebounds; the sign is set by receptor complement.

◦ In the Idling Tick, weak drive yields a regular baseline of roughly 1 to 5 hertz; confidence moderate.

• THE TRANSITION TABLE.

◦ A mossy-fibre burst - from Idling Tick into Slow Echo (ON subtype) or Held Breath (OFF subtype) - within milliseconds, with the after-discharge then decaying over seconds.

◦ Depletion of the slow current - from Slow Echo back to Idling Tick - over hundreds of milliseconds to seconds intrinsically, not thrown from above.

• FAULT REGIMES AND WATCHDOGS.

◦ Runaway self-reexcitation: because UBCs form feedforward chains among themselves, excessive amplification could in principle sustain pathological reverberation; the biological watchdog is Golgi-cell inhibition of the granular layer, and no distinct human disease is known to isolate this regime.


ENTRY 120. STELLATE CELLS (keyed to neuron catalogue Chapter 120)

MODES AND MODE TRANSITIONS:

The molecular-layer stellate cell is a mode-poor tonic inhibitor: a slow intrinsic pacemaker whose output is set by how much parallel-fibre excitation rides on top of its baseline.

It is a feedforward brake on Purkinje cells, not a switch.

• THE MODE REGISTER.

◦ Spontaneous tonic firing - the Background Tick - an intrinsic pacemaker baseline of a few to low tens of hertz that provides standing inhibition to Purkinje cells.

◦ Parallel-fibre-driven firing - the Beam Brake - the raised-rate condition when a parallel-fibre beam excites the cell, sharpening Purkinje output by feedforward inhibition.

• PER-MODE TRANSFER FUNCTIONS.

◦ In the Background Tick, with no phasic input the cell self-fires at roughly 5 to 30 hertz; confidence moderate.

◦ In the Beam Brake, parallel-fibre excitation adds linearly to the baseline, output climbing with input rate up to several tens of hertz, delivering timed GABAergic inhibition onto Purkinje dendrites.

• THE TRANSITION TABLE.

◦ Arrival of a parallel-fibre volley - from Background Tick to Beam Brake - within milliseconds.

◦ Endocannabinoid or monoamine modulation of excitability, partly thrown from above - shifting the baseline of the Background Tick up or down - over seconds.

• FAULT REGIMES AND WATCHDOGS.

◦ No distinctive pathological mode is established for stellate cells; loss of their tonic inhibition disinhibits Purkinje cells, but that is a network consequence rather than a fault regime of the cell itself, and the honest statement is that no notable isolated fault regime is documented.


ENTRY 121. CEREBELLAR BASKET CELLS (keyed to neuron catalogue Chapter 121)

MODES AND MODE TRANSITIONS:

The basket cell is a mode-poor tonic inhibitor like its stellate cousin, but with one special trick: its pinceau ending around the Purkinje axon initial segment lets it exert an ultra-fast, partly non-synaptic (ephaptic) veto.

It is a fast feedforward brake, not a multi-mode integrator.

• THE MODE REGISTER.

◦ Spontaneous tonic firing - the Background Tick - an intrinsic baseline of a few to low tens of hertz giving standing inhibition at the Purkinje axon hillock.

◦ Parallel-fibre-driven veto - the Axon Clamp - the raised-rate, pinceau-mediated condition that can abruptly silence Purkinje output at the initial segment through combined synaptic and ephaptic action.

• PER-MODE TRANSFER FUNCTIONS.

◦ In the Background Tick, baseline self-firing is roughly 5 to 30 hertz; confidence moderate.

◦ In the Axon Clamp, a parallel-fibre volley maps to a rapid GABAergic-plus-ephaptic inhibition that can clamp Purkinje spike initiation within a millisecond, an unusually fast transfer.

• THE TRANSITION TABLE.

◦ Arrival of a parallel-fibre beam - from Background Tick to Axon Clamp - within about a millisecond, faster than a purely chemical synapse because of the ephaptic component.

◦ Neuromodulatory shift of baseline excitability, partly thrown from above - moving the Background Tick set-point - over seconds.

• FAULT REGIMES AND WATCHDOGS.

◦ No distinctive isolated fault regime is documented; runaway inhibition of Purkinje cells would be a circuit-level effect, and the honest statement is that basket cells have no well-characterised private pathological mode.


ENTRY 122. LARGE GLUTAMATERGIC PROJECTION NEURONS (Movement-Dispatching Output Neuron) (keyed to neuron catalogue Chapter 122)

MODES AND MODE TRANSITIONS:

The large glutamatergic projection neuron of the deep cerebellar nuclei is a mode-rich output cell: a fast intrinsic pacemaker that carries the cerebellum's message continuously and adds a distinctive rebound burst whenever Purkinje inhibition pauses.

It deserves the fuller treatment because it is the funnel through which the whole cortex speaks.

• THE MODE REGISTER.

◦ Tonic high-rate firing - the Steady Dispatch - the intrinsic pacemaker baseline that carries a continuous rate-coded movement command.

◦ Rebound burst - the Bounce-Back - a transient high-frequency burst triggered when strong Purkinje inhibition releases, deinactivating T-type (transient, low-threshold) calcium channels.

◦ Inhibition-shaped pause - the Held Command - the suppressed condition during a synchronous Purkinje volley, which is itself informative because it carves the output.

• PER-MODE TRANSFER FUNCTIONS.

◦ In the Steady Dispatch, with balanced input the cell self-fires at roughly 30 to 90 hertz, output tracking the difference between excitatory mossy or climbing collateral drive and Purkinje inhibition; confidence moderate to good.

◦ In the Bounce-Back, release from a hyperpolarising Purkinje barrage maps to a burst that can briefly exceed 100 hertz and lasts up to about 250 milliseconds; confidence moderate.

◦ In the Held Command, a synchronous Purkinje volley maps output towards zero for the volley's duration.

• THE TRANSITION TABLE.

◦ Offset of a synchronous Purkinje pause - from Held Command to Bounce-Back - within milliseconds, an intrinsic release phenomenon.

◦ Decay of the T-type calcium drive - from Bounce-Back back to Steady Dispatch - over tens to a couple of hundred milliseconds.

◦ Overall arousal and movement state, thrown from above via mossy and climbing collateral drive - scaling the Steady Dispatch baseline - over hundreds of milliseconds.

• FAULT REGIMES AND WATCHDOGS.

◦ Depolarisation block: sustained excessive excitation can drive the cell so high it inactivates its spike mechanism and falls silent; this is not a used mode, and its watchdog is the standing Purkinje inhibition that keeps the operating point below block.

◦ Loss of Purkinje input (cortical degeneration) removes the sculpting pauses and flattens the output into featureless tonic firing, a recognised failure signature in ataxia.


ENTRY 123. GABAERGIC NUCLEO-OLIVARY NEURONS (Climbing-Input Damping Neuron) (keyed to neuron catalogue Chapter 123)

MODES AND MODE TRANSITIONS:

The GABAergic nucleo-olivary neuron is a mode-poor tonic controller whose single job is to meter inhibition back onto the inferior olive, adjusting how strongly the olive oscillates and couples.

It is essentially a slow rate-coded valve, not a switch.

• THE MODE REGISTER.

◦ Tonic inhibitory firing - the Steady Damping - an ongoing low-rate discharge that sets a continuous brake on olivary excitability and electrical coupling.

◦ Climbing-feedback modulation - the Error Trim - a rate change following cerebellar activity that closes the olivo-cortico-nuclear loop, turning the damping up after error signals.

• PER-MODE TRANSFER FUNCTIONS.

◦ In the Steady Damping, baseline firing is on the order of a few to low tens of hertz, its gamma-aminobutyric acid (GABA) release lengthening the olivary subthreshold-oscillation period and reducing gap-junction coupling; confidence moderate.

◦ In the Error Trim, increased drive from the nuclei raises the rate and deepens olivary inhibition, shaping how many spikes the next olivary burst contains; confidence moderate.

• THE TRANSITION TABLE.

◦ A rise in cerebellar nuclear activity after a movement error - from Steady Damping to Error Trim - over tens to hundreds of milliseconds.

◦ Slow behavioural-state modulation, partly thrown from above - shifting the Steady Damping baseline - over seconds.

• FAULT REGIMES AND WATCHDOGS.

◦ Loss of this damping releases the olive into hypersynchronous oscillation, a plausible substrate for tremor; this is a network fault rather than a private mode, and the honest statement is that the cell itself has no distinct pathological regime of its own.


ENTRY 124. GLYCINERGIC NEURONS (Same-Side Restraint Output Neuron) (keyed to neuron catalogue Chapter 124)

MODES AND MODE TRANSITIONS:

The glycinergic projection neuron of the deep cerebellar nuclei is a mode-poor tonic inhibitor that delivers ipsilateral (same-side) restraint to brainstem targets, working as a rate-coded brake rather than a bursting switch.

• THE MODE REGISTER.

◦ Tonic inhibitory firing - the Steady Restraint - an ongoing discharge that holds down ipsilateral premotor targets in proportion to cerebellar drive.

◦ Rebound-shaped firing - the Bounce Restraint - a transient rate rise after release from Purkinje inhibition, by analogy with its glutamatergic nuclear neighbours; confidence lower for this glycinergic type.

• PER-MODE TRANSFER FUNCTIONS.

◦ In the Steady Restraint, balanced input yields a baseline of roughly 10 to 50 hertz of glycine release; confidence moderate.

◦ In the Bounce Restraint, release from a strong Purkinje pause produces a brief burst that transiently deepens inhibition; confidence low pending direct data.

• THE TRANSITION TABLE.

◦ Offset of a Purkinje pause - from Steady Restraint to Bounce Restraint - within milliseconds.

◦ Movement-state drive through mossy and climbing collaterals, thrown from above - scaling the Steady Restraint baseline - over hundreds of milliseconds.

• FAULT REGIMES AND WATCHDOGS.

◦ No distinctive isolated pathological mode is documented; the honest statement is that its fault behaviour is inferred from the nuclear family rather than measured directly.


ENTRY 125. SMALL LOCAL INTERNEURONS (Close-Quarters Inhibiting Neuron) (keyed to neuron catalogue Chapter 125)

MODES AND MODE TRANSITIONS:

The small local interneuron of the deep cerebellar nuclei is a mode-poor local inhibitor whose regime set is sparsely documented, so the minimal defensible register is a low tonic baseline with input-driven firing.

It provides close-range feedforward and feedback inhibition inside the nucleus.

• THE MODE REGISTER.

◦ Low tonic baseline - the Standing Hush - a slow resting discharge that keeps local nuclear excitability trimmed.

◦ Input-driven firing - the Local Brake - a raised-rate condition when nearby mossy, climbing collateral, or projection-neuron activity recruits it.

• PER-MODE TRANSFER FUNCTIONS.

◦ In the Standing Hush, sparse drive yields a few hertz of output; confidence low.

◦ In the Local Brake, local excitation maps to a firing rate of tens of hertz delivering GABAergic or glycinergic inhibition within a small nuclear neighbourhood; confidence low.

• THE TRANSITION TABLE.

◦ Arrival of local excitation - from Standing Hush to Local Brake - within milliseconds.

◦ Withdrawal of that drive - from Local Brake back to Standing Hush - over tens of milliseconds.

• FAULT REGIMES AND WATCHDOGS.

◦ The literature is thin for this type, so no distinct pathological regime is established and it should be recorded as a known unknown.


ENTRY 126. HUSH-LIFTING LEARNING NEURON (keyed to neuron catalogue Chapter 126)

MODES AND MODE TRANSITIONS:

This is a proposed or under-construction (PUC) cell type whose defining role is to lift tonic inhibition so that learning can proceed, so its register is framed conceptually with an explicit low-confidence flag.

It is best treated as a two-state disinhibitory gate.

• THE MODE REGISTER.

◦ Gate-closed baseline - the Held Hush - the resting condition in which downstream targets remain under standing inhibition and plasticity is gated off.

◦ Gate-open disinhibition - the Hush Lift - the active condition in which the cell removes that inhibition, opening a window for learning; confidence low because this is a projected type.

• PER-MODE TRANSFER FUNCTIONS.

◦ In the Held Hush, without the enabling signal the cell holds inhibition in place and downstream plasticity is suppressed; parameters are speculative.

◦ In the Hush Lift, an enabling teaching or neuromodulatory signal maps to a firing change that disinhibits the target and permits synaptic change during the open window; parameters speculative, low confidence.

• THE TRANSITION TABLE.

◦ Arrival of a teaching or neuromodulatory enable signal, likely thrown from above - from Held Hush to Hush Lift - over hundreds of milliseconds to seconds.

◦ Offset of that signal - from Hush Lift back to Held Hush - closing the learning window over seconds.

• FAULT REGIMES AND WATCHDOGS.

◦ Because this is a proposed type, its fault behaviour is undefined; the honest statement is that no measured pathological regime exists and any register here is provisional.


ENTRY 127. GENERAL SOMATIC EFFERENT MOTOR NEURONS (Eye-And-Tongue Aiming Neuron) (keyed to neuron catalogue Chapter 127)

MODES AND MODE TRANSITIONS:

The general somatic efferent cranial motor neuron is a two-mode output element with an unusually crisp version of the classic motor pattern: a tonic rate that holds a position and a phasic burst that changes it, most sharply seen in the oculomotor pulse-step.

It is a rate-and-burst command line for the eyes and tongue.

• THE MODE REGISTER.

◦ Tonic position-holding firing - the Steady Hold - the eye-position or tongue-posture signal, a maintained rate that opposes elastic restoring forces.

◦ Phasic saccadic burst - the Aim Pulse - the high-frequency burst that drives a rapid movement (the pulse of the pulse-step command).

◦ Recruitment-graded ramp - the Effort Climb - progressive activation across the motor pool as more force is demanded.

• PER-MODE TRANSFER FUNCTIONS.

◦ In the Steady Hold, firing rate is monotonically related to eye position, typically a few tens of hertz, holding gaze against orbital elasticity; confidence good.

◦ In the Aim Pulse, a saccadic command maps to a burst that can reach several hundred hertz for tens of milliseconds, its size and duration scaling with movement amplitude; confidence good.

◦ In the Effort Climb, increasing central drive recruits units in order of size and raises rate, following the size principle; confidence good.

• THE TRANSITION TABLE.

◦ A saccadic burst command from the brainstem burst generator - from Steady Hold to Aim Pulse - within milliseconds.

◦ End of the movement and integrator-set new position - from Aim Pulse back to a new Steady Hold - within tens of milliseconds.

◦ Overall drive and alertness, partly thrown from above - scaling the Effort Climb - over hundreds of milliseconds.

• FAULT REGIMES AND WATCHDOGS.

◦ Fatigue-driven rate failure at the neuromuscular junction (as in myasthenia gravis) breaks the Steady Hold and produces drooping and double vision; the watchdog is the safety factor of transmission, and this is a synaptic failure rather than a native firing mode.


ENTRY 128. SPECIAL VISCERAL EFFERENT, ALSO CALLED BRANCHIAL MOTOR, NEURONS (Face-And-Throat Moving Neuron) (keyed to neuron catalogue Chapter 128)

MODES AND MODE TRANSITIONS:

The special visceral efferent (branchial motor) neuron drives the muscles of facial expression, chewing, and swallowing, and it is a two-mode motor cell: a low tonic tone plus recruited phasic bursts, often organised into rhythmic patterns by brainstem generators.

• THE MODE REGISTER.

◦ Tonic postural tone - the Steady Set - a low maintained rate giving facial and pharyngeal muscle tone.

◦ Phasic recruited burst - the Action Drive - the burst that powers a chew, a blink, or a swallow.

◦ Rhythmic patterned firing - the Chew-Swallow Beat - participation in central-pattern-generator rhythms for mastication and swallowing.

• PER-MODE TRANSFER FUNCTIONS.

◦ In the Steady Set, baseline firing is a few to low tens of hertz sustaining tone; confidence moderate.

◦ In the Action Drive, a motor command maps to bursts rising to a hundred hertz or more, scaled by required force via orderly recruitment; confidence good.

◦ In the Chew-Swallow Beat, a brainstem generator imposes alternating burst-pause cycles at roughly 1 to 3 hertz for chewing; confidence moderate.

• THE TRANSITION TABLE.

◦ A voluntary or reflex motor command - from Steady Set to Action Drive - within milliseconds.

◦ Engagement of the masticatory or swallowing generator, thrown from above - from Steady Set to Chew-Swallow Beat - over hundreds of milliseconds.

◦ Return to rest - back to Steady Set - within tens to hundreds of milliseconds.

• FAULT REGIMES AND WATCHDOGS.

◦ Neuromuscular transmission failure or nerve injury silences the pool and produces facial palsy; this is a transmission fault, not a used mode, and the honest statement is that the cell has no distinctive private pathological firing regime.


ENTRY 129. GENERAL VISCERAL EFFERENT PREGANGLIONIC PARASYMPATHETIC NEURONS (Rest-And-Digest Outflow Neuron) (keyed to neuron catalogue Chapter 129)

MODES AND MODE TRANSITIONS:

The preganglionic parasympathetic neuron is a mode-poor autonomic output that runs at a low tonic rate and is nudged reflexively, so it behaves as a slow rate-coded valve for rest-and-digest functions.

• THE MODE REGISTER.

◦ Tonic parasympathetic tone - the Resting Outflow - a low maintained discharge that sets baseline heart-slowing, secretion, and gut activity.

◦ Reflex-driven surge - the Digest Push - a raised rate during feeding, baroreflex, or other visceral reflexes.

• PER-MODE TRANSFER FUNCTIONS.

◦ In the Resting Outflow, baseline firing is low, on the order of a fraction of a hertz to a few hertz, its acetylcholine release setting standing vagal tone; confidence moderate.

◦ In the Digest Push, reflex excitation raises the rate to drive glandular secretion or cardiac slowing, output graded by reflex strength; confidence moderate.

• THE TRANSITION TABLE.

◦ A visceral reflex or feeding cue - from Resting Outflow to Digest Push - over hundreds of milliseconds to seconds.

◦ Sleep-wake and arousal state, thrown from above by hypothalamic and brainstem autonomic centres - scaling the Resting Outflow baseline - over seconds to minutes.

• FAULT REGIMES AND WATCHDOGS.

◦ No distinctive pathological firing mode is established; autonomic failure appears as loss of tone rather than a new regime, so the honest statement is that no notable private fault mode exists.


ENTRY 130. SENSORY RELAY NEURONS OF THE TRIGEMINAL, SOLITARY, COCHLEAR, AND VESTIBULAR NUCLEI (First-Stop Sense-Sorting Neuron) (keyed to neuron catalogue Chapter 130)

MODES AND MODE TRANSITIONS:

This first-stop relay neuron is mostly a faithful relay with a light integrative overlay: it passes primary sensory input onward while its gain is adjusted by state, so its register is a tonic relay plus a modulated variant.

• THE MODE REGISTER.

◦ Faithful tonic relay - the Faithful Relay - the default in which each afferent volley is passed on with high fidelity and modest transformation.

◦ Gain-adjusted relay - the Turned Gain - a state in which descending or local modulation raises or lowers the transfer gain.

◦ Adapting response - the Fading Report - a rapidly adapting variant in some subpopulations that reports change rather than steady level.

• PER-MODE TRANSFER FUNCTIONS.

◦ In the Faithful Relay, output firing tracks afferent input roughly linearly, up to tens or low hundreds of hertz depending on modality; confidence good.

◦ In the Turned Gain, a modulatory signal multiplies the input-output slope without changing its shape; confidence moderate.

◦ In the Fading Report, a maintained input yields a decaying output that emphasises onsets; confidence moderate and subtype-dependent.

• THE TRANSITION TABLE.

◦ Descending or neuromodulatory input, partly thrown from above by attention and arousal - from Faithful Relay to Turned Gain - over hundreds of milliseconds.

◦ Sustained stimulation engaging adaptation currents - from Faithful Relay to Fading Report - over tens to hundreds of milliseconds, intrinsic.

• FAULT REGIMES AND WATCHDOGS.

◦ Deafferentation can release some relay cells into abnormal spontaneous firing (a plausible substrate for tinnitus or phantom sensation); this is a fault triggered by input loss, and the watchdog is intact afferent drive.


ENTRY 131. MESENCEPHALIC TRIGEMINAL NUCLEUS NEURONS (Brain-Resident Jaw-Stretch Neuron) (keyed to neuron catalogue Chapter 131)

MODES AND MODE TRANSITIONS:

The mesencephalic trigeminal neuron is a primary sensory cell that unusually lives inside the brain, and it is a mostly mode-poor stretch reporter that fires phasic bursts on jaw-muscle stretch, with intrinsic bursting machinery.

• THE MODE REGISTER.

◦ Stretch-driven phasic firing - the Stretch Report - the working mode in which muscle-spindle and periodontal input drives firing that encodes jaw stretch and bite force.

◦ Intrinsic burst firing - the Self Burst - a tendency to fire in bursts driven by persistent sodium and other intrinsic currents, sharpening the signal.

◦ Low tonic baseline - the Standing Tick - a modest resting discharge between stretches; confidence moderate.

• PER-MODE TRANSFER FUNCTIONS.

◦ In the Stretch Report, mechanical input maps to firing rate scaling with stretch velocity and amplitude, up to tens of hertz; confidence good.

◦ In the Self Burst, suprathreshold drive triggers short high-frequency bursts through a persistent sodium current; confidence moderate.

◦ In the Standing Tick, weak input yields a low baseline; confidence moderate.

• THE TRANSITION TABLE.

◦ Jaw-muscle stretch or tooth loading - from Standing Tick to Stretch Report - within milliseconds.

◦ Crossing the intrinsic burst threshold - from Stretch Report to Self Burst - within milliseconds, intrinsic.

• FAULT REGIMES AND WATCHDOGS.

◦ No distinctive disease-linked private mode is established for this unusual cell; the honest statement is that its fault behaviour is largely uncharacterised.


ENTRY 132. LOCUS COERULEUS NEURONS (Brain-Wide Alerting Neuron) (keyed to neuron catalogue Chapter 132)

MODES AND MODE TRANSITIONS:

The locus coeruleus noradrenergic neuron is one of the great mode-rich broadcasters: a slow intrinsic pacemaker whose tonic level sets global arousal and whose phasic bursts flag salient events, with near-silence in rapid-eye-movement sleep.

It earns the full treatment because its mode sets the mode of much of the rest of the brain.

• THE MODE REGISTER.

◦ Tonic baseline firing - the Background Alert - the slow regular pacemaker discharge that sets overall arousal and behavioural flexibility.

◦ Phasic burst firing - the Salience Flag - short high-rate bursts locked to salient or task-relevant events that punctuate the broadcast.

◦ Silent state - the Sleep Silence - the near-cessation of firing during rapid-eye-movement sleep and deep anaesthesia, thrown from above.

• PER-MODE TRANSFER FUNCTIONS.

◦ In the Background Alert, intrinsic pacing gives about 1 to 2 hertz at rest, rising to roughly 5 to 6 hertz in stress and hyperarousal, its noradrenaline release setting cortical gain; confidence good.

◦ In the Salience Flag, a salient stimulus maps to a brief burst reaching up to about 15 hertz for a few hundred milliseconds, superimposed on baseline; confidence good.

◦ In the Sleep Silence, the cell falls to near zero hertz, withdrawing noradrenergic tone; confidence good.

• THE TRANSITION TABLE.

◦ A salient or task-relevant stimulus - from Background Alert to Salience Flag - within tens of milliseconds, returning to baseline within a few hundred milliseconds.

◦ Rising stress drive through corticotropin-releasing factor, thrown from above by the stress axis - raising the Background Alert set-point - over seconds.

◦ Transition into rapid-eye-movement sleep, thrown from above by sleep-switch circuitry - from Background Alert to Sleep Silence - over seconds to minutes.

• FAULT REGIMES AND WATCHDOGS.

◦ Over-driven tonic firing (chronic stress) pushes the cell into a high-tonic regime that degrades attention and, sustained, contributes to anxiety states; the watchdog is autoinhibition through alpha-2 adrenergic autoreceptors, which caps runaway firing.

◦ Progressive degeneration of these pigmented cells (as in Parkinson disease and Alzheimer disease) collapses the whole register towards silence, a recognised early failure signature.


ENTRY 133. RAPHE NUCLEI NEURONS (Mood-Steadying Patience Neuron) (keyed to neuron catalogue Chapter 133)

MODES AND MODE TRANSITIONS:

The raphe serotonergic neuron is a metronome: a slow clock-like intrinsic pacemaker that steadies mood and patience, gated down by sleep and shaped modestly by reward and punishment.

Its defining feature is regularity, so its register is built around one dominant tonic mode.

• THE MODE REGISTER.

◦ Slow clock-like tonic firing - the Patient Metronome - the regular pacemaker discharge that provides steady serotonergic tone.

◦ Phasic reward-and-punishment modulation - the Outcome Nudge - modest rate changes on multiple timescales that report reward and punishment.

◦ Silent state - the Dream Silence - the near-cessation during rapid-eye-movement sleep, thrown from above.

• PER-MODE TRANSFER FUNCTIONS.

◦ In the Patient Metronome, intrinsic pacing gives about 1 to 2 hertz with a very regular interspike interval (coefficient of variation below 0.5), largely independent of moment-to-moment input; confidence good.

◦ In the Outcome Nudge, reward or punishment maps to slow rate shifts around baseline over seconds to minutes, and in some cells brief phasic responses; confidence moderate.

◦ In the Dream Silence, firing falls to near zero during rapid-eye-movement sleep; confidence good.

• THE TRANSITION TABLE.

◦ Reward or punishment signals - from Patient Metronome to Outcome Nudge - over hundreds of milliseconds to minutes.

◦ Wake-to-rapid-eye-movement-sleep transition, thrown from above - from Patient Metronome to Dream Silence - over seconds to minutes.

◦ Autoreceptor feedback through 5-HT1A receptors - trimming the Patient Metronome rate back to baseline - over seconds.

• FAULT REGIMES AND WATCHDOGS.

◦ Autoreceptor over-suppression can drive the cell into pathological under-firing linked to depressed mood, and the therapeutic delay of serotonin-reuptake inhibitors reflects the slow desensitisation of that 5-HT1A watchdog; this is a regulatory fault rather than a native mode.


ENTRY 134. PEDUNCULOPONTINE AND LATERODORSAL TEGMENTAL NUCLEUS NEURONS (Walk-Starting Arousal Neuron) (keyed to neuron catalogue Chapter 134)

MODES AND MODE TRANSITIONS:

The pedunculopontine and laterodorsal tegmental cholinergic neuron is a mode-rich state cell: it switches between single-spike tonic firing and rhythmic bursting across the sleep-wake cycle and helps launch locomotion, so it earns fuller treatment.

• THE MODE REGISTER.

◦ Tonic single-spike firing - the Waking Tick - the regular firing of wakefulness that supports cortical activation and arousal.

◦ Rhythmic burst firing - the Dream Burst - low-threshold-calcium-driven bursts most prominent in rapid-eye-movement sleep and drowsy transitions.

◦ Locomotor-drive firing - the Walk Start - increased firing that engages the mesencephalic locomotor region to initiate and sustain gait.

◦ Quiescent state - the Deep-Sleep Hush - reduced firing in slow-wave sleep.

• PER-MODE TRANSFER FUNCTIONS.

◦ In the Waking Tick, the cell fires single spikes at a few to low tens of hertz, releasing acetylcholine to thalamus and cortex; confidence moderate to good.

◦ In the Dream Burst, deinactivated T-type calcium channels produce rhythmic bursts during rapid-eye-movement sleep; confidence moderate.

◦ In the Walk Start, ramped firing scales locomotor drive; confidence moderate.

◦ In the Deep-Sleep Hush, firing falls low in slow-wave sleep; confidence moderate.

• THE TRANSITION TABLE.

◦ Membrane hyperpolarisation with sleep onset, thrown from above - from Waking Tick to Dream Burst or Deep-Sleep Hush - over seconds.

◦ Locomotor command - from Waking Tick to Walk Start - over hundreds of milliseconds.

◦ Arousal and wake onset, thrown from above - from bursting or hush back to Waking Tick - over seconds.

• FAULT REGIMES AND WATCHDOGS.

◦ Degeneration of these cholinergic cells (prominent in Parkinson disease) weakens the Walk Start mode and contributes to gait freezing and falls, a recognised clinical failure signature rather than a used regime.


ENTRY 135. VENTROLATERAL MEDULLARY ADRENERGIC NEURONS (Pressure-Holding Emergency Neuron) (keyed to neuron catalogue Chapter 135)

MODES AND MODE TRANSITIONS:

The rostral ventrolateral medullary adrenergic (C1) neuron is a mode-rich cardiovascular commander: a tonic pacemaker that sets baseline blood pressure and a surge mode that mounts emergency responses, so it merits the fuller treatment.

• THE MODE REGISTER.

◦ Tonic sympathetic drive - the Pressure Hold - the ongoing discharge that sets resting sympathetic vasomotor tone and arterial pressure.

◦ Baroreflex-modulated firing - the Baro Trim - beat-by-beat rate adjustment that buffers blood pressure against the baroreceptor signal.

◦ Emergency surge - the Alarm Surge - a large rate increase during hypotension, hypoxia, hypoglycaemia, or stress that raises pressure and mobilises defence.

• PER-MODE TRANSFER FUNCTIONS.

◦ In the Pressure Hold, tonic firing at a few hertz sets standing sympathetic outflow; confidence moderate.

◦ In the Baro Trim, rising arterial pressure inhibits firing and falling pressure disinhibits it, an inverse map operating within the cardiac cycle; confidence good.

◦ In the Alarm Surge, threat signals map to a several-fold rate increase, driving catecholamine release and vasoconstriction; confidence moderate.

• THE TRANSITION TABLE.

◦ A fall in arterial pressure sensed by baroreceptors - from Pressure Hold towards Alarm Surge via Baro Trim - within a heartbeat to seconds.

◦ Hypoxia, hypoglycaemia, or psychological stress, partly thrown from above - from Pressure Hold to Alarm Surge - over seconds.

◦ Restoration of pressure - from Alarm Surge back to Pressure Hold - over seconds to minutes.

• FAULT REGIMES AND WATCHDOGS.

◦ Chronically elevated tonic firing sustains neurogenic hypertension, a maladaptive lock into the surge range; the watchdog is the baroreflex, and its blunting is itself part of the pathology.


ENTRY 136. PERIAQUEDUCTAL GREY NEURONS (Freeze-Or-Flee Selecting Neuron) (keyed to neuron catalogue Chapter 136)

MODES AND MODE TRANSITIONS:

The periaqueductal grey neuron is a mode-selecting cell organised into columns that pick between defensive strategies, so it behaves as a state-selector with distinct output modes for freezing, fleeing, and analgesia.

• THE MODE REGISTER.

◦ Passive-coping output - the Freeze Command - the ventrolateral-column pattern that drives immobility and quiescence.

◦ Active-coping output - the Flee Command - the dorsolateral and lateral-column pattern that drives flight or fight with a sympathetic surge.

◦ Analgesic output - the Pain Gate - descending drive that suppresses pain transmission during threat.

◦ Low tonic baseline - the Watchful Idle - the resting discharge between threats.

• PER-MODE TRANSFER FUNCTIONS.

◦ In the Watchful Idle, baseline firing is low, a few hertz; confidence moderate.

◦ In the Freeze Command, threat input to the ventrolateral column maps to output that halts movement and slows the heart; confidence moderate.

◦ In the Flee Command, threat input to dorsolateral and lateral columns maps to output that raises pressure and drives escape; confidence moderate.

◦ In the Pain Gate, activation recruits descending serotonergic and noradrenergic pathways that inhibit dorsal-horn transmission; confidence good.

• THE TRANSITION TABLE.

◦ Threat appraisal from amygdala and prefrontal cortex, thrown from above - from Watchful Idle to Freeze or Flee Command - within hundreds of milliseconds.

◦ Escalation of threat (proximity) - from Freeze Command to Flee Command - over seconds.

◦ Sustained threat - engaging the Pain Gate alongside a defensive mode - over seconds.

• FAULT REGIMES AND WATCHDOGS.

◦ Mis-set threshold or over-weighted flight output is implicated in panic attacks, an inappropriate entry into the Flee Command without real threat; the watchdog is prefrontal appraisal, whose weakening biases the selector.


ENTRY 137. SPHERICAL BUSHY CELLS (keyed to neuron catalogue Chapter 137)

MODES AND MODE TRANSITIONS:

The spherical bushy cell of the cochlear nucleus is a near-pure relay whose whole design is to preserve the timing of low-frequency sound, so its register is dominated by one faithful phase-locking mode.

A register of essentially one mode is legitimate and worth stating proudly.

• THE MODE REGISTER.

◦ Primary-like phase-locked relay - the Faithful Relay - the working mode in which large endbulb-of-Held synapses drive spikes locked to the fine structure of the sound waveform, even sharpening the phase-locking of the auditory nerve.

◦ Gain-adjusted relay - the Turned Gain - a modest state in which inhibitory and efferent input trims the transfer, admitted with lower confidence.

• PER-MODE TRANSFER FUNCTIONS.

◦ In the Faithful Relay, each strong endbulb input maps to a well-timed output spike, following low-frequency tones with sub-millisecond precision and firing up to a few hundred hertz; confidence good.

◦ In the Turned Gain, inhibitory sidebands and descending input adjust threshold and rate without disrupting timing; confidence moderate.

• THE TRANSITION TABLE.

◦ A sound onset - engaging the Faithful Relay from silence - within a millisecond.

◦ Descending and inhibitory modulation, partly thrown from above by attention - shifting into Turned Gain - over tens to hundreds of milliseconds.

• FAULT REGIMES AND WATCHDOGS.

◦ Synaptic depression at very high sustained rates can degrade fidelity, but the large secure endbulb is built with a high safety factor as its own watchdog, so no distinctive private pathological mode is established.


ENTRY 138. GLOBULAR BUSHY CELLS (keyed to neuron catalogue Chapter 138)

MODES AND MODE TRANSITIONS:

The globular bushy cell is a timing relay one step more specialised than its spherical cousin: it converts converging auditory-nerve input into extremely precise spikes that feed the binaural timing pathway, so it is essentially a single faithful mode with high temporal precision.

• THE MODE REGISTER.

◦ Primary-like-with-notch phase-locked relay - the Precise Relay - the working mode in which many converging inputs produce a very sharply timed output that drives the medial nucleus of the trapezoid body.

◦ Gain-adjusted relay - the Turned Gain - modest inhibitory and efferent trimming; admitted with lower confidence.

• PER-MODE TRANSFER FUNCTIONS.

◦ In the Precise Relay, convergence of several auditory-nerve fibres maps to output spikes with enhanced timing precision and phase-locking, firing up to several hundred hertz with a characteristic post-onset notch; confidence good.

◦ In the Turned Gain, inhibition and efferent input adjust rate without spoiling timing; confidence moderate.

• THE TRANSITION TABLE.

◦ A sound onset - engaging the Precise Relay from silence - within a millisecond.

◦ Inhibitory and descending modulation, partly thrown from above - shifting into Turned Gain - over tens of milliseconds.

• FAULT REGIMES AND WATCHDOGS.

◦ Loss of timing precision here degrades sound localisation; the large secure calyceal downstream synapse and convergence redundancy act as watchdogs, and no distinct private pathological firing mode is documented.


ENTRY 139. STELLATE OR MULTIPOLAR CELLS, T-STELLATE TYPE (Narrow-Band Spectrum-Reporting Neuron) (keyed to neuron catalogue Chapter 139)

MODES AND MODE TRANSITIONS:

The T-stellate (chopper) cell reports the energy in a narrow frequency band, and its signature is an intrinsic chopping rhythm, so it is a mode-poor rhythmic encoder with a regular firing mode.

• THE MODE REGISTER.

◦ Chopper firing - the Steady Chop - the working mode in which intrinsic membrane properties impose a regular chopping rhythm largely independent of the stimulus fine structure, encoding sound-level in a narrow band.

◦ Sub-threshold or silent baseline - the Standing Quiet - the resting condition between sounds.

• PER-MODE TRANSFER FUNCTIONS.

◦ In the Steady Chop, a sustained tone maps to regular chopping at an intrinsic rate of roughly 100 to 500 hertz set by the membrane rather than the stimulus, with the rate coding sound level; confidence good.

◦ In the Standing Quiet, without input the cell is silent or fires slowly; confidence moderate.

• THE TRANSITION TABLE.

◦ A tone within the cell's band - from Standing Quiet to Steady Chop - within a couple of milliseconds.

◦ Offset of the tone - from Steady Chop back to Standing Quiet - within milliseconds.

• FAULT REGIMES AND WATCHDOGS.

◦ No distinctive isolated pathological firing mode is documented; the honest statement is that the cell has no well-characterised private fault regime.


ENTRY 140. STELLATE OR MULTIPOLAR CELLS, D-STELLATE TYPE (Broad-Band Sound-Damping Neuron) (keyed to neuron catalogue Chapter 140)

MODES AND MODE TRANSITIONS:

The D-stellate (onset-chopper) cell is a wideband inhibitory neuron that damps activity across many frequency channels, so it is a mode-poor onset-plus-sustained inhibitor.

• THE MODE REGISTER.

◦ Onset response - the Onset Stamp - a sharp burst at sound onset that marks the start of a sound across a wide band.

◦ Sustained wideband inhibition - the Broad Damp - continued firing during a sound that delivers cross-frequency inhibition to other cochlear-nucleus cells.

◦ Silent baseline - the Standing Quiet - the resting condition.

• PER-MODE TRANSFER FUNCTIONS.

◦ In the Onset Stamp, a wideband sound onset maps to a precise initial spike or short burst; confidence good.

◦ In the Broad Damp, a maintained sound maps to sustained firing at tens of hertz whose glycinergic output inhibits narrowband cells; confidence moderate.

◦ In the Standing Quiet, without input the cell is silent or slow; confidence moderate.

• THE TRANSITION TABLE.

◦ A wideband sound onset - from Standing Quiet to Onset Stamp then Broad Damp - within a couple of milliseconds.

◦ Offset of the sound - back to Standing Quiet - within milliseconds.

• FAULT REGIMES AND WATCHDOGS.

◦ No distinctive isolated pathological firing mode is documented; loss of its wideband inhibition would disinhibit downstream cells, but that is a circuit effect rather than a private mode.


ENTRY 141. OCTOPUS CELLS (keyed to neuron catalogue Chapter 141)

MODES AND MODE TRANSITIONS:

The octopus cell is a specialised coincidence detector with an almost single-purpose design, so it is a mode-poor onset detector: it fires one exquisitely timed spike to a broadband transient and little else.

A register of essentially one mode is legitimate and worth stating proudly.

• THE MODE REGISTER.

◦ Broadband onset detection - the Onset Stamp - the working mode in which coincident input across many frequencies drives a single, sub-millisecond-precise spike marking a sharp acoustic transient.

◦ Silent baseline - the Standing Quiet - the resting condition between transients, with a very low input resistance keeping the cell unresponsive to weak or slow input.

• PER-MODE TRANSFER FUNCTIONS.

◦ In the Onset Stamp, near-simultaneous auditory-nerve input maps to one precisely timed spike, and the cell can follow rapid click trains up to several hundred hertz one spike per event; confidence good.

◦ In the Standing Quiet, non-coincident or slow input fails to reach threshold because of the very fast, leaky membrane; confidence good.

• THE TRANSITION TABLE.

◦ A broadband transient (a click or the onset of a periodic sound) - from Standing Quiet to Onset Stamp - within a fraction of a millisecond.

◦ Return to rest - back to Standing Quiet - within about a millisecond, an intrinsic property.

• FAULT REGIMES AND WATCHDOGS.

◦ No distinctive isolated pathological mode is documented; the low input resistance is itself the watchdog against spurious firing, and the honest statement is that no private fault regime is established.


ENTRY 142. FUSIFORM CELLS, ALSO CALLED PYRAMIDAL CELLS, OF THE DORSAL COCHLEAR NUCLEUS (Sound-Elevation Reading Neuron) (keyed to neuron catalogue Chapter 142)

MODES AND MODE TRANSITIONS:

The fusiform (pyramidal) cell of the dorsal cochlear nucleus is a mode-rich integrator that reads spectral notches for sound elevation, and it shows classic buildup and pauser response patterns set by a transient potassium current, so it merits fuller treatment.

• THE MODE REGISTER.

◦ Tonic baseline firing - the Standing Tick - a spontaneous discharge that provides a working point around which sound modulates.

◦ Buildup response - the Slow Build - a gradually increasing firing pattern at sound onset, set by a transient (A-type) potassium current that delays the first spikes.

◦ Pauser response - the Held Pause - an initial spike followed by a silent gap and then resumed firing, the same current expressed differently.

◦ Spectral-notch reporting - the Notch Reader - the integrative use of excitation and inhibition to signal the frequency of a spectral notch that codes elevation.

• PER-MODE TRANSFER FUNCTIONS.

◦ In the Standing Tick, spontaneous firing runs at a few to tens of hertz; confidence moderate.

◦ In the Slow Build, a tone at onset maps to firing that ramps up over tens of milliseconds because of A-current inactivation; confidence good.

◦ In the Held Pause, a stronger or differently timed onset maps to a spike, a pause of tens of milliseconds, then resumption; confidence good.

◦ In the Notch Reader, combined excitation and glycinergic inhibition maps a spectral notch to a firing minimum that encodes sound elevation; confidence moderate.

• THE TRANSITION TABLE.

◦ Sound onset with the A-current deinactivated by prior hyperpolarisation - from Standing Tick to Slow Build or Held Pause - within milliseconds, the choice set by recent membrane history.

◦ Descending and multisensory input, partly thrown from above - shifting the Standing Tick set-point and the notch computation - over tens to hundreds of milliseconds.

• FAULT REGIMES AND WATCHDOGS.

◦ After hearing damage these cells can develop pathological hyperactivity and altered bursting linked to tinnitus, a genuine fault regime whose watchdog is intact inhibitory (glycinergic) input; when that inhibition weakens the cell drifts into the hyperactive regime.


ENTRY 143. GIANT CELLS (Deep-Layer Hearing-Output Neuron) (keyed to neuron catalogue Chapter 143)

MODES AND MODE TRANSITIONS:

The giant cell of the deep dorsal cochlear nucleus is an integrative output neuron that pools wide input and projects onward, and its documented register is thin, so the minimal defensible set is a tonic baseline plus a stimulus-driven integrative mode.

• THE MODE REGISTER.

◦ Tonic baseline firing - the Standing Tick - a spontaneous discharge giving a working point; confidence moderate.

◦ Integrative stimulus-driven firing - the Wide Gather - a raised-rate condition in which converging excitation and inhibition are pooled into an output sent to the inferior colliculus; confidence moderate.

• PER-MODE TRANSFER FUNCTIONS.

◦ In the Standing Tick, spontaneous firing runs at a few to tens of hertz; confidence low to moderate.

◦ In the Wide Gather, broad synaptic input maps to a graded output rate reflecting the balance of excitation and inhibition; confidence low to moderate.

• THE TRANSITION TABLE.

◦ Arrival of acoustic input - from Standing Tick to Wide Gather - within milliseconds.

◦ Descending modulation, partly thrown from above - shifting the baseline - over tens to hundreds of milliseconds.

• FAULT REGIMES AND WATCHDOGS.

◦ By analogy with neighbouring fusiform cells these deep-layer output cells may participate in post-damage hyperactivity, but direct evidence is limited, so this is flagged low-confidence rather than asserted as a private mode.


ENTRY 144. CARTWHEEL CELLS (keyed to neuron catalogue Chapter 144)

MODES AND MODE TRANSITIONS:

The cartwheel cell is a cerebellum-like inhibitory interneuron of the dorsal cochlear nucleus whose signature is complex-spike bursting, so it is a two-mode cell that alternates between simple and complex spiking much like a Purkinje cell.

• THE MODE REGISTER.

◦ Simple-spike tonic firing - the Simple Tick - a baseline of single sodium spikes providing ongoing glycinergic inhibition to fusiform cells.

◦ Complex-spike bursting - the Cartwheel Burst - the distinctive calcium-driven bursts of several spikelets that give the cell its name and deliver strong phasic inhibition.

• PER-MODE TRANSFER FUNCTIONS.

◦ In the Simple Tick, parallel-fibre input maps to single spikes at a few to tens of hertz; confidence good.

◦ In the Cartwheel Burst, stronger parallel-fibre drive triggers calcium-dependent complex spikes of two to several spikelets, delivering a large brief inhibition; confidence good.

• THE TRANSITION TABLE.

◦ A strong parallel-fibre volley crossing the calcium-spike threshold - from Simple Tick to Cartwheel Burst - within milliseconds, intrinsic.

◦ Decay of the calcium drive and refractory period - from Cartwheel Burst back to Simple Tick - within tens of milliseconds.

• FAULT REGIMES AND WATCHDOGS.

◦ No distinctive isolated disease-linked mode is established; loss of cartwheel inhibition would disinhibit fusiform cells and may contribute to tinnitus, but that is a circuit consequence, and the honest statement is that the cell has no well-defined private fault regime.


ENTRY 145. SUPERIOR OLIVARY COMPLEX NEURONS (Two-Ear Comparison Neuron) (keyed to neuron catalogue Chapter 145)

MODES AND MODE TRANSITIONS:

The superior olivary complex neuron is a binaural coincidence-and-comparison detector, so it is a mode-poor computational relay: it continuously compares the two ears rather than switching between behavioural states.

• THE MODE REGISTER.

◦ Coincidence-detection firing - the Two-Ear Match - the medial-superior-olive mode in which output peaks when excitatory inputs from the two ears arrive simultaneously, encoding interaural time difference.

◦ Excitation-inhibition balance firing - the Loudness Balance - the lateral-superior-olive mode in which ipsilateral excitation is weighed against contralateral inhibition to encode interaural level difference.

◦ Silent or low baseline - the Standing Quiet - the resting condition without sound.

• PER-MODE TRANSFER FUNCTIONS.

◦ In the Two-Ear Match, sub-millisecond coincidence of binaural input maps to a firing peak tuned to a best interaural delay; confidence good.

◦ In the Loudness Balance, the difference between excitatory and inhibitory drive maps to a firing rate that codes which ear is louder; confidence good.

◦ In the Standing Quiet, without sound the cell fires little; confidence moderate.

• THE TRANSITION TABLE.

◦ Onset of a binaural sound - from Standing Quiet into Two-Ear Match or Loudness Balance depending on subtype - within a millisecond.

◦ Olivocochlear and descending modulation, partly thrown from above - trimming the operating point - over tens of milliseconds.

• FAULT REGIMES AND WATCHDOGS.

◦ No distinctive isolated pathological firing mode is documented; degraded timing or inhibition impairs localisation, but that is a performance loss rather than a new regime.


ENTRY 146. MEDIAL OLIVOCOCHLEAR EFFERENT NEURONS (Cochlear-Gain Turning-Down Neuron) (keyed to neuron catalogue Chapter 146)

MODES AND MODE TRANSITIONS:

The medial olivocochlear efferent neuron is a slow feedback controller that turns down cochlear gain by acting on outer hair cells, so it is a two-mode cell: a low tonic set-point and a reflex-driven suppression mode.

• THE MODE REGISTER.

◦ Low tonic efferent tone - the Standing Turn - a baseline discharge that sets resting cochlear amplifier gain.

◦ Reflex-driven suppression - the Gain Cut - the raised-rate condition of the medial olivocochlear reflex that reduces outer-hair-cell gain in noise, improving signal detection and protecting the cochlea.

• PER-MODE TRANSFER FUNCTIONS.

◦ In the Standing Turn, tonic firing is low, a few hertz, its acetylcholine release setting baseline gain; confidence moderate.

◦ In the Gain Cut, sound (especially in the other ear) drives firing up to tens of hertz, and the effect on cochlear gain builds and decays over roughly 100 milliseconds because it is mediated by a slow cholinergic and calcium-activated potassium mechanism in the hair cell; confidence good.

• THE TRANSITION TABLE.

◦ Onset of sound or background noise triggering the reflex - from Standing Turn to Gain Cut - building over tens to about a hundred milliseconds.

◦ Attention and arousal, partly thrown from above - biasing the Standing Turn set-point - over seconds.

◦ Offset of sound - from Gain Cut back to Standing Turn - over about a hundred milliseconds.

• FAULT REGIMES AND WATCHDOGS.

◦ Weak efferent tone removes a protective gain control and is associated with greater vulnerability to noise-induced hearing damage; this is loss of a watchdog function rather than a distinct firing regime.


ENTRY 147. LATERAL OLIVOCOCHLEAR EFFERENT NEURONS (Inner-Ear Nerve Steadying Neuron) (keyed to neuron catalogue Chapter 147)

MODES AND MODE TRANSITIONS:

The lateral olivocochlear efferent neuron steadies and balances auditory-nerve activity by acting on the dendrites beneath inner hair cells, and its dynamics are slow and modulatory, so it is a mode-poor tonic balancer.

• THE MODE REGISTER.

◦ Tonic modulatory tone - the Standing Steady - a slow ongoing output that sets and balances auditory-nerve excitability between the two ears.

◦ Slow adjustment - the Balance Trim - graded shifts in that tone that rebalance the ears and protect against excitotoxicity over long timescales.

• PER-MODE TRANSFER FUNCTIONS.

◦ In the Standing Steady, low tonic firing releases a cocktail of transmitters and neuromodulators (including acetylcholine, dopamine, and peptides) that set auditory-nerve gain; confidence moderate.

◦ In the Balance Trim, sustained sound or interaural imbalance maps to slow up or down adjustments of that tone over seconds to minutes; confidence low to moderate because the system is slow and hard to record.

• THE TRANSITION TABLE.

◦ Prolonged sound or an interaural imbalance - from Standing Steady to Balance Trim - over seconds to minutes.

◦ Arousal and descending state, partly thrown from above - biasing the tone - over seconds.

• FAULT REGIMES AND WATCHDOGS.

◦ Loss of this steadying tone may allow excitotoxic overactivity of auditory-nerve dendrites, so its normal role is itself a protective watchdog; no distinct pathological firing mode of the cell is documented, and this should be recorded as a known unknown.


ENTRY 148. INFERIOR OLIVE NEURONS (Movement-Error Teaching Neuron) (keyed to neuron catalogue Chapter 148)

MODES AND MODE TRANSITIONS:

This is a mode-poor oscillator with a switchable coupling state, so its register is small but its transitions are unusually interesting, because the cerebellum reconfigures the whole nucleus moment by moment.

• THE MODE REGISTER.

◦ Subthreshold Oscillatory Coincidence-Detection - the Six-Hertz Metronome - a continuous membrane wobble of a few millivolts at one to ten hertz, most often four to eight hertz, during which the cell almost never spikes and simply keeps time.

◦ Synchronous Teaching Broadcast - the Clustered Complex-Spike - a cluster of gap-junction-coupled cells fires a climbing-fibre spike together when an error arrives at the depolarised phase, teaching a band of Purkinje cells at the same instant.

◦ Decoupled Solitary Mode - the Cut-Adrift Cell - inhibition shunts the gap junctions so a cell oscillates and fires on its own, letting the nucleus behave as many independent units.

• PER-MODE TRANSFER FUNCTIONS.

◦ In the metronome mode output is near one spike per second; an excitatory input either fires the cell or does nothing depending on oscillation phase, so the transfer is a phase-gated threshold rather than a rate map.

◦ In the synchronous broadcast mode one olivary spike drives five to ten Purkinje cells through roughly three hundred contacts each, with a compound response that never fails, briefly raising mean rate toward ten hertz.

◦ In the decoupled mode the same anatomy produces uncorrelated single spikes, so the teaching signal is delivered to scattered microzones rather than a synchronous band.

• THE TRANSITION TABLE.

◦ Metronome to synchronous - trigger is a phase-aligned error volley from spinal cord, trigeminal or mesodiencephalic sources - timescale one oscillation cycle, about one hundred and sixty milliseconds.

◦ Synchronous to decoupled - trigger is nucleo-olivary gamma-aminobutyric-acid (gamma-aminobutyric acid (GABA)) inhibition placed inside the glomerulus, which drains the coupling current - timescale tens of milliseconds; this is thrown from above by the cerebellar nuclei, not chosen by the cell.

◦ Coupling strength is also set slowly by serotonin and noradrenaline from raphe and locus coeruleus, shifting the whole population between ganged and solitary over seconds.

• FAULT REGIMES AND WATCHDOGS.

◦ Runaway hypersynchrony, as produced experimentally by harmaline, drives a violent whole-body tremor; the biological watchdog is the standing nucleo-olivary inhibition that keeps clusters small.

◦ Loss of the inhibitory input after a central tegmental tract lesion drives hypertrophic olivary degeneration, in which cells swell rather than die and generate palatal tremor at two to three beats per second; the warning signature is a slow rhythmic oscillation appearing weeks after the injury.


ENTRY 149. PONTINE NUCLEI NEURONS (Plan-Forwarding Cerebellum-Feeding Neuron) (keyed to neuron catalogue Chapter 149)

MODES AND MODE TRANSITIONS:

This is a mode-poor relay, and that plainness is deliberate, because the pons is built to supply a fixed reliable channel and leave the learning to the cerebellum.

• THE MODE REGISTER.

◦ Quiet Standby - the Idle Wire - a low spontaneous rate of a few spikes per second when the overlying cortex is not commanding.

◦ Faithful Forwarding - the Efference-Copy Relay - regular trains up to fifty to one hundred spikes per second that copy layer-five cortical drive across the midline as mossy fibre output.

• PER-MODE TRANSFER FUNCTIONS.

◦ In standby the cell rests near threshold with little bursting, little adaptation and no pacemaking, so output is essentially noise.

◦ In forwarding mode output is a near-linear function of weighted cortical drive above a threshold near twenty percent of maximum, with an input-output gain of about two spikes per second per unit drive, delivered to fifteen to twenty granule cells per rosette.

• THE TRANSITION TABLE.

◦ Standby to forwarding - trigger is convergent layer-five corticopontine excitation crossing the drive threshold - timescale one membrane time constant, about fifteen milliseconds.

◦ Gain is set by neuromodulatory acetylcholine and serotonin acting as a multiplier from about zero point eight to one point two; these are broadcast state terms rather than moment-to-moment commands.

◦ There is no intrinsic self-generated transition, since the cell has no pacemaker mode to fall into.

• FAULT REGIMES AND WATCHDOGS.

◦ Central pontine myelinolysis after rapid correction of low blood sodium silences the channel and can produce locked-in syndrome; the vulnerability is glial rather than neuronal, so the watchdog is careful sodium correction.

◦ Pontocerebellar hypoplasia and multiple system atrophy both remove the population outright; there is no self-limiting boundary the cell can defend, which is why loss is simply loss of the cerebellar plan copy.


ENTRY 150. RETICULAR FORMATION NEURONS (Whole-Body Readiness Neuron) (keyed to neuron catalogue Chapter 150)

MODES AND MODE TRANSITIONS:

This is a mode-rich, state-driven broadcaster whose firing pattern is not fixed but set from above by the sleep-wake and arousal systems, and that variety is the whole point of the tissue.

• THE MODE REGISTER.

◦ Tonic State-Setting - the Background Tick - steady firing at five to twenty spikes per second that changes slowly with behavioural state and sets the level of wakefulness, posture and tone.

◦ Rebound Burst - the Sudden Jump - a low-threshold calcium and hyperpolarisation-activated (Ih) burst thrown when the cell is released from inhibition, useful for a sudden coordinated movement.

◦ Startle Volley - the Door-Slam Reflex - the giant pontine cells sit almost silent then fire one high-frequency burst at three-to-eight-millisecond latency to drive the whole-body startle.

◦ Dream-Atonia Drive - the Night Watchman - glycinergic and GABAergic ventromedial medullary cells fire in rapid-eye-movement sleep to paralyse the body.

• PER-MODE TRANSFER FUNCTIONS.

◦ Tonic mode multiplies converging inputs by a state gain of about one point six in waking, zero point eight in non-rapid-eye-movement sleep and zero point four in rapid-eye-movement sleep, above a threshold near twelve spikes per second, and broadcasts the same spike to six to sixty targets.

◦ Rebound and startle modes convert silence-then-release into an all-or-none burst rather than a graded rate.

◦ Atonia drive delivers sustained inhibition to spinal motor circuits regardless of ongoing movement commands.

• THE TRANSITION TABLE.

◦ Tonic to startle - trigger is a loud abrupt stimulus - timescale three to eight milliseconds; habituates over tens of trials and is potentiated by amygdala fear input.

◦ Between tonic gain levels - trigger is the sleep-wake and arousal broadcast carried by orexin, noradrenaline, serotonin and acetylcholine - timescale seconds to minutes, thrown from Layer 10/11 state services.

◦ Into rebound burst - trigger is release from a preceding inhibition - timescale one membrane time constant of ten to thirty milliseconds.

• FAULT REGIMES AND WATCHDOGS.

◦ Bilateral paramedian midbrain and upper pontine damage collapses the ascending broadcast into coma; there is no local watchdog, since the system is its own arousal watchdog.

◦ Loss of the atonia-drive cells removes dream paralysis and produces rapid-eye-movement sleep behaviour disorder; the warning sign is dream enactment years before Parkinson disease.


ENTRY 151. PRE-BOTZINGER COMPLEX NEURONS (Breath-Rhythm Generating Neuron) (keyed to neuron catalogue Chapter 151)

MODES AND MODE TRANSITIONS:

This is a mode-rich network oscillator in which the rhythm is a group property, so the register mixes single-cell regimes with the emergent burstlet-to-burst decision that separates breathing rate from breathing depth.

• THE MODE REGISTER.

◦ Intrinsic Pacemaker Bursting - the Lone Metronome - a minority of cells burst rhythmically even with all synaptic input blocked, driven by the persistent sodium current.

◦ Group-Pacemaker Recruitment - the Chorus Breath - most cells are silent alone but are swept into the population burst by recurrent excitation, so the breath is built collectively.

◦ Burstlet Priming - the Half-Breath - a small low-amplitude pre-inspiratory ripple that sets rate; only some burstlets grow into full bursts, which is how amplitude and rate are controlled separately.

◦ Silent Expiratory Off-Phase - the Held Pause - active silence enforced by Botzinger and local glycinergic inhibition between breaths.

• PER-MODE TRANSFER FUNCTIONS.

◦ At rest the population bursts twelve to eighteen times a minute, zero point two to zero point three hertz, each burst about half a second to a second long.

◦ Pacemaker cells depolarise on a slowly de-inactivating sodium leak to a burst threshold near minus forty millivolts, with the calcium-activated non-selective cation current setting burst size not timing.

◦ Burstlet mode delivers a subthreshold ripple that either percolates into a full inspiratory command to the phrenic pool at C3 to C5 or decays.

• THE TRANSITION TABLE.

◦ Off-phase to burstlet to burst - trigger is recovery of sodium-current availability plus tonic drive from the retrotrapezoid nucleus and raphe - timescale seconds, which sets the breath period.

◦ Rate and depth are pushed up by substance P on the neurokinin-1 receptor and by rising carbon dioxide, and down by somatostatin, adenosine and opioids; state changes across sleep and waking are thrown from above.

◦ Astrocytic adenosine-triphosphate release adds a chemosensory boost that advances the next breath.

• FAULT REGIMES AND WATCHDOGS.

◦ Opioid drugs on the mu opioid receptor slow then stop the rhythm, the direct cause of death in overdose; there is no biological watchdog strong enough to override it, which is the danger.

◦ Severe hypoxia drives a biphasic collapse into gasping; loss of neurokinin-1 cells in multiple system atrophy produces central sleep apnoea, with the warning signature of inspiratory stridor and apnoea in sleep.


ENTRY 152. RETROTRAPEZOID NUCLEUS NEURONS (Acid-Sensing Air-Hunger Neuron) (keyed to neuron catalogue Chapter 152)

MODES AND MODE TRANSITIONS:

This is a two-mode chemosensory drive cell, essentially a graded acid meter that switches from near-silence to insistent output as blood carbon dioxide climbs.

• THE MODE REGISTER.

◦ Quiet Watch - the Normal-Air Silence - near-silence when blood carbon dioxide and therefore proton concentration are normal.

◦ Acid Alarm - the Air-Hunger Drive - accelerating firing as acidity rises, pushing the pre-Botzinger complex to breathe harder and faster.

• PER-MODE TRANSFER FUNCTIONS.

◦ In quiet watch the cell fires slowly and contributes little tonic drive, leaving room-air and low-oxygen breathing largely to other systems.

◦ In acid alarm the firing rate rises monotonically with proton concentration read by the potassium channel Task2 and the proton receptor Gpr4, and that rate is added as excitatory drive onto the inspiratory kernel.

• THE TRANSITION TABLE.

◦ Quiet to alarm - trigger is a fall in the pH of the surrounding fluid as carbon dioxide dissolves - timescale seconds, matching the build of the urge to breathe during a breath-hold.

◦ Astrocyte-released signals and local blood flow modulate the set point, and arousal state adjusts overall responsiveness from above.

◦ The return to quiet watch follows restoration of normal carbon dioxide over seconds.

• FAULT REGIMES AND WATCHDOGS.

◦ Loss of the master gene sharply blunts the high-carbon-dioxide response while sparing normal-air breathing; in humans mutations in that gene cause inadequate breathing in sleep, congenital central hypoventilation.

◦ The pathological boundary is chronic under-response leaving carbon dioxide to climb unchecked in sleep; the watchdog is the redundancy of other chemosensors, which is only partial.


ENTRY 153. RED NUCLEUS NEURONS (Limb-Path Correcting Neuron) (keyed to neuron catalogue Chapter 153)

MODES AND MODE TRANSITIONS:

This is a two-mode relay-and-copy cell whose most distinctive regime answers the end of inhibition rather than the arrival of excitation, so silence itself is one of its inputs.

• THE MODE REGISTER.

◦ Tonic Relay - the Steady Junction - resting discharge of five to fifty spikes per second that passes cerebellar movement commands onward and copies them to the inferior olive.

◦ Movement Onset Burst - the Reach Kick - a burst up to about two hundred spikes per second at the start of a movement.

◦ Rebound Burst - the Answer-to-Silence - a hyperpolarisation-activated (Ih) rebound that fires the cell when a preceding inhibition is released.

• PER-MODE TRANSFER FUNCTIONS.

◦ Tonic mode weights cerebellar interposed input heavily on the soma and cortical input weakly on distal dendrites, summing to a modest rate.

◦ Onset burst mode delivers a brief high-rate command to the contralateral upper cervical cord and an efference copy to the ipsilateral principal olive.

◦ Rebound mode converts a released inhibition into a spike through slow Ih activation over one hundred to two hundred milliseconds.

• THE TRANSITION TABLE.

◦ Tonic to onset burst - trigger is a phasic cerebellar command - timescale a few milliseconds.

◦ Tonic to rebound - trigger is the offset of local GABAergic inhibition - timescale one hundred to two hundred milliseconds set by Ih.

◦ Overall gain is nudged by acetylcholine from the pedunculopontine nucleus, an arousal-linked term set from above.

• FAULT REGIMES AND WATCHDOGS.

◦ Damage to the nucleus or the cerebellar fibres passing through it produces Holmes tremor at two to five cycles per second, present at rest and worst on reaching, appearing weeks to months after injury.

◦ There is no notable self-generated pathological regime; the fault here is imposed by loss of the return path rather than by the cell entering a bad state of its own.


ENTRY 154. SUPERIOR COLLICULUS NEURONS (Gaze-Shifting Orienting Neuron) (keyed to neuron catalogue Chapter 154)

MODES AND MODE TRANSITIONS:

This is a mode-rich map whose cells hold several complementary regimes, and its defining transition is the release of a basal-ganglia brake that is a Layer service rather than a local decision.

• THE MODE REGISTER.

◦ Visual Reporting - the What-Is-There Flash - superficial cells answer a flash after forty to seventy milliseconds with a brief burst.

◦ Buildup Ramp - the Rising Intention - intermediate and deep cells climb their firing from target onset until the eye moves.

◦ Saccade Burst - the Look-Now Command - deep cells fire the highest rates in the midbrain, near seven hundred and fifty spikes per second for small movements, then fall silent.

◦ Fixation Hold - the Steady Gaze - front-tip cells hold fifty to one hundred spikes per second while the eyes are still and pause during a movement.

• PER-MODE TRANSFER FUNCTIONS.

◦ Visual mode is a retinotopic burst proportional to stimulus salience.

◦ Buildup mode integrates evidence over tens to hundreds of milliseconds toward a burst threshold.

◦ Saccade mode encodes movement size by which cells fire and for how long, spreading the burst rather than firing harder.

◦ Fixation mode is a tonic hold that actively suppresses the burst layers.

• THE TRANSITION TABLE.

◦ Buildup to saccade burst - trigger is release of the tonic nigral gamma-aminobutyric acid (GABA) brake, which falls from forty to one hundred spikes per second toward zero sixty to one hundred milliseconds before the movement - timescale of that brake release; this is thrown from above by the basal ganglia.

◦ Fixation to saccade - trigger is the winning map site crossing threshold in the winner-take-all network - timescale tens of milliseconds.

◦ Cholinergic input in the intermediate layers turns steady drive into an explosive burst.

• FAULT REGIMES AND WATCHDOGS.

◦ The map physically cannot represent two saccade targets at once, so a genuinely ambiguous scene forces an averaging or a switch rather than a double command; long-range inhibition is the watchdog enforcing this.

◦ In progressive supranuclear palsy the vertical saccade machinery degenerates and up-and-down movements slow and fail while passive eye movement is preserved.


ENTRY 155. INFERIOR COLLICULUS NEURONS (Sound-Gathering Relay Neuron) (keyed to neuron catalogue Chapter 155)

MODES AND MODE TRANSITIONS:

This is a mode-poor obligatory relay with modest tuning states, honest about passing the result of hearing upward rather than terminating the computation.

• THE MODE REGISTER.

◦ Tonic Frequency Relay - the Faithful Tollgate - disc-shaped cells report one frequency sheet steadily, passing combined brainstem streams to the thalamus.

◦ Cross-Frequency Integration - the Chord Reader - stellate cells spread across sheets to combine pitches into a voice or a chord.

◦ Feature-Tuned Response - the Duration-And-Rhythm Filter - cells tuned to a particular sound duration or loudness-modulation rate fire selectively.

• PER-MODE TRANSFER FUNCTIONS.

◦ Tonic relay mode maps input sound level and frequency to firing rate within a single fibrodendritic lamina.

◦ Integration mode sums across laminae so output reflects spectral shape rather than a single frequency.

◦ Feature-tuned mode fires only when the temporal feature matches, sharpened by within-sheet GABAergic inhibition.

• THE TRANSITION TABLE.

◦ Between tonic and feature-tuned responses - trigger is the temporal structure of the sound itself - timescale milliseconds to tens of milliseconds.

◦ Overall gain and tuning sharpness are set by inhibition and by descending corticofugal and monoaminergic modulation reflecting attention and arousal from above.

• FAULT REGIMES AND WATCHDOGS.

◦ Because almost every ascending pathway is forced through this nucleus, its loss removes hearing at a single point; there is no self-generated pathological firing mode of note.

◦ The honest statement is that no dramatic fault regime is documented for the individual cell beyond loss and tinnitus-associated hyperexcitability, in which reduced inhibition lets tuning broaden.


ENTRY 156. VESTIBULAR NUCLEI NEURONS (Head-Motion Balance Neuron) (keyed to neuron catalogue Chapter 156)

MODES AND MODE TRANSITIONS:

This is a mode-rich pacemaker that computes with a bidirectional rate code around a standing baseline, so for this cell stillness is a number rather than silence.

• THE MODE REGISTER.

◦ Standing Pacemaker Baseline - the Resting Thirty - endogenous firing near thirty spikes per second with no input, the zero point of the whole system.

◦ Bidirectional Velocity Coding - the Turn Meter - firing rises with head rotation one way and falls the other, at about zero point four to one extra spike per second per degree per second.

◦ Self-Motion Cancellation - the Own-Move Subtractor - vestibular-only cells subtract an efference copy of a self-generated head turn and report only what the world did.

• PER-MODE TRANSFER FUNCTIONS.

◦ Baseline mode is maintained by a persistent sodium current and Ih at five to thirty-five spikes per second in a slice, faster in life.

◦ Velocity coding rides on the baseline, saturating at a floor of zero on the silenced side and a ceiling near three hundred spikes per second on the excited side, which is why fast turns rely on the excited side.

◦ Cancellation mode gates output multiplicatively by the predicted head velocity so intended movements produce little response.

• THE TRANSITION TABLE.

◦ Baseline to velocity coding - trigger is a real head rotation sensed by the vestibular nerve - timescale of the reflex, five to seven milliseconds.

◦ Push-pull rebalancing across the two sides is enforced by commissural inhibition, so the pair acts as one differential amplifier.

◦ Histamine and acetylcholine set overall excitability from above, which is why antihistamines and scopolamine treat vertigo.

• FAULT REGIMES AND WATCHDOGS.

◦ Sudden one-sided loss unbalances the push-pull pair and produces spinning vertigo, one-directional nystagmus and vomiting; the watchdog is vestibular compensation, which restores resting rate over two to three days with astrocyte help.

◦ Thiamine deficiency drives an early failure signalled by nystagmus, the Wernicke sign, because the never-resting cell exhausts its energy budget first.


ENTRY 157. BODY-ALARM BROADCAST NEURON (keyed to neuron catalogue Chapter 157)

MODES AND MODE TRANSITIONS:

This is a two-mode thresholded broadcaster, essentially a switch rather than a dial, because it releases its slow peptide only above a firing threshold.

• THE MODE REGISTER.

◦ Near-Silence Watch - the Quiet Sentinel - resting discharge of zero point five to three spikes per second that stays below the peptide-release threshold and so is invisible downstream.

◦ Alarm Burst - the Exclamation Mark - long bursts of ten to forty spikes per second lasting seconds to tens of seconds that flood the amygdala with calcitonin-gene-related-peptide (CGRP).

• PER-MODE TRANSFER FUNCTIONS.

◦ In watch mode fast glutamate may pass but no peptide is released, so the alarm is not raised.

◦ In alarm mode the cell encodes threat severity in burst length and height rather than in which cell fires, and releases CGRP from dense-core vesicles only above roughly five to ten spikes per second, raising cyclic adenosine monophosphate in the target for minutes.

• THE TRANSITION TABLE.

◦ Watch to alarm - trigger is convergent bad news from spinal lamina-one pain, solitary-nucleus gut and taste, and carbon-dioxide channels crossing the release threshold - timescale of evidence accumulation, about four hundred milliseconds.

◦ Descending insular and central-amygdala context can raise or lower the threshold, and mu-opioid tone suppresses the burst by up to ninety percent, which is why morphine takes the horror out of pain.

◦ Return to watch follows peptide clearance over about thirty seconds.

• FAULT REGIMES AND WATCHDOGS.

◦ Chronic over-activity is implicated in migraine, in the misery of chronic pain and in post-traumatic hypervigilance; the watchdog is opioid and descending inhibition, whose failure lets the synapse onto the amygdala strengthen and turn acute pain chronic.

◦ Complete loss would remove learned food aversion and blunt fear learning, an honest boundary rather than a firing pathology.


ENTRY 158. POISON-WATCH NAUSEA NEURON (keyed to neuron catalogue Chapter 158)

MODES AND MODE TRANSITIONS:

This barely fits the electrical mode picture at all, because its primary input is hormonal, so its regimes are set by receptor occupancy against blood concentration rather than by synaptic spikes.

• THE MODE REGISTER.

◦ Baseline Blood-Tasting - the Slow Sampler - steady firing of about one point five spikes per second while it reads circulating chemicals directly, outside the blood-brain barrier.

◦ Nausea Drive - the Send-It-Back Signal - firing above a nausea threshold near six spikes per second when a poison-like substance is detected, triggering nausea, vomiting and one-trial aversion learning.

• PER-MODE TRANSFER FUNCTIONS.

◦ Baseline mode is a weighted sum of receptor occupancies across dopamine D2, serotonin 5-HT3, neurokinin-1, glucagon-like-peptide-1, calcitonin and GFRAL sites, integrated over minutes.

◦ Nausea drive is the same sum crossing threshold, delivered over a short axon to the solitary nucleus, the dorsal motor vagus and the parabrachial alarm cell.

• THE TRANSITION TABLE.

◦ Baseline to nausea - trigger is a rise in the blood concentration of an emetic ligand raising occupancy - timescale seconds to minutes.

◦ Desensitisation turns receptor sensitivity down over about thirty minutes to weeks, which is why the nausea of a glucagon-like-peptide-1 drug fades even as the dose climbs.
