◦ Sensitisation in the other direction underlies anticipatory nausea, a learned advance of the transition.

• FAULT REGIMES AND WATCHDOGS.

◦ Autoimmune destruction produces area postrema syndrome, weeks of unstoppable vomiting and hiccups, opening a fraction of aquaporin-4-positive neuromyelitis optica cases.

◦ Over-activation is the dose-limiting side effect of chemotherapy and of glucagon-like-peptide-1 drugs; the watchdog is the intrinsic desensitisation that normally caps sustained drive.


ENTRY 159. DREAM-SLEEP STILLNESS NEURON (keyed to neuron catalogue Chapter 159)

MODES AND MODE TRANSITIONS:

This is a two-state flip-flop element that is silent almost all day, and its striking feature is that it causes paralysis by becoming excited.

• THE MODE REGISTER.

◦ Clamped Silence - the Daytime Off - under one spike per second in waking and dreamless sleep, held down by gamma-aminobutyric acid (GABA) from the ventrolateral periaqueductal grey and lateral pontine tegmentum.

◦ Rapid-Eye-Movement Atonia Drive - the Night Disconnect - steady firing of twelve to fifteen spikes per second through rapid-eye-movement (REM) sleep that excites glycine cells to paralyse the body.

• PER-MODE TRANSFER FUNCTIONS.

◦ In clamped silence the cell rests near minus sixty millivolts with a voltage sag from Ih and an available low-threshold calcium spike.

◦ In atonia drive glutamate output onto ventral medullary glycine and GABA premotor cells scales muscle tone toward zero, so its excitement becomes the sleeper's stillness.

• THE TRANSITION TABLE.

◦ Silence to atonia - trigger is accumulated REM pressure crossing a high threshold and the mutually inhibitory REM-off group falling silent - timescale twenty to sixty seconds, with hysteresis so episodes arrive discretely and last five to thirty minutes.

◦ The switch is thrown by the sleep-state flip-flop from above; noradrenaline, serotonin, orexin and acetylcholine all bias it.

◦ Release from inhibition can fire a rebound burst through the low-threshold calcium spike.

• FAULT REGIMES AND WATCHDOGS.

◦ Partial loss gives REM sleep behaviour disorder, in which sleepers act out dreams, converting to Parkinson-type disease at about six percent a year; the warning sign precedes tremor by years.

◦ Firing wrongly in waking produces the muscle collapse of cataplexy; the flip-flop hysteresis is the watchdog that normally keeps the two states cleanly separated.


ENTRY 160. ALPHA MOTOR NEURONS (keyed to neuron catalogue Chapter 160)

MODES AND MODE TRANSITIONS:

This is a two-mode output cell with a dendritic switch, since the final common path can hold itself firing through a plateau or track its input directly, depending on neuromodulatory state.

• THE MODE REGISTER.

◦ Input-Tracking Discharge - the Faithful Printer - firing at five to fifty impulses per second in proportion to synaptic drive, the ordinary graded output.

◦ Plateau Self-Sustained Firing - the Latched Hold - the persistent inward current (PIC), a self-reinforcing sodium and L-type calcium current in the dendrites, latches on so a brief input yields sustained firing and a brief inhibition switches it off.

• PER-MODE TRANSFER FUNCTIONS.

◦ In input-tracking mode the afterhyperpolarisation of thirty to one hundred and fifty milliseconds sets the rate, and identical commands make identical force.

◦ In plateau mode the persistent inward current amplifies input several-fold, so a small increase in drive produces a much larger increase in rate and force, and firing outlasts the input.

• THE TRANSITION TABLE.

◦ Tracking to plateau - trigger is depolarisation past the plateau-on point, greatly facilitated by serotonin and noradrenaline raising the persistent inward current - timescale of the current onset, tens of milliseconds; the monoamine gain is thrown from raphe and locus coeruleus by arousal.

◦ Plateau to tracking - trigger is a brief inhibition dropping the cell below the plateau-off point, delivered by Renshaw or reciprocal inhibition.

◦ Recruitment order across the pool follows Henneman's size principle, smallest first.

• FAULT REGIMES AND WATCHDOGS.

◦ After spinal cord injury the plateau current runs uncontrolled and produces spasticity; the watchdog is descending monoaminergic and inhibitory control, whose loss unmasks the latch.

◦ The cell dies in amyotrophic lateral sclerosis (ALS) and spinal muscular atrophy, an imposed failure rather than a firing mode.


ENTRY 161. FAST-FATIGABLE (FF) ALPHA MOTOR NEURONS (Seconds-Long Power Motor Neuron) (keyed to neuron catalogue Chapter 161)

MODES AND MODE TRANSITIONS:

This is a crisp on-off power cell held in reserve, with a weaker plateau than its slower siblings, so its register is short and its character is all-or-nothing.

• THE MODE REGISTER.

◦ Deep Reserve Silence - the Last Resort Off - unrecruited at all but the highest efforts, because its low input resistance leaks current and resists recruitment.

◦ High-Force Burst - the Emergency Kick - brief trains at forty to sixty spikes per second delivering thirty to one hundred and thirty millinewtons per unit when fresh.

• PER-MODE TRANSFER FUNCTIONS.

◦ Silence holds until drive exceeds a high rheobase of ten to twenty nanoamps, near eighty percent of maximum effort.

◦ Burst mode uses the briefest afterhyperpolarisation of the family, ten to twenty milliseconds, for high rates, with a weak and hard-to-latch persistent inward current giving a crisp on-and-off character; output is scaled down by a fatigue factor that collapses below a quarter within a minute.

• THE TRANSITION TABLE.

◦ Silence to burst - trigger is strong corticospinal and reticulospinal drive, the voluntary and startle pathways, crossing rheobase - timescale a few milliseconds; weak Renshaw inhibition means it is not easily reined in once recruited.

◦ Fatigue forces de-recruitment over twenty to sixty seconds of activity, with recovery over many minutes.

• FAULT REGIMES AND WATCHDOGS.

◦ It is the first to denervate in amyotrophic lateral sclerosis and the first lost in ageing, because its thin mitochondrial reserve leaves no margin; the warning is loss of power and speed before endurance.

◦ Its transmission safety margin is the first to collapse under repetitive firing, the neural component of fatigue; there is no strong watchdog beyond orderly recruitment keeping it mostly unused.


ENTRY 162. FAST FATIGUE-RESISTANT (FR) ALPHA MOTOR NEURONS (Minutes-Long Effort Motor Neuron) (keyed to neuron catalogue Chapter 162)

MODES AND MODE TRANSITIONS:

This is the trainable workhorse of the three motor-unit types, and its plateau is well developed and readily facilitated, so effort and arousal noticeably raise its gain.

• THE MODE REGISTER.

◦ Idle Below Threshold - the Waiting Worker - unrecruited at low effort, since it sits at intermediate excitability.

◦ Sustained Effort Discharge - the Running Rhythm - steady trains at fifteen to thirty-five spikes per second delivering ten to forty millinewtons for minutes.

◦ Plateau-Amplified Locomotor Firing - the Assisted Stride - the persistent inward current amplifies rhythmic pattern-generator drive during locomotion.

• PER-MODE TRANSFER FUNCTIONS.

◦ Discharge mode has a rheobase of five to twelve nanoamps and an afterhyperpolarisation half-decay of twenty to thirty milliseconds, supporting steady mid-range rates, with output scaled by an aerobic reserve that holds force near eighty percent at two minutes.

◦ Plateau mode turns a modest oscillating input into full-amplitude output during stepping.

• THE TRANSITION TABLE.

◦ Idle to sustained discharge - trigger is drive crossing rheobase at twenty-five to sixty percent of maximum effort - timescale a few milliseconds.

◦ Into plateau amplification - trigger is serotonin from the raphe raising the persistent inward current, and cholinergic C-bouton drive from V0c cells during locomotion - timescale of current onset; these are set from above during running.

◦ Fatigue lowers output over two to six minutes as the aerobic reserve drains.

• FAULT REGIMES AND WATCHDOGS.

◦ In amyotrophic lateral sclerosis it degenerates after the fast-fatigable and before the slow population; in chronic heart or lung disease its fibres lose oxidative capacity and it behaves like a fast-fatigable unit, which is much of exercise intolerance.

◦ The watchdog is its trainability, since endurance use rebuilds mitochondria and pushes neighbouring fast units toward this safer phenotype.


ENTRY 163. SLOW (S) ALPHA MOTOR NEURONS (Hours-Long Posture Motor Neuron) (keyed to neuron catalogue Chapter 163)

MODES AND MODE TRANSITIONS:

This is a near-always-on postural cell whose defining regime is a self-sustaining plateau, so posture is held by a membrane state rather than by continuous instruction from the brain.

• THE MODE REGISTER.

◦ Tonic Postural Firing - the Standing Hum - near-continuous trains at eight to twenty-five spikes per second holding posture and tone, first recruited and last released.

◦ Plateau Self-Hold - the Set-And-Forget - the strongest and most easily latched persistent inward current of the three types sustains firing so the brain need not keep commanding.

• PER-MODE TRANSFER FUNCTIONS.

◦ Tonic mode is capped by the longest afterhyperpolarisation of any motor neuron, a half-decay of thirty to fifty milliseconds and total duration over one hundred milliseconds, matching the slow twitch for smooth fused force.

◦ Plateau mode holds a low-force output for hours with negligible fatigue, the force barely faded after tens of thousands of impulses.

• THE TRANSITION TABLE.

◦ Silence to tonic firing - trigger is any small drive above a low rheobase of three to six nanoamps, under twenty percent of effort - timescale a few milliseconds.

◦ Into and out of plateau - trigger is serotonin switching the plateau on and inhibition switching it off; because raphe output falls almost silent in rapid-eye-movement sleep, the plateau collapses and tone drops, a transition thrown from above.

• FAULT REGIMES AND WATCHDOGS.

◦ After spinal cord injury the plateau runs uncontrolled and produces the sustained involuntary contractions of spasticity; the watchdog is descending serotonergic and inhibitory control.

◦ Being the most disease-resistant motor neuron it is left overloaded in late amyotrophic lateral sclerosis and finally fails too, an imposed rather than intrinsic boundary.


ENTRY 164. GAMMA MOTOR NEURONS, ALSO CALLED FUSIMOTOR NEURONS (Spindle-Tightening Motor Neuron) (keyed to neuron catalogue Chapter 164)

MODES AND MODE TRANSITIONS:

This is a mode-poor tonic bias-setter that makes no movement, so its regimes are levels of a background drive rather than bursts of output.

• THE MODE REGISTER.

◦ Resting Fusimotor Tone - the Idle Bias - tonic firing at ten to thirty impulses per second that keeps the spindle reporting at rest.

◦ Task-Raised Drive - the Difficulty Setting - firing above fifty impulses per second during movement, raised when a task is unfamiliar, difficult or unpredictable.

• PER-MODE TRANSFER FUNCTIONS.

◦ Both modes deliver a smooth non-bursty train to intrafusal fibres, contracting the spindle poles to take up slack, so the output changes the gain of group-Ia and group-II afferents rather than producing force.

◦ The bias adds roughly zero point zero-zero-three strain units per hertz of drive, keeping the sensor from falling silent as the muscle shortens.

• THE TRANSITION TABLE.

◦ Resting tone to task-raised drive - trigger is descending reticulospinal, vestibulospinal and rubrospinal command tracking intended effort, since it receives no monosynaptic group-Ia input - timescale of the fusimotor set, seconds as the task changes.

◦ Alpha-gamma coactivation splits one descending command into a force order and a sensor-bias order together, an arrangement set from above.

• FAULT REGIMES AND WATCHDOGS.

◦ Too little drive gives sensory silence during movement and clumsiness in the dark; too much gives an over-sensitive stretch reflex contributing to spasticity and some dystonias.

◦ In amyotrophic lateral sclerosis these cells survive while alpha cells die, and their continued drive on a failing system appears to worsen the disease; the deliberate absence of a group-Ia loop is the watchdog preventing runaway sensor tightening.


ENTRY 165. STATIC GAMMA MOTOR NEURONS (Length-Sense Sharpening Motor Neuron) (keyed to neuron catalogue Chapter 165)

MODES AND MODE TRANSITIONS:

This is a mode-poor bias-setter that changes a sensor's transfer function rather than adding to a signal, biasing the spindle toward reporting position rather than speed.

• THE MODE REGISTER.

◦ Spontaneous Background Bias - the Resting Odometer - firing at ten to thirty impulses per second even in a relaxed limb, which is why a resting spindle has any background discharge at all.

◦ Posture-Raised Static Drive - the Ruler Setting - higher tonic drive during steady posture, slow deliberate movement and precision holding, tracking the size of the intended posture change.

• PER-MODE TRANSFER FUNCTIONS.

◦ Driving the bag2 and nuclear chain fibres through diffuse trail endings raises the spindle's static gain, from about ninety toward one hundred and twenty-three impulses per second per strain unit at sixty hertz of drive, while lowering velocity gain from about two hundred toward one hundred and sixteen.

◦ The effect is multiplicative on the afferent's transfer function and produces a slow well-fused contraction rather than a twitch.

• THE TRANSITION TABLE.

◦ Background bias to posture-raised drive - trigger is reticulospinal and vestibulospinal drive, raised by skin and flexor-reflex input on unstable ground - timescale seconds, as the static fusimotor set is chosen.

◦ Static and dynamic drive are adjusted independently, so the nervous system genuinely selects its sensor characteristics moment to moment from above.

• FAULT REGIMES AND WATCHDOGS.

◦ Excessive static drive makes a spindle report high tension at every length and contributes to rigidity and exaggerated tendon reflexes after stroke and spinal injury.

◦ Loss of static drive, and age-related loss of spindles, degrades position sense and balance while leaving strength intact; the honest note is that no dramatic firing pathology exists beyond too much or too little drive.


ENTRY 166. DYNAMIC GAMMA MOTOR NEURONS (Speed-Sense Sharpening Motor Neuron) (keyed to neuron catalogue Chapter 166)

MODES AND MODE TRANSITIONS:

This is a mode-poor velocity-gain knob that moves nothing, held steady during walking rather than pulsed like its static cousin.

• THE MODE REGISTER.

◦ Resting Velocity Bias - the Idle Speedometer - tonic firing at ten to fifty spikes per second that keeps the bag1 fibre modestly loaded.

◦ Task-Raised Dynamic Drive - the Catch Setting - higher steady drive when quick corrections matter, sharpening the spindle's report of stretch velocity.

• PER-MODE TRANSFER FUNCTIONS.

◦ Squeezing the nuclear bag1 poles raises the velocity gain of that spindle's group-Ia afferent up to about sixty extra afferent spikes per second per unit stretch velocity, with the bag1 fibre contracting slowly and without propagating.

◦ Output is a change in another cell's measuring instrument, not force, and it builds over about one hundred and fifty milliseconds.

• THE TRANSITION TABLE.

◦ Resting to raised drive - trigger is descending reticulospinal, vestibulospinal and rubrospinal command, since it takes almost no direct spindle sensory contacts - timescale of the fusimotor set, seconds.

◦ Bag1 stiffening lags the impulse train by about one hundred and fifty milliseconds, so the mode change is not instantaneous.

• FAULT REGIMES AND WATCHDOGS.

◦ Too little drive gives hypotonia and a lost tendon-jerk snap; too much contributes to rigidity and clonus.

◦ These cells survive in amyotrophic lateral sclerosis while alpha cells die, so the gamma loop keeps driving a shrinking pool; the absence of a group-Ia input is again the watchdog against a self-reinforcing loop.


ENTRY 167. BETA MOTOR NEURONS, ALSO CALLED SKELETOFUSIMOTOR NEURONS (Move-And-Monitor Motor Neuron) (keyed to neuron catalogue Chapter 167)

MODES AND MODE TRANSITIONS:

This is essentially a single-mode dual-output cell, because one indivisible spike train drives both force fibres and the sensor at once, so it cannot separate the two.

• THE MODE REGISTER.

◦ Coupled Move-And-Monitor Discharge - the One-Rope Trick - a steady train at five to thirty spikes per second that shortens the muscle and in the same instant tightens its spindle in proportion.

• PER-MODE TRANSFER FUNCTIONS.

◦ The dynamic beta subset drives bag1 fibres and boosts velocity sensitivity while pulling extrafusal fibres; the static subset drives chain fibres and boosts length sensitivity.

◦ A low rheobase and an afterhyperpolarisation up to one hundred milliseconds cap the rate, and a persistent inward current turns a brief input into a long plateau, matching the slow fatigue-resistant units it serves.

• THE TRANSITION TABLE.

◦ Silence to coupled discharge - trigger is the full alpha input set, monosynaptic group-Ia excitation plus corticospinal, reticulospinal and vestibulospinal drive - timescale a few milliseconds.

◦ There is no separate sensor-bias mode to switch into, since force and bias share one spike train; the only modulation is overall drive level.

• FAULT REGIMES AND WATCHDOGS.

◦ The coupling closes a positive feedback loop, contraction drives the sensor, the sensor excites the cell monosynaptically, the cell drives more contraction, which must be damped or it runs away; that damping is the required watchdog.

◦ In amyotrophic lateral sclerosis these cells die with the alpha population so spindle feedback collapses faster than motor-unit loss alone predicts, and aberrant beta connections after nerve regeneration create sensor signals uncoupled from real length.


ENTRY 168. SYMPATHETIC PREGANGLIONIC NEURONS (keyed to neuron catalogue Chapter 168)

MODES AND MODE TRANSITIONS:

This is a mode-poor tonic autonomic output cell whose few regimes are levels of a heartbeat-locked drive, coupled into clusters that burst together.

• THE MODE REGISTER.

◦ Cardiac-Locked Basal Tone - the Pressure Hum - slow irregular firing under five spikes per second, locked to the heartbeat and modulated by breathing, giving the two-to-six-hertz sympathetic rhythm.

◦ Reflex-Raised Drive - the Stand-Up Surge - increased firing when blood pressure falls or effort and stress rise, setting vessel calibre, heart rate and adrenaline release.

• PER-MODE TRANSFER FUNCTIONS.

◦ A transient potassium current delays the first spike, making the cell an integrator; a coupled cluster of up to thirty cells synchronises through connexin-36 gap junctions to generate the bursting rhythm.

◦ Output is a nicotinic drive amplified onto ten to two hundred ganglion cells per axon, so the cell is an amplifier rather than a relay.

• THE TRANSITION TABLE.

◦ Basal tone to reflex-raised drive - trigger is baroreceptor error relayed through the rostral ventrolateral medulla, with respiratory phase as a multiplicative gate - timescale of the baroreflex loop, about one second.

◦ Circulating angiotensin-2 and aldosterone raise drive hormonally over minutes, and hypothalamic and orexin input set state from above.

• FAULT REGIMES AND WATCHDOGS.

◦ Multiple system atrophy destroys more than half of these cells, producing severe orthostatic hypotension.

◦ Spinal cord injury above the sixth thoracic segment leaves them disconnected and, over weeks, hyperexcitable, so a full bladder drives blood pressure dangerously high in autonomic dysreflexia; the missing watchdog is the lost descending baroreflex control.


ENTRY 169. SACRAL PARASYMPATHETIC PREGANGLIONIC NEURONS (keyed to neuron catalogue Chapter 169)

MODES AND MODE TRANSITIONS:

This is one of the clearest biological bistable switches in the body, with two stable states and a jump between them, so it must be treated as a flip-flop and not a graded response.

• THE MODE REGISTER.

◦ Storage Silence - the Held Bladder - held below threshold so a healthy bladder fills for hours without leaking.

◦ Voiding Burst - the Release Command - a sustained burst of ten to thirty spikes per second that empties the bladder and bowel and drives erectile vasodilation.

• PER-MODE TRANSFER FUNCTIONS.

◦ Storage mode keeps output at zero while afferent drive climbs with volume; a transient potassium current delays any first spike.

◦ Voiding mode delivers nicotinic drive to pelvic and intramural ganglia, and the switch shows hysteresis, turning on at high volume and off only far below it.

• THE TRANSITION TABLE.

◦ Storage to voiding - trigger is bladder volume crossing about three hundred millilitres together with the descending permission gate from Barrington's nucleus in the pons - timescale of the spinobulbospinal loop, fifty to seventy milliseconds; the permission is granted from above by the forebrain.

◦ Voiding to storage - trigger is volume falling below about thirty millilitres.

◦ Opioids gate the switch on both sides of the synapse, which is why morphine causes urinary retention.

• FAULT REGIMES AND WATCHDOGS.

◦ Spinal cord injury separates them from the pons and gives detrusor-sphincter dyssynergia with dangerous back pressure on the kidneys, and a new C-fibre-driven reflex emerges.

◦ Multiple system atrophy and diabetic autonomic neuropathy weaken them into a large poorly emptying bladder; the descending gate is the watchdog whose loss unmasks the disorder.


ENTRY 170. RENSHAW CELLS (keyed to neuron catalogue Chapter 170)

MODES AND MODE TRANSITIONS:

This is a two-mode automatic-gain-control cell whose signature regime is an extraordinary opening burst, so it is essentially quiet-until-driven and then explosive.

• THE MODE REGISTER.

◦ Quiet Between Volleys - the Waiting Brake - near-silence when the motor pool is not firing, with the soma blanketed in inhibition.

◦ Recurrent-Inhibition Burst - the Shout-Down - a long-lasting response opening at an instantaneous frequency near fifteen hundred spikes per second and decaying over thirty to fifty milliseconds after a motor volley.

• PER-MODE TRANSFER FUNCTIONS.

◦ The opening burst turns a brief synchronous motor volley into a graded decaying inhibition whose strength encodes how much of the pool just fired.

◦ Two transmitters from one terminal give a two-timescale output, glycine fast at about ten milliseconds and gamma-aminobutyric acid (GABA) slower at about thirty milliseconds, so recurrent inhibition outlasts reciprocal inhibition.

• THE TRANSITION TABLE.

◦ Quiet to burst - trigger is acetylcholine from motor-axon recurrent collaterals, co-released with glutamate, onto nicotinic and muscarinic receptors - timescale one to two milliseconds after the motor neuron's own spike.

◦ Descending gain of zero point five to two and mutual Renshaw inhibition set how hard the brake bites, and because it inhibits Ia inhibitory interneurons, driving a pool also permits deliberate co-contraction.

• FAULT REGIMES AND WATCHDOGS.

◦ Strychnine blocking its glycine receptors and tetanus toxin blocking its release both remove the brake and produce runaway motor firing and rigid spasm.

◦ In amyotrophic lateral sclerosis models the circuit degrades early, removing a brake from stressed motor neurons; the self-tuning of its own gephyrin clusters to its drive is the watchdog that normally matches brake to load.


ENTRY 171. IA INHIBITORY INTERNEURONS (Counter-Muscle Silencing Neuron) (keyed to neuron catalogue Chapter 171)

MODES AND MODE TRANSITIONS:

This is a fast phasic sign-inverting adder, and paired with its mirror image it behaves as a flip-flop, one of the building blocks of the alternating rhythm of stepping.

• THE MODE REGISTER.

◦ Resting Quiet - the Idle Gate - silent at rest, waiting on a synchronous volley.

◦ Reciprocal-Inhibition Discharge - the Antagonist Silencer - one to three spikes at disynaptic latency that inhibit the opposing muscle's motor neurons so mover and relaxer never fight.

◦ Command-Copy Tonic Firing - the One-Order Relaxer - tonic activity once voluntary movement begins, carrying a copy of the brain's command to relax the antagonist.

• PER-MODE TRANSFER FUNCTIONS.

◦ Discharge mode turns a group-Ia volley into glycine inhibition of antagonist alpha and gamma motor neurons one synapse and about one millisecond behind the direct reflex, with an inhibitory gain of about zero point three to zero point four.

◦ Command-copy mode sums descending corticospinal, rubrospinal, vestibulospinal and reticulospinal drive so one order both contracts and relaxes.

• THE TRANSITION TABLE.

◦ Quiet to reciprocal inhibition - trigger is a monosynaptic group-Ia volley from the agonist spindle - timescale the disynaptic delay, about one millisecond beyond the reflex.

◦ Into command-copy firing - trigger is descending drive at movement onset, thrown from the motor cortex above.

◦ Its group-Ia input is gated presynaptically by gamma-aminobutyric acid (GABA) on the afferent terminals, a multiplicative term from zero point two to one.

• FAULT REGIMES AND WATCHDOGS.

◦ Reduced reciprocal inhibition after stroke, in cerebral palsy and in dystonia produces co-contraction and spasticity.

◦ Loss of glycine-receptor function in hyperekplexia or strychnine poisoning removes the inhibition entirely and produces exaggerated startle and rigidity; the mutual inhibition of the flexor and extensor versions is the watchdog keeping the pair balanced.


ENTRY 172. IB INHIBITORY INTERNEURONS (Tendon-Load Restraining Neuron) (keyed to neuron catalogue Chapter 172)

MODES AND MODE TRANSITIONS:

This is a state-dependent force comparator whose most remarkable feature is reflex reversal, so the sign of its effect is a property of the network state, not of the wire.

• THE MODE REGISTER.

◦ Resting Quiet - the Unloaded Idle - silent when no tendon force is being generated.

◦ Standing Restraint - the Force Trimmer - during quiet standing it inhibits the extensors that hold you up, trimming output as tension climbs.

◦ Stance-Phase Assist - the Load Booster - during the stance phase of walking the same input is routed through excitatory partners so heavier loading increases extensor drive and stops the leg collapsing.

• PER-MODE TRANSFER FUNCTIONS.

◦ In both active modes firing climbs smoothly with tendon force up to one hundred hertz or more, delivered at a disynaptic central delay of about two point five milliseconds.

◦ Restraint mode delivers glycine and gamma-aminobutyric acid (GABA) inhibition to the homonymous motor pool; assist mode delivers, via excitatory partners, a positive drive of the opposite sign.

• THE TRANSITION TABLE.

◦ Restraint to assist - trigger is the locomotor phase flag switching from stance-hold to active stance, set by the pattern generator - timescale of the gait cycle, hundreds of milliseconds; any simulation that hard-codes the sign is wrong for half the step.

◦ Descending gain from zero to two and presynaptic inhibition of the Ib terminals from zero to one edit the input before integration.

• FAULT REGIMES AND WATCHDOGS.

◦ Loss or silencing produces unopposed extensor drive under load, the stiff poorly graded contraction of spasticity after spinal injury or stroke.

◦ Loss of glycine-receptor function in hyperekplexia makes the motor neuron deaf to this cell and produces violent startle and neonatal rigidity; correct chloride handling by the potassium-chloride cotransporter KCC2 is the watchdog, since its failure turns glycine from inhibitory to excitatory.


ENTRY 173. PROPRIOSPINAL INTERNEURONS (Spine-Spanning Relay Neuron) (keyed to neuron catalogue Chapter 173)

MODES AND MODE TRANSITIONS:

This is a delay-line relay in which the axon length is itself the computation, and its most distinctive regime is a slow structural remodelling that rebuilds detour circuits after injury.

• THE MODE REGISTER.

◦ Intersegmental Relay - the Timing Bridge - carries descending commands and reflex signals between segments, setting the phase relationship between limbs by conduction delay.

◦ Plateau Holding - the Postural Latch - a persistent inward current sustains firing so a brief command becomes a held posture.

◦ Detour-Forming Sprout - the Spare-Parts Rewire - after injury the cell grows new axon branches around the damage to reconnect brain to motor pool.

• PER-MODE TRANSFER FUNCTIONS.

◦ Relay mode passes excitation or inhibition to target segments after a conduction delay of one to one hundred milliseconds set by distance and a velocity of three to thirty metres per second.

◦ Plateau mode outlasts its input for half a second to five seconds.

◦ Sprout mode changes the wiring diagram itself, requiring axonal protein synthesis and altered gene transcription over two to twelve weeks.

• THE TRANSITION TABLE.

◦ Relay to plateau - trigger is depolarisation activating the persistent inward current, biased by serotonin, noradrenaline and dopamine reaching by diffusion from above - timescale of current onset.

◦ Into detour-forming sprout - trigger is spinal injury and the resulting loss of descending drive - timescale weeks, aided by growth-permissive glia and rehabilitation, epidural stimulation and serotonergic drugs.

• FAULT REGIMES AND WATCHDOGS.

◦ Unstable propriospinal networks produce propriospinal myoclonus, sudden trunk jerks propagating slowly up and down the body; failure to remyelinate leaves preserved anatomy with unusable timing.

◦ The watchdog is microglial pruning of excess sprouts in the first month, whose failure leaves a jerky unstable circuit.


ENTRY 174. COMMISSURAL INTERNEURONS (Across-Midline Coordinating Neuron) (keyed to neuron catalogue Chapter 174)

MODES AND MODE TRANSITIONS:

This is a phase-locked coordinating relay whose register is defined by the left-right relationship it imposes, most often alternation but sometimes synchrony.

• THE MODE REGISTER.

◦ Alternation Enforcement - the Wait-Your-Turn Signal - crossing inhibition, largely through inhibitory members, that holds the two sides out of phase during walking.

◦ Synchrony Enforcement - the Both-Together Drive - crossing excitation, through excitatory members, that couples the two sides in phase during hopping or bounding.

• PER-MODE TRANSFER FUNCTIONS.

◦ Both modes fire in bursts locked to one phase of the step cycle and deliver their conductance to contralateral interneurons and motor neurons after a crossing delay of a few milliseconds.

◦ Alternation mode delivers glycine and gamma-aminobutyric acid (GABA) to silence the opposite side during a side's active phase; synchrony mode delivers glutamate to co-activate it.

• THE TRANSITION TABLE.

◦ Alternation to synchrony - trigger is a rise in locomotor speed shifting the balance from inhibitory toward excitatory commissural members - timescale of gait change, seconds; the descending speed command is set from above.

◦ Ipsilateral rhythm drive from V2a and Shox2 cells sets which phase each cell fires in.

• FAULT REGIMES AND WATCHDOGS.

◦ Loss of the crossing pathway removes left-right negotiation, so the two halves of the cord stop coordinating and gait collapses toward hopping.

◦ There is no notable self-generated pathological firing mode; the honest boundary is that the coordination is only as good as the crossing axons that survive, and their early loss in contusion injury is why crossed coordination often fails before strength.


ENTRY 175. CENTRAL PATTERN GENERATOR INTERNEURONS (keyed to neuron catalogue Chapter 175)

MODES AND MODE TRANSITIONS:

This is a mode-rich conditional oscillator whose very existence is chemically gated, so it is silent clockwork until the right neuromodulators switch it into rhythm.

• THE MODE REGISTER.

◦ Chemically Gated Silence - the Unprimed Clock - quiescence when the enabling neuromodulators are absent, which is why an isolated cord is silent until serotonin and N-methyl-D-aspartate are added.

◦ Conditional Bursting - the Fictive Step - rhythmic burst packets driven by the persistent sodium current whose slow inactivation is the clock, at roughly zero point two to one hertz in the isolated cord and up to about two hertz in fast walking.

◦ Sensory-Entrained Bursting - the Reactive Stride - the same rhythm reset and lengthened by load feedback, making the circuit a hybrid of clockwork and reflex.

• PER-MODE TRANSFER FUNCTIONS.

◦ In bursting mode the persistent sodium current, carried largely by Nav1.6, builds slow inactivation over hundreds of milliseconds to a second, and connexin-36 gap junctions synchronise the rhythm-generating cells into one oscillating sheet.

◦ In entrained mode group-Ib load feedback can lengthen stance and reset the whole cycle, and the sodium-potassium pump acts as a slow membrane current shaping burst frequency.

• THE TRANSITION TABLE.

◦ Silence to bursting - trigger is tonic descending drive from the mesencephalic locomotor region together with serotonin, dopamine and noradrenaline enabling oscillation - timescale seconds; the enabling state is thrown from above.

◦ Bursting to entrained - trigger is group-Ib or hip-angle afferent feedback within a cycle - timescale milliseconds to a cycle.

• FAULT REGIMES AND WATCHDOGS.

◦ After complete spinal cord injury the circuit survives but loses descending drive, which is why epidural stimulation at twenty-five to sixty hertz can restore stepping; excessive excitability produces clonus and flexor spasms.

◦ The watchdog is astrocytic potassium buffering, since if it fails the rhythm speeds up and destabilises.


ENTRY 176. DORSAL CLASSES DI1 THROUGH DI6, PLUS LATE-BORN DILA AND DILB (Back-Half Sensory Founder Neuron) (keyed to neuron catalogue Chapter 176)

MODES AND MODE TRANSITIONS:

This is a family of birth cohorts rather than a single working cell, so the honest register is a developmental one, in which the electrical modes are inherited class by class from position and target.

• THE MODE REGISTER.

◦ Progenitor Specification - the Fortnight Stamp - the founding developmental state in which a bone-morphogenetic-protein and Wnt gradient from the roof plate switches on class-defining genes.

◦ Tonic Inhibitory Gating - the Steady Damper - the adult firing mode of dILA and dI4 inhibitory cells, discharging steadily while driven.

◦ Delayed and Transient Excitatory Relay - the Held-Then-Sent Signal - the adult firing mode of dILB cells, in which an A-type potassium current holds off the first spike by tens of milliseconds or a T-type calcium current gives a burst.

• PER-MODE TRANSFER FUNCTIONS.

◦ Specification is a slow one-time transition of gene expression, not a firing rule.

◦ Tonic gating maps sustained afferent drive to sustained inhibition of pain and touch pathways.

◦ Delayed and transient relay convert timed afferent input into precisely delayed or bursting output; dI3 cells fire tonically and integrate over hundreds of milliseconds for load-tracking grip.

• THE TRANSITION TABLE.

◦ Progenitor to adult cell type - trigger is the position along the dorsoventral gradient during roughly a fortnight of embryonic life - timescale of development, irreversible.

◦ In the adult, transitions between quiet and active firing follow the afferent class that lands on each cohort, and descending serotonergic and noradrenergic fibres damp pain transmission from above.

• FAULT REGIMES AND WATCHDOGS.

◦ Because these are lineages, the notable failure is developmental miswiring rather than a single firing pathology; loss of a class removes a whole sensory circuit, as silencing dI3 abolishes the ability to increase grip with load.

◦ The honest note is that no shared adult fault regime spans all eight classes, since their jobs and vulnerabilities diverge.


ENTRY 177. VENTRAL CLASSES V0, V1, V2, AND V3 (Walking-Circuit Founder Neuron) (keyed to neuron catalogue Chapter 177)

MODES AND MODE TRANSITIONS:

This too is a family of birth cohorts, so its register is developmental at the parent level, with each lineage carrying its own adult firing mode read off a sonic-hedgehog gradient from below.

• THE MODE REGISTER.

◦ Progenitor Specification - the Belly-Side Stamp - the founding state in which the sonic-hedgehog gradient from the notochord and floor plate assigns the p0 to p3 domains their master genes.

◦ Recurrent-Inhibition Bursting - the Renshaw Answer - the V1 Renshaw mode, a high-frequency burst up to several hundred hertz to a single motor-neuron spike, then silence.

◦ Speed-Recruited Tonic Firing - the Locomotor Gain - the V2a mode, tonic firing recruited progressively as locomotor speed rises.

◦ Burst-Terminating Transition Firing - the Phase Ender - the V1-population mode firing at the transitions of the step cycle to terminate bursts.

• PER-MODE TRANSFER FUNCTIONS.

◦ Specification is a one-time gene-expression transition, not a firing rule.

◦ V0d, V1 and V2b deliver gamma-aminobutyric acid (GABA) and glycine inhibition; V0v, V2a and V3 deliver glutamate excitation; V0c cholinergic C-boutons multiply motor-neuron output by inhibiting the medium afterhyperpolarisation through muscarinic M2 receptors, a gain control rather than a command.

• THE TRANSITION TABLE.

◦ Progenitor to adult cell type - trigger is the sonic-hedgehog concentration band during development, held at the p2-to-motor-neuron boundary by mutual repression of Irx3 and Olig2 - timescale of development, irreversible.

◦ In the adult, V1 firing terminates bursts and V2a is recruited as speed rises, transitions driven by rhythm-generator and reticulospinal drive from above.

• FAULT REGIMES AND WATCHDOGS.

◦ Silencing V1 cells slows locomotion in mice and zebrafish; in amyotrophic lateral sclerosis the V0c C-bouton interface enlarges and misregulates motor-neuron excitability.

◦ The honest note is that a shared fault regime is not defined across all four lineages, since their outputs and vulnerabilities differ.


ENTRY 178. V0 (Left-Right Alternating Neuron) (keyed to neuron catalogue Chapter 178)

MODES AND MODE TRANSITIONS:

This is a phase-locked crossing coordinator whose register is a speed-dependent handover between an inhibitory and an excitatory member, a computation performed by the population rather than by any cell.

• THE MODE REGISTER.

◦ Slow-Speed Alternation - the Walk Keeper - the V0D inhibitory mode, releasing glycine and gamma-aminobutyric acid (GABA) across the midline onto contralateral motor neurons to hold the two legs out of phase at walking speeds.

◦ Fast-Speed Alternation - the Trot Keeper - the V0V excitatory mode, working across the midline through an intermediate inhibitory cell to maintain alternation at fast speeds.

◦ Ipsilateral Gain Setting - the C-Bouton Boost - the V0C cholinergic mode, multiplying ipsilateral motor-neuron output by one point zero to one point six through muscarinic M2 receptors.

• PER-MODE TRANSFER FUNCTIONS.

◦ Each mode fires in bursts locked to one phase of the step cycle at five to forty spikes per second, with a crossing delay of one to five milliseconds.

◦ V0D delivers inhibitory conductance up to five nanosiemens to contralateral motor neurons; V0V delivers excitation to contralateral inhibitory cells; V0C retunes the ipsilateral target through the C-bouton and its subsurface cistern.

• THE TRANSITION TABLE.

◦ V0D to V0V handover - trigger is a rise in locomotor speed shifting the population weighting - timescale of the speed change, seconds; the descending speed command comes from above through reticulospinal drive.

◦ Ipsilateral rhythm drive from V2a and Shox2 cells sets each cell's phase, and V0C coupling through connexin-36 lets the cholinergic cells act partly as one sheet.

• FAULT REGIMES AND WATCHDOGS.

◦ Loss of the inhibitory members leaves alternation only at running speeds; loss of the excitatory members leaves a hop as soon as speed rises; total loss produces a synchronous hopping gait like a rabbit.

◦ In amyotrophic lateral sclerosis the V0C interface enlarges and misregulates motor-neuron excitability; the population handover is the watchdog that normally keeps alternation stable across the full speed range.


ENTRY 179. V1 (Pace-Quickening Damping Neuron) (keyed to neuron catalogue Chapter 179)

MODES AND MODE TRANSITIONS:

This is a two-mode fast inhibitory switch, built to be silent until commanded and then to fire one tight brake pulse, because its whole worth is well-timed inhibition rather than continuous output.

• THE MODE REGISTER.

◦ Quiescent gate - the Waiting Brake - the resting state in which the cell sits below threshold, poised to interrupt but adding nothing to the circuit.

◦ Recurrent burst - the Return Volley - a brief high-frequency discharge fired the moment a motor command arrives, feeding a copy of that command back as inhibition.

• PER-MODE TRANSFER FUNCTIONS.

◦ In the Waiting Brake the cell holds near minus 60 millivolts (the resting voltage) with output essentially zero, threshold near minus 45 millivolts.

◦ In the Return Volley one motor-axon volley drives a Renshaw cell to several hundred spikes per second that decays over 30 to 50 milliseconds, gain 0.1 to 0.5, loop closing within 1 to 3 milliseconds; confidence high for Renshaw cells, lower for the Foxp2 and Ia inhibitory interneuron subclasses.

• THE TRANSITION TABLE.

◦ Motor-axon collateral acetylcholine plus glutamate, or an Ia afferent (the fast muscle-stretch fibre) volley - Waiting Brake to Return Volley - within 1 to 3 milliseconds.

◦ Burst decay and short-term depression at the collateral input - Return Volley back to Waiting Brake - 30 to 50 milliseconds.

◦ Corticospinal and reticulospinal drive presets reflex gain from above, a Layer 10/11 service that biases how readily the switch throws before a movement.

• FAULT REGIMES AND WATCHDOGS.

◦ Glycine receptor failure at these synapses is a boundary regime giving hyperekplexia, in which a small surprise stiffens the whole body; the watchdog is the tuned strength of glycine and gamma-aminobutyric acid (GABA) co-release anchored by large gephyrin clusters.

◦ In amyotrophic lateral sclerosis (ALS) the terminals vanish before the motor neurons die, an early warning sign rather than a used mode.


ENTRY 180. V2 (Step-Strength Steadying Neuron) (keyed to neuron catalogue Chapter 180)

MODES AND MODE TRANSITIONS:

This is a mode-rich integrator whose excitatory half (V2a) carries a genuine bistable memory element, so it can either follow input moment to moment or latch into a self-sustaining plateau.

• THE MODE REGISTER.

◦ Tonic follower - the Faithful Engine - the default state in which the cell fires for as long as it is depolarised, translating drive into output.

◦ Serotonergic plateau - the Latched Burst - a self-sustaining depolarisation that outlasts its input, switched on by brainstem serotonin and off by a brief inhibition.

◦ Onset-adapting quiet (V2b inhibitory sibling) - the Alternation Keeper - a steadier inhibitory output that, with V1, holds a joint's bending and straightening muscles in turns.

• PER-MODE TRANSFER FUNCTIONS.

◦ In the Faithful Engine the V2a cell fires tonically with little adaptation, capacitance about 21 picofarad, persistent sodium conductance 2 to 6 nanosiemens.

◦ In the Latched Burst a persistent sodium current plus L-type calcium channels hold a plateau whose duration is set by persistent-sodium inactivation of 1 to 6 seconds, matching the 0.4 to 2 second step cycle.

◦ In the Alternation Keeper V2b delivers glycinergic inhibitory conductance of 0 to 8 nanosiemens onto motor neurons; confidence moderate.

• THE TRANSITION TABLE.

◦ Ambient serotonin from the brainstem (a Layer 10/11 broadcast) - Faithful Engine to Latched Burst - seconds to minutes.

◦ A brief inhibitory input - Latched Burst back to Faithful Engine - milliseconds.

◦ Reticulospinal drive sets whether the population runs at all, thrown from above.

• FAULT REGIMES AND WATCHDOGS.

◦ Excess persistent sodium current after injury drives spasticity, a boundary regime; the watchdog is the sodium-channel blocker sensitivity that clinically reduces spasms.

◦ V2a cells degenerate after V1 and before motor neurons in ALS models, a warning marker rather than a used mode.


ENTRY 181. V3 (Two-Sided Rhythm Balancing Neuron) (keyed to neuron catalogue Chapter 181)

MODES AND MODE TRANSITIONS:

This is a two-mode crossing excitatory cell split by subtype, one ventral group a steady relay and one dorsal group an onset detector, together supplying the excitatory glue that keeps a fast rhythm even.

• THE MODE REGISTER.

◦ Tonic drive (ventral V3) - the Even Keel - low-threshold sustained firing that converts steady excitation into steady contralateral output.

◦ Onset burst (dorsal V3) - the First-Beat Flag - a fast-adapting response firing mainly at stimulus onset, gated by an A-type potassium current (a fast-inactivating current that delays firing).

• PER-MODE TRANSFER FUNCTIONS.

◦ In the Even Keel the ventral cell has low rheobase (10 to 40 picoamps), little adaptation, locomotor firing 5 to 50 spikes per second, rest near minus 60 millivolts.

◦ In the First-Beat Flag the dorsal cell adapts quickly and often fires only once, threshold near minus 44 millivolts; confidence moderate, from rodent data.

• THE TRANSITION TABLE.

◦ Sustained ipsilateral rhythm drive - into the Even Keel - held over the step cycle of 0.4 to 2 seconds.

◦ A fresh depolarising step against a primed A-type current - into the First-Beat Flag - milliseconds, resetting as the current recovers.

◦ Reticulospinal drive from above sets overall recruitment; N-methyl-D-aspartate (NMDA)-receptor conductance of 50 to 200 milliseconds on targets lets the population average over several cycles to remove variance.

• FAULT REGIMES AND WATCHDOGS.

◦ After spinal injury the same recurrent excitation drives spasms and clonus, a boundary regime; the watchdog is glutamate clearance, whose failure produces synchronous self-sustaining discharge.


ENTRY 182. LAMINA I PROJECTION NEURONS (Pain-Reporting Brain-Bound Neuron) (keyed to neuron catalogue Chapter 182)

MODES AND MODE TRANSITIONS:

This is a mode-rich temporal integrator, because on top of several firing patterns it carries wind-up, a slow build-up that is itself a distinct sensitised operating regime.

• THE MODE REGISTER.

◦ Tonic firing - the Steady Report - sustained firing that tracks ongoing noxious drive.

◦ Delayed firing - the Held Report - an A-type potassium current imposes a pause before firing, filtering weak brief inputs.

◦ Phasic or single-spike firing - the Brief Report - an onset-only signal for change.

◦ Wind-up - the Rising Alarm - a normal short-term amplification in which identical C-fibre volleys (the slowest pain fibres) at about one per second give steadily more spikes.

• PER-MODE TRANSFER FUNCTIONS.

◦ Steady Report tracks input at 0 to 60 spikes per second, rest near minus 65 millivolts, threshold near minus 40 millivolts.

◦ Held Report inserts a delay set by A-type inactivation before the first spike.

◦ Brief Report fires once or briefly then falls silent.

◦ Rising Alarm builds a slow depolarisation from substance P (a pain peptide acting through a G-protein-coupled receptor) and N-methyl-D-aspartate (NMDA)-receptor currents, accumulating over tens of seconds; confidence high.

• THE TRANSITION TABLE.

◦ Repeated one-per-second C-fibre volleys expelling the magnesium block from NMDA receptors - any firing mode into Rising Alarm - tens of seconds.

◦ Rest between volleys - Rising Alarm back toward baseline - the wind-up envelope decays over tens of seconds.

◦ Descending monoamine modulation from the brainstem (a Layer 10/11 service) sets gain from above.

• FAULT REGIMES AND WATCHDOGS.

◦ Loss of the chloride pump KCC2 flips inhibition into excitation, giving allodynia (pain from light touch) and hyperalgesia, a boundary regime; the watchdog is KCC2 activity, whose loss runs over hours to days.

◦ Runaway wind-up plus descending facilitation produces chronic pain that outlasts the injury.


ENTRY 183. ISLET CELLS (keyed to neuron catalogue Chapter 183)

MODES AND MODE TRANSITIONS:

This is a mode-poor tonic inhibitor with one steady output regime and a pacemaker-like tendency, since its job is to hold down traffic in the pain layer continuously.

• THE MODE REGISTER.

◦ Tonic inhibitory discharge - the Standing Brake - a discharge lasting the whole of a depolarising step rather than adapting away, supplying steady gamma-aminobutyric acid (GABA) to the substantia gelatinosa (the jelly-like pain layer, lamina II).

◦ Post-inhibition rebound - the Spring-Back - an Ih-driven return toward threshold after inhibition, giving a mild pacemaker rhythm. (Ih is the hyperpolarisation-activated current through HCN channels.)

• PER-MODE TRANSFER FUNCTIONS.

◦ In the Standing Brake the cell rests more negative than minus 60 millivolts, threshold near minus 45 millivolts, delivering 0 to 3 nanosiemens of GABA type A inhibition, membrane time constant 10 to 20 milliseconds.

◦ In the Spring-Back Ih turns on over 100 to 500 milliseconds and produces a characteristic sag that carries the membrane back toward firing; confidence moderate.

• THE TRANSITION TABLE.

◦ A hyperpolarising input activating Ih - Standing Brake to Spring-Back - 100 to 500 milliseconds, self-resetting.

◦ Descending monoamine modulation sets output gain from above.

• FAULT REGIMES AND WATCHDOGS.

◦ Reduced GABA or glycine output is a boundary regime giving mechanical allodynia and poorly localised pain; the watchdog is presynaptic inhibition, whose loss lets every arriving volley release its full transmitter load.

◦ Astrocytic reverse GABA release can convert the layer's inhibition into tonic excitation after injury, over days to weeks.


ENTRY 184. VERTICAL CELLS, ALSO CALLED STALKED CELLS (keyed to neuron catalogue Chapter 184)

MODES AND MODE TRANSITIONS:

This is a mode-poor excitatory coincidence detector with essentially one firing regime shaped by a dendritic filter, the last local relay before pain leaves the cord.

• THE MODE REGISTER.

◦ Delayed coincidence firing - the Patient Adder - a hesitation imposed by the A-type potassium current of Kv4 channels, so single weak inputs are filtered out while several arriving together outrun it.

• PER-MODE TRANSFER FUNCTIONS.

◦ In the Patient Adder rest is near minus 64 millivolts, threshold near minus 40 millivolts, the dendritic A-type current activating in about 2 milliseconds and inactivating over 30 to 60 milliseconds so the cell behaves as a coincidence detector rather than a summing adder; confidence moderate.

• THE TRANSITION TABLE.

◦ Convergent C-fibre and A-delta input arriving within the A-current window - into firing - tens of milliseconds.

◦ Retrograde endocannabinoid signalling (2-arachidonoylglycerol travelling backward) silences the terminals inhibiting it, briefly raising excitability from below over seconds.

• FAULT REGIMES AND WATCHDOGS.

◦ Loss of inhibitory gating after nerve injury lets touch alone drive the cell, a boundary regime producing allodynia; the watchdog is the tonic gamma-aminobutyric acid (GABA) and glycine input, and the collapse runs over days.


ENTRY 185. RADIAL CELLS (keyed to neuron catalogue Chapter 185)

MODES AND MODE TRANSITIONS:

This is a mode-poor excitatory amplifier that, uniquely, approximates a true summing junction, with a small family of A-type firing patterns rather than genuinely separate operating regimes.

• THE MODE REGISTER.

◦ A-type delayed or gap firing - the Even Summer - the single working regime, in which a symmetric compact tree lets the cell sum inputs from every direction and fire after an A-current pause, sometimes with a gap (spike, silence, then a train).

• PER-MODE TRANSFER FUNCTIONS.

◦ In the Even Summer rest is near minus 62 millivolts, threshold near minus 40 millivolts, peak A conductance 0.2 to 0.4 microsiemens across 4 to 8 equal sectors, so summation is close to position-independent; confidence moderate, from one rat study.

• THE TRANSITION TABLE.

◦ Convergent nociceptor and local excitatory input - into firing - after an A-current pause of tens of milliseconds, recovering over 100 to 300 milliseconds.

◦ Somatostatin release above about 10 hertz spreads by volume transmission 20 to 50 micrometres, biasing neighbours it never contacts.

• FAULT REGIMES AND WATCHDOGS.

◦ Shrinkage of the A-type current after nerve injury is a boundary regime that turns a reluctant amplifier into an eager one, feeding central sensitisation and mechanical hypersensitivity; the watchdog is the size of that A-current.


ENTRY 186. CENTRAL CELLS (Close-Range Relay Neuron) (keyed to neuron catalogue Chapter 186)

MODES AND MODE TRANSITIONS:

This is a two-flavour class in which the best-documented excitatory members run essentially one transient regime, reporting change rather than level, so it is a mode-poor differentiator.

• THE MODE REGISTER.

◦ Transient onset firing (excitatory transient central cell) - the Change Caller - a few spikes in the first ten or twenty milliseconds then silence, handing downstream circuits a derivative.

◦ Tonic presynaptic damping (inhibitory subtype) - the Terminal Curb - a steadier output that damps incoming terminals by presynaptic inhibition.

• PER-MODE TRANSFER FUNCTIONS.

◦ In the Change Caller the cell rests unusually depolarised at minus 50 to minus 60 millivolts and fires briefly because its sodium channels inactivate without recovering, availability lost over 20 to 30 milliseconds and recovering over 200 to 400 milliseconds; confidence moderate.

◦ In the Terminal Curb the inhibitory subtype releases gamma-aminobutyric acid (GABA) and glycine onto excitatory interneurons and afferent terminals.

• THE TRANSITION TABLE.

◦ A step of input onto available sodium channels - rest into the Change Caller - within milliseconds, then self-terminating as channels inactivate.

◦ Retrograde endocannabinoids silence the cell from the far side, briefly, over seconds.

• FAULT REGIMES AND WATCHDOGS.

◦ When inhibition fails the touch-driven route through these cells to vertical cells opens, a boundary regime producing allodynia; the watchdog is tonic GABA and glycine gating.


ENTRY 187. ANTENNA CELLS (keyed to neuron catalogue Chapter 187)

MODES AND MODE TRANSITIONS:

This is a compartmentalised comparator rather than a multi-mode firer, its two dendritic fields acting as semi-independent units so its main regime axis is which field is driving.

• THE MODE REGISTER.

◦ Convergent comparison firing - the Two-Way Listener - the working regime in which pain-stream input on the dorsal field and touch-stream input on the ventral field are compared rather than merely summed.

◦ Neurokinin-1 slow-facilitated firing - the Lingering Memory - a slow depolarisation lasting many seconds after a noxious event, a chemical trace of it.

• PER-MODE TRANSFER FUNCTIONS.

◦ In the Two-Way Listener the two fields, joined by a thin stem, reach the soma along different cables, so inhibition on one leaves the other untouched; rest near minus 60 millivolts, threshold near minus 42 millivolts.

◦ In the Lingering Memory substance P on neurokinin-1 receptors leaves a slow current rising over 300 to 500 milliseconds and decaying over several seconds; confidence moderate.

• THE TRANSITION TABLE.

◦ A noxious volley releasing substance P - Two-Way Listener into Lingering Memory - onset over several hundred milliseconds, decaying over seconds.

◦ Descending inhibition adjusts the balance between the two fields from above.

• FAULT REGIMES AND WATCHDOGS.

◦ Loss of inhibition on the ventral field lets touch alone reach threshold, a boundary regime and one cellular route to allodynia; the watchdog is field-specific inhibition.


ENTRY 188. LAMINA V WIDE DYNAMIC RANGE NEURONS (Touch-To-Damage Grading Neuron) (keyed to neuron catalogue Chapter 188)

MODES AND MODE TRANSITIONS:

This is a mode-rich intensity-grading integrator whose signature is wind-up, a normal short-term sensitisation that is a genuine operating regime layered on a graded firing dial.

• THE MODE REGISTER.

◦ Graded intensity coding - the Loudness Dial - the baseline regime in which firing rate scales smoothly from brush through pressure to pinch or burn.

◦ Wind-up - the Rising Count - a used short-term regime in which one-per-second C-fibre stimulation makes each response two to five times larger over sixteen stimuli.

• PER-MODE TRANSFER FUNCTIONS.

◦ In the Loudness Dial rest is near minus 62 millivolts with background 0.1 to 5 spikes per second, output 0 to about 100 hertz, threshold near minus 45 millivolts.

◦ In the Rising Count each volley leaves a slow depolarisation that has not decayed before the next arrives, expelling the magnesium plug of the N-methyl-D-aspartate (NMDA) channel, windup increment 3 to 4 millivolts per volley saturating near 25 to 40 millivolts, decaying over 2 to 5 seconds; confidence high.

• THE TRANSITION TABLE.

◦ Repeated one-per-second noxious C-fibre volleys - Loudness Dial into Rising Count - over sixteen stimuli, decaying over 2 to 5 seconds of rest.

◦ Descending serotonin and noradrenaline set gain from 0.3 to 2.0, thrown from above.

• FAULT REGIMES AND WATCHDOGS.

◦ Persistent central sensitisation is a boundary regime underlying neuropathic pain; loss of diffuse noxious inhibitory control is measurable in fibromyalgia, and prolonged calcium loading can kill the measuring cell itself. The watchdog is the decay of the wind-up envelope back to baseline.


ENTRY 189. CLARKE COLUMN NEURONS, ALSO CALLED THE POSTERIOR THORACIC NUCLEUS (keyed to neuron catalogue Chapter 189)

MODES AND MODE TRANSITIONS:

This is a mode-poor high-fidelity relay, essentially a single always-faithful regime, because the cerebellum needs a continuous stream rather than a summary.

• THE MODE REGISTER.

◦ Tonic faithful following - the Faithful Relay - the one working regime, firing even when the limb is still so both stretch and shortening register as changes from a baseline, and following input into the hundreds of hertz.

• PER-MODE TRANSFER FUNCTIONS.

◦ In the Faithful Relay giant VGLUT1 boutons make transmission close to deterministic, firing 5 to 300 hertz, rest near minus 63 millivolts, threshold near minus 45 millivolts, length gain about 6 nanoamps per unit fractional length and velocity gain about 0.3; confidence high.

• THE TRANSITION TABLE.

◦ There is no discrete mode switch; a corollary-discharge copy of the descending motor command (an internal copy of the outgoing command) offsets the baseline continuously, adjusted from above.

• FAULT REGIMES AND WATCHDOGS.

◦ In Friedreich ataxia the feeding sensory neurons degenerate and Clarke cells atrophy and die, a boundary regime so consistent it defines the disease.

◦ Demyelination of the tract in multiple sclerosis or vitamin B12 deficiency slows the report and gives same-side sensory ataxia; the watchdog is conduction speed along the uncrossed tract.


ENTRY 190. LAMINA X NEURONS (Deep-Core Crossroads Neuron) (keyed to neuron catalogue Chapter 190)

MODES AND MODE TRANSITIONS:

This is a mixed convergence class carrying several firing patterns plus a genuine chemosensory regime in its fluid-contacting members, so it is moderately mode-rich for a poorly studied region.

• THE MODE REGISTER.

◦ Tonic firing - the Steady Merge - the commonest pattern (9 of 21 rat cells), merging gut and skin signals continuously.

◦ Transient, delayed or single-spike firing - the Brief Merge - onset-weighted patterns (transient 6, delayed 3, single-spiking 2).

◦ Fluid-sensing discharge - the Canal Taster - the chemosensory regime of cerebrospinal-fluid-contacting cells reading acidity and flow with slow 3.9 millisecond upstrokes.

• PER-MODE TRANSFER FUNCTIONS.

◦ Steady Merge tracks input at rest near minus 56 millivolts, input resistance 1000 megaohm, threshold near minus 40 millivolts.

◦ Brief Merge signals change and falls silent.

◦ Canal Taster reads pH 7.20 to 7.40 through PKD2L1 and acid-sensing ion channel 3, and flow through hair bending, then broadcasts peptides into the fluid; confidence moderate, few cells recorded.

• THE TRANSITION TABLE.

◦ A falling canal pH or wall bending - into the Canal Taster regime - membrane time constant 25 milliseconds, peptide output over seconds.

◦ Dorsal-root drive up to 200 hertz recruits the merging modes.

• FAULT REGIMES AND WATCHDOGS.

◦ Syringomyelia hollows this sleeve, a boundary regime giving cape-like loss of pain and temperature; the watchdog is the integrity of the peri-canal ring.


ENTRY 191. LISSAUER TRACT INTERNEURONS (Neighbouring-Segment Spreading Neuron) (keyed to neuron catalogue Chapter 191)

MODES AND MODE TRANSITIONS:

This is a two-mode local spreader, normally a presynaptic inhibitor that can flip into a backward-firing reflex, though its numbers are inferred rather than recorded in humans.

• THE MODE REGISTER.

◦ Presynaptic inhibition - the Neighbour Curb - the default regime, making axoaxonic contacts (onto another cell's output terminal) to damp arriving fibres and share input one or two segments away.

◦ Dorsal root reflex - the Backfire - a used regime in which strong drive flips the cell into firing backward into the skin.

• PER-MODE TRANSFER FUNCTIONS.

◦ In the Neighbour Curb reach is about 2 segments, relay fraction 0.45, maximum inhibition 0.6, conduction 0.3 to 1.0 metres per second; confidence low, bounded from the tract itself.

◦ In the Backfire a large primary-afferent depolarisation reverses the terminal and drives an antidromic volley into the periphery.

• THE TRANSITION TABLE.

◦ A large enough dorsal root potential (lasting 100 to 300 milliseconds) - Neighbour Curb into Backfire - within that window.

◦ A supernormal period up to 20 milliseconds after each impulse speeds conduction by 25 percent, briefly raising excitability from within.

◦ Descending serotonin biases spread from above.

• FAULT REGIMES AND WATCHDOGS.

◦ Excess spread widens the painful field beyond the injured dermatome, as in root avulsion, a boundary regime; the watchdog is the two-segment reach limit.


ENTRY 192. ROD PHOTORECEPTORS (keyed to neuron catalogue Chapter 192)

MODES AND MODE TRANSITIONS:

This is a graded non-spiking sensor whose modes are operating regimes of the dark current, and the literature treats dark-adapted single-photon detection and light-adapted background operation as genuinely distinct regimes.

• THE MODE REGISTER.

◦ Dark current, dark-adapted - the Single-Photon Listener - the resting scotopic regime in which the cell can announce one photon.

◦ Light-adapted gain reduction - the Daylight Restraint - a used regime in which sustained light lowers cascade gain so the rod does not simply saturate.

◦ Saturation - the Blinded Ceiling - reached in bright steady light where the circulating current is near zero; admitted because rods progressively escape it to contribute in daylight, so it is a normal boundary of operation.

• PER-MODE TRANSFER FUNCTIONS.

◦ Single-Photon Listener: rest about minus 40 millivolts on a dark current of 20 to 50 picoamperes, single-photon response about 1 picoampere, quantum efficiency 0.67; one rhodopsin switches on around 100 transducin molecules.

◦ Daylight Restraint: a fast phase within seconds desensitises up to 80-fold, a slow phase over tens of seconds engages only when more than half the dark current is suppressed; confidence high.

◦ Blinded Ceiling: response saturates above roughly 10,000 photoisomerisations per rod per second.

• THE TRANSITION TABLE.

◦ Rising background light closing cGMP-gated channels and driving arrestin into the outer segment - Single-Photon Listener to Daylight Restraint - seconds (fast) to tens of seconds (slow).

◦ Onset of darkness with falling calcium acting as a backward messenger - Daylight Restraint to Single-Photon Listener - full dark adaptation over 20 to 40 minutes.

◦ Circadian control from above also tunes rod dark adaptation.

• FAULT REGIMES AND WATCHDOGS.

◦ Rhodopsin mutations cause about a quarter of dominant retinitis pigmentosa, killing rods first and then starving cones, a boundary regime; the watchdog is calcium-driven cascade recovery.


ENTRY 193. CONE PHOTORECEPTORS (keyed to neuron catalogue Chapter 193)

MODES AND MODE TRANSITIONS:

This is a graded non-spiking sensor built never to saturate, so its modes are adaptation regimes spanning starlight-irrelevant dimness to full daylight, all in graded voltage.

• THE MODE REGISTER.

◦ Dark resting current - the Idle Detector - the baseline regime at low light, releasing glutamate steadily.

◦ Photopic gain control - the Daylight Worker - the working regime running calcium-dependent gain control within milliseconds so response is preserved across a vast intensity range.

• PER-MODE TRANSFER FUNCTIONS.

◦ Idle Detector: rest near minus 40 millivolts on a dark current of 20 to 40 picoamperes, response peak 20 to 50 milliseconds.

◦ Daylight Worker: hyperpolarises only to about minus 55 to minus 60 millivolts even in bright light, adapting over cone dark adaptation of 5 to 10 minutes, sensitivity about one hundredth of a rod; confidence high.

• THE TRANSITION TABLE.

◦ Rising light with calcium-dependent feedback and horizontal-cell feedback at the pedicle - Idle Detector to Daylight Worker - milliseconds for gain, minutes for full adaptation.

◦ Onset of darkness - Daylight Worker to Idle Detector - bleach recovery about 100 milliseconds, cone dark adaptation 5 to 10 minutes.

• FAULT REGIMES AND WATCHDOGS.

◦ Achromatopsia (total cone loss, roughly 1 in 30,000, usually CNGA3 or CNGB3 mutations) leaves night vision but removes colour and acuity, a boundary regime; the watchdog is calcium-dependent gain control keeping the cell out of saturation.


ENTRY 194. S-CONES, OR SHORT-WAVELENGTH CONES (Short-Wave Blue-Catching Cell) (keyed to neuron catalogue Chapter 194)

MODES AND MODE TRANSITIONS:

This is a graded non-spiking sensor electrically identical to any cone, so its modes are the same dark and light-adapted regimes tuned to a short-wavelength pigment, and it runs a slow channel.

• THE MODE REGISTER.

◦ Dark resting current - the Idle Blue Detector - baseline steady glutamate release.

◦ Photopic adapted signalling - the Slow Blue Worker - the light-adapted regime feeding the slowest colour channel.

• PER-MODE TRANSFER FUNCTIONS.

◦ Idle Blue Detector: rest near minus 40 millivolts, peak sensitivity near 419 to 426 nanometres, flash response peak 20 to 50 milliseconds.

◦ Slow Blue Worker: the blue-yellow channel resolves flicker only to about 10 hertz; confidence high for the electrical behaviour, which is ordinary cone physiology.

• THE TRANSITION TABLE.

◦ Rising short-wavelength light with H2 horizontal-cell feedback - Idle Blue Detector to Slow Blue Worker - milliseconds to seconds.

◦ Darkness returns the cell to baseline over the standard cone adaptation course.

• FAULT REGIMES AND WATCHDOGS.

◦ Tritan colour deficiency, from OPN1SW mutations (incidence near 1 in 500), removes blue-yellow discrimination, a boundary regime; the watchdog is exclusion from the connexin-36 network, which keeps the blue signal from being smeared into luminance.


ENTRY 195. M-CONES, OR MIDDLE-WAVELENGTH CONES (Middle-Wave Green-Catching Cell) (keyed to neuron catalogue Chapter 195)

MODES AND MODE TRANSITIONS:

This is a graded non-spiking sensor whose electrical modes are the ordinary cone dark and light-adapted regimes, differing from the L-cone only in which photons it absorbs.

• THE MODE REGISTER.

◦ Dark resting current - the Idle Green Detector - steady baseline release.

◦ Photopic adapted signalling - the Green Worker - the light-adapted daytime regime feeding red-green and luminance channels.

• PER-MODE TRANSFER FUNCTIONS.

◦ Idle Green Detector: rest near minus 40 millivolts, peak near 530 to 534 nanometres.

◦ Green Worker: fast responses peak in 20 to 50 milliseconds; the red-green channel resolves flicker to roughly 10 to 15 hertz, far slower than luminance; confidence high.

• THE TRANSITION TABLE.

◦ Rising light with H1 horizontal-cell feedback and gap-junction current from neighbours - Idle Green Detector to Green Worker - milliseconds to seconds.

◦ Darkness returns the cell to baseline over the standard cone adaptation course.

• FAULT REGIMES AND WATCHDOGS.

◦ Deuteranomaly and deuteranopia together affect about 6 percent of northern-European men, a boundary regime from unequal recombination in the X-linked opsin array; the watchdog is the private midget wiring that preserves the tiny M-versus-L difference.


ENTRY 196. L-CONES, OR LONG-WAVELENGTH CONES (Long-Wave Red-Catching Cell) (keyed to neuron catalogue Chapter 196)

MODES AND MODE TRANSITIONS:

This is a graded non-spiking sensor with the standard cone dark and light-adapted regimes, notable for feeding the fast luminance channel as well as red-green.

• THE MODE REGISTER.

◦ Dark resting current - the Idle Red Detector - steady baseline release.

◦ Photopic adapted signalling - the Fast Red Worker - the light-adapted regime serving both colour and the fast brightness signal.

• PER-MODE TRANSFER FUNCTIONS.

◦ Idle Red Detector: rest near minus 40 millivolts, peak near 555 to 560 nanometres.

◦ Fast Red Worker: flash response peaks in 20 to 50 milliseconds; luminance flicker is resolved to about 60 hertz, far faster than the red-green channel it also serves; confidence high.

• THE TRANSITION TABLE.

◦ Rising light with H1 feedback and neighbour gap-junction current - Idle Red Detector to Fast Red Worker - milliseconds to seconds.

◦ Darkness returns the cell to baseline over the standard cone adaptation course.

• FAULT REGIMES AND WATCHDOGS.

◦ Protanomaly and protanopia together affect roughly 2 percent of men; blue-cone monochromacy, silencing the whole L and M array (about 1 in 100,000), leaves acuity near 20/100 with nystagmus, a boundary regime. The watchdog is the stochastic one-gene-per-cone choice that keeps L and M cells distinct.


ENTRY 197. ROD BIPOLAR CELLS (keyed to neuron catalogue Chapter 197)

MODES AND MODE TRANSITIONS:

This is a graded non-spiking relay whose modes are signalling regimes set by background light, since it deliberately thresholds in darkness and re-linearises as light rises.

• THE MODE REGISTER.

◦ Thresholded scotopic regime - the Deaf Filter - the dim-light regime applying a nonlinear threshold to each rod input separately, discarding constant rod noise while keeping the rare single-photon event.

◦ Light-adapted linearised regime - the Open Ear - a used regime in which a dim background linearises the response-intensity slope, mediated by calcium entry.

• PER-MODE TRANSFER FUNCTIONS.

◦ Deaf Filter: resting potential minus 45 to minus 55 millivolts, light response 10 to 25 millivolts, threshold near one single-photon response, pooling 15 to 45 rods; the sign is flipped through the metabotropic mGluR6 to TRPM1 cascade, so the pathway is slow (onset 20 to 60 milliseconds, peak 100 to 200 milliseconds).

◦ Open Ear: a dim background linearises the slope so small intensity differences are reported; confidence moderate to high.

• THE TRANSITION TABLE.

◦ Rising background light with postsynaptic calcium entry - Deaf Filter to Open Ear - threshold readjusts over seconds to minutes.

◦ Onset of darkness - Open Ear back to Deaf Filter - seconds to minutes.

◦ Reciprocal A17 amacrine feedback shapes each terminal locally throughout.

• FAULT REGIMES AND WATCHDOGS.

◦ Complete congenital stationary night blindness from mutations in NYX, TRPM1, GRM6, GPR179 or LRIT3 is a boundary regime; the same signature appears when antibodies attack TRPM1 in melanoma-associated retinopathy. The watchdog is the mGluR6 cascade itself.


ENTRY 198. CONE BIPOLAR CELLS (keyed to neuron catalogue Chapter 198)

MODES AND MODE TRANSITIONS:

This is a graded non-spiking splitter whose overarching regime axis is polarity and kinetics, since the family fans one cone's voltage into parallel brightening, darkening, sustained and transient channels.

• THE MODE REGISTER.

◦ Graded sign-preserving or sign-inverting signalling - the Parallel Split - the working regime, each member reading the same cone and filing a differently-signed report.

◦ Terminal calcium-spike boosting - the Local Spark - a used regime in which calcium spikes occur in terminals even though the soma never fires.

• PER-MODE TRANSFER FUNCTIONS.

◦ Parallel Split: no somatic action potentials, graded voltage 10 to 20 millivolts around a rest near minus 50 to minus 60 millivolts, membrane time constant 10 to 30 milliseconds, polarity sign minus 1 for ON and plus 1 for OFF, gain 1 to 2.

◦ Local Spark: terminal calcium spikes sharpen and regenerate output locally; the transporter EAAT5 gives a chloride-current feedback within a millisecond; confidence moderate.

• THE TRANSITION TABLE.

◦ A change in cleft glutamate from the cone - into signalling of the appropriate sign - synaptic transfer 1 to 20 milliseconds.

◦ Slow adaptation averages over 100 to 1000 milliseconds, shifting the operating point.

◦ Horizontal-cell feedback (partly ephaptic, an electric-field effect with no transmitter) sets the surround.

• FAULT REGIMES AND WATCHDOGS.

◦ Loss of transmission from cones gives a negative electroretinogram with no b-wave, a boundary regime; dendritic retraction in retinitis pigmentosa disconnects the inner retina. The watchdog is the maintained cone-to-bipolar synapse.


ENTRY 199. ON BIPOLAR CELLS (Brightening-Signalling Neuron) (keyed to neuron catalogue Chapter 199)

MODES AND MODE TRANSITIONS:

This is a graded non-spiking sign-inverting relay, essentially a single biochemical-amplifier regime that strains against its own machinery in darkness and releases it in light.

• THE MODE REGISTER.

◦ Sign-inverting depolarising signalling - the Brightening Answer - the one working regime, in which a fall in cone glutamate becomes a rise in this cell's voltage.

• PER-MODE TRANSFER FUNCTIONS.

◦ Brightening Answer: graded, no somatic spikes, rest near minus 55 to minus 60 millivolts in darkness, depolarising 15 to 20 millivolts in light, half-activation glutamate constant near 0.2 normalised units; TRPM1 is open by default and held shut by G-protein activity, so the cascade adds 5 to 15 milliseconds of latency over the OFF pathway; confidence high.

• THE TRANSITION TABLE.

◦ Light reducing cone glutamate, releasing TRPM1 from G-protein suppression - dark rest into the Brightening Answer - cascade 20 to 50 milliseconds.

◦ Darkness restores suppression - Brightening Answer back to rest - similar timescale.

◦ Horizontal-cell ephaptic feedback and AII amacrine junctional current tune the response from the side.

• FAULT REGIMES AND WATCHDOGS.

◦ Mutations in GRM6, TRPM1, NYX, GPR179 or LRIT3 cause complete congenital stationary night blindness, a boundary regime flattening the electroretinogram b-wave, as do anti-TRPM1 antibodies in melanoma-associated retinopathy. The watchdog is nyctalopin anchoring TRPM1 in place.


ENTRY 200. OFF BIPOLAR CELLS (Darkening-Signalling Neuron) (keyed to neuron catalogue Chapter 200)

MODES AND MODE TRANSITIONS:

This is a graded non-spiking sign-conserving relay, the faster twin, with a working regime and a used desensitisation regime that turns a step into a pulse.

• THE MODE REGISTER.

◦ Sign-conserving signalling - the Darkening Answer - the working regime, hyperpolarising to light because ionotropic receptors pass the cone message unflipped.

◦ Desensitised transient signalling - the Pulse-Maker - a used regime in which receptor desensitisation converts a maintained step into a brief pulse.

◦ Terminal sodium spiking (some types) - the Terminal Tick - a subset generates tetrodotoxin-sensitive sodium spikes in the axon terminal.

• PER-MODE TRANSFER FUNCTIONS.

◦ Darkening Answer: rest near minus 45 to minus 50 millivolts because ionotropic channels are already partly open, answering 5 to 15 milliseconds sooner than the ON partner, opening time constant about 1 millisecond.

◦ Pulse-Maker: desensitisation strength 0.3 to 0.6, recovery 40 to 100 milliseconds.

◦ Terminal Tick: all-or-nothing events ride on the graded output; confidence moderate.

• THE TRANSITION TABLE.

◦ A maintained light step - Darkening Answer into Pulse-Maker - closing over 10 to 20 milliseconds, recovering over 40 to 100 milliseconds.

◦ Strong terminal depolarisation - into the Terminal Tick - milliseconds.

• FAULT REGIMES AND WATCHDOGS.

◦ No isolated human disease removes only OFF cells, but CACNA1F mutations (incomplete congenital stationary night blindness) degrade cone transmission to both ON and OFF cells, a boundary regime; the watchdog is the presynaptic calcium channel at the cone.


ENTRY 201. MIDGET BIPOLAR CELLS (keyed to neuron catalogue Chapter 201)

MODES AND MODE TRANSITIONS:

This is a graded non-spiking private line running one sustained regime, its single-cone input and mixed-cone surround creating colour opponency by wiring rather than by any mode switch.

• THE MODE REGISTER.

◦ Sustained one-cone signalling - the Private Whisper - the sole working regime, carrying one cone's centre against a mixed-cone surround with no fast desensitisation.

• PER-MODE TRANSFER FUNCTIONS.

◦ Private Whisper: graded and spikeless, rest near minus 50 millivolts, light response about 10 millivolts, input resistance several gigaohms so a handful of channels gives a large voltage change; surround weight 0.6 to 0.8, membrane time constant 10 to 20 milliseconds, sustained response; confidence high.

• THE TRANSITION TABLE.

◦ There is no discrete mode switch; H1 horizontal-cell surround feedback (partly ephaptic) shifts the operating point over 5 to 20 milliseconds, and the ON or OFF sign is fixed by receptor type.

• FAULT REGIMES AND WATCHDOGS.

◦ Loss in age-related macular degeneration and macular dystrophies is a boundary regime destroying central acuity permanently; in retinal remodelling the one-to-one line breaks so no prosthesis can reconstruct it. The watchdog is the maintained single-cone contact.


ENTRY 202. DIFFUSE BIPOLAR CELLS (keyed to neuron catalogue Chapter 202)

MODES AND MODE TRANSITIONS:

This is a graded non-spiking pooler whose modes span a sustained summing regime and strongly transient variants, plus a rod-borrowing regime through electrical coupling.

• THE MODE REGISTER.

◦ Sustained pooled signalling - the Choir Average - the baseline regime, summing 5 to 14 cones to buy signal-to-noise at the cost of detail.

◦ Transient high-pass signalling - the Motion Flick - a used regime in strongly transient types (DB2, DB3b) expressing the kainate subunit GluK1.

◦ Rod-relay coupling (ON types) - the Night Borrow - a used regime in which connexin-36 coupling to AII amacrine cells makes these the entry point for rod signals into daylight circuitry.

• PER-MODE TRANSFER FUNCTIONS.

◦ Choir Average: rest near minus 50 millivolts, responses 10 to 20 millivolts, convergence 6.2 to 12.5 cones by type, membrane time constant 10 to 15 milliseconds.

◦ Motion Flick: receptor desensitisation and vesicle depletion give a temporal high-pass over 50 to 200 milliseconds; some types add terminal sodium spikes.

◦ Night Borrow: junctional current from AII cells carries rod signal into the cone pathway; confidence moderate to high.

• THE TRANSITION TABLE.

◦ A maintained versus a changing stimulus - Choir Average to Motion Flick - 50 to 200 milliseconds, with adaptation over 100 to 1000 milliseconds.

◦ Falling light recruiting AII coupling - into Night Borrow - set by scotopic conditions and dopaminergic volume transmission from above.
