• FAULT REGIMES AND WATCHDOGS.

◦ Degeneration of these cells or their parasol targets, as in glaucoma, is a boundary regime causing early loss of motion and contrast sensitivity while letter acuity still reads normal; the watchdog is intact cone pooling.


ENTRY 203. BLUE-CONE BIPOLAR CELLS (keyed to neuron catalogue Chapter 203)

MODES AND MODE TRANSITIONS:

This is a graded non-spiking ON relay running a single slow sustained regime, selecting S-cones by identity and feeding the blue-yellow axis.

• THE MODE REGISTER.

◦ Sustained S-cone signalling - the Blue Reach - the sole working regime, depolarising when blue light increases and ignoring the L and M cones it reaches past.

• PER-MODE TRANSFER FUNCTIONS.

◦ Blue Reach: graded, spikeless, ON polarity, rest near minus 55 millivolts, slow sustained responses decaying over hundreds of milliseconds, S-cone convergence about 1.7, dendritic reach 30 to 80 micrometres; it receives feed-forward rather than feedback inhibition; confidence moderate to high.

• THE TRANSITION TABLE.

◦ Rising blue light reducing S-cone glutamate through the mGluR6 to TRPM1 cascade - dark rest into the Blue Reach - cascade 20 to 50 milliseconds, membrane 15 to 25 milliseconds.

◦ HII horizontal-cell feed-forward inhibition, maximal in darkness, sets the operating point.

• FAULT REGIMES AND WATCHDOGS.

◦ This pathway is among the first to weaken in glaucoma and diabetic retinopathy, a boundary regime detectable by short-wavelength automated perimetry (a blue-on-yellow field test) before standard testing; the watchdog is the maintained S-cone-specific contact.


ENTRY 204. GIANT BIPOLAR CELLS (Largest-Spread Two-Ended Neuron) (keyed to neuron catalogue Chapter 204)

MODES AND MODE TRANSITIONS:

This is a graded non-spiking wide coarse sampler running one analogue regime, never integrating to threshold and never firing, so any spiking model of it is simply wrong.

• THE MODE REGISTER.

◦ Sustained wide-field analogue signalling - the Broad Average - the sole working regime, reporting the average brightness of a patch by a weighted sum over a chosen subset of cones minus a horizontal-cell average.

• PER-MODE TRANSFER FUNCTIONS.

◦ Broad Average: graded voltage, rest near minus 45 to minus 35 millivolts in darkness moving only a few millivolts to a bright flash, input resistance 1 to 3 gigaohms, capacitance 3 to 8 picofarads, dendritic field 70 to 100 micrometres contacting about 52 percent (range 27 to 82 percent) of 15 to 20 cones in reach; Cav1.4 L-type calcium channels do not inactivate, allowing release to continue for hours; confidence moderate, the downstream target unsettled.

• THE TRANSITION TABLE.

◦ There is no discrete mode switch; the output passes through the saturating nonlinearity of calcium-driven ribbon release, with light adaptation shifting gain over tens of seconds.

◦ Presynaptic inhibition from gamma-aminobutyric acid (GABA)- and glycine-releasing amacrine cells sets terminal gain from below.

• FAULT REGIMES AND WATCHDOGS.

◦ In photoreceptor degeneration the dendrites lose their cone partners, retract and sprout, so the wide coarse channel first falls silent and is then corrupted by spontaneous inner-retinal activity, a boundary regime; the watchdog is the maintained sparse cone contact.


ENTRY 205. H1 CELLS (Red-Green Backdrop Neuron) (keyed to neuron catalogue Chapter 205)

MODES AND MODE TRANSITIONS:

This is a graded non-spiking horizontal cell whose modes are set by which of its two compartments is driving and by the light level, since its dendrites serve cones and its long axon terminal serves rods.

• THE MODE REGISTER.

◦ Photopic cone-feedback signalling - the Cone Backdrop - the daylight regime, averaging 6 to 18 L and M cones and pushing that average back as negative feedback.

◦ Scotopic rod-feedback signalling - the Rod Backdrop - a used regime at the axon terminal, active only in dim light below about 0.01 candela per square metre, serving rod spherules.

• PER-MODE TRANSFER FUNCTIONS.

◦ Cone Backdrop: never spikes, rest depolarised near minus 40 to minus 30 millivolts, hyperpolarising up to 20 to 30 millivolts roughly with the logarithm of intensity, ephaptic feedback under 1 millisecond and proton feedback over 100 to 300 milliseconds.

◦ Rod Backdrop: the electrically isolated axon terminal performs the same averaging on rods; the two compartments compute different things at once; confidence high.

• THE TRANSITION TABLE.

◦ Light falling below about 0.01 candela per square metre - Cone Backdrop to Rod Backdrop at the far compartment - set by ambient level.

◦ Dopamine changes gap-junction coupling, resizing the electrical sheet over 1 to 10 minutes, a modulatory service thrown partly from above.

• FAULT REGIMES AND WATCHDOGS.

◦ When feedback fails, as when connexin hemichannels are lost, the surround collapses and contrast sensitivity falls, a boundary regime; after photoreceptor death the cells sprout and wire wrongly. The watchdog is intact gap-junction and proton feedback.


ENTRY 206. H2 CELLS (Blue-Sampling Backdrop Neuron) (keyed to neuron catalogue Chapter 206)

MODES AND MODE TRANSITIONS:

This is a graded non-spiking horizontal cell running essentially one non-opponent regime despite blue-biased wiring, a warning that connectivity does not equal function.

• THE MODE REGISTER.

◦ Sustained luminosity signalling - the Blue-Fed Backdrop - the sole working regime, hyperpolarising to light of every wavelength and never inverting sign even though it seeks out S-cones.

• PER-MODE TRANSFER FUNCTIONS.

◦ Blue-Fed Backdrop: non-spiking, rest near minus 40 millivolts in darkness, hyperpolarising up to about 20 millivolts, sustained with no adaptation to zero; strong drive from 1 to 5 S-cones (weight about 0.7) plus weaker drive from 8 to 25 L and M cones (weight about 0.3) still summing to a monophasic response; membrane 8 to 15 milliseconds; confidence high.

• THE TRANSITION TABLE.

◦ There is no sign-flipping mode; feed-forward gamma-aminobutyric acid (GABA) onto blue-cone bipolar dendrites is maximal in darkness and released as light falls, over the proton-feedback timescale of 100 to 300 milliseconds.

◦ Dopamine modulates the private H2-only gap-junction network over 1 to 10 minutes.

• FAULT REGIMES AND WATCHDOGS.

◦ When S-cone circuits degrade early in glaucoma and diabetic retinopathy, blue-yellow discrimination fails first, producing the acquired tritan defect used clinically as an early warning; the watchdog is the maintained S-cone-biased wiring.


ENTRY 207. H3 CELLS (Broad-Sweep Backdrop Neuron) (keyed to neuron catalogue Chapter 207)

MODES AND MODE TRANSITIONS:

This is a graded non-spiking horizontal cell whose very existence is contested, so honesty requires an inferred single regime carried with a low-confidence existence flag.

• THE MODE REGISTER.

◦ Inferred sustained luminosity signalling - the Wide Backdrop - the presumed sole regime, a wider and deliberately lopsided version of the H1 cone backdrop that skips S-cones.

• PER-MODE TRANSFER FUNCTIONS.

◦ Wide Backdrop: no confirmed human recording exists, so physiology is inferred; by anatomy it should be non-spiking and graded, resting near minus 40 millivolts and hyperpolarising in light with a sustained, slightly slower and smoother response, dendritic tree about one third larger than a nearby H1 cell and asymmetric (axis ratio 1.5 to 2 in the periphery) so it computes an oriented spatial filter; confidence low.

• THE TRANSITION TABLE.

◦ There is no demonstrated mode switch; by analogy with H1 it should show ephaptic feedback under 1 millisecond, proton feedback over 100 to 300 milliseconds, and dopamine-modulated coupling over 1 to 10 minutes, all unverified for this type.

• FAULT REGIMES AND WATCHDOGS.

◦ The chief hazard is epistemic: the model must treat the type as a hypothesis with a switch, not a fact. If real, its loss would broaden and isotropise the outer-retinal surround; in photoreceptor degeneration it would retract and sprout like every horizontal cell.


ENTRY 208. AII AMACRINE CELLS (Night-Vision Splitting Neuron) (keyed to neuron catalogue Chapter 208)

MODES AND MODE TRANSITIONS:

This is a mode-rich hybrid device, chemical on one side and electrical on the other, with a genuine spikelet regime and a night-versus-day service split, making it one of the retina's true mode-machines.

• THE MODE REGISTER.

◦ Scotopic rod-splitting - the Night Splitter - the dim-light regime taking one rod-bipolar signal and dividing it two ways, electrically to ON cone bipolars and sign-inverted by glycine to OFF cone bipolars.

◦ Photopic crossover inhibition - the Daylight Corrector - a used daytime regime supplying glycine that stops OFF cone bipolars saturating at high light.

◦ Spikelet firing - the Little Booster - a regenerative regime firing small fast sodium spikelets (10 to 30 millivolts, one to two milliseconds) on the proximal dendrite to boost signals locally.

• PER-MODE TRANSFER FUNCTIONS.

◦ Night Splitter: rest near minus 60 millivolts, input resistance about 1 gigaohm, responding to a single photon in its rod pool with a one to two millivolt blip; glutamate from 25 to 30 rod-bipolar synapses onto alpha-amino-3-hydroxy-5-methyl-4-isoxazolepropionic acid (AMPA) receptors.

◦ Daylight Corrector: glycine release from lobular appendages onto OFF terminals, sign-inverting.

◦ Little Booster: spikelet threshold about minus 48 millivolts, resonance 10 to 100 hertz; confidence high.

• THE TRANSITION TABLE.

◦ Rising light shifting the retina from rod to cone circuitry - Night Splitter to Daylight Corrector - set by ambient level, with dopamine (0 to 100 nanomolar) and nitric oxide resizing the gap-junction sheet over 1 to 10 minutes, thrown partly from above.

◦ A suprathreshold local depolarisation - into the Little Booster - one to two milliseconds.

• FAULT REGIMES AND WATCHDOGS.

◦ After photoreceptor loss in retinitis pigmentosa the cell hyperpolarises, its intrinsic resonance is unmasked as an oscillation near 10 hertz, and that rhythm spreads through the coupled network to drive ganglion-cell hyperactivity that masks any restored light response, a boundary regime; the watchdog is connexin-36 phosphorylation, whose rise increases the coupling that carries the oscillation, which is why gap-junction blockers improve optogenetic vision restoration.


ENTRY 209. STARBURST AMACRINE CELLS (keyed to neuron catalogue Chapter 209)

MODES AND MODE TRANSITIONS:

This is a two-mode dendritic computer whose cell body barely spikes, so its regimes are graded-calcium states and one developmental rhythm, not firing patterns.

• THE MODE REGISTER.

◦ Adult graded direction mode - the Spoke Ledger - each dendrite integrates its own inputs and releases transmitter by local calcium to compute outward motion.

◦ Developmental wave mode - the Wave Maker - before eye opening the cell fires slow recurrent cholinergic bursts that sweep retinal waves across the sheet.

• PER-MODE TRANSFER FUNCTIONS.

◦ In the Spoke Ledger the input is glutamate spread along each dendrite and the output is per-varicosity calcium and dual transmitter release, with a direction selectivity index of 0.3 to 0.8 and resting voltage near minus 60 millivolts; there are no somatic spikes.

◦ In the Wave Maker the cell fires bursts driven by recurrent acetylcholine among neighbours and gated by a slow after-hyperpolarisation, launching a wave roughly once every 30 to 120 seconds.

• THE TRANSITION TABLE.

◦ Wave Maker to Spoke Ledger - triggered by developmental maturation and the switch to visually driven transmission around and after eye opening - timescale days to weeks, thrown from the developmental programme, not from moment-to-moment brain state.

◦ Gain trimming within the Spoke Ledger - triggered by dopamine and the day-night broadcast acting on the inner-retinal network - timescale minutes.

• FAULT REGIMES AND WATCHDOGS.

◦ Loss of FRMD7-dependent asymmetric wiring leaves inhibition symmetric, a boundary signature of failed direction computation; the watchdog is developmental activity, and its failure produces X-linked infantile nystagmus.


ENTRY 210. A17 AMACRINE CELLS (keyed to neuron catalogue Chapter 210)

MODES AND MODE TRANSITIONS:

This is a mode-poor graded cell, best seen as a thousand independent feedback loops that are either engaged for dim vision or stood down in daylight.

• THE MODE REGISTER.

◦ Night feedback mode - the Night Curb - each varicosity returns gamma-aminobutyric acid, known as GABA, to the rod bipolar terminal that just excited it, giving automatic gain control for starlight.

◦ Daylight stand-down mode - the Daylight Rest - in bright conditions dopamine uncouples the cells and the feedback synapses depress, so the loop quietens.

• PER-MODE TRANSFER FUNCTIONS.

◦ In the Night Curb the input is glutamate through calcium-permeable receptors and the output is graded GABA release per varicosity, swinging 10 to 40 millivolts from a rest near minus 60 millivolts, entirely without spikes.

◦ In the Daylight Rest the transfer is attenuated as synapses depress over minutes and gap-junction coupling falls, so private loops shrink and feedback weakens.

• THE TRANSITION TABLE.

◦ Night Curb to Daylight Rest - triggered by dopamine released from dopaminergic amacrine cells as ambient light rises - timescale minutes, thrown from above by the retinal day-night broadcast.

• FAULT REGIMES AND WATCHDOGS.

◦ When rods die in retinitis pigmentosa the loop remodels and breaks, the boundary signature being oversised sluggish rod responses; the watchdog is fast glial GABA clearance that keeps each varicosity private, and its failure blurs the thousand loops into one.


ENTRY 211. DOPAMINERGIC AMACRINE CELLS (keyed to neuron catalogue Chapter 211)

MODES AND MODE TRANSITIONS:

This is a mode-rich intrinsic pacemaker that spikes day and night and doubles as the retina's day-night gearbox, so its regimes are firing rates coupled to a hormone-like output.

• THE MODE REGISTER.

◦ Dark pacemaker mode - the Background Tick - the cell ticks at 3 to 10 spikes per second in complete darkness with no input, keeping the night circuitry engaged.

◦ Daylight broadcast mode - the Day Switch - light drives firing to 10 to 30 spikes per second and floods the tissue with dopamine, uncoupling gap junctions and installing high-acuity vision.

• PER-MODE TRANSFER FUNCTIONS.

◦ In the Background Tick the metronome is a slow calcium-activated potassium current after each spike, at a few hertz, with low tonic dopamine output.

◦ In the Day Switch glutamate from ON bipolar and melanopsin ganglion cells raises rate several-fold and dopamine reaches nanomolar to low micromolar in the surrounding tissue by volume transmission.

• THE TRANSITION TABLE.

◦ Background Tick to Day Switch - triggered by ambient light and by melanopsin ganglion cell drive - timescale seconds to minutes for firing, minutes for gap-junction uncoupling.

◦ Circadian bias - triggered by the internal clock through tyrosine hydroxylase control - period 24 hours, thrown from above so dopamine peaks by day and opposes melatonin at night.

• FAULT REGIMES AND WATCHDOGS.

◦ In Parkinson's disease the population falls by a quarter to a half, the boundary signature being lost contrast sensitivity, poor colour discrimination and disturbed sleep; the watchdog is antioxidant defence against oxidising dopamine by-products.


ENTRY 212. POLYAXONAL AMACRINE CELLS (keyed to neuron catalogue Chapter 212)

MODES AND MODE TRANSITIONS:

This is a two-mode spiking alarm cell, silent most of the time and firing a synchronised travelling wave when the whole image jumps.

• THE MODE REGISTER.

◦ Watch mode - the Quiet Watch - the cell rests nearly silent, sampling rectified bipolar input across a small dendritic field.

◦ Alarm mode - the Elsewhere Wave - a sudden global change fires sodium spikes that propagate outward along every axon at once, delivering long-range inhibition.

• PER-MODE TRANSFER FUNCTIONS.

◦ In the Quiet Watch input is glutamate from ON and OFF bipolar cells, output near zero, with strong transient tuning and an antagonistic surround.

◦ In the Elsewhere Wave the cell fires sparse transient bursts up to about 60 hertz that travel at roughly 0.5 metres per second, so distance becomes delay and gamma-aminobutyric acid (GABA) is dropped at thousands of distant varicosities.

• THE TRANSITION TABLE.

◦ Quiet Watch to Elsewhere Wave - triggered by a rapid, spatially broad contrast change - timescale milliseconds, with a refractory period of about 10 milliseconds before the next wave.

◦ Group size change - triggered by dopamine and nitric oxide modulating gap junctions - timescale minutes, thrown from above by the day-night broadcast.

• FAULT REGIMES AND WATCHDOGS.

◦ In advanced glaucoma and post-degeneration remodelling these cells fire spontaneous synchronised bursts, a boundary signature of flickering phantom images; the watchdog is one-way axonal conduction and glial GABA uptake at the far varicosities.


ENTRY 213. INTERPLEXIFORM CELLS (keyed to neuron catalogue Chapter 213)

MODES AND MODE TRANSITIONS:

This is a mode-poor state reporter carrying a slow average signal backwards from inner to outer retina, so its regimes are sustained output levels rather than fast firing patterns.

• THE MODE REGISTER.

◦ Dark low-output mode - the Dim Report - in prolonged darkness the cell releases little transmitter and leaves the first visual synapse broadly coupled.

◦ Light high-output mode - the Bright Report - sustained bright light raises output, and in the dopaminergic form closes horizontal cell gap junctions through second messengers.

• PER-MODE TRANSFER FUNCTIONS.

◦ In the Dim Report the dopaminergic form fires slow sustained spikes at a few hertz and the GABAergic form sits graded and quiet, with wide horizontal cell coupling downstream.

◦ In the Bright Report firing and transmitter output rise, dopamine acts on D1 receptors to raise cyclic adenosine monophosphate and uncouple horizontal cells, sharpening every nearby surround.

• THE TRANSITION TABLE.

◦ Dim Report to Bright Report - triggered by ambient light integrated over seconds and by inner-retinal drive - timescale seconds for firing, 30 seconds to several minutes for gap-junction uncoupling, with day-night effects over hours; largely thrown from above by the light-state broadcast.

• FAULT REGIMES AND WATCHDOGS.

◦ In Parkinson's disease the dopaminergic population shrinks by a quarter to a half, the boundary signature being lost contrast sensitivity and poor adaptation; the watchdog is glial clearance in the small outer-layer extracellular space that limits how far the state signal spreads.


ENTRY 214. MIDGET GANGLION CELLS (keyed to neuron catalogue Chapter 214)

MODES AND MODE TRANSITIONS:

This is a mode-poor sustained relay, a private-line colour-opponent channel with essentially one working regime plus a fast adaptive adjustment.

• THE MODE REGISTER.

◦ Sustained opponent mode - the Faithful Detail - the cell holds a maintained discharge and reports the red-green cone difference faithfully, the register of one being legitimate and worth stating proudly.

• PER-MODE TRANSFER FUNCTIONS.

◦ In the Faithful Detail the input is glutamate from one midget bipolar cell centrally and a few peripherally, the output true spikes on a maintained 10 to 20 spikes per second rising to about 150 hertz, with low contrast gain that keeps growing rather than saturating and temporal resolution falling above 10 to 20 hertz.

• THE TRANSITION TABLE.

◦ Contrast adaptation within the one mode - triggered by recent contrast - timescale hundreds of milliseconds, an internal gain adjustment rather than a true mode change.

◦ Day-night gain bias - triggered by dopamine acting on the inner retina - timescale minutes, thrown from above.

• FAULT REGIMES AND WATCHDOGS.

◦ In glaucoma the dendrites shrink and mitochondria fragment before the cell dies, and in mitochondrial optic neuropathies the thin papillomacular axons fail first, the boundary signature being a dense central blind spot; the watchdog is the nerve-fibre-layer astrocyte supply of lactate and potassium buffering to the bare intraretinal axon.


ENTRY 215. PARASOL GANGLION CELLS (keyed to neuron catalogue Chapter 215)

MODES AND MODE TRANSITIONS:

This is a two-regime transient alarm relay whose signature is a fast burst under continuous divisive gain control.

• THE MODE REGISTER.

◦ Maintained watch mode - the Idle Alarm - the cell rests near 5 to 20 spikes per second, pooling separately rectified bipolar subunits.

◦ Transient burst mode - the Wake-Up Flash - a change or movement drives a fast burst above 100 spikes per second that decays within 50 to 100 milliseconds even while the stimulus continues.

• PER-MODE TRANSFER FUNCTIONS.

◦ In the Idle Alarm input is summed rectified glutamate from dozens of diffuse bipolar cells and output is a low maintained rate with high contrast sensitivity, several times that of a midget cell.

◦ In the Wake-Up Flash the cell answers at twice the frequency of a mean-zero grating and follows flicker to 50 to 60 hertz, then divides its own gain within about 50 milliseconds to stay in range.

• THE TRANSITION TABLE.

◦ Idle Alarm to Wake-Up Flash - triggered by a rapid contrast or luminance change - timescale milliseconds, with transient decay over 50 to 100 milliseconds.

◦ Contrast gain control - triggered by a rise in recent contrast - timescale about 50 milliseconds, an internal divisive adjustment.

• FAULT REGIMES AND WATCHDOGS.

◦ In glaucoma these large cells shrink and are lost early, the boundary signature being lost low-contrast flicker and motion in the mid-periphery; the watchdog is the lamina cribrosa astrocyte mesh and lactate supply to the thick unmyelinated axon.


ENTRY 216. SMALL BISTRATIFIED GANGLION CELLS (keyed to neuron catalogue Chapter 216)

MODES AND MODE TRANSITIONS:

This is a mode-poor sluggish opponent relay carrying the blue-yellow colour dimension, with one working regime and a slow gain bias.

• THE MODE REGISTER.

◦ Blue-ON opponent mode - the Sky Difference - the cell adds short-wavelength blue drive to its inner tier and subtracts the long and middle wavelength yellow drive on its outer tier, reporting the difference.

• PER-MODE TRANSFER FUNCTIONS.

◦ In the Sky Difference the cell idles at a few spikes per second and reaches 50 to 100 for strong blue light, sluggish in time and coarse in space, receiving its blue input 10 to 20 milliseconds later than its yellow input so it compares blue now against yellow a moment ago.

• THE TRANSITION TABLE.

◦ Day-night gain reset - triggered by dopamine released into the fluid by volume transmission - timescale minutes, thrown from above by the retinal light-state broadcast.

• FAULT REGIMES AND WATCHDOGS.

◦ It is among the first casualties of glaucoma, the boundary signature being blue-yellow, or tritan, loss detectable by short-wavelength perimetry before standard fields change, with further decline in diabetes and ageing; there is no notable fault regime the healthy cell itself uses, and the honest statement is that this is a single-mode channel whose main risk is early death.


ENTRY 217. INTRINSICALLY PHOTOSENSITIVE RETINAL GANGLION CELLS (keyed to neuron catalogue Chapter 217)

MODES AND MODE TRANSITIONS:

This is a mode-rich slow integrator that is photoreceptor, computer and transmitter in one, summing a fast synaptic estimate of brightness with its own very slow melanopsin signal.

• THE MODE REGISTER.

◦ Sub-threshold quiet mode - the Night Hold - below daylight the insensitive melanopsin stays quiet and the cell rides only the fast rod and cone drive.

◦ Sustained irradiance mode - the Daylight Gauge - bright blue-rich light drives a slow sustained few to few-tens of spikes per second that reports total light averaged over minutes.

◦ Persistent after-light mode - the Long Afterglow - the intrinsic response continues many seconds after light stops, holding the pupil and the clock signal.

• PER-MODE TRANSFER FUNCTIONS.

◦ In the Night Hold output is set by ON bipolar glutamate carrying the rod and cone brightness estimate, with melanopsin below threshold near log irradiance 11.

◦ In the Daylight Gauge melanopsin activates the Gq cascade and opens canonical channels, giving a sustained 0 to 20 spikes per second plus slow peptide release.

◦ In the Long Afterglow the cascade decays slowly over 10 to 30 seconds so firing outlasts the stimulus.

• THE TRANSITION TABLE.

◦ Night Hold to Daylight Gauge - triggered by irradiance crossing threshold near log 11 - timescale 1 to 5 seconds rise.

◦ Daylight Gauge to Long Afterglow - triggered by light offset - timescale 10 to 30 seconds decay, with light-history adaptation over hours to days.

• FAULT REGIMES AND WATCHDOGS.

◦ Oversensitivity produces the light aversion of migraine, and loss of about 31 percent of these cells by age 70, worse in Alzheimer and Parkinson disease, breaks circadian entrainment; the watchdog is bistable melanopsin, reset by long-wavelength light.


ENTRY 218. DIRECTION-SELECTIVE GANGLION CELLS (keyed to neuron catalogue Chapter 218)

MODES AND MODE TRANSITIONS:

This is a two-state dendritic voting machine whose regime is set by the direction of motion, computed by timed shunting inhibition before any spike leaves.

• THE MODE REGISTER.

◦ Preferred-direction mode - the Green Light - motion the right way escapes the null-side veto and the cell bursts.

◦ Null-direction mode - the Red Light - motion the wrong way arrives with timed starburst gamma-aminobutyric acid (GABA) that shunts the drive and the cell falls almost silent.

• PER-MODE TRANSFER FUNCTIONS.

◦ In the Green Light glutamate plus untuned acetylcholine drive 30 to 100 spikes per second, since the inhibition is mistimed relative to the passing edge.

◦ In the Red Light null-side shunting GABA, in a roughly three-to-one ratio over excitation, divides the drive so only a small fraction of preferred firing survives, giving a direction selectivity index of 0.3 to 0.8.

• THE TRANSITION TABLE.

◦ Green Light to Red Light - triggered by reversal of stimulus motion direction - timescale the transit time of the edge, with inhibition rising in 5 to 20 milliseconds, computed locally in dendrites rather than thrown from above.

• FAULT REGIMES AND WATCHDOGS.

◦ Mutation of FRMD7 removes the asymmetric inhibition onto horizontally tuned cells, a boundary signature of abolished horizontal optokinetic reflex and infantile nystagmus; the watchdog is fast glial GABA clearance that keeps the null-side veto sharp.


ENTRY 219. SMOOTH MONOSTRATIFIED, LARGE SPARSE, BROAD THORNY, AND RECURSIVE BISTRATIFIED GANGLION CELLS (keyed to neuron catalogue Chapter 219)

MODES AND MODE TRANSITIONS:

These four rare wide-field types share a nonlinear subunit design, so each runs a quiet baseline and a transient response, and the group is best treated as mode-poor transient reporters.

• THE MODE REGISTER.

◦ Baseline pooling mode - the Wide Idle - each cell rectifies many small bipolar subunits and rests at a low maintained rate.

◦ Transient report mode - the Coarse Flash - a change in the wide field drives a strongly transient burst that then stops.

• PER-MODE TRANSFER FUNCTIONS.

◦ In the Wide Idle the smooth monostratified cell rectifies before summing, so a fine grating averaging to zero still leaves a standing drive, with preferred temporal frequency about 13 hertz.

◦ In the Coarse Flash the smooth monostratified cell peaks near 50 spikes per second at latency 38 to 90 milliseconds, and the broad thorny cell answers edges crossing its centre at 4 to 12 degrees per second while a full-field drift almost silences it, its surround suppression index reaching 0.86.

• THE TRANSITION TABLE.

◦ Wide Idle to Coarse Flash - triggered by local motion or an object slipping across the centre - timescale tens of milliseconds, with transient adaptation over 50 to 200 milliseconds.

◦ Day-night gain bias - triggered by diffuse dopamine - timescale minutes, thrown from above.

• FAULT REGIMES AND WATCHDOGS.

◦ These large cells are disproportionately lost early in glaucoma, the boundary signature being lost coarse peripheral motion and texture while reading acuity survives; the watchdog is tight glial ensheathment of individual bipolar terminals that keeps the subunits separate and the response nonlinear.


ENTRY 220. OLFACTORY SENSORY NEURONS (keyed to neuron catalogue Chapter 220)

MODES AND MODE TRANSITIONS:

This is a genuine three-mode sensor, and unusually it uses depolarisation block as a normal working regime, so that block belongs in the register rather than among the faults.

• THE MODE REGISTER.

◦ Silent mode - the Clean-Air Rest - below the odorant threshold the cell is quiet, firing rarely.

◦ Tonic firing mode - the Odour Report - within its concentration band the cell fires up to about 30 spikes per second, coding odorant presence.

◦ Functional depolarisation block mode - the Overload Hush - at concentrations about a thousandfold above threshold sodium channels slowly inactivate and the high-affinity cell falls silent, sparsening the population code at high concentration.

• PER-MODE TRANSFER FUNCTIONS.

◦ In the Clean-Air Rest the ciliary cyclic adenosine monophosphate cascade is idle and current is near zero against an input resistance of several gigaohms.

◦ In the Odour Report bound-receptor fraction drives the cascade, calcium opens the ANO2 chloride channel that supplies up to 90 percent of the current, and firing rises with concentration; confidence in the exact rate is moderate.

◦ In the Overload Hush the spike generator is inhibited by slow sodium-channel inactivation, so firing collapses even as odour rises, which defines the cell's upper receptive-field edge.

• THE TRANSITION TABLE.

◦ Clean-Air Rest to Odour Report - triggered by odorant binding at 1 nanomolar to 100 micromolar - timescale transduction 150 to 500 milliseconds.

◦ Odour Report to Overload Hush - triggered by very high odorant concentration - timescale within the stimulus, a self-imposed cellular limit rather than a brain-state command.

◦ Adaptation within the Odour Report - triggered by calcium-calmodulin desensitisation and NCKX4 clearance - timescale 0.5 to 5 seconds.

• FAULT REGIMES AND WATCHDOGS.

◦ Viral loss of supporting cells causes anosmia and cribriform shear cuts the axons; the watchdog is the sustentacular cell, this neuron's private detoxifying liver, and monthly replacement of the whole population.


ENTRY 221. MITRAL CELLS (keyed to neuron catalogue Chapter 221)

MODES AND MODE TRANSITIONS:

This is a mode-rich oscillatory output cell that flips between a quiet down-state and an active oscillating up-state and reports odour late but sharply.

• THE MODE REGISTER.

◦ Down-state mode - the Quiet Floor - the cell rests near minus 60 millivolts, below threshold and silent.

◦ Oscillating up-state mode - the Metronome Up - persistent sodium current opposed by potassium rings the membrane at 10 to 50 hertz, gating spike timing.

◦ Sniff-locked firing mode - the Late Report - glomerular drive during a sniff produces spikes at a longer latency and lower rate than tufted cells, sharpened by a strong inhibitory surround.

• PER-MODE TRANSFER FUNCTIONS.

◦ In the Quiet Floor there is no output and the cell awaits drive.

◦ In the Metronome Up subthreshold oscillation at 20 to 100 millisecond periods sets when a spike can occur.

◦ In the Late Report output is glutamate at 0 to 80 hertz to olfactory cortex in one synaptic step, curbed by granule cell dendrodendritic inhibition lasting 50 to several hundred milliseconds.

• THE TRANSITION TABLE.

◦ Quiet Floor to Metronome Up - triggered by glomerular and external tufted drive crossing threshold near minus 45 millivolts - timescale tens of milliseconds.

◦ Firing shaped by state - triggered by acetylcholine and noradrenaline from the basal forebrain and locus coeruleus - timescale sniff-to-sniff, thrown from above by arousal and sleep-wake state.

• FAULT REGIMES AND WATCHDOGS.

◦ Alpha-synuclein reaches the bulb at Braak stage 1 of Parkinson's disease so smell fails years before tremor, the boundary signature being early hyposmia; the watchdog is tight astrocytic glutamate clearance at the reciprocal synapse that fixes the width of lateral inhibition.


ENTRY 222. TUFTED CELLS (keyed to neuron catalogue Chapter 222)

MODES AND MODE TRANSITIONS:

This is a two-mode early-reporting output cell whose external tufted subtype is a genuine intrinsic oscillator, so it computes time as well as relaying odour.

• THE MODE REGISTER.

◦ Intrinsic bursting mode - the Glomerular Pacemaker - external tufted cells burst at 1 to 8 hertz with no input, driven by persistent sodium and hyperpolarisation-activated currents.

◦ Odour-driven early report mode - the First Word - low-threshold high-input-resistance firing at fainter odours and tens of milliseconds earlier in the sniff than mitral cells, so phase lead encodes odour strength.

• PER-MODE TRANSFER FUNCTIONS.

◦ In the Glomerular Pacemaker the cell fires three to ten spikes per burst at 5 to 20 millisecond intervals, period 125 to 1000 milliseconds, and one such cell can ignite every mitral cell in its glomerulus.

◦ In the First Word direct olfactory sensory neuron glutamate at 0 to 100 hertz drives high-rate output to nearby targets, with short-term depression giving automatic gain control across successive sniffs.

• THE TRANSITION TABLE.

◦ Glomerular Pacemaker to First Word - triggered by direct nose input arriving on the burst cycle - timescale within one sniff, roughly 125 to 1000 milliseconds.

◦ Depression recovery - triggered by rest between sniffs - timescale 200 to 1000 milliseconds.

• FAULT REGIMES AND WATCHDOGS.

◦ Runaway synchrony is the danger of a constant burster, the boundary signature being seizure-like glomerular activity; the watchdog is glomerular astrocyte potassium uptake, and loss of the whole channel simply raises the detection threshold so faint odours go unreported.


ENTRY 223. PERIGLOMERULAR CELLS (keyed to neuron catalogue Chapter 223)

MODES AND MODE TRANSITIONS:

This is a mode-poor inhibitory gain-setter for one odour channel, essentially a tonic-plus-driven interneuron.

• THE MODE REGISTER.

◦ Tonic guard mode - the Doorway Hush - the cell holds a low background inhibition on its single glomerulus.

◦ Driven suppression mode - the Volume Turn-Down - stronger glomerular drive raises its gamma-aminobutyric acid (GABA) output both presynaptically on sensory terminals and postsynaptically on output cells.

• PER-MODE TRANSFER FUNCTIONS.

◦ In the Doorway Hush the small uniglomerular cell releases GABA at a low rate from a single bushy dendrite that is both ear and mouth.

◦ In the Volume Turn-Down glutamate from nose axons or external tufted cells drives graded, intensity-scaled inhibition that stops a hundredfold louder odour from saturating the channel, keeping the suppression within one channel.

• THE TRANSITION TABLE.

◦ Doorway Hush to Volume Turn-Down - triggered by rising drive within the parent glomerulus - timescale milliseconds to tens of milliseconds.

◦ Gain bias - triggered by dopamine and how much the nostril is used - timescale hours.

• FAULT REGIMES AND WATCHDOGS.

◦ There is no notable pathological regime the healthy cell adopts; its failure simply removes per-channel gain control so a strong odour saturates the output, and the honest statement is that this is a small, robust, two-state gain device.


ENTRY 224. SHORT-AXON CELLS, INCLUDING SUPERFICIAL SHORT-AXON CELLS (keyed to neuron catalogue Chapter 224)

MODES AND MODE TRANSITIONS:

This is a two-signal co-transmitting interneuron that writes a fast negative and a slow positive onto one wire, and its deep Blanes variant adds a bistable persistent regime formalised in its own entry.

• THE MODE REGISTER.

◦ Tonic interglomerular mode - the Neighbour Watch - superficial cells fire tonically at a few hertz and knit distant glomeruli into a contrast network.

◦ Biphasic co-transmission mode - the Turn-Down-Then-Up - a burst delivers fast gamma-aminobutyric acid (GABA) inhibition followed by slower dopamine excitation onto distant external tufted cells.

• PER-MODE TRANSFER FUNCTIONS.

◦ In the Neighbour Watch the cell tracks odour input with sustained firing at 1 to 10 hertz, contacting from 5 to several hundred glomeruli.

◦ In the Turn-Down-Then-Up GABA type A inhibition acts within 10 to 30 milliseconds and dopamine D1-like excitation follows with onset 200 to 500 milliseconds, so one presynaptic burst carries two opposite instructions separated in time.

• THE TRANSITION TABLE.

◦ Neighbour Watch to Turn-Down-Then-Up - triggered by a burst of external tufted drive - timescale milliseconds for GABA, hundreds of milliseconds for dopamine.

◦ Dopamine tone bias - triggered by how much the nostril is used - timescale hours.

• FAULT REGIMES AND WATCHDOGS.

◦ Without interglomerular normalisation a target odour cannot be found against a strong background, a boundary signature reported in post-viral smell disorder; the watchdog is astrocytic dopamine uptake, since the bulb has few dopamine transporters, which sets how far one cell reaches chemically.


ENTRY 225. OLFACTORY BULB GRANULE CELLS (keyed to neuron catalogue Chapter 225)

MODES AND MODE TRANSITIONS:

This is a two-mode axonless dendritic computer, running each giant spine as a local unit or firing one global spike that arms every spine at once.

• THE MODE REGISTER.

◦ Local spine mode - the Private Whisper - a single gemmule fires a dendritic spike on its own N-methyl-D-aspartate receptors and low-voltage calcium channels and releases gamma-aminobutyric acid (GABA) back to its one mitral partner, without the soma acting.

◦ Global spike mode - the Whole-Cell Sweep - a somatic spike travels up the dendrite and enables release at every gemmule together.

• PER-MODE TRANSFER FUNCTIONS.

◦ In the Private Whisper input is glutamate at one gemmule at 0 to 80 hertz and output is recurrent and lateral GABA at that spine only, computing local contrast.

◦ In the Whole-Cell Sweep the global spike broadcasts inhibition across hundreds to thousands of gemmules, building the bulb's 25 millisecond gamma cycle.

• THE TRANSITION TABLE.

◦ Private Whisper to Whole-Cell Sweep - triggered by summed spine drive crossing the somatic threshold, and by cortical feedback on basal dendrites - timescale synchronous GABA within 10 milliseconds, asynchronous release 100 to 1000 milliseconds.

◦ State modulation - triggered by acetylcholine, noradrenaline and serotonin - timescale seconds, thrown from above.

• FAULT REGIMES AND WATCHDOGS.

◦ Weakened granule inhibition widens odour tuning, the boundary signature being parosmia after viral injury; the watchdog is close astrocytic wrapping of the reciprocal synapse that sets how long glutamate lingers and thus how much GABA returns.


ENTRY 226. BLANES CELLS (keyed to neuron catalogue Chapter 226)

MODES AND MODE TRANSITIONS:

This is a genuine bistable switch, the deep short-axon cell that latches into minutes of self-sustained firing and thereby holds the bulb's short-term smell memory.

• THE MODE REGISTER.

◦ Reactive silent mode - the Cold Switch - at rest near minus 65 millivolts the cell only responds while driven.

◦ Persistent firing mode - the Latched Hold - a brief barrage opens a calcium-activated non-selective cation current, called I-CAN, whose afterdepolarisation sustains firing for minutes.

• PER-MODE TRANSFER FUNCTIONS.

◦ In the Cold Switch glutamate from mitral and tufted collaterals produces only transient firing.

◦ In the Latched Hold the cell fires persistently at 1 to 15 hertz, releasing gamma-aminobutyric acid (GABA) onto granule cells so that silencing the silencers disinhibits the output cells, until a hyperpolarising step switches it off.

• THE TRANSITION TABLE.

◦ Cold Switch to Latched Hold - triggered by a brief excitatory barrage admitting calcium - timescale onset within hundreds of milliseconds, persistent epoch tens of seconds to minutes.

◦ Latched Hold to Cold Switch - triggered by a hyperpolarising, GABAergic reset step of 5 to 20 millivolts - timescale immediate.

◦ Gain of the latch - triggered by acetylcholine and noradrenaline scaling peak I-CAN between 0.5 and 2 - timescale seconds, thrown from above.

• FAULT REGIMES AND WATCHDOGS.

◦ Weak I-CAN costs the bulb its odour memory and may feed the early smell loss of Parkinson and Alzheimer disease, while over-active I-CAN silences granule cells and lets bulb output run away; the watchdog is astrocytic siphoning of the continuous potassium this minutes-long firing sheds.


ENTRY 227. MIGRATORY GONADOTROPIN-RELEASING HORMONE NEURONS (keyed to neuron catalogue Chapter 227)

MODES AND MODE TRANSITIONS:

This is a mode-rich neuroendocrine cell whose life has a developmental migratory phase and then a secretory pulse generator, and whose output message is carried by the interval between pulses.

• THE MODE REGISTER.

◦ Migratory mode - the Long Walk - in the embryo the cell crawls from nasal placode into the hypothalamus, a regime before any electrical duty.

◦ Baseline quiescent mode - the Slow Idle - the mature cell fires slowly and irregularly at under one spike per second between pulses.

◦ Synchronised pulse mode - the Feeding Pulse - every 60 to 360 minutes the population bursts for one to five minutes and empties gonadotropin-releasing hormone, known as GnRH, into portal blood.

• PER-MODE TRANSFER FUNCTIONS.

◦ In the Long Walk output is cytoskeletal movement, not spikes, over about ten weeks in the human embryo.

◦ In the Slow Idle spikes begin 90 micrometres out along the dendron and secretion is minimal.

◦ In the Feeding Pulse a burst releases a hormone pulse whose interval sets the message, since fast pulses near 60 to 95 minutes favour luteinising hormone and slow pulses of four to six hours favour follicle-stimulating hormone.

• THE TRANSITION TABLE.

◦ Slow Idle to Feeding Pulse - triggered by kisspeptin from KNDy neurons at 0.2 to 1 per hour - timescale burst 1 to 5 minutes, thrown by the KNDy pulse generator and biased by circulating oestradiol, progesterone and testosterone.

◦ Interpulse retiming - triggered by menstrual-cycle steroid feedback - timescale the cycle, so the interval shortens near ovulation and lengthens in the luteal phase.

• FAULT REGIMES AND WATCHDOGS.

◦ Failed migration gives Kallmann syndrome, anosmia plus congenital hypogonadotropic hypogonadism, the boundary signature being neurons arrested in a tangle above the cribriform plate; the watchdogs are olfactory ensheathing cells that escort the migration and tanycytes that gate terminal access to portal blood.


ENTRY 228. TYPE I SPIRAL GANGLION NEURONS (keyed to neuron catalogue Chapter 228)

MODES AND MODE TRANSITIONS:

This is a mode-rich stochastic relay whose resting regime is set at birth by a spontaneous-rate class and whose driven behaviour is an onset burst settling to an adapted, phase-locked rate.

• THE MODE REGISTER.

◦ Spontaneous-rate class - the Idle Chatter - each fibre holds a fixed background, whispering below one spike per second in low-rate fibres or chattering at 40 to 80 in high-rate fibres, splitting quiet and loud across separate channels.

◦ Onset-and-adapt driven mode - the Opening Volley - sound gives a burst of several hundred spikes per second that settles within milliseconds to a sustained 100 to 250.

◦ Phase-locked mode - the Wave-Rider - up to about 4 to 5 kilohertz the spikes lock to the phase of the tone, above which only the envelope is followed.

• PER-MODE TRANSFER FUNCTIONS.

◦ In the Idle Chatter timing is stochastic, driven by random vesicle arrivals at one ribbon, with the class fixed by molecular subtype and synapse position.

◦ In the Opening Volley a readily releasable pool empties then refills over 10 to 50 milliseconds, giving rapid adaptation of 1 to 5 milliseconds and short-term adaptation of 40 to 100 milliseconds.

◦ In the Wave-Rider spike phase tracks the fine structure of the wave to within tens of microseconds, subject to the 0.3 to 0.7 millisecond refractory period.

• THE TRANSITION TABLE.

◦ Idle Chatter to Opening Volley - triggered by transmitter release from the one inner hair cell - timescale sub-millisecond onset.

◦ Gain trim - triggered by lateral olivocochlear efferent release of dopamine, acetylcholine and peptides - modelled as a gain scalar of 0.5 to 1.2, thrown from the brainstem.

• FAULT REGIMES AND WATCHDOGS.

◦ Noise or ageing destroys ribbon synapses and low-spontaneous-rate fibres go first, the boundary signature being cochlear synaptopathy, or hidden hearing loss, with a normal audiogram but poor speech in noise; the watchdog is supporting-cell glutamate clearance that prevents excitotoxic swelling of the terminal.


ENTRY 229. TYPE II SPIRAL GANGLION NEURONS (keyed to neuron catalogue Chapter 229)

MODES AND MODE TRANSITIONS:

This is a two-mode cochlear nociceptor, nearly silent to ordinary sound and firing only when the organ is being hurt, so it is a switch rather than a graded relay.

• THE MODE REGISTER.

◦ Near-silent watch mode - the Deaf Sentinel - the cell stays quiet at conversational levels because each of its many outer hair cell inputs is tiny.

◦ Damage-driven mode - the Harm Alarm - injury and very loud sound, and the adenosine triphosphate spilled from damaged tissue, drive sparse firing.

• PER-MODE TRANSFER FUNCTIONS.

◦ In the Deaf Sentinel hundreds of inputs must arrive together to reach a high threshold, so spontaneous activity is very low or absent.

◦ In the Harm Alarm summed glutamate from 10 to 100 outer hair cells plus purinergic drive from extracellular adenosine triphosphate produces modest firing of single figures to a few tens of spikes per second, spatially integrated over up to a millimetre of cochlea.

• THE TRANSITION TABLE.

◦ Deaf Sentinel to Harm Alarm - triggered by tissue damage releasing adenosine triphosphate onto P2X receptors - timescale seconds to minutes as the damage signal builds.

◦ Alarm gating - triggered by inhibitory gamma-aminobutyric acid from lateral olivocochlear efferents - timescale of the reflex, letting the brain mute its own damage alarm.

• FAULT REGIMES AND WATCHDOGS.

◦ If it fails, painfully loud sound stops feeling painful and protective reflexes weaken, the boundary signature being continued exposure of an ear already being destroyed; the watchdog is the Deiters supporting cell that sets both the chemical and mechanical environment of the fibre.


ENTRY 230. VESTIBULAR GANGLION NEURONS, ALSO CALLED SCARPA GANGLION NEURONS (keyed to neuron catalogue Chapter 230)

MODES AND MODE TRANSITIONS:

This is a mode-rich tonically active afferent whose high resting rate is itself a computation, turning a one-directional sensor into a bidirectional signal, with the calyx adding a second physics of non-quantal transmission.

• THE MODE REGISTER.

◦ Resting tonic mode - the Never-Silent Hum - the fibre idles at roughly 60 to 100 spikes per second even with the head still.

◦ Excitatory modulation mode - the Speed-Up - hair-bundle deflection in the on direction raises the rate toward several hundred spikes per second.

◦ Inhibitory cutoff mode - the Ewald Floor - deflection the other way slows and can silence the fibre, since firing cannot go below zero.

• PER-MODE TRANSFER FUNCTIONS.

◦ In the Never-Silent Hum the resting rate is held by quantal glutamate and, at calyces, by direct potassium accumulation in the sealed cleft.

◦ In the Speed-Up rotational sensitivity is 0.2 to 2 spikes per second per degree per second, saturating near 300 to 400.

◦ In the Ewald Floor the fibre bottoms out at zero, which is why the opposite ear must carry the strong-inhibition signal.

• THE TRANSITION TABLE.

◦ Hum to Speed-Up or Ewald Floor - triggered by head motion deflecting the bundle - timescale membrane 1 to 10 milliseconds, canal cupula 4 to 6 seconds, non-quantal transmission under one millisecond.

◦ Gain and regularity retune - triggered by cholinergic efferents reducing KCNQ, also called Kv7, conductance at the calyx - timescale under a second, thrown from the brainstem, for example during self-commanded head movement.

• FAULT REGIMES AND WATCHDOGS.

◦ Sudden unilateral loss, as in vestibular neuritis, leaves the two sides unbalanced and produces violent vertigo until central compensation rebalances over days to weeks; the watchdog is central compensation, plus the Schwann cells of the Obersteiner and Redlich zone, whose disorder gives vestibular schwannoma.


ENTRY 231. REGULAR-FIRING VESTIBULAR AFFERENTS (keyed to neuron catalogue Chapter 231)

MODES AND MODE TRANSITIONS:

This is a proudly mode-poor cell, a single metronomic tonic regime whose whole value is the evenness of its firing, so a register of one is the honest answer.

• THE MODE REGISTER.

◦ Metronomic tonic mode - the Faithful Metronome - a steady 60 to 100 spikes per second with a normalised coefficient of variation below 0.1, tracking head position and slow velocity linearly.

• PER-MODE TRANSFER FUNCTIONS.

◦ In the Faithful Metronome many small bouton inputs are averaged, giving low rotational gain of 0.2 to 0.5 spikes per second per degree per second and slow adaptation, with excellent thresholds near 4 degrees per second because a small change stands out against the quiet background; the transfer is tonic, tracking the stimulus itself rather than its rate of change.

• THE TRANSITION TABLE.

◦ Operating-point shift within the one mode - triggered by weak cholinergic efferent drive - timescale seconds, thrown from the brainstem, and by design it shifts gain without destroying regularity.

◦ Canal versus otolith dynamics - a structural difference rather than a switchable mode, since canal fibres inherit the 4 to 6 second cupula fade while otolith fibres hold steady through a sustained tilt.

• FAULT REGIMES AND WATCHDOGS.

◦ Selective loss of type two hair cells or these fibres degrades low-frequency and static balance, the boundary signature being drift and unsteadiness worst in the dark; the watchdog is spatial averaging over many independent bouton inputs, which is the noise-reduction that makes the metronome reliable.


ENTRY 232. IRREGULAR-FIRING VESTIBULAR AFFERENTS (keyed to neuron catalogue Chapter 232)

MODES AND MODE TRANSITIONS:

This is a two-mode phasic emergency line, noisy at rest and wildly sensitive to fast transients, and it is the vestibular class most retuned by the brain's own efferents.

• THE MODE REGISTER.

◦ Irregular resting mode - the Restless Idle - a background of 60 to 100 spikes per second whose intervals scatter, with a normalised coefficient of variation above 0.1 and often above 0.3.

◦ Phasic transient mode - the Sudden-Jolt - a fast head movement drives a high-gain burst that adapts away within tens to hundreds of milliseconds.

• PER-MODE TRANSFER FUNCTIONS.

◦ In the Restless Idle a large low-voltage-activated potassium conductance, principally KCNQ with Kv1, makes the cell phasic, so it prefers to fire once at a change; slow-rotation thresholds are worse, near 8 degrees per second.

◦ In the Sudden-Jolt rotational gain is two to five times that of regular fibres and rises with frequency, a high-pass response driven partly by near-instantaneous non-quantal transmission in the calyx cleft, explaining reflex latencies as short as 5 milliseconds.

• THE TRANSITION TABLE.

◦ Restless Idle to Sudden-Jolt - triggered by a quick head transient - timescale membrane 2 to 5 milliseconds, non-quantal transmission under one millisecond, adaptation 20 to 200 milliseconds.

◦ Gain and regularity retune - triggered by cholinergic efferents closing KCNQ channels at muscarinic receptors - timescale within a second, thrown strongly from above during voluntary head turns.

• FAULT REGIMES AND WATCHDOGS.

◦ Aminoglycoside antibiotics concentrate in type one hair cells and cause calyx retraction, the boundary signature being afferents that keep a normal resting rate but no longer modulate, so fast balance responses and clear vision during motion are lost; the watchdog is active potassium-conductance adaptation that resets the fibre for the next transient.


ENTRY 233. GENICULATE GANGLION NEURONS (keyed to neuron catalogue Chapter 233)

MODES AND MODE TRANSITIONS:

This is a two-mode first-order taste and mouth-sensation relay, silent until a taste bud speaks, then firing an adapting burst.

• THE MODE REGISTER.

◦ Silent mode - the Empty Mouth - between stimuli the pseudounipolar cell is quiet, with no dendrites and no synapses on the soma.

◦ Taste-driven adapting mode - the Flavour Burst - a taste stimulus drives 20 to 60 spikes per second that fades within a second or two.

• PER-MODE TRANSFER FUNCTIONS.

◦ In the Empty Mouth resting voltage sits near minus 55 to minus 60 millivolts and tetrodotoxin-resistant Nav1.8 and Nav1.9 channels keep the cell ready.

◦ In the Flavour Burst type II taste cells push adenosine triphosphate onto P2X2 and P2X3 receptors and type III cells release serotonin, several taste buds are summed inside one arbor before a spike, and the labelled-line identity is carried to the nucleus of the solitary tract; receptor desensitisation over about 200 milliseconds and adaptation over about a second shape the fade.

• THE TRANSITION TABLE.

◦ Empty Mouth to Flavour Burst - triggered by transmitter in the taste-bud cleft - timescale membrane about 4 milliseconds, taste reaching the brainstem in roughly 10 milliseconds.

◦ Slow remodelling - triggered by taste-bud turnover every ten days - timescale days, so the cell must keep rebuilding its contacts.

• FAULT REGIMES AND WATCHDOGS.

◦ Varicella zoster reactivation as Ramsay Hunt syndrome causes facial palsy and taste loss, the boundary signature being ear blisters with hemifacial weakness; the watchdog is the type I taste-bud cell acting as local astrocyte, whose enzyme NTPDase2 destroys adenosine triphosphate fast enough to keep one mouthful from smearing into the next.


ENTRY 234. PETROSAL GANGLION NEURONS (keyed to neuron catalogue Chapter 234)

MODES AND MODE TRANSITIONS:

This is a mode-rich multi-duty afferent whose chemoreceptor line is never silent while baroreceptor and taste lines run their own regimes, so its modes differ by which peripheral job is engaged.

• THE MODE REGISTER.

◦ Tonic chemoreceptor mode - the Oxygen Vigil - the carotid-body afferent ticks at 0.5 to 3 spikes per second in room air, a resting discharge that is the running proof of oxygen.

◦ Hypoxic climb mode - the Air-Hunger Surge - as arterial oxygen falls toward 40 millimetres of mercury the rate climbs to 30 to 80 spikes per second.

◦ Pulse-locked baroreceptor mode - the Heartbeat Volley - carotid-sinus afferents fire volleys locked to each heartbeat, coding a rhythm rather than a rate.

• PER-MODE TRANSFER FUNCTIONS.

◦ In the Oxygen Vigil glomus cells release adenosine triphosphate and acetylcholine onto purinergic and nicotinic receptors, half-maximal near 55 millimetres of mercury with steepness about 8, and carbon dioxide adds gain of about 0.01 per millimetre of mercury.

◦ In the Air-Hunger Surge output rises steeply and drives ventilation and sympathetic outflow, adapting over about 20 seconds.

◦ In the Heartbeat Volley firing tracks carotid-sinus wall strain at the pulse rate for moment-to-moment blood-pressure control.

• THE TRANSITION TABLE.

◦ Oxygen Vigil to Air-Hunger Surge - triggered by falling arterial oxygen - timescale onset 1 to 5 seconds.

◦ Sensitisation shift - triggered by two days at altitude, or by sleep apnoea and heart failure - timescale 1 to 3 days, raising gain, harmfully in disease.

• FAULT REGIMES AND WATCHDOGS.

◦ Bilateral loss abolishes the drive to breathe in low oxygen, and pathological sensitisation drives hypertension, the boundary signature being an over-reactive reflex with chronic sympathetic overactivity; the watchdog is the type II sustentacular cell, which amplifies the signal and can become a new glomus cell in chronic hypoxia.


ENTRY 235. NODOSE GANGLION NEURONS (keyed to neuron catalogue Chapter 235)

MODES AND MODE TRANSITIONS:

This is a mode-rich visceral status reporter whose slowly adapting stretch signal is multiplied by gut hormones, so its regimes are set both by mechanics and by chemical gain.

• THE MODE REGISTER.

◦ Quiet baseline mode - the Empty Organ - with organs unloaded the fibre is near silent.

◦ Sustained stretch mode - the Fullness Report - stomach or airway stretch holds a steady 5 to 30 spikes per second for as long as the organ stays full.

◦ Hormone-amplified mode - the After-Meal Multiply - gut hormones such as cholecystokinin multiply the response to the same stretch, so the same volume reports as fuller.

• PER-MODE TRANSFER FUNCTIONS.

◦ In the Empty Organ strain sits below the threshold near 0.02 fractional stretch.

◦ In the Fullness Report PIEZO2 converts membrane tension to current, with static sensitivity about 80 hertz per unit strain and dynamic sensitivity about 15 hertz per unit strain per second, slowly adapting over 5 to 30 seconds.

◦ In the After-Meal Multiply a hormonal gain of 1.0 to 2.5 scales the stretch response, without the hormone firing the cell by itself.

• THE TRANSITION TABLE.

◦ Empty Organ to Fullness Report - triggered by organ-wall strain - timescale mechanotransduction under one millisecond.

◦ Fullness Report to After-Meal Multiply - triggered by cholecystokinin, glucagon-like peptide 1, serotonin and leptin - timescale 5 to 30 minutes; a sustained high-fat diet blunts the same gain over days to weeks.

• FAULT REGIMES AND WATCHDOGS.

◦ Diabetic vagal neuropathy gives gastroparesis, the boundary signature being a stomach that neither empties nor reports, and blunted signalling contributes to overeating; the watchdog is enteric glia wrapping the intraganglionic laminar endings and handling glutamate and potassium around them.


ENTRY 236. JUGULAR GANGLION NEURONS (keyed to neuron catalogue Chapter 236)

MODES AND MODE TRANSITIONS:

This is a neural-crest airway nociceptor, a silent-until-hurt switch that can be turned up for months and that causes inflammation as well as reporting it.

• THE MODE REGISTER.

◦ Silent high-threshold mode - the Still Airway - the cell stays quiet until something is wrong.

◦ Irritant-driven mode - the Cough Alarm - acids, smoke, heat and spilled adenosine triphosphate drive slow irregular firing at 1 to 20 spikes per second.

◦ Sensitised mode - the Hair-Trigger - nerve growth factor from airway inflammation raises TRPV1 and substance P so the sensor fires to gentler stimuli.

• PER-MODE TRANSFER FUNCTIONS.

◦ In the Still Airway tetrodotoxin-resistant Nav1.8 and Nav1.9 channels hold a high threshold near minus 55 to minus 60 millivolts.

◦ In the Cough Alarm TRPV1, TRPA1 and P2X3 receptors sense tissue directly, and the terminal releases glutamate with substance P onto the paratrigeminal nucleus; the axon reflex also dumps substance P and calcitonin gene related peptide, known as CGRP, back into the tissue.

◦ In the Hair-Trigger a sensitisation multiplier of 1.0 to 4.0 scales all drive.

• THE TRANSITION TABLE.

◦ Still Airway to Cough Alarm - triggered by irritant chemistry crossing threshold - timescale membrane about 20 milliseconds, with tens of milliseconds from irritation to first cough.

◦ Cough Alarm to Hair-Trigger - triggered by nerve growth factor and prostaglandin E2 from inflammation - timescale hours to weeks, the mechanism of cough hypersensitivity.

• FAULT REGIMES AND WATCHDOGS.

◦ Persistent sensitisation gives refractory chronic cough, the boundary signature being cough continuing after infection clears, now targeted by P2X3 blockers such as gefapixant, while loss causes silent aspiration; the watchdog is normal desensitisation, undermined when satellite glia raise connexin 43 and couple neighbouring cell bodies after inflammation.


ENTRY 237. TRIGEMINAL GANGLION NEURONS (keyed to neuron catalogue Chapter 237)

MODES AND MODE TRANSITIONS:

This is a mode-rich mixed facial afferent covering fast touch through sharp and burning pain, and its nociceptors add an axon-reflex output that dilates the arteries they report on.

• THE MODE REGISTER.

◦ Silent mode - the Calm Face - the cell rests quiet with, in health, no synapses between neurons.

◦ Touch mode - the Light Contact - fast A-beta mechanoreceptors fire narrow spikes at 0 to 200 hertz and conduct at 30 to 70 metres per second.

◦ Nociceptor mode - the Sharp-And-Burn - A-delta and C nociceptors fire 0 to 40 hertz to mechanical, thermal and chemical harm and drive the axon reflex.

◦ Sensitised mode - the Sore Face - repeated firing drives central sensitisation so brushing the hair hurts.

• PER-MODE TRANSFER FUNCTIONS.

◦ In the Light Contact fast fibres relay touch with high fidelity, Nav1.7 setting threshold.

◦ In the Sharp-And-Burn broad tetrodotoxin-resistant Nav1.8 spikes carry pain and, antidromically, release CGRP and substance P into skin, cornea, tooth pulp and dura, dilating dural arteries.

◦ In the Sore Face a sensitisation term multiplies drive at the brainstem relay, giving cutaneous allodynia.

• THE TRANSITION TABLE.

◦ Calm Face to Touch or Sharp-And-Burn - triggered by mechanical force, temperature beyond 25 or 42 degrees Celsius, or tissue acid and inflammatory mediators - timescale membrane 8 to 15 milliseconds.

◦ Sharp-And-Burn to Sore Face - triggered by repeated nociceptor activation and CGRP release - timescale minutes to hours, with CGRP refill over about 30 seconds.

• FAULT REGIMES AND WATCHDOGS.

◦ An artery pressing at the Obersteiner-Redlich zone causes trigeminal neuralgia and runaway CGRP release causes migraine, the boundary signatures being shock-like facial pain and throbbing headache; the watchdog is normally independent satellite-glial capsules, which after injury raise connexin 43 and cross-excite neighbours, and there is no blood-brain barrier here, which is why anti-CGRP antibodies reach these cells.


ENTRY 238. PROPRIOCEPTORS (keyed to neuron catalogue Chapter 238)

MODES AND MODE TRANSITIONS:

This is a mode-rich mechanosensory afferent whose group Ia ending reports both static length and dynamic velocity, and whose sensitivity is reset from above by the fusimotor system.

• THE MODE REGISTER.

◦ Resting tonic mode - the Background Tick - at rest the Ia ending holds about 10 to 20 spikes per second, keeping a running account of limb position.

◦ Static length mode - the Steady Hold - during a maintained stretch the fibre fires 20 to 100 spikes per second in proportion to length.

◦ Dynamic burst mode - the Fast Pull - a rapid stretch drives a burst above 200 spikes per second that reports velocity, shaped by sticky cross-bridges and history-dependence, called thixotropy.

• PER-MODE TRANSFER FUNCTIONS.

◦ In the Steady Hold position gain is about 60 spikes per second per unit fractional stretch, transduced by PIEZO2 under membrane tension.

◦ In the Fast Pull a velocity term of about 12 spikes per second per square-root unit adds on top, with an initial burst decaying over 20 to 50 milliseconds; conduction is 50 to 120 metres per second, the fastest sensory wire in the body.

◦ Ceiling is about 320 spikes per second, imposed by the refractory period and the low-pass filtering T junction.

• THE TRANSITION TABLE.

◦ Background Tick to Steady Hold or Fast Pull - triggered by muscle lengthening and its velocity - timescale transduction under one millisecond, initial-burst decay 20 to 50 milliseconds.

◦ Fusimotor set - triggered by gamma motor neurons contracting the intrafusal fibres, and by GABAergic presynaptic inhibition gating the terminal - timescale 50 to 200 milliseconds, thrown from above so the encoder gain is reset before and during movement.

• FAULT REGIMES AND WATCHDOGS.

◦ In Friedreich ataxia and large-fibre sensory neuronopathy these cells die first, the boundary signature being sensory ataxia and absent tendon reflexes, and PIEZO2 loss-of-function impairs proprioception and touch; the watchdogs are satellite glial potassium buffering around the giant soma and the afferent's own maintenance of the spindle, which degenerates when denervated.


ENTRY 239. LOW-THRESHOLD MECHANORECEPTORS (LTMRS) (Gentle-Touch Sensing Neuron) (keyed to neuron catalogue Chapter 239)

MODES AND MODE TRANSITIONS:

This is a stimulus-driven encoder rather than a mode-switching integrator, so its register is thin: it is a family umbrella whose members differ mostly by adaptation speed, not by discrete internal states.

Its one important state transition is pathological rather than physiological, the switch from touch pathway to pain pathway after nerve injury.

• THE MODE REGISTER.

◦ Evoked Encoding - the Faithful Report - firing that tracks skin deformation from rest up to about 500 spikes per second, the only mode healthy touch actually uses.

◦ Resting Tonic Idle - the Background Tick - a low ongoing discharge in the slowly adapting members that lets the cell signal a decrease as well as an increase.

• PER-MODE TRANSFER FUNCTIONS.

◦ Evoked Encoding: output equals gain times indentation or velocity, gain 0.4 to 9 spikes per second per micrometre, threshold 0.01 to 15 micrometres by subtype, conduction 0.5 to 75 metres per second, confidence high.

◦ Resting Tonic Idle: a floor of a few spikes per second with no stimulus, present mainly in slowly adapting type 2 units, confidence moderate for the family as a whole.

• THE TRANSITION TABLE.

◦ Contact onset - Resting Idle to Evoked Encoding - milliseconds, driven bottom-up by the stimulus, not thrown from above.

◦ Nerve injury sensitisation - Evoked Encoding to an ectopic pain-driving regime - hours to days, a slow biochemical switch in which some A beta cells turn on substance P and recruit microglia.

• FAULT REGIMES AND WATCHDOGS.

◦ Ectopic allodynia firing: boundary signature is the same gentle input driving pain circuits, warning condition is spontaneous discharge after axotomy, watchdog is central gate control and descending inhibition.

◦ Die-back silence: diabetic and chemotherapy neuropathy strip the longest fibres first, and PIEZO2 loss abolishes discriminative touch outright, leaving numb skin with intact pain.


ENTRY 240. A-BETA SLOWLY ADAPTING TYPE 1 (SA1) (Fine-Edge Pressure Neuron) (keyed to neuron catalogue Chapter 240)

MODES AND MODE TRANSITIONS:

This is a two-mode dynamic encoder, a fast onset response layered on a sustained response, because it runs two transducers at two speeds.

It is mode-poor in the switching sense but has a clear dynamic-versus-static division of labour.

• THE MODE REGISTER.

◦ Dynamic Ramp Response - the Edge Catch - a high burst during the indentation ramp that reports edges, corners and curvature.

◦ Static Sustained Response - the Steady Press - an irregular maintained discharge that reports ongoing pressure while the skin stays pressed.

• PER-MODE TRANSFER FUNCTIONS.

◦ Dynamic Ramp Response: 100 to 200 spikes per second during the ramp in, edge gain about six times static gain, driven by the nerve terminal's own PIEZO2, confidence high.

◦ Static Sustained Response: 20 to 60 spikes per second irregular discharge, static gain about 1 spike per second per micrometre, produced by the Merkel cell, threshold 8 to 30 micrometres, confidence high.

• THE TRANSITION TABLE.

◦ Ramp onset - Static to Dynamic - milliseconds, stimulus-driven from below.

◦ Ramp end into hold - Dynamic to Static - fast adaptation 5 to 15 milliseconds then slow adaptation 2 to 5 seconds, stimulus-driven.

• FAULT REGIMES AND WATCHDOGS.

◦ Merkel loss: ageing thins the Merkel population and PIEZO2 loss of function abolishes fine form perception while pain remains, watchdog is redundancy across the dense fingertip array.

◦ Die-back: diabetic and chemotherapy neuropathy kill the longest SA1 axons first, and leprosy strips these endings from the cooler skin of the fingers.


ENTRY 241. A-BETA SLOWLY ADAPTING TYPE 2 (SA2) (Skin-Stretch Posture Neuron) (keyed to neuron catalogue Chapter 241)

MODES AND MODE TRANSITIONS:

This is a tonically active strain gauge, a one-and-a-half mode encoder that sits at a non-zero resting rate and can report by both rising and falling.

Its distinguishing feature is a genuine resting mode, because resting skin is never fully slack.

• THE MODE REGISTER.

◦ Resting Metronome - the Background Tick - a regular ongoing discharge of 5 to 20 spikes per second even when nothing touches the skin.

◦ Directional Stretch Encoding - the Strain Report - firing tuned to one preferred axis of lateral pull that signals finger posture and grip direction.

• PER-MODE TRANSFER FUNCTIONS.

◦ Resting Metronome: 5 to 20 spikes per second, regular interval, a floor above which stretch adds and below which release subtracts, confidence high.

◦ Directional Stretch Encoding: strain gain about 200 spikes per second per unit strain following a cosine of pull direction, up to about 100 spikes per second, viscoelastic creep makes a held stretch sag over tens of seconds, confidence high.

• THE TRANSITION TABLE.

◦ Skin loading - Resting Metronome to Directional Stretch Encoding - milliseconds, stimulus-driven from below.

◦ Held load - Stretch Encoding sags via collagen creep - 10 to 60 seconds, mechanical not neural.

• FAULT REGIMES AND WATCHDOGS.

◦ Phantom tightness: nerve injury turns the ongoing discharge into a felt tension with no stretch, watchdog is central comparison across the SA2 population.

◦ Stiffened gain: ageing and diabetic stiffening of dermal collagen distort the gain so force direction is misjudged, one cause of dropped objects in later life.


ENTRY 242. A-BETA RAPIDLY ADAPTING TYPE 1 (RA1) (Slip-Catching Flutter Neuron) (keyed to neuron catalogue Chapter 242)

MODES AND MODE TRANSITIONS:

This is a change detector with two operating modes, a transient edge-of-event mode and a phase-locked entrainment mode, and it is silent otherwise.

It reports change rather than state, so a steady press finds it in neither mode.

• THE MODE REGISTER.

◦ Transient Onset-Offset - the Slip Catch - one or two spikes at stimulus onset and again at release, reporting the first microscopic slip that grip control needs.

◦ Flutter Entrainment - the Flutter Lock - one spike per cycle phase-locked to vibration in the flutter band.

• PER-MODE TRANSFER FUNCTIONS.

◦ Transient Onset-Offset: burst of one to two spikes at onset, silence during hold, burst at release, effective drive is indentation velocity, confidence high.

◦ Flutter Entrainment: phase-locked firing over roughly 5 to 50 hertz, best sensitivity 20 to 40 hertz, threshold a few micrometres, up to about 250 spikes per second, conduction 35 to 75 metres per second, confidence high.

• THE TRANSITION TABLE.

◦ Contact onset or offset - Silence to Transient Onset-Offset - milliseconds, stimulus-driven, PIEZO2 inactivates within 5 to 15 milliseconds.

◦ Onset of vibration - Transient to Flutter Entrainment - within one cycle, stimulus-driven from below.

• FAULT REGIMES AND WATCHDOGS.

◦ Corpuscle loss: diabetic neuropathy destroys Meissner corpuscles before nerve conduction studies turn abnormal, watchdog is vision substituting for grip feedback.

◦ Compression block: carpal tunnel compression blocks these fibres early, giving tingling and clumsiness, and PIEZO2 loss abolishes the response outright.


ENTRY 243. A-BETA RAPIDLY ADAPTING TYPE 2 (RA2) (Whole-Hand Vibration Neuron) (keyed to neuron catalogue Chapter 243)

MODES AND MODE TRANSITIONS:

This is the most extreme change detector in the body, effectively a single high-frequency entrainment mode with a bare onset-offset twitch, its adaptation performed by the capsule it lives inside.

Its mode set is thin because the Pacinian corpuscle discards steady pressure mechanically before any channel is involved.

• THE MODE REGISTER.

◦ Onset-Offset Twitch - the Bare Blip - exactly one spike at onset and one at release to a steady press, the most extreme adaptation of any receptor.

◦ High-Frequency Entrainment - the Buzz Lock - one spike per cycle phase-locked to vibration across the high band.

• PER-MODE TRANSFER FUNCTIONS.

◦ Onset-Offset Twitch: two spikes total for a sustained press, nothing in between, confidence high.

◦ High-Frequency Entrainment: entrains from roughly 20 to 1000 hertz, best frequency near 250 hertz where threshold is about ten nanometres, up to about 500 spikes per second saturating at one spike per cycle, refractory 1 to 2 milliseconds sets the ceiling, confidence high.

• THE TRANSITION TABLE.

◦ Contact transient - Silence to Onset-Offset Twitch - under a millisecond, stimulus-driven.

◦ Tissue-borne vibration arriving - Twitch to High-Frequency Entrainment - within one cycle, stimulus-driven from below, from sources centimetres away.

• FAULT REGIMES AND WATCHDOGS.

◦ Early die-back: diabetic and chemotherapy neuropathy destroy these afferents early, which is why tuning-fork testing at the toe is the standard screen, watchdog is that same clinical test.

◦ Overuse blanching: chronic vibrating-tool use produces hand-arm vibration syndrome with numbness and blanching fingers, and PIEZO2 loss removes vibration sense entirely.


ENTRY 244. A-DELTA LOW-THRESHOLD MECHANORECEPTORS, ALSO CALLED D-HAIR CELLS (Faint-Breeze Hair Neuron) (keyed to neuron catalogue Chapter 244)

MODES AND MODE TRANSITIONS:

This is a movement-only encoder with a single direction-selective evoked mode, its selectivity solved by anatomy before any electrical processing happens.

It holds the lowest mechanical threshold ever measured in mammalian skin, so its one mode is exquisitely sensitive rather than multiple.

• THE MODE REGISTER.

◦ Directional Movement Response - the Against-The-Grain Sweep - firing only while a hair moves, hardest when the stroke runs against the lie of the hair.

• PER-MODE TRANSFER FUNCTIONS.

◦ Directional Movement Response: threshold below 0.1 millinewtons, direction gain ratio near 4 to 1 along the best axis, conduction 5 to 15 metres per second, adaptation 10 to 30 milliseconds, Cav3.2 amplifies movement below the noise floor of most neurons, confidence high.

• THE TRANSITION TABLE.

◦ Hair movement onset - Silence to Directional Movement Response - under a millisecond transduction, stimulus-driven from below.

◦ Presynaptic inhibition - Response muted at the central terminal - milliseconds, thrown from the cord above so the segment can gate the cell.

◦ Cooling shift - responsiveness rises - seconds, because cooling makes temperature an input to this touch cell.

• FAULT REGIMES AND WATCHDOGS.

◦ Ending retraction: endings retract in small fibre neuropathy and light touch dulls, watchdog is the dense follicle field so loss is graded.

◦ Injury allodynia: after nerve injury these terminals help drive pain from a gentle stroke.


ENTRY 245. C LOW-THRESHOLD MECHANORECEPTORS (C-LTMRS) (Caress-Speed Comfort Neuron) (keyed to neuron catalogue Chapter 245)

MODES AND MODE TRANSITIONS:

This is a band-pass affective encoder with one preferred-velocity mode gated by temperature, and a slow fatigue state that carries a memory of recent use.

Its single evoked mode is tuned to the physiology of a caress rather than switched between regimes.

• THE MODE REGISTER.

◦ Optimal Stroke Encoding - the Caress Report - firing that peaks to gentle stroking near 3 centimetres per second at skin-warm temperature and tracks how pleasant the touch feels.

◦ Fatigued Slowing - the Worn Groove - a use-dependent state in which repeated strokes slow conduction and lower output, a short memory of recent activity.

• PER-MODE TRANSFER FUNCTIONS.

◦ Optimal Stroke Encoding: peak 30 to 60 impulses per second, optimal velocity about 3 centimetres per second, optimal temperature 32 degrees Celsius, band-passing velocity and rejecting slower and faster strokes, conduction 0.5 to 1.5 metres per second, confidence high.

◦ Fatigued Slowing: firing falls and conduction slows with recent spike count, recovery 10 to 60 seconds, confidence moderate.

• THE TRANSITION TABLE.

◦ Stroke onset - Silence to Optimal Stroke Encoding - about a second foot-to-cord delay, stimulus-driven from below.

◦ Repeated stroking - Encoding to Fatigued Slowing - 0.5 to 2 seconds adaptation, activity-driven not thrown from above.

• FAULT REGIMES AND WATCHDOGS.

◦ Affective die-back: in small fibre neuropathy these endings die back and pleasant touch dulls before pain does, watchdog is the redundant glomerular array.

◦ Injury rerouting: after nerve injury their input is rerouted into pain circuits, producing allodynia.


ENTRY 246. NOCICEPTORS (keyed to neuron catalogue Chapter 246)

MODES AND MODE TRANSITIONS:

This is the alarm-layer umbrella, a high-threshold detector whose signature transition is sensitisation, the shift from a guarded silent baseline to a hair-trigger sensitised state.

It is mode-rich in the sense that injury reprograms it, but under healthy conditions it is largely a silent-until-threatened detector.

• THE MODE REGISTER.

◦ Silent Guard - the Quiet Sentry - the healthy resting state, high threshold, little or no ongoing firing.

◦ Evoked Alarm - the Damage Report - firing to stimuli strong enough to threaten tissue, whether burning heat, crushing force, freezing cold or irritant chemicals.

◦ Sensitised Firing - the Lowered Guard - a temporarily lowered threshold and raised gain in inflamed tissue.

• PER-MODE TRANSFER FUNCTIONS.

◦ Silent Guard: at rest near minus 55 millivolts, heat threshold 42 to 45 degrees Celsius, mechanical threshold above 5 millinewtons, confidence high.

◦ Evoked Alarm: broad Nav1.8 and Nav1.9 spikes up to about 50 impulses per second, plus axon-reflex peptide release into tissue, confidence high.

◦ Sensitised Firing: peripheral sensitisation over seconds to minutes drops threshold and adds spontaneous activity, confidence high.

• THE TRANSITION TABLE.

◦ Threatening stimulus - Silent Guard to Evoked Alarm - milliseconds, stimulus-driven from below.

◦ Inflammatory mediators - Evoked Alarm to Sensitised Firing - seconds to minutes, driven locally by bradykinin, prostaglandins and nerve growth factor.

◦ Injury transcription - Sensitised Firing to a reprogrammed state - hours to days, a gene-expression change in the cell body.

• FAULT REGIMES AND WATCHDOGS.

◦ Congenital silence: suprachiasmatic (SCN)9A loss of function abolishes pain entirely, watchdog absent, which is itself the danger.

◦ Runaway firing: SCN9A gain of function causes the burning attacks of inherited erythromelalgia, and die-back gives painful small fibre neuropathy.


ENTRY 247. PEPTIDERGIC NOCICEPTORS (Swelling-Driven Pain Neuron) (keyed to neuron catalogue Chapter 247)

MODES AND MODE TRANSITIONS:

This is a nociceptor that doubles as a secretory gland, so its register adds a peptide-release mode gated by burst firing on top of ordinary alarm and sensitisation.

It does not merely report damage, it inflames the tissue it reports on.

• THE MODE REGISTER.

◦ Silent Guard - the Quiet Sentry - the high-threshold resting state.

◦ Evoked Alarm - the Damage Report - glutamate onto lamina I projection neurons when a noxious stimulus arrives.

◦ Neurogenic Flare - the Inflaming Spread - burst-gated peptide release from both ends that widens vessels and leaks plasma.

• PER-MODE TRANSFER FUNCTIONS.

◦ Silent Guard: rest near minus 55 millivolts, heat threshold 42 to 45 degrees Celsius, confidence high.

◦ Evoked Alarm: broad Nav1.8 and Nav1.9 spikes, conduction 0.5 to 2 metres per second, confidence high.
