◦ Neurogenic Flare: substance P and calcitonin gene-related peptide released only in bursts above about 5 impulses per second, flare develops over 20 to 60 seconds, peptide resupply takes hours, confidence high.

• THE TRANSITION TABLE.

◦ Noxious stimulus - Silent Guard to Evoked Alarm - milliseconds, stimulus-driven.

◦ Burst firing above about 5 impulses per second - Evoked Alarm to Neurogenic Flare - tens of seconds, activity-driven via the axon reflex, not thrown from above.

◦ Nerve growth factor rise - toward a sensitised transcriptional state - hours to days, driven by the inflamed target.

• FAULT REGIMES AND WATCHDOGS.

◦ NTRK1 loss: losing the TrkA gene causes hereditary sensory and autonomic neuropathy type four, no pain and no sweat, watchdog absent.

◦ Peptide overactivity: the same peptides drive migraine, and blocking calcitonin gene-related peptide prevents attacks.


ENTRY 248. NON-PEPTIDERGIC NOCICEPTORS (Bare-Skin Injury Neuron) (keyed to neuron catalogue Chapter 248)

MODES AND MODE TRANSITIONS:

This is a polymodal damage detector without peptide cargo, so it has no flare mode, and its register is a silent guard, an evoked alarm and a chemical damage-reading mode.

It reads the chemistry of broken cells directly, since spilled adenosine triphosphate is its natural agonist.

• THE MODE REGISTER.

◦ Silent Guard - the Quiet Sentry - the high-threshold resting state.

◦ Evoked Alarm - the Damage Report - firing to pinch, noxious heat and irritant chemicals.

◦ Purinergic Reading - the Broken-Cell Sniff - firing driven by adenosine triphosphate leaking from damaged tissue.

• PER-MODE TRANSFER FUNCTIONS.

◦ Silent Guard: rest near minus 55 millivolts, mechanical threshold 5 to 20 millinewtons, heat threshold about 43 degrees Celsius, confidence high.

◦ Evoked Alarm: Nav1.8-carried broad spikes up to about 20 impulses per second, conduction 0.5 to 2 metres per second, confidence high.

◦ Purinergic Reading: P2X3-gated inward current, activation 10 to 50 milliseconds then desensitising over seconds, confidence high.

• THE TRANSITION TABLE.

◦ Noxious stimulus - Silent Guard to Evoked Alarm - milliseconds, stimulus-driven.

◦ Tissue damage releasing adenosine triphosphate - Evoked Alarm to Purinergic Reading - tens of milliseconds, driven by spilled agonist from below.

• FAULT REGIMES AND WATCHDOGS.

◦ Early die-back: these endings are lost early in diabetic and chemotherapy-induced small fibre neuropathy, watchdog is the overlap with peptidergic afferents.

◦ Overactive itch: their overactivity after nerve injury contributes to allodynia and to chronic itch.


ENTRY 249. A-DELTA NOCICEPTORS (First-Pain Pricking Neuron) (keyed to neuron catalogue Chapter 249)

MODES AND MODE TRANSITIONS:

This is a fast first-pain detector split into two type populations, each a distinct threshold regime, with a use-dependent sensitisation state after burns.

Its speed lets the cord run a protective reflex before conscious pain exists, so one spike serves two timescales.

• THE MODE REGISTER.

◦ Type One High-Heat Guard - the Scald Alarm - low mechanical threshold but very high heat threshold above 53 degrees Celsius.

◦ Type Two Sharp Prick - the First-Pain Prick - heat threshold about 46 degrees Celsius but very high mechanical threshold, carrying the well-located sharp pain.

◦ Burn Sensitised Firing - the Scorched Trigger - a lowered heat threshold after burn injury.

• PER-MODE TRANSFER FUNCTIONS.

◦ Type One: heat threshold above 53 degrees Celsius, conduction 5 to 30 metres per second, up to about 100 impulses per second, confidence high.

◦ Type Two: heat threshold about 46 degrees Celsius, very high mechanical threshold, confidence high.

◦ Burn Sensitised Firing: striking use-dependent threshold drop over minutes to hours after burn, confidence high.

• THE TRANSITION TABLE.

◦ Noxious stimulus - resting to Type One or Type Two Alarm - foot-to-cord delay 30 to 60 milliseconds, stimulus-driven.

◦ Burn injury - Type One to Burn Sensitised Firing - minutes to hours, driven by local injury.

• FAULT REGIMES AND WATCHDOGS.

◦ Demyelinating silence: pressure and demyelinating neuropathy silence these fibres first, removing sharp pain and slowing withdrawal, watchdog is the parallel C-fibre channel.

◦ Ectopic shooting: after nerve injury the same fibres fire spontaneously and produce shooting electric pain.


ENTRY 250. C-FIBRE POLYMODAL NOCICEPTORS (Second-Pain Burning Neuron) (keyed to neuron catalogue Chapter 250)

MODES AND MODE TRANSITIONS:

This is the slow burning-pain workhorse, a rate-limited alarm with a strong sensitisation mode and a self-limiting activity-dependent slowing that acts as automatic gain control.

Polymodal means it answers crushing pressure, burning heat and irritant chemicals alike.

• THE MODE REGISTER.

◦ Evoked Ache - the Slow Burn - firing that carries the ache outlasting an injury, held low by a long afterhyperpolarisation.

◦ Sensitised Firing - the Inflamed Trigger - a dropped heat threshold in inflamed tissue.

◦ Neurogenic Flare - the Inflaming Spread - axon-reflex release of calcitonin gene-related peptide and substance P with no message to the brain.

• PER-MODE TRANSFER FUNCTIONS.

◦ Evoked Ache: 0.5 to 20 spikes per second, afterhyperpolarisation 50 to 150 milliseconds, spike 2 to 5 milliseconds, conduction 0.5 to 2 metres per second, confidence high.

◦ Sensitised Firing: heat threshold falls from 43 to about 32 degrees Celsius when inflamed, over minutes, confidence high.

◦ Neurogenic Flare: peripheral peptide release onto arterioles and mast cells, activity-dependent conduction slowing rises with use as a self-limiting brake, confidence high.

• THE TRANSITION TABLE.

◦ Noxious stimulus - rest to Evoked Ache - foot-to-cord delay about a second, stimulus-driven.

◦ Inflammatory mediators - Evoked Ache to Sensitised Firing - minutes, driven locally.

◦ Sustained firing - toward slowed conduction and Neurogenic Flare - seconds for slowing, driven by activity from below.

• FAULT REGIMES AND WATCHDOGS.

◦ suprachiasmatic (SCN)9A loss: loss of function causes congenital insensitivity to pain, watchdog absent.

◦ SCN9A gain: gain of function causes inherited erythromelalgia with burning red hands and feet.


ENTRY 251. A-BETA NOCICEPTORS AND MODERATE PRESSURE RECEPTORS (Ultrafast Bruise-Signalling Neuron) (keyed to neuron catalogue Chapter 251)

MODES AND MODE TRANSITIONS:

This is an ultrafast damage reporter with two threshold classes and a slowly adapting sustained response, plus an injury-switch fault mode.

It uses thick fast axons to report damage rather than gentle touch.

• THE MODE REGISTER.

◦ Moderate Pressure Encoding - the Steady Squeeze - a slowly adapting train in the lower-threshold moderate pressure receptor.

◦ High-Threshold Alarm - the Bruise Warning - firing in the A beta nociceptor proper only to tissue-threatening pressure.

• PER-MODE TRANSFER FUNCTIONS.

◦ Moderate Pressure Encoding: threshold about 20 millinewtons, slowly adapting from tens to low hundreds of spikes per second settling to applied force, spike 0.8 to 2 milliseconds, conduction 30 to 100 metres per second, confidence high.

◦ High-Threshold Alarm: threshold about 90 millinewtons, slowly adapting train up to about 200 hertz onto laminae I to V, confidence high.

• THE TRANSITION TABLE.

◦ Increasing force - Moderate Pressure Encoding through into High-Threshold Alarm - milliseconds, stimulus-driven from below.

◦ Held force - discharge settles - adaptation about 600 milliseconds, mechanical.

◦ Injury switch - normal encoding to ectopic pain firing - 3 to 14 days, a slow phenotypic switch after injury.

• FAULT REGIMES AND WATCHDOGS.

◦ Ectopic allodynia: after injury or demyelination it fires ectopically into pain circuits so a light brush is agony, watchdog is central gating.

◦ Early large-fibre loss: in large fibre neuropathies it is lost early, leaving only slow burning pain with no sense of where the damage is.


ENTRY 252. SILENT OR SLEEPING NOCICEPTORS (Inflammation-Woken Ache Neuron) (keyed to neuron catalogue Chapter 252)

MODES AND MODE TRANSITIONS:

This is a genuine two-state switch cell, mechanically deaf when healthy and unsilenced into a hair-trigger ache by injury, its transition a slow biochemical gene-expression change.

It is the reserve alarm, and its whole point is the switch.

• THE MODE REGISTER.

◦ Mechanically Deaf Sleep - the Sleeping Guard - born deaf, fires at no squeeze, poke or stretch in healthy tissue.

◦ Unsilenced Ache - the Woken Alarm - a low irregular discharge once injury has switched it on.

• PER-MODE TRANSFER FUNCTIONS.

◦ Mechanically Deaf Sleep: resting threshold above 750 millinewtons, effectively no output, confidence high.

◦ Unsilenced Ache: 0.1 to 5 spikes per second, awakened threshold near 30 to 60 millinewtons, extreme activity-dependent slowing shedding over 30 per cent of speed at 2 hertz, wide-area peptide release across three or more segments, confidence high.

• THE TRANSITION TABLE.

◦ Injury and inflammation - Deaf Sleep to Unsilenced Ache - unsilencing rises with a time constant near 6 hours, driven locally by nerve growth factor, bradykinin and protons.

◦ Resolution - Unsilenced Ache back toward Sleep - decays over about 24 hours, driven by the settling of inflammation.

• FAULT REGIMES AND WATCHDOGS.

◦ Permanent waking: chronic inflammation keeps these fibres permanently awake, a major driver of pain in arthritis, interstitial cystitis and inflammatory bowel disease, watchdog is the normal 24 hour decay that fails here.

◦ Note: silencing them is an attractive drug target precisely because protective acute pain would survive.


ENTRY 253. THERMORECEPTORS (keyed to neuron catalogue Chapter 253)

MODES AND MODE TRANSITIONS:

This is a tonically active ratio encoder that never falls silent, and its notable feature is a rate mode that reorganises into a bursting mode below about 30 degrees Celsius.

Because it always fires, the brain reads a ratio between warm and cool populations rather than a threshold crossing.

• THE MODE REGISTER.

◦ Static Rate Coding - the Steady Thermometer - an ongoing discharge whose rate reports absolute temperature around neutral skin.

◦ Dynamic Overshoot - the Change Flag - a large transient when temperature steps, signalling the direction of change.

◦ Cold Bursting - the Shiver Pattern - a reorganisation into rhythmic bursts below about 30 degrees Celsius that encodes temperature in spikes per burst.

• PER-MODE TRANSFER FUNCTIONS.

◦ Static Rate Coding: cool fibres tick 2 to 10 spikes per second at 33 to 34 degrees Celsius, peaking near 27 degrees, warm fibres peak near 42, up to about 40 hertz, confidence high.

◦ Dynamic Overshoot: a 9 degree step briefly drives firing far above its settled level, adapting reference follows skin with a time constant near 1.5 seconds, confidence high.

◦ Cold Bursting: below 30 degrees the discharge organises into bursts recurring every 0.2 to 1 second, spikes per burst encoding temperature independently of rate, confidence high.

• THE TRANSITION TABLE.

◦ Temperature step - Static Rate to Dynamic Overshoot and back - about 1.5 seconds, stimulus-driven from below.

◦ Cooling past about 30 degrees Celsius - Static Rate to Cold Bursting - within seconds, stimulus-driven.

• FAULT REGIMES AND WATCHDOGS.

◦ Oxaliplatin cold pain: the chemotherapy drug oxaliplatin makes cold agonising within hours by altering sodium channels, watchdog is normal channel kinetics.

◦ Die-back widening: in small fibre neuropathy thermal thresholds widen, the basis of quantitative sensory testing.


ENTRY 254. PRURICEPTORS (Itch-Sensing Scratch-Urge Neuron) (keyed to neuron catalogue Chapter 254)

MODES AND MODE TRANSITIONS:

This is a labelled-line chemical detector with a strikingly low firing ceiling, whose modes are set by which chemical pathway drives it rather than by firing pattern.

Its identity rather than its rate sets the itch sensation, a design principle a simulator must encode explicitly.

• THE MODE REGISTER.

◦ Histaminergic Itch - the Histamine Itch - a slow low discharge driven by histamine, mechanically insensitive.

◦ Non-Histaminergic Itch - the Cytokine Itch - a slow discharge driven by interleukin 31, chloroquine-like ligands and bile acids.

◦ Scratch-Suppressed Quiet - the Scratch Relief - a transiently silenced state after scratching recruits spinal inhibition.

• PER-MODE TRANSFER FUNCTIONS.

◦ Histaminergic Itch: 0.1 to 5 spikes per second, histamine response about 20 seconds, natriuretic peptide B released alongside glutamate, confidence high.

◦ Non-Histaminergic Itch: similar low rates, cytokine response about 2 minutes, peak firing about 15 hertz, conduction 0.5 to 1 metre per second, confidence high.

◦ Scratch-Suppressed Quiet: scratch inhibition depth about 85 per cent for about 8 seconds, confidence high.

• THE TRANSITION TABLE.

◦ Pruritogen binding - Quiet to Histaminergic or Non-Histaminergic Itch - 20 seconds to 2 minutes, chemically driven from below.

◦ Scratching - Itch to Scratch-Suppressed Quiet - about 8 seconds, a behavioural feedback loop recruiting spinal inhibition.

• FAULT REGIMES AND WATCHDOGS.

◦ Chronic itch-scratch cycle: in atopic dermatitis and prurigo nodularis these fibres fire for months, watchdog is the scratch-driven inhibition that fails to keep pace.

◦ Note: nemolizumab against the interleukin 31 receptor and difelikefalin, a peripheral kappa opioid agonist, prove the mechanism.


ENTRY 255. VISCERAL AFFERENTS (Inner-Organ Stretch-Reporting Neuron) (keyed to neuron catalogue Chapter 255)

MODES AND MODE TRANSITIONS:

This is an organ-wall encoder with two functional classes, a proportional muscular class and a silent-until-uncomfortable serosal class, plus a sensitisation mode that turns normal filling into agony.

It reports stretch, chemical irritation and damage as fullness, urgency, nausea, cramp and deep aching pain.

• THE MODE REGISTER.

◦ Proportional Fullness Encoding - the Volume Gauge - muscular afferents firing slowly in proportion to organ volume.

◦ Serosal Silent Guard - the Distension Alarm - serosal units silent until distension becomes uncomfortable then climbing steeply.

◦ Sensitised Firing - the Inflamed Report - a lowered threshold so ordinary filling drives strong firing.

• PER-MODE TRANSFER FUNCTIONS.

◦ Proportional Fullness Encoding: mucosal threshold near 0.1 millinewtons, muscular in the low tens of millinewtons, wide 2 to 5 millisecond spikes, confidence high.

◦ Serosal Silent Guard: serosal threshold about 91 millinewtons, climbing to 20 or 40 spikes per second, confidence high.

◦ Sensitised Firing: serosal threshold falls to about 31 millinewtons when inflamed, over minutes to months, confidence high.

• THE TRANSITION TABLE.

◦ Organ filling - Proportional Encoding through into Serosal Alarm - seconds, stimulus-driven from below.

◦ Inflammation - toward Sensitised Firing - minutes for acute, months for post-infectious, driven locally by mediators and by serotonin from enterochromaffin cells.

• FAULT REGIMES AND WATCHDOGS.

◦ Normal-volume agony: in irritable bowel syndrome and bladder pain syndrome these afferents fire at normal organ volumes, watchdog is descending inhibition.

◦ Note: linaclotide raises cyclic guanosine monophosphate in the gut lining and quietens these endings directly.


ENTRY 256. SYMPATHETIC POSTGANGLIONIC NEURONS (keyed to neuron catalogue Chapter 256)

MODES AND MODE TRANSITIONS:

This is a gain-controlled ganglion cell with two documented electrical personalities, phasic and tonic, switched by closure of the M current, a slow potassium leak that keeps the cell hyperpolarised.

Shutting off the M current is how the ganglion changes gear, making weak inputs suddenly effective.

• THE MODE REGISTER.

◦ Phasic Accommodating - the One-Shot Gate - fires one or two spikes at the start of a long input then falls silent, held back by the M current.

◦ Tonic Firing - the Steady Passer - fires steadily throughout a maintained input, with little M current.

◦ M-Current-Off High Gain - the Open Gate - a muscarinically boosted state in which previously useless weak inputs fire the cell.

• PER-MODE TRANSFER FUNCTIONS.

◦ Phasic Accommodating: rest about minus 58 millivolts, threshold near minus 45 millivolts, one to two spikes per step, confidence high.

◦ Tonic Firing: sustained output tracking input, confidence high, a distinct population identified electrophysiologically.

◦ M-Current-Off High Gain: muscarinic closure multiplies throughput without adding synapses, neuropeptide Y released only at high rates as a self-limiting brake, confidence high.

• THE TRANSITION TABLE.

◦ Strong nicotinic volley - resting to firing - fast nicotinic potential 5 to 50 milliseconds, driven by preganglionic input.

◦ Muscarinic drive above about 5 impulses per second - Phasic toward M-Current-Off High Gain - slow potential onset about 1 second lasting 30 to 60 seconds, driven from the same preganglionic axons and ultimately set by brainstem sympathetic tone above.

◦ Late peptide potential - further gain shift - 1 to 5 minutes.

• FAULT REGIMES AND WATCHDOGS.

◦ Autoimmune block: antibodies against the alpha3 ganglionic nicotinic subunit break transmission, causing fainting, dry mouth and a paralysed gut, watchdog is receptor turnover.

◦ Synucleinopathy: alpha synuclein deposits in pure autonomic failure and Parkinson disease cause a blood pressure fall on standing, and loss produces Horner syndrome or postural hypotension.


ENTRY 257. SUDOMOTOR SYMPATHETIC NEURONS (Sweat-Releasing Cooling Neuron) (keyed to neuron catalogue Chapter 257)

MODES AND MODE TRANSITIONS:

This is a low-rate cholinergic driver with two functional recruitment modes, a thermoregulatory mode scaled to core temperature and an emotional mode arriving as brief volleys, both riding on a very low mean firing rate.

Its bursts are not locked to the heartbeat, which is how a physiologist tells it from the vasoconstrictor neuron beside it.

• THE MODE REGISTER.

◦ Thermoregulatory Sweating - the Cooling Drive - firing scaled by core temperature error to drive evaporative cooling.

◦ Emotional Sweating - the Fright Sweat - brief irregular volleys from arousal pathways that dampen palms.

• PER-MODE TRANSFER FUNCTIONS.

◦ Thermoregulatory Sweating: 0.5 impulses per second at rest up to about 8 during maximal heat stress, burst participation about 0.3, acetylcholine onto gland M3 receptors giving 0 to 2 nanolitres per gland per minute, confidence high.

◦ Emotional Sweating: irregular volleys not scaled to temperature, same cholinergic output, confidence moderate on exact rates.

• THE TRANSITION TABLE.

◦ Rising core temperature - quiet to Thermoregulatory Sweating - thermoregulatory loop tens of seconds to minutes, thrown from the hypothalamus above.

◦ Arousal, noise or fright - quiet to Emotional Sweating - within a second, thrown from limbic arousal pathways above.

◦ Sweat onset after a burst - 1 to 3 seconds at the gland.

• FAULT REGIMES AND WATCHDOGS.

◦ Ross syndrome: these cholinergic neurons degenerate segmentally, leaving non-sweating patches beside over-sweating ones, watchdog is the compensatory drive that overshoots.

◦ Focal hyperhidrosis and early diabetic loss: drenching palms treated by cutting the thoracic chain, and sudomotor loss precedes measurable sensory loss in diabetes.


ENTRY 258. VASOCONSTRICTOR, VASODILATOR, PILOMOTOR, AND CARDIOMOTOR SYMPATHETIC NEURONS (Blood-Flow-Setting Readiness Neuron) (keyed to neuron catalogue Chapter 258)

MODES AND MODE TRANSITIONS:

These are four functional classes that share one striking mode principle: the message is carried by how many neurons fire in a burst, not by how fast any one fires, so the register is about recruitment and a burst-gated peptide layer.

The muscle vasoconstrictor neuron has the best documented firing pattern of any neuron in the awake human body.

• THE MODE REGISTER.

◦ Baro-Locked Tonic Constriction - the Pressure Hold - muscle vasoconstrictor firing locked to the pulse that holds arteries partly shut all day.

◦ Unlocked Irregular Firing - the Free Volley - cutaneous vasoconstrictor and pilomotor firing not locked to the pulse.

◦ Tonic Cardiac Drive - the Heart Push - cardiomotor firing tonically at rest to set rate and force.

◦ Burst Peptide Hormone - the Slow Squeeze - neuropeptide Y released only in bursts, a slow hormone layered on the fast one.

• PER-MODE TRANSFER FUNCTIONS.

◦ Baro-Locked Tonic Constriction: about 0.5 impulses per second, usually once per heartbeat, ceiling near 1 even in disease because the system recruits more neurons, confidence high.

◦ Unlocked Irregular Firing: irregular, not pulse-locked, set by temperature signals, confidence high.

◦ Tonic Cardiac Drive: 1 to 4 impulses per second at rest onto beta1 receptors, confidence high.

◦ Burst Peptide Hormone: neuropeptide Y above about 2 impulses per second in bursts, action 5 to 20 minutes, confidence high.

• THE TRANSITION TABLE.

◦ Baroreceptor phase signal 0.8 to 2 hertz - sets pulse-locked firing - beat by beat, thrown from the brainstem baroreflex above.

◦ Core and skin temperature - drives cutaneous classes - tens of seconds, thrown from the hypothalamus above.

◦ Rising demand - recruitment of more neurons rather than faster firing - seconds, thrown from central sympathetic tone above.

• FAULT REGIMES AND WATCHDOGS.

◦ Overactive constriction: high burst counts drive hypertension, heart failure and sleep apnoea, watchdog is the baroreflex whose gain is lost here.

◦ Uneven cardiac sprouting: after a heart attack this causes ventricular arrhythmia, which is why the left stellate ganglion is blocked or removed; loss of cutaneous vasodilators gives heat intolerance with intact sweating.


ENTRY 259. PARASYMPATHETIC PREGANGLIONIC NEURONS (keyed to neuron catalogue Chapter 259)

MODES AND MODE TRANSITIONS:

This is a rest-and-digest command cell that comes in two firing personalities, an intrinsic pacemaker type and a respiration-gated silent type, so its modes are set as much by rhythm as by input.

Silence in its inputs does not mean silence in its output, because the pacemaker type generates its own rhythm.

• THE MODE REGISTER.

◦ Intrinsic Pacemaker Tick - the Background Tick - dorsal motor nucleus cells firing slowly and regularly even with every synapse blocked.

◦ Inspiration-Gated Silence - the Breath Gate - nucleus ambiguus cardiac vagal cells held silent during inspiration and released in expiration.

• PER-MODE TRANSFER FUNCTIONS.

◦ Intrinsic Pacemaker Tick: 0.5 to 2 impulses per second driven by a persistent sodium current and shaped by an A type potassium current, confidence high.

◦ Inspiration-Gated Silence: near silent at rest, firing during expiration up to 15 impulses per second, the basis of respiratory sinus arrhythmia, confidence high.

• THE TRANSITION TABLE.

◦ Expiration - Inspiration-Gated Silence to firing - respiratory cycle every 3 to 5 seconds, thrown by the brainstem respiratory rhythm above via gamma aminobutyric acid and glycine.

◦ Solitary-tract glutamate - modulates pacemaker rate - beat by beat, driven by baroreceptor and gut signals, vagal effect on the heart within 200 milliseconds and over within 1 second.

◦ Circulating glucose, leptin and cholecystokinin - shift firing directly - minutes, sensed through a leaky barrier.

• FAULT REGIMES AND WATCHDOGS.

◦ Diabetic vagal neuropathy: a fixed fast pulse, gastroparesis and doubled cardiac risk, watchdog is the redundant pacemaker drive.

◦ Synucleinopathy and achalasia: alpha synuclein in the dorsal motor nucleus is the earliest Parkinson stage, and loss of nitric oxide pathways stops the lower oesophagus relaxing.


ENTRY 260. PARASYMPATHETIC POSTGANGLIONIC NEURONS (keyed to neuron catalogue Chapter 260)

MODES AND MODE TRANSITIONS:

This is mostly a faithful relay with a very high safety factor, but a subset in the heart and airway walls breaks that rule and runs as a local network, so the register spans relay and local-processor modes.

Its quiet jobs are constricting the pupil, making tears and saliva, slowing the heart and producing an erection.

• THE MODE REGISTER.

◦ Faithful Relay - the Faithful Relay - phasic cells where one preganglionic input fires the cell every time, as in the ciliary ganglion.

◦ Local Network Processing - the Little Reflex - intramural cardiac and airway cells with spontaneous activity and long afterhyperpolarisations running local reflexes.

◦ Nitrergic Volume Relaxation - the Diffuse Relax - a firing state making nitric oxide on demand that relaxes a whole patch of tissue.

• PER-MODE TRANSFER FUNCTIONS.

◦ Faithful Relay: rest about minus 55 millivolts, phasic one to two spikes held by the M current, safety factor above 1, confidence high.

◦ Local Network Processing: spontaneous activity, long afterhyperpolarisations, complete reflexes with the brain disconnected, confidence high.

◦ Nitrergic Volume Relaxation: nitric oxide made at firing and diffusing several cell diameters, action 1 to 10 seconds, confidence high.

• THE TRANSITION TABLE.

◦ Preganglionic spike - rest to Faithful Relay output - muscarinic effect onset 100 to 300 milliseconds, driven from above.

◦ Passing sympathetic noradrenaline - relay damped at the terminal - milliseconds, local alpha2 inhibition not from the parasympathetic command.

◦ Local organ-wall input - into Local Network Processing - milliseconds to seconds, driven by intramural sensory neurons.

• FAULT REGIMES AND WATCHDOGS.

◦ Misrouted regrowth: Adie tonic pupil after ciliary ganglion damage gives light-near dissociation, watchdog is accurate reinnervation which fails.

◦ Nitrergic loss and Chagas: erectile dysfunction after cavernous nerve injury and achalasia from oesophageal nitrergic loss, with Chagas destroying intramural ganglia into megaoesophagus and megacolon.


ENTRY 261. ADRENAL CHROMAFFIN CELLS (Fight-Or-Flight Hormone Cell) (keyed to neuron catalogue Chapter 261)

MODES AND MODE TRANSITIONS:

This is a mode-rich neuroendocrine cell, a sympathetic neuron that became a gland, and it operates on a continuum from low-frequency basal secretion to a splanchnic-driven burst mode during fight-or-flight, with an autonomous chemosensory mode on top.

Volume transmission into the blood is its entire output, so one cell reaches every organ at once, and it deserves the fuller treatment given to the great secretory cells.

• THE MODE REGISTER.

◦ Basal Tonic Secretion - the Trickle Tone - slow spontaneous firing that maintains a low resting catecholamine level in plasma.

◦ Burst Surge Secretion - the Flight Surge - a splanchnic-driven high-rate burst mode that pours a catecholamine surge into the blood.

◦ Autonomous Chemosensory Secretion - the No-Nerve Alarm - secretion driven directly by hypoxia, acidosis and low glucose with no neural instruction, important in the newborn before splanchnic innervation matures.

• PER-MODE TRANSFER FUNCTIONS.

◦ Basal Tonic Secretion: spontaneous firing at 1 to 2 hertz driven by L-type calcium channels and braked by BK potassium channels, plasma catecholamine 0.1 to 0.5 nanomolar, adrenaline fraction about 0.8, confidence high.

◦ Burst Surge Secretion: firing rises to 10 to 20 hertz when acetylcholine opens nicotinic receptors, plasma catecholamine 5 to 10 nanomolar, graded across readily releasable, slowly releasable and sustained reserve pools, fast pool fusion 10 to 50 milliseconds and refilling 5 to 20 seconds, confidence high.

◦ Autonomous Chemosensory Secretion: driven by glucose 2 to 8 millimolar, oxygen 20 to 100 millimetres of mercury, and acidosis, independent of the splanchnic nerve, confidence moderate to high.

• THE TRANSITION TABLE.

◦ Splanchnic acetylcholine 0.2 to 20 hertz - Basal Tonic to Burst Surge Secretion - seconds, thrown from central sympathetic and stress-axis command above; electrical stimulation of the splanchnic nerve reproduces the spontaneous burst pattern.

◦ Falling oxygen, pH or glucose - toward Autonomous Chemosensory Secretion - seconds to minutes, driven locally by the blood the cell bathes in.

◦ Circulating adrenaline and cortisol feedback - trim the gain - minutes, adrenaline acting on the cell's own beta-adrenoceptors and cortisol raising PNMT so more noradrenaline is methylated to adrenaline over hours.

• FAULT REGIMES AND WATCHDOGS.

◦ Pheochromocytoma: a catecholamine-secreting tumour, combined incidence with paraganglioma roughly 3 per million per year, causing episodic hypertension, headache, palpitations and sweating, watchdog is normal growth control and negative feedback that the tumour escapes.

◦ Secretory failure: loss of function causes hypoglycaemia unawareness, because the emergency glucose-defending surge no longer fires.


ENTRY 262. SMALL INTENSELY FLUORESCENT (SIF) CELLS (Signal-Dimming Local Cell) (keyed to neuron catalogue Chapter 262)

MODES AND MODE TRANSITIONS:

This is a graded dopaminergic modulator rather than a spiking relay, so its main axis is not firing pattern but the smooth calcium-driven release of dopamine that dims the ganglion's gain.

Its small size gives a high input resistance, so a tiny current makes a large voltage change.

• THE MODE REGISTER.

◦ Graded Tonic Damping - the Gain Dimmer - continuous calcium-driven dopamine release that turns the relay's gain down.

◦ Muscarinically Boosted Release - the Turned-Up Brake - a raised dopamine turnover when muscarinic agonists act on the cell.

• PER-MODE TRANSFER FUNCTIONS.

◦ Graded Tonic Damping: dopamine into the interstitium at high nanomolar to low micromolar near the release site, much of it graded rather than spike-locked, clearance 0.5 to 5 seconds, confidence moderate.

◦ Muscarinically Boosted Release: muscarinic agonists sharply raise dopamine turnover, glucocorticoid phenotype shift over days sets how much transmitter is made, confidence moderate.

• THE TRANSITION TABLE.

◦ Preganglionic acetylcholine 0.1 to 20 hertz - rest to Graded Tonic Damping - calcium transient 50 to 500 milliseconds, driven from below.

◦ Muscarinic drive - into Boosted Release - seconds, driven by the same preganglionic traffic.

◦ Circulating glucocorticoid - resets transmitter phenotype - days, thrown from the stress axis above.

• FAULT REGIMES AND WATCHDOGS.

◦ Lost damping: a proposed contributor to sympathetic overactivity in hypertension and heart failure, watchdog is the dopamine brake that weakens.

◦ Note: these cells are a candidate origin for small paraganglial tumours, and their dopamine confounds pheochromocytoma testing by raising plasma methoxytyramine.


ENTRY 263. INTRINSIC CARDIAC GANGLION NEURONS (Heartbeat Local-Reflex Neuron) (keyed to neuron catalogue Chapter 263)

MODES AND MODE TRANSITIONS:

This is the little brain of the heart, a local reflex processor whose cells split into a majority phasic filtering mode and a minority adapting mode, filtering high-frequency traffic rather than relaying it.

It can run whole reflex loops without the brain, sensing atrial stretch and adjusting rate, conduction and contraction.

• THE MODE REGISTER.

◦ Phasic Filtering - the Traffic Sieve - about 77 per cent of cells firing one to three spikes at onset then falling silent however hard driven.

◦ Adapting Train - the Fading Report - about 23 per cent firing a decaying train with a shorter afterhyperpolarisation.

◦ Local Reflex Integration - the Little Reflex - a state integrating convergent vagal, sympathetic and sensory input to run a complete arc.

• PER-MODE TRANSFER FUNCTIONS.

◦ Phasic Filtering: rest minus 50 to minus 60 millivolts, threshold about minus 40 millivolts, one to three spikes, input resistance 100 to 400 megaohms, held by the M current, confidence high.

◦ Adapting Train: decaying discharge, shorter afterhyperpolarisation 50 to 300 milliseconds, confidence high.

◦ Local Reflex Integration: convergent integration shifting heart rate by up to plus or minus 40 beats per minute, confidence high.

• THE TRANSITION TABLE.

◦ Vagal acetylcholine 0 to 20 hertz or sympathetic noradrenaline 0 to 10 hertz - rest to firing - nicotinic potential milliseconds, driven from above and locally.

◦ Rising atrial pressure - drives Local Reflex Integration - within a beat, driven by intrinsic afferents from below.

◦ Ischaemic signals and interleukin-6 - shift excitability - seconds to days for remodelling, driven locally.

• FAULT REGIMES AND WATCHDOGS.

◦ Plexus overactivity: pulmonary vein plexuses trigger atrial fibrillation, and ablating them can abolish inducible fibrillation, watchdog is the normal filtering that fails.

◦ Neuropathic loss and remodelling: diabetic loss gives a fixed heart rate and raised sudden-death risk, and post-infarct remodelling promotes ventricular arrhythmia.


ENTRY 264. PELVIC GANGLION NEURONS (Pelvic-Organ Relay Neuron) (keyed to neuron catalogue Chapter 264)

MODES AND MODE TRANSITIONS:

This is a mixed sympathetic-and-parasympathetic ganglion whose cells fall into tonic and phasic firers set by the M current, with a distinct nitrergic volume-relaxation mode, closer to a relay than a filter because most cells lack dendrites.

It is the final output for the bladder, bowel, sphincters, prostate, uterus and erectile tissue.

• THE MODE REGISTER.

◦ Tonic Firing - the Steady Passer - cells firing throughout an input.

◦ Phasic Accommodating - the One-Shot Gate - cells firing one or two spikes then silenced by the M current.

◦ Nitrergic Volume Relaxation - the Diffuse Relax - nitrergic cells relaxing a volume of erectile or sphincter tissue rather than a point.

• PER-MODE TRANSFER FUNCTIONS.

◦ Tonic Firing: rest minus 55 to minus 65 millivolts, threshold about minus 40 millivolts, sustained output, input resistance 150 to 500 megaohms, confidence high.

◦ Phasic Accommodating: one to two spikes held by KCNQ-carried M current, confidence high.

◦ Nitrergic Volume Relaxation: nitric oxide over a diffusion radius of roughly 100 to 200 micrometres, action 0.1 to 5 seconds, nitrergic fraction roughly 30 to 50 per cent of the sacral population, confidence high.

• THE TRANSITION TABLE.

◦ Sacral or lumbar preganglionic acetylcholine 0 to 20 hertz - rest to firing - nicotinic potential 5 to 20 milliseconds, driven from above.

◦ Muscarinic slow excitation - Phasic toward a re-set firing pattern - 2 to 30 seconds, a genuine state change closing the M current.

◦ Nerve growth factor and sex steroids - reset innervation and transcription - hours to days, the organ and the endocrine system instructing the cell.

• FAULT REGIMES AND WATCHDOGS.

◦ Surgical nitrergic death: cavernous nerve injury triggers RhoA and caspase-3 death of nitrergic neurons, causing erectile dysfunction, watchdog is nerve-sparing surgery.

◦ Disconnection and diabetes: spinal cord injury leaves these neurons disconnected, giving detrusor-sphincter dyssynergia, and diabetic neuropathy degrades cholinergic and nitrergic function.


ENTRY 265. AIRWAY, BLADDER, AND PANCREATIC GANGLION NEURONS (Organ-Wall Filtering Neuron) (keyed to neuron catalogue Chapter 265)

MODES AND MODE TRANSITIONS:

This is a deliberately bad relay, a variable-gain synaptic filter whose central mode is arithmetic rather than relay, discarding most incoming commands so the organ sets how loudly it listens to the brain.

Its measured signature is low transmission fidelity, well below one output spike per input spike.

• THE MODE REGISTER.

◦ Low-Fidelity Filtering - the Deaf Relay - the resting mode passing only a fraction of preganglionic spikes.

◦ Tachykinin-Boosted Gain - the Opened Ear - a raised-gain state set by sensory peptide input for minutes.

◦ Dendritic Summation - the Vote Counter - in airway neurons, a state where summed subthreshold inputs decide whether threshold is crossed.

• PER-MODE TRANSFER FUNCTIONS.

◦ Low-Fidelity Filtering: rest minus 50 to minus 60 millivolts, single excitatory potential 5 to 20 millivolts against a threshold near minus 40, baseline fidelity 0.1 to 0.5, input resistance 100 to 500 megaohms, confidence high.

◦ Tachykinin-Boosted Gain: substance P and neurokinin A raise fidelity towards 0.8, gain change over 30 seconds to 10 minutes, confidence high.

◦ Dendritic Summation: dendritic geometry decides threshold crossing in airway cells, confidence high.

• THE TRANSITION TABLE.

◦ Preganglionic acetylcholine 0 to 20 hertz - rest to filtered output - potential decay 10 to 40 milliseconds, driven from above.

◦ Sensory tachykinin release - Low-Fidelity Filtering to Boosted Gain - 30 seconds to 10 minutes, driven locally by organ-wall sensory endings, not from central command.

◦ Glucose 3 to 15 millimolar - drives the pancreatic population directly - seconds, sensed locally.

• FAULT REGIMES AND WATCHDOGS.

◦ Asthmatic filter opening: under tachykinin drive the filter opens so discarded vagal traffic narrows the airway, which is why tiotropium works, watchdog is the normal high threshold.

◦ Bladder and pancreatic faults: rising purinergic gain gives urgency in overactive bladder, and loss of pancreatic input removes the cephalic phase of insulin secretion.


ENTRY 266. INTRINSIC PRIMARY AFFERENT NEURONS (IPANS), ALSO CALLED ENTERIC SENSORY NEURONS (Gut-Content Sensing Neuron) (keyed to neuron catalogue Chapter 266)

MODES AND MODE TRANSITIONS:

This is a polymodal enteric sensor of the AH type, where AH means afterhyperpolarising, whose defining feature is a long post-spike trough that acts as automatic gain control, plus a short-term memory mode of mutual excitation.

One cell answers distortion of the lining, muscle tension and food chemistry.

• THE MODE REGISTER.

◦ Gated Sensing - the Braked Report - firing followed by a long trough that limits how fast the cell can fire again.

◦ Mutual Excitation Memory - the Held Chatter - a state where slow potentials hold a group of IPANs excited for about a minute.

• PER-MODE TRANSFER FUNCTIONS.

◦ Gated Sensing: rest minus 60 millivolts, threshold minus 35 millivolts, broad spike then a trough of 1 to 20 seconds from calcium-activated potassium channels, brake about 25 millivolts per micromolar calcium, confidence high.

◦ Mutual Excitation Memory: slow synaptic potentials sustain excitation across a coupled group for tens of seconds, confidence high.

• THE TRANSITION TABLE.

◦ Mucosal distortion, muscle tension or luminal serotonin - rest to Gated Sensing - membrane about 10 milliseconds, driven from below.

◦ Convergent IPAN input - Gated Sensing to Mutual Excitation Memory - held for about a minute, driven locally within the enteric network.

• FAULT REGIMES AND WATCHDOGS.

◦ Sensitisation: gives the pain of irritable bowel syndrome, watchdog is the long afterhyperpolarisation acting as gain control.

◦ Loss: losing these cells stops the gut starting propulsion.


ENTRY 267. INTERNEURONS (Gut-Circuit Bridging Neuron) (keyed to neuron catalogue Chapter 267)

MODES AND MODE TRANSITIONS:

This is a fast S-type enteric relay, where S means synaptic, with a brief fast-excitation mode and a slow P2Y1 sustained mode that holds a chain excited long enough for relaxation to outrun the contraction behind it.

They talk only to other nerve cells, forming chains running lengthways along the gut.

• THE MODE REGISTER.

◦ Fast Relay - the Quick Pass - brief nicotinic, 5-HT3 and P2X excitation passing a spike to the next cell.

◦ Slow Sustained Drive - the Held Chain - a P2Y1-mediated state holding a chain excited for hundreds of milliseconds.

• PER-MODE TRANSFER FUNCTIONS.

◦ Fast Relay: rest minus 60 millivolts, threshold minus 40 millivolts, fast potential 15 to 30 milliseconds, chain reach 4 to 7 centimetres, confidence high.

◦ Slow Sustained Drive: P2Y1 potential 0.3 to 2 seconds, confidence high.

• THE TRANSITION TABLE.

◦ Fast synaptic input - rest to Fast Relay - membrane 6 milliseconds, driven from the previous cell.

◦ P2Y1 receptor activation - Fast Relay to Slow Sustained Drive - 0.3 to 2 seconds, driven locally by adenosine triphosphate.

◦ Sympathetic noradrenaline - presynaptic inhibition silencing a chain - milliseconds, thrown from the sympathetic system above without touching muscle.

• FAULT REGIMES AND WATCHDOGS.

◦ Fragmented propagation: losing these relays gives slow-transit constipation, watchdog is redundancy across parallel chains.

◦ Chagas destruction: Chagas disease destroys them, producing megacolon.


ENTRY 268. ASCENDING INTERNEURONS (Behind-The-Food Relay Neuron) (keyed to neuron catalogue Chapter 268)

MODES AND MODE TRANSITIONS:

This is an oral-directed S-type relay with a fast nicotinic mode and a tachykinin slow mode that biases the gut towards repeating the movement it just made, a genuine short-term memory.

Its axon runs towards the mouth so the muscle behind a bolus contracts.

• THE MODE REGISTER.

◦ Fast Relay - the Quick Pass - fast nicotinic potentials carrying the ascending signal.

◦ Tachykinin Primed Drive - the Repeat Bias - an NK3-mediated slow depolarisation that lowers threshold and favours repeating the last movement.

• PER-MODE TRANSFER FUNCTIONS.

◦ Fast Relay: rest minus 58 millivolts, threshold minus 42 millivolts, nicotinic potentials 10 to 25 millivolts, 10 to 30 impulses per second, relay delay 8 to 15 milliseconds, confidence high.

◦ Tachykinin Primed Drive: slow depolarisation lowering threshold by up to 6 millivolts, tachykinin potential 5 to 20 seconds, confidence high.

• THE TRANSITION TABLE.

◦ Nicotinic input - rest to Fast Relay - membrane 7 milliseconds, driven from the previous cell.

◦ NK3 tachykinin activation - Fast Relay to Tachykinin Primed Drive - 5 to 20 seconds, driven locally.

◦ Presynaptic alpha-2 adrenergic drive - release vetoed without changing firing - milliseconds, thrown from the sympathetic system above.

• FAULT REGIMES AND WATCHDOGS.

◦ Opioid silencing: silenced by opioids, giving constipation, watchdog is the redundant relay chain.

◦ Chagas destruction: destroyed in Chagas disease and megacolon, where contraction behind the bolus fails.


ENTRY 269. DESCENDING INTERNEURONS (Ahead-Of-The-Food Relay Neuron) (keyed to neuron catalogue Chapter 269)

MODES AND MODE TRANSITIONS:

This is an anally-directed S-type relay whose signature is a long P2Y1 slow potential acting as a chemical timer, turning a millisecond spike into a second of readiness so the chain becomes a delay line.

Its axon runs towards the anus so the muscle in front of a bolus relaxes before the lump arrives.

• THE MODE REGISTER.

◦ Fast Relay - the Quick Pass - fast nicotinic and P2X excitation.

◦ Slow Timer Drive - the Delay Line - a P2Y1 slow potential that keeps a once-started chain running.

• PER-MODE TRANSFER FUNCTIONS.

◦ Fast Relay: rest minus 60 millivolts, threshold minus 40 millivolts, membrane 8 milliseconds, confidence high.

◦ Slow Timer Drive: P2Y1 potential 0.3 to 2 seconds up to 30 millivolts, nitric oxide acting on anything within about 100 micrometres, wave travel 1 to 4 millimetres per second, confidence high.

• THE TRANSITION TABLE.

◦ Fast input - rest to Fast Relay - milliseconds, driven from the previous cell.

◦ P2Y1 activation - Fast Relay to Slow Timer Drive - 0.3 to 2 seconds, driven locally by adenosine triphosphate.

◦ Presynaptic alpha-2 adrenergic drive - release inhibited - milliseconds, thrown from the sympathetic system above.

• FAULT REGIMES AND WATCHDOGS.

◦ Failed inhibition: failed descending inhibition is the core lesion of achalasia and Hirschsprung disease, watchdog is the redundant nitrergic drive.

◦ Diabetic decay: its decay in diabetes gives gastroparesis.


ENTRY 270. EXCITATORY MUSCLE MOTOR NEURONS (keyed to neuron catalogue Chapter 270)

MODES AND MODE TRANSITIONS:

This is the final S-type path to gut muscle, firing in trains between silent intervals, with a tachykinin slow mode that adds sustained drive, and it writes into a shared electrical medium rather than commanding muscle directly.

Its acetylcholine acts first on interstitial cells of Cajal, which pass it on through gap junctions.

• THE MODE REGISTER.

◦ Train Firing - the Contract Volley - trains of impulses between silent intervals that drive contraction.

◦ Tachykinin Sustained Drive - the Held Push - a slow tachykinin depolarisation adding to acetylcholine drive.

• PER-MODE TRANSFER FUNCTIONS.

◦ Train Firing: fast nicotinic potentials 10 to 25 millivolts, trough of tens of milliseconds, 5 to 30 impulses per second, acetylcholine gain about 9 millivolts per unit onto M2 and M3 receptors, confidence high.

◦ Tachykinin Sustained Drive: substance P onto NK1 and NK2 receptors, tachykinin gain about 7 millivolts per unit, clearance about 2.5 seconds, confidence high.

• THE TRANSITION TABLE.

◦ Nicotinic input - silence to Train Firing - acetylcholine clearance 60 milliseconds, driven from interneurons above in the enteric circuit.

◦ Tachykinin release - Train Firing to Tachykinin Sustained Drive - about 2.5 seconds, driven locally.

◦ Presynaptic alpha-2 adrenergic drive - output reduced - milliseconds, thrown from the sympathetic system above.

• FAULT REGIMES AND WATCHDOGS.

◦ Flaccid bowel: losing cholinergic drive gives the flaccid bowel of pseudo-obstruction, watchdog is the balancing inhibitory motor neuron.

◦ Spasm: unopposed drive gives spasm and a tight sphincter.
LAYER 6 MODES MANUSCRIPT - ENTRIES FOR NEURON CATALOGUE CHAPTERS 271 TO 301

(Enteric nervous system, other peripheral and special types, and neurons named for what they encode. Written to the entry specification. Plain text, British spelling.)


ENTRY 271. INHIBITORY MUSCLE MOTOR NEURONS (keyed to neuron catalogue Chapter 271)

MODES AND MODE TRANSITIONS:

This is a two-to-three mode switch built around a default of continuous inhibition, because gut muscle carries its own rhythm and tone and must be actively told to relax.

Its unusual feature is that its most important act is to fall silent, since switching this cell off is what lets muscle contract.

• THE MODE REGISTER.

◦ Tonic Inhibitory Hold - the Open Gate - steady low-rate firing that keeps a sphincter or muscle segment relaxed by default.

◦ Phasic Relaxation Burst - the Let-Go - a volley that produces the two-phase inhibitory junction potential of receptive relaxation ahead of a bolus.

◦ Commanded Silence - the Clamp-Ready Pause - active shut-off of the cell so the muscle it guards is allowed to contract.

• PER-MODE TRANSFER FUNCTIONS.

◦ In the Open Gate the cell fires at roughly one to a few impulses per second, holding muscle near minus 55 millivolts (a millivolt being one thousandth of a volt) through a maintained trickle of nitric oxide, a gas made on demand.

◦ In the Let-Go a burst near 20 hertz (cycles per second) drives a fast purinergic dip at about 120 milliseconds and a slow nitrergic trough at about 900 milliseconds, summing to a junction potential of 10 to 25 millivolts.

◦ In the Clamp-Ready Pause firing drops toward zero, nitric oxide production stops within seconds, and the muscle depolarises toward its contractile threshold; confidence high on direction, moderate on exact rates.

• THE TRANSITION TABLE.

◦ Descending interneuron and vagal excitation - Open Gate to Let-Go - tens of milliseconds; often thrown from above during swallowing and receptive relaxation.

◦ Withdrawal of excitatory drive plus excitatory motor neuron activity - Open Gate to Clamp-Ready Pause - hundreds of milliseconds, set by the peristaltic programme.

◦ Return of tonic input - Pause back to Open Gate - hundreds of milliseconds.

• FAULT REGIMES AND WATCHDOGS.

◦ Permanent silence from loss of these cells is a genuine pathology, not a used mode, and its signature is a sphincter that will not open, seen in achalasia, pyloric stenosis, gastroparesis and Hirschsprung disease.

◦ The biological watchdog is the balance between spindle glia and muscularis macrophages, whose reactive oxygen destroys nitric oxide within seconds, so glial protection failing is the warning condition.


ENTRY 272. SECRETOMOTOR NEURONS (keyed to neuron catalogue Chapter 272)

MODES AND MODE TRANSITIONS:

This is a faithful-follower cell with three secretory-drive modes, because it is an S neuron, meaning a synaptic type with no long silent period after a spike, so it tracks its input rather than imposing a rhythm of its own.

• THE MODE REGISTER.

◦ Basal Secretory Trickle - the Background Wash - low ongoing firing that keeps a thin protective fluid film over the crypts.

◦ Stimulated Secretory Burst - the Feeding Flush - fast excitatory-postsynaptic-potential-driven firing that pushes chloride and water into the gut.

◦ Sympathetically Braked Silence - the Fasting Shut-off - alpha-2 adrenergic inhibition that halts secretion during exertion or blood loss.

• PER-MODE TRANSFER FUNCTIONS.

◦ In the Background Wash the cell rests near minus 58 millivolts and fires at a few hertz, sustaining a low chloride flux measured in microamperes per square centimetre of lining.

◦ In the Feeding Flush nicotinic fast excitatory postsynaptic potentials (brief voltage nudges from an incoming signal) of 3 to 25 millivolts arrive at 0 to 40 hertz and reliably trigger spikes, raising crypt-cell cyclic adenosine monophosphate over about 8 seconds and calcium over about 1 second.

◦ In the Fasting Shut-off firing falls toward zero under alpha-2 drive at 0 to 10 hertz and chloride flux collapses; confidence high.

• THE TRANSITION TABLE.

◦ Sensory and interneuron input reporting luminal content - Background Wash to Feeding Flush - milliseconds to seconds.

◦ Sympathetic alpha-2 activation - either active mode to Fasting Shut-off - milliseconds; thrown from above by exercise, stress and haemorrhage.

◦ Slow excitatory postsynaptic potentials from serotonin and tachykinins - lift baseline gain over 10 to 60 seconds without a hard mode jump.

• FAULT REGIMES AND WATCHDOGS.

◦ Runaway firing is a used-looking but pathological regime driving secretory diarrhoea, as in cholera, where protein-kinase-A changes leave the cell hyperexcitable for 1 to 7 days.

◦ The watchdog is sympathetic and peptide (neuropeptide Y and somatostatin) restraint; when both fail the boundary signature is unstoppable fluid loss.


ENTRY 273. SECRETOMOTOR AND VASODILATOR NEURONS (keyed to neuron catalogue Chapter 273)

MODES AND MODE TRANSITIONS:

This is a three-mode allocator that broadcasts one spike train down a forked axon to two tissues at once, the crypts and the arterioles, so its modes are levels of a coupled secretion-and-flow drive rather than independent channels.

• THE MODE REGISTER.

◦ Between-Meals Baseline - the Idle Coupling - continuous low firing that keeps a resting trickle of both secretion and blood flow.

◦ Postprandial Dual Broadcast - the Supply-and-Demand Pulse - raised firing that opens fluid secretion and matching arteriolar dilation together.

◦ Sympathetic Shutdown - the Shock Silence - simultaneous halt of both jobs under noradrenaline.

• PER-MODE TRANSFER FUNCTIONS.

◦ In the Idle Coupling the cell fires tonically at 0.5 to 2 hertz near minus 57 millivolts, holding flow near 1.0 times resting.

◦ In the Supply-and-Demand Pulse slow excitatory postsynaptic potentials lift firing for tens of seconds, driving secretion up and flow up to about 3 times resting, with arteriolar smooth muscle relaxing over 3 to 6 seconds.

◦ In the Shock Silence alpha-2 inhibition at 0 to 15 hertz drives firing toward zero and both outputs collapse; confidence high on coupling, low on absolute rates.

• THE TRANSITION TABLE.

◦ Luminal nutrient signal relayed through sensory neurons and interneurons - Idle to Pulse - seconds.

◦ Sympathetic noradrenergic drive - any mode to Shock Silence - milliseconds; thrown from above during exercise and haemorrhage.

◦ Clearance of transmitter over about 5 seconds - Pulse back toward Idle.

• FAULT REGIMES AND WATCHDOGS.

◦ Uncoupling of the two branches, from chronic inflammation, is a fault whose signature is secretion without matching flow and villus-tip drying during a meal.

◦ The watchdog is the internal deficit accumulator of water demanded but not supplied; a persistent deficit warns of impending villus-tip ischaemia.


ENTRY 274. VASOMOTOR NEURONS (keyed to neuron catalogue Chapter 274)

MODES AND MODE TRANSITIONS:

This is a dilator-only cell with a three-mode register, honestly asymmetric because the enteric nervous system (the gut's own nerve net) supplies the accelerator while the constrictor command arrives from outside along sympathetic nerves.

• THE MODE REGISTER.

◦ Resting Tone Tick - the Background Tick - continuous low firing that sets a baseline vessel calibre.

◦ Postprandial Dilation - the Working Flush - sustained slow-potential-driven firing that widens the arteriole to feed a working mucosa.

◦ Sympathetically Silenced - the Held Breath - noradrenaline hyperpolarising the cell so it cannot dilate.

• PER-MODE TRANSFER FUNCTIONS.

◦ In the Background Tick low tonic firing near minus 55 millivolts holds arteriolar radius near 12 micrometres (a micrometre being one thousandth of a millimetre), and flow scales with radius to the fourth power.

◦ In the Working Flush slow excitatory postsynaptic potentials sustain dilation for tens of seconds toward a radius near 20 micrometres, multiplying flow severalfold.

◦ In the Held Breath noradrenaline at 0 to 20 hertz silences the cell and radius falls toward 6 micrometres; confidence moderate given the poor human count of this class.

• THE TRANSITION TABLE.

◦ Sensory and interneuron drive plus local metabolites (carbon dioxide, adenosine) - Tick to Flush - seconds.

◦ Sympathetic alpha-2 hyperpolarisation - any mode to Held Breath - milliseconds; thrown from above in shock and cardiac failure.

◦ Nitric oxide clearance under one second and smooth-muscle relaxation over 3 seconds set the return timescale.

• FAULT REGIMES AND WATCHDOGS.

◦ Unopposed sympathetic drive in shock is a pathological silencing whose boundary signature is non-occlusive mesenteric ischaemia, the gut dying with its arteries still open.

◦ The watchdog is the parallel dilator system of extrinsic calcitonin-gene-related-peptide fibres; when both dilator systems are down, villus tips fail first.


ENTRY 275. INTESTINOFUGAL NEURONS (keyed to neuron catalogue Chapter 275)

MODES AND MODE TRANSITIONS:

This is a tonically active load reporter with a graded three-mode register, unusual because it is mechanosensitive at the cell body itself and sends its axon out of the gut to a sympathetic ganglion, converting wall tension into a long-range command to slow down.

• THE MODE REGISTER.

◦ Baseline Tonic Report - the Idle Tick - slow firing in an empty gut, a running null reading.

◦ Distension-Graded Firing - the Load Ramp - rate rising in proportion to circumferential wall tension.

◦ Contraction-Locked Bursting - the Contraction Beat - bursts phase-locked to each passing contraction, reporting rhythm as well as load.

• PER-MODE TRANSFER FUNCTIONS.

◦ In the Idle Tick the cell fires near 0.5 spikes per second at a resting minus 50 to minus 60 millivolts.

◦ In the Load Ramp firing climbs at roughly one extra spike per second per gram of wall tension to a ceiling of about 12 to 15 hertz, with an adaptation term that decays a sustained distension over 5 to 30 seconds.

◦ In the Contraction Beat firing is modulated in step with the muscle cycle, adding a rhythmic envelope; confidence moderate, from animal recordings.

• THE TRANSITION TABLE.

◦ Rising wall tension sensed at the soma - Idle Tick to Load Ramp - membrane timescale of about 20 milliseconds.

◦ Onset of a contractile wave - Load Ramp to Contraction Beat - locked to the gut's own rhythm.

◦ The full reflex loop, distension to inhibition of a distant segment, carries an axonal conduction delay of 200 to 800 milliseconds and must be modelled as a delay line, not an instant coupling.

• FAULT REGIMES AND WATCHDOGS.

◦ Overactivity locks the inhibitory loop on and stops the gut, a major component of post-operative ileus (paralysed bowel after surgery).

◦ Underactivity or loss, in diabetes, ageing or Chagas disease, lets one segment keep loading another; after spinal cord injury the loop runs unsupervised, the warning condition being loss of central supervision at the ganglion.


ENTRY 276. DOGIEL TYPE I (keyed to neuron catalogue Chapter 276)

MODES AND MODE TRANSITIONS:

This is the default enteric chassis and a fast-in, fast-out follower, so its register is deliberately simple: it is an S neuron with a large fast nicotinic input and only a brief afterhyperpolarisation, meaning the short silent dip after a spike.

• THE MODE REGISTER.

◦ Ready Silence - the Waiting Chassis - resting and quiet until a synaptic volley arrives.

◦ Faithful Following Train - the Faithful Relay - firing that tracks its input up to high rates with no long pause between spikes.

◦ Slow-Potential Primed State - the Primed Chassis - a raised-excitability mode set by slow excitatory postsynaptic potentials.

• PER-MODE TRANSFER FUNCTIONS.

◦ In the Waiting Chassis the cell rests near minus 55 millivolts with threshold near minus 35.

◦ In the Faithful Relay fast nicotinic potentials of 5 to 30 millivolts, big enough to fire the cell singly, drive trains up to 30 to 40 hertz with a refractory period of 2 to 5 milliseconds.

◦ In the Primed Chassis tachykinin and serotonin slow potentials of 2 to 15 millivolts close potassium channels and lift excitability for 10 to 60 seconds through protein kinases; confidence high.

• THE TRANSITION TABLE.

◦ Nicotinic volley - Waiting to Faithful Relay - tens of milliseconds.

◦ Slow excitatory postsynaptic potential - Waiting or Relay to Primed - seconds.

◦ Presynaptic inhibition from noradrenaline and opioids - scales input by a factor between 0 and 1, dropping the cell back toward Waiting; sympathetic drive here is thrown from above.

• FAULT REGIMES AND WATCHDOGS.

◦ Congenital absence (Hirschsprung disease) and destruction (Chagas disease) are losses, not modes, whose signature is a segment that cannot act.

◦ Post-inflammatory hyperexcitability with basket remodelling is the warning condition; the watchdog is muscularis-macrophage support, which these neurons need to survive.


ENTRY 277. DOGIEL TYPE II (keyed to neuron catalogue Chapter 277)

MODES AND MODE TRANSITIONS:

This is one of the gut's great mode-rich cells, an AH neuron whose defining afterhyperpolarisation (a hyperpolarising dip lasting seconds) makes it a single-cell memory, and whose self-exciting assemblies give the network a working memory measured in tens of seconds.

• THE MODE REGISTER.

◦ Reluctant Rest - the Deep Quiet - sitting hyperpolarised and silent until strongly recruited.

◦ Post-Spike Sulk - the Long Sulk - the enforced silence following each action potential while calcium-activated potassium channels stay open.

◦ Recruited Assembly - the Held Chorus - a slow-potential-switched state in which a group of these cells excite one another and stay active after the stimulus has gone.

• PER-MODE TRANSFER FUNCTIONS.

◦ In the Deep Quiet the cell rests near minus 62 millivolts and does not follow ordinary input.

◦ In the Long Sulk a broad 2 to 4 millisecond spike admits calcium that opens intermediate-conductance potassium channels, forbidding firing for a medium dip near 0.8 seconds or a slow dip averaging about 4 seconds in pig, ranging 2 to 20 seconds.

◦ In the Held Chorus slow excitatory postsynaptic potentials cut the afterhyperpolarisation conductance by up to 80 percent for tens of seconds, converting a cell firing once every several seconds into one firing steadily; confidence high.

• THE TRANSITION TABLE.

◦ Serotonin from enterochromaffin cells plus wall distortion - Deep Quiet to firing - milliseconds.

◦ Each spike - firing to Long Sulk - set by calcium clearance over 2 to 20 seconds.

◦ Tachykinin slow potential, or protein-kinase-A modulation - into the Held Chorus - tens of seconds; this can be nudged by neuromodulators from above and by immune signalling.

• FAULT REGIMES AND WATCHDOGS.

◦ Post-infectious hyperexcitability, where the afterhyperpolarisation shrinks and outlasts the inflammation by weeks, is a used-looking but pathological regime and a leading candidate for post-infectious irritable bowel syndrome.

◦ Blocking the intermediate-conductance potassium channel removes the brake entirely and gives continuous firing; the watchdog is that channel plus the near-complete glial wrapping that buffers each calcium load.


ENTRY 278. DOGIEL TYPE III (keyed to neuron catalogue Chapter 278)

MODES AND MODE TRANSITIONS:

This is a presumed S-type relay interneuron that computes with dendritic distance, and because it has never been recorded directly its register is given at the minimal defensible level with a low-confidence note.

• THE MODE REGISTER.

◦ Ready Silence - the Waiting Relay - quiet until driven, the presumed default.

◦ Distance-Weighted Relay Firing - the Weighing Relay - firing set by inputs discounted along the long tapering dendrites.

◦ Slow-Potential Gain-Raised State - the Primed Relay - raised excitability from slow synaptic events.

• PER-MODE TRANSFER FUNCTIONS.

◦ In the Waiting Relay the cell rests near minus 58 to minus 76 millivolts with threshold near minus 40.

◦ In the Weighing Relay fast nicotinic events of 3.5 to 16.5 millivolts are filtered by a dendritic length constant of 200 to 300 micrometres, so a distant synapse delivers a fraction of its voltage, and output runs at 0 to 30 hertz.

◦ In the Primed Relay slow events lasting 10 to 300 seconds raise gain by a factor of 1.0 to 2.0; confidence low throughout, inferred from morphology and the general enteric S class.

• THE TRANSITION TABLE.

◦ Nicotinic input from sensory cells and interneurons - Waiting to Weighing - milliseconds.

◦ Slow synaptic event - into Primed - tens to hundreds of seconds.

◦ Withdrawal of drive - back to Waiting - membrane timescale about 20 milliseconds.

• FAULT REGIMES AND WATCHDOGS.

◦ No distinctive fault regime is documented for this poorly studied type; the honest statement is that loss of cholinergic myenteric interneurons breaks the reflex chain and contributes to pseudo-obstruction, but a type-specific boundary signature is not established.


ENTRY 279. FILAMENTOUS NEURONS (keyed to neuron catalogue Chapter 279)

MODES AND MODE TRANSITIONS:

This is a fair-minded load counter with a mostly two-mode register, an S neuron whose fourteen short filaments deliver roughly equal weight, so it sums distension over seconds and holds a rate encoding accumulated load.

• THE MODE REGISTER.

◦ Baseline Tonic Report - the Idle Count - slow steady firing at rest.

◦ Pressure-Graded Tonic Ramp - the Load Count - firing rising with intraluminal pressure.

• PER-MODE TRANSFER FUNCTIONS.

◦ In the Idle Count the cell fires near 0.5 hertz at a resting minus 58 to minus 76 millivolts.

◦ In the Load Count firing rises with pressure over 0 to 40 millimetres of mercury toward about 20 hertz, integrated over 1 to 10 seconds, and in the intestinofugal members carried out of the gut with an extramural delay of 40 to 1500 milliseconds; confidence low to moderate.

• THE TRANSITION TABLE.

◦ Rising wall pressure on the filaments - Idle Count to Load Count - integration over 1 to 10 seconds.

◦ Falling pressure - back toward Idle Count - same timescale.

◦ Peptide co-release from varicosities lifts prevertebral-ganglion excitability for tens of seconds, a slow gain change layered on the rate.

• FAULT REGIMES AND WATCHDOGS.

◦ Chronic distension and partial obstruction shift activity and peptide cargo, a plastic drift rather than a used mode.

◦ Loss of this class abolishes the reflex by which a distended loop inhibits its neighbours, absent outright in the aganglionic bowel of Hirschsprung disease.


ENTRY 280. DENDRITIC TYPE II NEURONS (keyed to neuron catalogue Chapter 280)

MODES AND MODE TRANSITIONS:

This is the bearded variant of the gut's sensing cell, an AH neuron like its plain type II cousin, so it carries the same calcium-controlled slow-afterhyperpolarisation memory, with its human function still uncertain and noted as such.

• THE MODE REGISTER.

◦ Reluctant Rest - the Deep Quiet - silent until recruited.

◦ Post-Spike Slump - the Long Sulk - the seconds-long silence enforced after each spike.

◦ Recruited Assembly - the Held Chorus - a slow-potential-switched, self-reinforcing active state.

• PER-MODE TRANSFER FUNCTIONS.

◦ In the Deep Quiet the cell rests near minus 65 millivolts, threshold near minus 40.

◦ In the Long Sulk a broad 2.9 millisecond spike opens calcium-activated potassium channels, forbidding firing for a slump averaging about 7 seconds over a 2 to 10 second range.

◦ In the Held Chorus a slow synaptic event closes those channels for minutes through protein kinase C, roughly quadrupling output at the same drive; confidence moderate to high, largely by analogy with type II.

• THE TRANSITION TABLE.

◦ Mucosal serotonin (0.1 to 10 micromolar) and stretch - Deep Quiet to firing - milliseconds.

◦ Each spike - firing to Long Sulk - calcium clearance over seconds.

◦ Tachykinin slow event - into the Held Chorus - 10 to 300 seconds; can be sensitised by mast-cell mediators.

• FAULT REGIMES AND WATCHDOGS.

◦ Post-infectious hyperexcitability from a shrunken slump is a pathological regime tied to irritable bowel syndrome; loss of the cell removes the peristaltic trigger.

◦ The watchdog is heavy glial wrapping and calcium buffering; a separate warning is accumulation of misfolded alpha-synuclein years before Parkinsonian tremor.


ENTRY 281. CAROTID BODY AND AORTIC BODY GLOMUS CELL-ASSOCIATED AFFERENTS (keyed to neuron catalogue Chapter 281)

MODES AND MODE TRANSITIONS:

This is a two-mode alarm afferent that does not own its transducer, since the neighbouring glomus cell senses oxygen and floods the ending with transmitter, and the ending barely adapts because it is a siren, not a gauge.

• THE MODE REGISTER.

◦ Room-Air Idle Tick - the Standing Watch - a few impulses per second in normally oxygenated blood.

◦ Hypoxic Alarm Escalation - the Wake-Up Call - steeply rising, near-non-adapting firing as arterial oxygen falls.

• PER-MODE TRANSFER FUNCTIONS.

◦ In the Standing Watch the ending fires at 1 to 4 impulses per second, receiving adenosine triphosphate and acetylcholine from glomus cells.

◦ In the Wake-Up Call firing climbs along a hyperbola with a half-maximal oxygen pressure near 40 millimetres of mercury and a Hill exponent near 2, turning steeply below about 60 millimetres of mercury toward a ceiling of 60 to 100 impulses per second, with the whole reflex complete in 1 to 3 seconds; confidence high on shape.

• THE TRANSITION TABLE.

◦ Falling arterial oxygen, rising carbon dioxide or falling pH - Standing Watch to Wake-Up Call - a few milliseconds of synaptic delay.

◦ Restored oxygen - back to Standing Watch - seconds.

◦ Nitric oxide from surrounding cells damps the ending by volume transmission, a modulatory scaling rather than a mode jump.

• FAULT REGIMES AND WATCHDOGS.

◦ Chronic overactivity is a maladaptive but real regime in sleep apnoea and heart failure, driving sympathetic overdrive and resistant hypertension; removing one carotid body cut systolic pressure by about 26 millimetres of mercury in some resistant patients.

◦ The watchdog is type II sustentacular cells; repeated hypoxia sensitises the pathway and enlarges the organ through the oxygen-sensing factor HIF-2 alpha, the warning condition.


ENTRY 282. MERKEL CELLS (keyed to neuron catalogue Chapter 282)

MODES AND MODE TRANSITIONS:

This is a receptosecretory skin cell, not a neuron, and it is a three-mode mechanosensor whose partnership with an afferent produces the slowly adapting type 1 response, written SA1, that resolves edges and curvature; its modes are secretory and mechanical, not spiking rhythms.

• THE MODE REGISTER.

◦ Quiescent Rest - the Untouched Quiet - no release with the skin at rest.

◦ Dynamic Onset Burst - the First-Contact Flurry - a high-rate discharge while the skin is being indented.

◦ Static Sustained Hold - the Steady Read - maintained lower-rate firing during a held displacement.

• PER-MODE TRANSFER FUNCTIONS.

◦ In the Untouched Quiet the releasable serotonin pool is full and the afferent is near silent.

◦ In the First-Contact Flurry the mechanically gated channel PIEZO2 (a stretch-opened pore) inactivates in about 10 milliseconds, driving the SA1 discharge near 100 impulses per second, with dynamic gain near 20 impulses per second per millimetre per second of indentation.

◦ In the Steady Read firing settles to 10 to 40 impulses per second with a static gain near 25 impulses per second per millimetre, sustained partly by PIEZO2 in the nerve ending; confidence high, the two-receptor split being well supported.

• THE TRANSITION TABLE.

◦ Onset of indentation past about 10 micrometres - Untouched Quiet to First-Contact Flurry - about 10 milliseconds.

◦ Indentation held constant - Flurry to Steady Read - over the PIEZO2 inactivation time.

◦ Release of pressure - back to Untouched Quiet - with slow adaptation over 1 to 10 seconds; cooling raises firing across modes.

• FAULT REGIMES AND WATCHDOGS.

◦ Loss of PIEZO2 function abolishes touch and limb-position sense while pain survives, a definitional failure rather than a mode.

◦ Malignant change gives Merkel cell carcinoma at about 0.7 cases per 100,000 people yearly; the watchdog is the weeks-to-months replacement of the cell population by local progenitors.


ENTRY 283. PINEALOCYTES (keyed to neuron catalogue Chapter 283)

MODES AND MODE TRANSITIONS:

This is a non-neuronal, non-spiking endocrine cell and, at heart, a two-mode day-night secretory switch whose output is graded internal messenger and secreted hormone, measured in picograms per millilitre rather than impulses per second, with the duration of the night mode encoding the length of the night.

• THE MODE REGISTER.

◦ Day Silence - the Daylight Rest - near-zero melatonin production while sympathetic drive is withheld.

◦ Night Secretion - the Night Report - sustained melatonin synthesis and release through the hours of darkness.

◦ Light-Struck Collapse - the Lights-On Cutoff - abrupt shutdown of production when light falls at night.

• PER-MODE TRANSFER FUNCTIONS.

◦ In the Daylight Rest the suprachiasmatic clock holds noradrenaline off, cyclic adenosine monophosphate stays low, and the rate-limiting enzyme arylalkylamine N-acetyltransferase (written AANAT) is scarce.

◦ In the Night Report noradrenaline at beta-1 adrenoceptors raises cyclic adenosine monophosphate over 5 to 15 minutes, AANAT rises many-fold, and plasma melatonin peaks near 60 to 80 picograms per millilitre for an 8 to 10 hour pulse.

◦ In the Lights-On Cutoff AANAT is destroyed by the proteasome within minutes, collapsing output; confidence high, this being the classic finding.

• THE TRANSITION TABLE.

◦ Dusk, gating the sympathetic pathway on through the superior cervical ganglion - Daylight Rest to Night Report - minutes; thrown from above by the suprachiasmatic clock.

◦ Nocturnal light of the order of tens of lux - Night Report to Light-Struck Collapse - minutes.

◦ Dawn - Night Report to Daylight Rest - governed by melatonin half-life of 30 to 50 minutes.

• FAULT REGIMES AND WATCHDOGS.

◦ Gland loss or sympathetic denervation (as in Horner syndrome) abolishes the rhythm entirely, a loss rather than a mode.

◦ The warning condition is a single bright light at 2 in the morning collapsing the night pulse; the biological safeguard is duration coding, since season is read from pulse length, not height.


ENTRY 284. PARANEURONS (keyed to neuron catalogue Chapter 284)

MODES AND MODE TRANSITIONS:

This is a family rather than a single cell, so its register is stated along the axes the class actually varies on, secretory drive and delivery route, since a paraneuron senses a stimulus and answers by secreting, but lacks an axon; the modes below are shared idioms across gut, airway, taste, carotid body, skin and pineal members.

• THE MODE REGISTER.

◦ Basal Surveillance - the Standing Sample - low resting secretion while the sensor watches its surface.

◦ Stimulated Release - the Answer Pulse - stimulus-triggered discharge of granule contents.

◦ Wired Reflex-Speed Delivery - the Fast Whisper - synaptic release onto a named afferent in milliseconds (as in Merkel, glomus, taste and neuropod cells).

◦ Volume Endocrine Delivery - the Slow Broadcast - release into blood acting over seconds to hours (as in hormone-secreting gut cells and pinealocytes).

• PER-MODE TRANSFER FUNCTIONS.

◦ In the Standing Sample intracellular calcium is low and the readily releasable vesicle pool is held in reserve.

◦ In the Answer Pulse a subtype-specific stimulus (luminal glucose in millimoles per litre, oxygen pressure in millimetres of mercury, indentation in millimetres) raises calcium and empties the pool, with excitable members firing sodium or calcium spikes.

◦ The two delivery modes differ in latency by about four orders of magnitude, 1 to 10 milliseconds for wired versus seconds to hours for endocrine, and must be modelled separately; confidence high on the taxonomy.

• THE TRANSITION TABLE.

◦ Arrival of the adequate stimulus - Standing Sample to Answer Pulse - milliseconds to seconds by subtype.

◦ Clearance of stimulus and calcium - back to Standing Sample - subtype-specific.

◦ Cell birth and death is itself a slow structural transition, since these sensors are replaced over days (gut) to years (glomus), so a simulation must model turnover, not only state.

• FAULT REGIMES AND WATCHDOGS.

◦ Uncontrolled proliferation gives neuroendocrine tumours including carcinoid, phaeochromocytoma and Merkel cell carcinoma, a boundary the class shares.

◦ Cross-references: the reactive and repair registers of Merkel cells (Chapter 282) and pinealocytes (Chapter 283) are formalised in those entries; this entry is the family-level umbrella.


ENTRY 285. PLACE CELLS (keyed to neuron catalogue Chapter 285)

MODES AND MODE TRANSITIONS:

This is a genuinely mode-rich cell of the hippocampus (the curled memory sheet in each temporal lobe), and its register properly includes a network-assigned replay regime, because replay during rest is a real, used, normal operating condition, not a fault.

• THE MODE REGISTER.

◦ Out-of-Field Near-Silence - the Watchful Hush - almost no firing while the body is outside the cell's place field.

◦ In-Field Theta Coding - the Here-I-Am - reliable firing, phase-advancing against the theta rhythm, when the body is in the field.

◦ Sharp-Wave-Ripple Replay - the Fast Rehearsal - compressed re-firing of learned sequences during rest and sleep.

• PER-MODE TRANSFER FUNCTIONS.

◦ In the Watchful Hush the cell fires below 0.1 spikes per second.

◦ In the Here-I-Am it reaches 5 to 20 spikes per second in bursts up to 150 to 200 hertz, precessing to ever earlier phases of the 6 to 12 hertz theta oscillation as the field is crossed, a phase code carrying position beyond rate.

◦ In the Fast Rehearsal it fires within 100 millisecond sharp-wave ripples at 150 to 250 hertz at up to twenty times real speed, and recent work shows a ripple-band phase precession analogous to the theta code; confidence high.

• THE TRANSITION TABLE.

◦ Entry into the place field during movement - Watchful Hush to Here-I-Am - within a theta cycle of about 125 milliseconds.

◦ Onset of quiescent rest or slow-wave sleep, when the network enters the sharp-wave-ripple regime - active coding to Replay - thrown from above by brain state.

◦ Return to locomotion and theta - Replay back to active coding - set by behavioural state.

• FAULT REGIMES AND WATCHDOGS.

◦ In temporal lobe epilepsy synchronous discharge scrambles the code, a pathological hypersynchrony distinct from ordered replay.

◦ In Alzheimer's disease degrading entorhinal input makes fields drift; the watchdog is astrocytic supply of D-serine, a molecule the plasticity receptor needs before it opens.


ENTRY 286. GRID CELLS (keyed to neuron catalogue Chapter 286)

MODES AND MODE TRANSITIONS:

This is an attractor-driven metric cell of the medial entorhinal cortex (the gateway in front of the hippocampus), mode-rich because the same continuous-attractor machinery is read out three ways, and its correlation structure is preserved from waking into sleep, a signature of that attractor.

• THE MODE REGISTER.

◦ Out-of-Vertex Low Firing - the Between-Points Quiet - near silence away from lattice vertices.

◦ In-Field Active Path Integration - the Ruler Read - theta-coupled firing at the vertices during movement, tracking position by summing self-motion.

◦ Replay Wavefront - the Rest Sweep - traveling waves of activity during quiescence and sleep, decoded as replayed trajectories.

• PER-MODE TRANSFER FUNCTIONS.

◦ In the Between-Points Quiet firing stays under a spike per second.

◦ In the Ruler Read in-field rates reach 5 to 20 spikes per second with phase precession, the activity bump translated across a toroidal attractor by speed and head-direction inputs, with a prospective bias of 50 to 80 milliseconds.

◦ In the Rest Sweep decreasing drive to the sheet produces traveling wavefronts read out as replays, preserving the waking offsets between cells; confidence high, from attractor recordings across waking, rapid-eye-movement and non-rapid-eye-movement sleep.

• THE TRANSITION TABLE.

◦ Movement with intact velocity input - Quiet to Ruler Read - membrane and theta timescales of tens to about 125 milliseconds.

◦ Entry into rest or sleep - active coding to Rest Sweep - thrown from above by brain state.

◦ Cholinergic novelty signal from the medial septum sets the anchoring; blocking it flattens the grid.

• FAULT REGIMES AND WATCHDOGS.

◦ In mild Alzheimer's disease layer II neurons die by about 60 percent and young APOE epsilon-4 carriers show weak grid-like signals, a degeneration rather than a used regime.

◦ The watchdog is the rigidity of the lattice itself, which survives a change of room while its anchor rotates to the walls.


ENTRY 287. HEAD DIRECTION CELLS (keyed to neuron catalogue Chapter 287)

MODES AND MODE TRANSITIONS:

This is the clearest mammalian ring attractor, a compass whose register is dominated by whether the head points its way, and which crucially maintains its state in darkness and sleep, so state-holding is a normal mode, not an idle.

• THE MODE REGISTER.

◦ On-Bearing Firing - the Compass Point - strong firing when the head points the preferred way.

◦ Off-Bearing Silence - the Wrong-Way Hush - near silence at other headings.

◦ Dead-Reckoning Hold - the Kept Needle - the bump of activity persisting and moving with head turns even in the dark and during sleep.

• PER-MODE TRANSFER FUNCTIONS.

◦ In the Compass Point the cell fires at 20 to 100 spikes per second across a tuning curve 60 to 100 degrees wide, thalamic cells anticipating the future heading by about 25 milliseconds.

◦ In the Wrong-Way Hush firing falls to near zero.

◦ In the Kept Needle a velocity signal from the vestibular nuclei pushes the single activity bump around the ring, holding a heading estimate with no landmark visible; confidence high, this being a textbook continuous attractor.

• THE TRANSITION TABLE.

◦ Head rotation sensed by the vestibular system - shifts the bump, moving cells between Compass Point and Hush - membrane timescale of 10 to 20 milliseconds.

◦ Turning off the lights - into pure Dead-Reckoning Hold - immediate; the bump persists.

◦ A rotated landmark cue re-anchors every cell in lockstep over about 1 second.

• FAULT REGIMES AND WATCHDOGS.
