◦ Retrosplenial stroke causes heading disorientation, the patient knowing every landmark yet unable to face the right way, a loss rather than a mode.

◦ These nuclei are unusually sensitive to the vitamin thiamine, so glial transport failure causes rapid amnesia (Korsakoff syndrome), the warning condition.


ENTRY 288. BORDER CELLS AND BOUNDARY VECTOR CELLS (keyed to neuron catalogue Chapter 288)

MODES AND MODE TRANSITIONS:

This is a mode-poor geometric anchor, essentially a two-mode detector that fires in a band along a boundary and is otherwise quiet, its register kept small because its output follows the room's geometry rather than any internal state.

• THE MODE REGISTER.

◦ Mid-Room Silence - the Open-Floor Quiet - near silence away from boundaries.

◦ Boundary Firing Band - the Wall Hug - firing at a preferred distance and bearing from an edge.

• PER-MODE TRANSFER FUNCTIONS.

◦ In the Open-Floor Quiet firing is negligible through the long stretches spent mid-room.

◦ In the Wall Hug peak rates reach 5 to 15 spikes per second in the band, with subicular cells bursting at 100 to 200 hertz from dendritic calcium spikes, tuned to a preferred distance of 0 to 0.6 metres and a bearing 20 to 40 degrees wide, in a frame set by head direction; confidence high.

• THE TRANSITION TABLE.

◦ Approach to within the preferred distance of a wall - Open-Floor Quiet to Wall Hug - membrane timescale of 10 to 20 milliseconds.

◦ Appearance of a new wall - a fresh firing band grows within minutes, without training.

◦ Head direction rotates the vector frame first, so the same cell hugs the same relative edge in any orientation.

• FAULT REGIMES AND WATCHDOGS.

◦ Subicular damage and early Alzheimer's pathology weaken this anchor, so the grid slides and place fields stop tracking the room shape, a degeneration rather than a used mode.

◦ No distinctive fault regime beyond that loss is described; the safeguard is that firing follows geometry directly and needs no memory.


ENTRY 289. SPEED CELLS (keyed to neuron catalogue Chapter 289)

MODES AND MODE TRANSITIONS:

This is a mode-poor analogue speedometer, best described as a single graded encoding regime with a resting intercept, since its rate rises in a near-straight line with running speed and gives the same rate for the same speed in any room.

• THE MODE REGISTER.

◦ Resting Intercept - the Idling Needle - a low baseline rate when the body is still.

◦ Speed-Proportional Ramp - the Rising Needle - rate climbing linearly with movement speed.

• PER-MODE TRANSFER FUNCTIONS.

◦ In the Idling Needle the cell holds an intercept of 2 to 15 spikes per second at rest.

◦ In the Rising Needle rate adds 5 to 20 spikes per second per metre per second of speed, leading actual speed by 50 to 80 milliseconds, with linear, saturating and negative subtypes; many of the strongest are parvalbumin fast-spiking interneurons, small inhibitory cells that fire fast without tiring; confidence high.

• THE TRANSITION TABLE.

◦ Onset of movement - Idling Needle to Rising Needle - the 50 to 80 millisecond lead time, since the signal is prospective.

◦ Stopping - back to Idling Needle - promptly.

◦ Vestibular, proprioceptive, optic-flow and efference-copy inputs all feed the same rate, so there is no hidden second state to switch into.

• FAULT REGIMES AND WATCHDOGS.

◦ No distinctive pathological regime is documented for this type; its honest failure is simply loss of the velocity signal that grid cells integrate, degrading path integration.

◦ The safeguard is redundancy of speed inputs, so one failing source is covered by the others.


ENTRY 290. TIME CELLS (keyed to neuron catalogue Chapter 290)

MODES AND MODE TRANSITIONS:

This is a mode-rich internally-generated sequencer, the same pyramidal cells that serve as place cells, and its register includes replay as a real, used regime, since the timed sequence is produced even in an animal standing still in the dark.

• THE MODE REGISTER.

◦ Out-of-Slot Silence - the Waiting Quiet - near silence outside the cell's moment.

◦ In-Slot Timed Firing - the Baton Pass - firing at one particular elapsed moment of a delay.

◦ Sharp-Wave-Ripple Replay - the Fast Rehearsal - compressed re-firing of the same sequence during rest.

• PER-MODE TRANSFER FUNCTIONS.

◦ In the Waiting Quiet firing is near zero.

◦ In the Baton Pass in-field rates reach 5 to 15 spikes per second, with fields growing wider the later they fall, so the timeline is compressed toward a logarithmic scale (Weber fraction 0.2 to 0.35), peak times spanning 0.1 to 30 seconds after the cue.

◦ In the Fast Rehearsal the sequence replays in ripples at up to twenty times speed; confidence high.

• THE TRANSITION TABLE.

◦ A cue onset starting the interval - Waiting Quiet to the cell's Baton Pass at its own peak time - carved into steps by the theta rhythm.

◦ Onset of rest - active timing to Replay - thrown from above by brain state.

◦ Stretching the delay rescales the sequence rather than exhausting it, a graceful re-timing rather than a mode jump.

• FAULT REGIMES AND WATCHDOGS.

◦ Silencing hippocampal field CA2 scrambles the sequence and septal inactivation abolishes the code, experimental lesions rather than used modes.

◦ In Alzheimer's disease and Korsakoff syndrome the ordering of memories fails first; astrocytic calcium signals over seconds plausibly feed the slow drift the code needs, the supporting watchdog.


ENTRY 291. OBJECT VECTOR CELLS (keyed to neuron catalogue Chapter 291)

MODES AND MODE TRANSITIONS:

This is a mode-poor vector detector, a two-mode cell that fires at one arrow, a distance and a bearing, from any freestanding object and is otherwise quiet, its fields appearing pre-built on the first pass so no learning mode is needed.

• THE MODE REGISTER.

◦ Off-Vector Silence - the No-Object Quiet - under one spike per second away from the preferred vector or with no object present.

◦ On-Vector Firing - the Arrow Lock - 5 to 20 spikes per second when the body stands at the preferred distance and bearing from an object.

• PER-MODE TRANSFER FUNCTIONS.

◦ In the No-Object Quiet firing is negligible.

◦ In the Arrow Lock the strongest-matching object wins, driving firing tuned to a preferred distance of 5 to 100 centimetres (width 5 to 20 centimetres) and a bearing 20 to 60 degrees wide, on stellate cells whose HCN1 channels (a hyperpolarisation-activated pore) tune them to ring at 4 to 12 hertz; confidence moderate, no human recording yet.

• THE TRANSITION TABLE.

◦ Moving to the preferred vector from any object - No-Object Quiet to Arrow Lock - field formation under one second.

◦ Removing the object - Arrow Lock back to No-Object Quiet - immediate, unlike a grid cell's field, which survives an empty room.

◦ Head direction rotates the allocentric frame that defines the vector.

• FAULT REGIMES AND WATCHDOGS.

◦ Alzheimer's disease kills about 60 percent of entorhinal layer II neurons at the mildest dementia, so patients lose orientation by landmarks, a degeneration not a mode.

◦ No distinctive used fault regime; the safeguard is that the vector code is pre-built and needs no training.


ENTRY 292. CONCEPT CELLS (keyed to neuron catalogue Chapter 292)

MODES AND MODE TRANSITIONS:

This is a mode-rich sparse recogniser of the human medial temporal lobe, notable for how loudly its silence speaks, and its register includes a recall mode and a replay mode alongside its recognition burst, all normal and used.

• THE MODE REGISTER.

◦ Deep Baseline Silence - the Long Hush - well under one spike per second for the overwhelming majority of stimuli.

◦ Recognition Burst - the Meaning Flash - a late, hard burst when its one concept is present in any sense.

◦ Recall Echo - the Inner Call - firing when the concept is merely remembered with nothing on the screen.

◦ Sharp-Wave-Ripple Replay - the Night Rehearsal - compressed re-firing during rest and sleep for consolidation.

• PER-MODE TRANSFER FUNCTIONS.

◦ In the Long Hush baseline is 0.05 to 1 spikes per second, with only about 0.1 percent of the population answering any one image.

◦ In the Meaning Flash a burst of 10 to 30 spikes per second arrives late, near 204 milliseconds in hippocampus and 220 in amygdala, triggered when a normalised identity vector crosses a cosine-similarity match threshold of 0.5 to 0.8.

◦ In the Recall Echo the same prototype fires from internal drive; in Replay it re-fires in ripples at 80 to 200 hertz; confidence high on the burst, moderate on recall and replay specifics.

• THE TRANSITION TABLE.

◦ A matching picture, name or spoken name - Long Hush to Meaning Flash - a 200 to 400 millisecond latency.

◦ Internally cued recollection - Long Hush to Recall Echo - behavioural timescale.

◦ Onset of rest or sleep - into Replay - thrown from above by brain state.

• FAULT REGIMES AND WATCHDOGS.

◦ Hippocampal sclerosis in temporal lobe epilepsy destroys the parent CA1 and CA3 cells, leaving patients unable to form new memories.

◦ The watchdog on sparseness is parvalbumin basket-cell inhibition, which silences every cell that is merely nearly right; its failure would collapse the sparse code.


ENTRY 293. MIRROR NEURONS (keyed to neuron catalogue Chapter 293)

MODES AND MODE TRANSITIONS:

This is a two-to-three mode resonant motor cell, firing both when you act and when you watch the same goal-directed action, so its register is organised by whether the cell is driving or echoing, with a genuine anti-mirror suppression mode in some cells.

• THE MODE REGISTER.

◦ Execution Drive - the Doing Burst - strong firing while the cell's own goal-directed action is performed.

◦ Observation Echo - the Watching Ring - reduced firing while the same action is merely observed.

◦ Anti-Mirror Suppression - the Do-Not-Copy Brake - a fall below baseline during observation in the minority anti-mirror cells, which may stop imitation.

• PER-MODE TRANSFER FUNCTIONS.

◦ In the Doing Burst the cell fires 40 to 100 spikes per second, bursting from apical-dendrite calcium spikes, execution gain set to 1.0.

◦ In the Watching Ring firing runs at observation gain 0.3 to 0.7 of that, roughly half rate, and unusually does not adapt to the same action watched again and again.

◦ In the Do-Not-Copy Brake firing dips below a baseline of 1 to 5 spikes per second during observation; confidence moderate, from a small human sample of about 68 mirror cells.

• THE TRANSITION TABLE.

◦ Beginning one's own action - baseline to Doing Burst - visual-to-motor latency 100 to 200 milliseconds.

◦ Watching another's matching action - baseline to Watching Ring or, in anti-mirror cells, to the Brake - same latency.

◦ Brief training pairing an observed action with an incompatible executed one can reverse the mapping within tens of minutes, so the mode assignment is learned.

• FAULT REGIMES AND WATCHDOGS.

◦ The broken-mirror account of autism is not supported in its pure form; the well-established failures strike the parent cells, in amyotrophic lateral sclerosis and in premotor stroke causing apraxia (loss of skilled movement).

◦ The watchdog is local gamma-aminobutyric acid (GABA)-releasing inhibition that stops observation turning into imitation.


ENTRY 294. FACE CELLS (keyed to neuron catalogue Chapter 294)

MODES AND MODE TRANSITIONS:

This is a mode-poor axis-coding recogniser, essentially a two-mode cell that is quiet until a face appears and then ramps along one axis of a shared face space, firing in a sustained rather than sparse manner.

• THE MODE REGISTER.

◦ Off-Face Idle - the Faceless Quiet - low baseline when no face is in view.

◦ Face-Axis Ramp - the Measuring Gaze - graded firing set by the face's position along this cell's own axis.

• PER-MODE TRANSFER FUNCTIONS.

◦ In the Faceless Quiet the cell fires at 1 to 5 spikes per second.

◦ In the Measuring Gaze a preferred face drives 50 to over 100 spikes per second, arriving in 80 to 130 milliseconds, as a straight ramp rising with the face's value along this cell's axis and flat along every other, so about 200 such cells can reconstruct a face; firing is sustained, not a sparse burst; confidence high.

• THE TRANSITION TABLE.

◦ A face entering the large 10 to 30 degree receptive field - Faceless Quiet to Measuring Gaze - the 80 to 130 millisecond latency, or 30 to 50 milliseconds by the crude subcortical route.

◦ The face leaving view - back to Faceless Quiet - promptly.

◦ Attentional acetylcholine and oxytocin set the gain of the ramp without a hard mode switch.

• FAULT REGIMES AND WATCHDOGS.

◦ Damage to the inferior longitudinal fasciculus, the temporal highway, causes face blindness even when the patches survive, a disconnection rather than a mode.

◦ These patches must be built by experience; monkeys reared without faces never develop them, the developmental watchdog.


ENTRY 295. ORIENTATION-SELECTIVE AND DIRECTION-SELECTIVE CELLS (keyed to neuron catalogue Chapter 295)

MODES AND MODE TRANSITIONS:

This is a mode-poor feature detector, a two-mode cell that shouts for its preferred edge or motion and falls silent for the orthogonal or null case, its selectivity built into the wiring rather than switched by state.

• THE MODE REGISTER.

◦ Null Silence - the Wrong-Way Hush - near silence for the orthogonal edge or the null direction.

◦ Preferred Drive - the Right-Way Shout - firing for an edge at the preferred angle or motion in the preferred direction.

• PER-MODE TRANSFER FUNCTIONS.

◦ In the Wrong-Way Hush firing falls under two spikes per second, and in the retinal detector the starburst amacrine cell delivers its inhibition already vetoing wrong-way motion.

◦ In the Right-Way Shout primary visual cortex cells fire 20 to 80 spikes per second, orientation bandwidth 15 to 90 degrees at half height, direction index 0 to 1, sharpened by divisive normalisation (dividing the response by pooled local activity); confidence high.

• THE TRANSITION TABLE.

◦ An edge rotating toward the preferred angle - Hush to Shout - cortical latency 40 to 70 milliseconds.

◦ Motion reversing to the null direction - Shout to Hush - within the integration window of 20 to 100 milliseconds.

◦ Prolonged viewing adapts tuning over 1 to 10 seconds, a gain drift rather than a mode jump.

• FAULT REGIMES AND WATCHDOGS.

◦ Bilateral damage to area V5/MT causes akinetopsia, motion blindness, the world seen as still frames, a lesion not a used mode.

◦ Failure of orientation tuning during the critical period (the early mouldable window) causes amblyopia; the watchdog is that period's patterned-vision refinement.


ENTRY 296. SIMPLE CELLS (keyed to neuron catalogue Chapter 296)

MODES AND MODE TRANSITIONS:

This is a mode-poor measuring instrument, a two-mode cell whose on and off zones never overlap, so it beats in time with a passing grating and reports exactly where an edge lies, identified by a modulation ratio above one.

• THE MODE REGISTER.

◦ Off-Position Silence - the Wrong-Place Hush - silence when the edge sits over the wrong stripe.

◦ Position-Locked Modulated Firing - the Stripe Beat - firing that rises and falls once per passing stripe.

• PER-MODE TRANSFER FUNCTIONS.

◦ In the Wrong-Place Hush the summed light in the on-zones and off-zones cancels, so response is near zero.

◦ In the Stripe Beat the aligned on-centre and off-centre thalamic inputs, with antiphase inhibition, give a modulation ratio above one, firing 0 to 100 hertz, contrast-invariant through divisive normalisation with an exponent near 2; confidence high.

• THE TRANSITION TABLE.

◦ An edge shifting into a preferred position - Hush to Stripe Beat - synaptic timescale of 2 to 10 milliseconds.

◦ An edge shifting half a stripe - Stripe Beat to Hush - the same fast timescale, since position matters absolutely.

◦ Adaptation reshapes the tuning curve over seconds to minutes after prolonged viewing.

• FAULT REGIMES AND WATCHDOGS.

◦ Loss of antiphase inhibition merges the on and off stripes, collapsing the modulation ratio and turning the measuring instrument into a mere detector, the boundary signature.

◦ The watchdog is that stripe-by-stripe inhibition arriving out of phase with excitation.


ENTRY 297. COMPLEX CELLS (keyed to neuron catalogue Chapter 297)

MODES AND MODE TRANSITIONS:

This is a mode-poor invariance detector, a two-mode cell whose overlapping on and off zones let it fire for its preferred orientation anywhere in its window, identified by a modulation ratio below one, the first cortical step toward recognising a shape independent of position.

• THE MODE REGISTER.

◦ Wrong-Orientation Silence - the Off-Tilt Hush - near silence for an edge turned ninety degrees.

◦ Position-Invariant Sustained Firing - the Steady Tone - a constant firing tone for the preferred orientation wherever the edge lies.

• PER-MODE TRANSFER FUNCTIONS.

◦ In the Off-Tilt Hush firing is negligible.

◦ In the Steady Tone the cell squares and sums a quadrature pair of simple-cell subunits (two fixed-edge cells a quarter cycle apart) to yield orientation energy blind to stripe position, firing 0 to 100 hertz at a modulation ratio below one, with sustained pooled inhibition; confidence high, this being the clearest nonlinear computation in the brain.

• THE TRANSITION TABLE.

◦ A preferred-orientation edge appearing anywhere in the field - Off-Tilt Hush to Steady Tone - synaptic timescale of 2 to 10 milliseconds.

◦ The edge rotating off preferred - Steady Tone to Hush - same timescale.

◦ Adaptation reshapes tuning over seconds to minutes.

• FAULT REGIMES AND WATCHDOGS.

◦ Loss of pooling collapses the cell back to a position-sensitive detector, so translation tolerance is lost and recognition becomes dependent on precise fixation, the boundary signature.

◦ The watchdog is the pooling of two to eight subunits that grants invariance.


ENTRY 298. COMMAND NEURONS (keyed to neuron catalogue Chapter 298)

MODES AND MODE TRANSITIONS:

This is a mode-rich trigger cell defined by an experiment rather than a shape, and its register spans a poised wait, a one-shot trigger and a sustained gating hold, since real command elements come in trigger, gating and scaling flavours.

• THE MODE REGISTER.

◦ Poised Sub-Threshold Wait - the Loaded Spring - sitting quiet below threshold, integrating drive.

◦ One-Shot Trigger - the Snap - a single spike or brief burst that releases a whole behaviour.

◦ Gating Plateau Hold - the Held Command - a plateau potential sustaining a behaviour for seconds.

• PER-MODE TRANSFER FUNCTIONS.

◦ In the Loaded Spring the cell sits below threshold with little spontaneous firing, accumulating sensory drive minus a veto.

◦ In the Snap crossing a threshold near 12 arbitrary units fires one all-or-none command to 20 to 200 follower pools plus a corollary-discharge copy, then locks out for 200 to 400 milliseconds; conduction is fast at 60 to 120 metres per second.

◦ In the Held Command a plateau potential outlasts its input, holding a gating behaviour (such as an attack or a breath rhythm) for seconds; confidence moderate, human single-cell tests being absent.

• THE TRANSITION TABLE.

◦ Sensory drive crossing threshold - Loaded Spring to Snap - integration over about 10 milliseconds.

◦ Sustained excitation engaging a plateau - Loaded Spring to Held Command - seconds.

◦ Feedforward inhibitory collar and lockout timer return the cell to the Loaded Spring, ensuring only one command wins and it fires once.

• FAULT REGIMES AND WATCHDOGS.

◦ Freezing of gait in Parkinson's disease, where the legs work but the start signal never comes, is a failure to reach the Snap.

◦ The watchdog is the axon-cap ephaptic veto (an electric-field inhibition) and gap junctions that stop both left and right partners firing.


ENTRY 299. SPARSE CODING NEURONS (keyed to neuron catalogue Chapter 299)

MODES AND MODE TRANSITIONS:

This is a mode-rich but deliberately quiet cell that carries meaning by staying silent, so its register is a deep silence, a rare winner burst and a replay regime, with sparseness itself actively defended.

• THE MODE REGISTER.

◦ Deep Silence - the Silent Majority - saying nothing to the overwhelming majority of stimuli.

◦ Winner Burst - the Chosen Voice - a vigorous burst when one particular meaning is present.

◦ Sharp-Wave-Ripple Replay - the Rehearsal - compressed re-firing during rest for consolidation.

• PER-MODE TRANSFER FUNCTIONS.

◦ In the Silent Majority dentate granule cells rest near minus 80 millivolts with low input resistance and average well below one spike per second, roughly one cell in a hundred active.

◦ In the Chosen Voice a cell whose many thousands of fibres agree crosses threshold and bursts, its mossy-fibre contact onto field CA3 a detonator that can fire its target with one spike, with under 2.5 percent lifetime and population sparseness.

◦ In the Rehearsal the winners re-fire in ripples; confidence moderate, from samples biased toward active cells.

• THE TRANSITION TABLE.

◦ A matching, sufficiently supported input - Silent Majority to Winner Burst - fast, then basket cells silence every neighbour.

◦ Onset of rest - into Replay - thrown from above by brain state.

◦ Homeostatic threshold adjustment regulates the population statistic over the longer term, defending sparseness.

• FAULT REGIMES AND WATCHDOGS.

◦ Loss of the sparse coders removes pattern separation, so today's and yesterday's parking spaces are written on the same cells and the second erases the first, a degradation rather than a used mode.

◦ The watchdog is competitive basket-cell inhibition turning graded input into a discrete winner.


ENTRY 300. ERROR AND REWARD PREDICTION ERROR NEURONS (keyed to neuron catalogue Chapter 300)

MODES AND MODE TRANSITIONS:

This is a clean three-mode teaching cell that reports not what happened but how wrong the forecast was, so its register is a baseline tick, a better-than-expected burst and a worse-than-expected pause, an inherently signed code.

• THE MODE REGISTER.

◦ Baseline Pacemaker Tick - the Background Tick - steady tonic firing when the outcome matches the prediction.

◦ Positive-Error Burst - the Better-Than-Expected Exclamation - a burst when the outcome beats the prediction.

◦ Negative-Error Pause - the Worse-Than-Expected Dip - a brief pause below baseline when the outcome falls short.

• PER-MODE TRANSFER FUNCTIONS.

◦ In the Background Tick the dopamine cell paces at 3 to 5 spikes per second, saying nothing new.

◦ In the Better-Than-Expected Exclamation it bursts to 20 or 30 spikes per second for some 100 to 200 milliseconds, a rise of about 25 spikes per second above baseline.

◦ In the Worse-Than-Expected Dip it falls toward zero, but only about 5 spikes per second below baseline, so the code is asymmetric, floored at zero; learning rate 0.1 to 0.5; confidence high.

• THE TRANSITION TABLE.

◦ An unexpectedly good outcome - Tick to Exclamation - burst onset within 100 milliseconds, driven by pedunculopontine excitation.

◦ An omitted or poor outcome - Tick to Dip - inhibition from the rostromedial tegmental nucleus, itself inverted from the habenula.

◦ Reuptake over 100 to 500 milliseconds returns the cell to the Background Tick, and the prediction is updated by striatal feedback.

• FAULT REGIMES AND WATCHDOGS.

◦ In Parkinson's disease motor signs appear after roughly 30 percent of these cells are lost and 50 to 80 percent by diagnosis, a degeneration rather than a mode.

◦ The watchdog is high substantia-nigra microglial density reacting to spilled neuromelanin; the fragility comes from a bare, four-metre, pacemaking axon under heavy oxidative load.


ENTRY 301. NOCICEPTIVE-SPECIFIC NEURONS (keyed to neuron catalogue Chapter 301)

MODES AND MODE TRANSITIONS:

This is a three-mode alarm cell of the spinal dorsal horn (the grey matter where body sensation first arrives), whose flat silence during ordinary contact is as important as its firing during injury, and whose response can be hushed from above.

• THE MODE REGISTER.

◦ Innocuous Silence - the Flat Quiet - saying nothing to brushing and warmth.

◦ Injury Alarm - the Sudden Eruption - firing when a stimulus reaches the tissue-threatening range.

◦ Descending-Suppressed State - the Placebo Hush - firing quietened by top-down control even when the stimulus is unchanged.

• PER-MODE TRANSFER FUNCTIONS.

◦ In the Flat Quiet the cell sits silent, receiving only thin A-delta and bare C fibres and none of the thick A-beta touch fibres, which is what makes it specific.

◦ In the Sudden Eruption it fires above a threshold set in the noxious range, in tidy classes described as tonic, phasic, delayed-onset and single-spike, without the wind-up escalation of its grading counterpart, sending 0 to 60 spikes per stimulus to thalamus and parabrachial nucleus.

◦ In the Placebo Hush descending drive from the periaqueductal grey and rostral ventromedial medulla scales the response down by a gain factor over seconds; confidence high.

• THE TRANSITION TABLE.

◦ A stimulus crossing the noxious threshold - Flat Quiet to Sudden Eruption - membrane timescale of 10 to 20 milliseconds.

◦ An expectation formed in the frontal lobe - into the Placebo Hush - seconds, thrown from above and the machinery of placebo analgesia.

◦ Inflammation recruits a silent subpopulation, so the cell class physically grows after injury.

• FAULT REGIMES AND WATCHDOGS.

◦ Loss of the chloride pump KCC2 lets inhibition turn excitatory, so touch fibres gain access to the alarm line, the cellular basis of allodynia (pain from an innocuous stimulus), a used-looking but pathological regime.

◦ The watchdog is microglial restraint; dorsal-horn microglia releasing brain-derived neurotrophic factor drive that KCC2 loss, the warning condition over hours to days.


PART TWO. THE OTHER-CELLS CATALOGUE, CHAPTERS 1 TO 85


ENTRY 1. PROTOPLASMIC ASTROCYTE (keyed to other-cells catalogue Chapter 1)

MODES AND MODE TRANSITIONS:

The protoplasmic astrocyte is a mode-moderate chemical integrator whose modes are set not by membrane voltage, which barely moves, but by the amplitude and reach of its calcium activity and by a slow, injury-thrown switch into a defensive register.

Its everyday life is a two-band calcium machine, and its emergency life is a whole separate state formalised in Chapter 12.

• THE MODE REGISTER.

◦ Microdomain Housekeeping - the Quiet Tide - local per-process calcium blips that tune single synapses while the cell stays chemically calm across its domain.

◦ Whole-Cell and Network Surge - the Rising Wave - a coordinated calcium rise that spreads across the cell and into neighbours, releasing gliotransmitters and commanding blood flow.

◦ Reactive Register - the Emergency Brigade - the injury- and disease-induced defensive state catalogued in full as Chapter 12 of this volume; it is a mode of this cell, not a separate type, and is cross-referenced rather than re-derived here.

• PER-MODE TRANSFER FUNCTIONS.

◦ Quiet Tide: spillover glutamate at low micromolar levels drives calcium transients of 0.5 to 5 seconds in individual fine processes near 100 nanomolar rising several-fold, adjusting leaflet coverage and local plasticity without whole-cell output.

◦ Rising Wave: strong or synchronised synaptic drive, or arousal noradrenaline, escalates local blips into a whole-cell surge and an intercellular wave travelling at 10 to 27 micrometres per second, releasing glutamate, adenosine triphosphate (ATP) that is converted to adenosine, and D-serine, and dilating arterioles.

◦ Reactive Register: see Chapter 12; membrane depolarises modestly as the inwardly rectifying potassium channel Kir4.1 falls, calcium signalling becomes exaggerated, and secretory output turns to cytokines and scar components.

• THE TRANSITION TABLE.

◦ Rising synchronised glutamate or noradrenaline (arousal, thrown from above by Layer 10/11 ascending systems) - Quiet Tide to Rising Wave - seconds.

◦ Falling activity and calcium buffering - Rising Wave back to Quiet Tide - seconds to tens of seconds.

◦ Damage signals (ATP from dying cells, microglial interleukin-1 alpha, blood proteins) - either normal mode into Reactive Register - hours to days; largely reversible for mild grades, permanent at scar borders.

• FAULT REGIMES AND WATCHDOGS.

◦ Calcium overload and glutamate-uptake failure: runaway calcium with collapsed EAAT2 clearance floods neurons with excitotoxic glutamate; the warning sign is rising extracellular glutamate and potassium, and the watchdog is the sodium-pump-backed transporter reserve.

◦ Cytotoxic swelling in hepatic encephalopathy, when ammonia derails the glutamine cycle, is a boundary the healthy cell never uses; osmotic balance is its watchdog.


ENTRY 2. FIBROUS ASTROCYTE (keyed to other-cells catalogue Chapter 2)

MODES AND MODE TRANSITIONS:

The fibrous astrocyte is a mode-poor substrate cell whose day job is one near-continuous transport regime, potassium siphoning through a shared network, with a single injury-thrown defensive register above it.

• THE MODE REGISTER.

◦ Syncytial Siphoning - the Background Drain - the standing regime in which potassium ejected at nodes of Ranvier is passed through the panglial network and dumped at distant vessels.

◦ Reactive Register - the Emergency Brigade - the injury-induced state catalogued as Chapter 12; it is a mode of this cell, cross-referenced there.

• PER-MODE TRANSFER FUNCTIONS.

◦ Background Drain: nodal potassium rising from 3 toward 10 millimolar is buffered over hundreds of milliseconds to seconds and redistributed by pure geometry through gap junctions coupling tens of neighbours, with calcium transients riding on axonal activity but no secretory decision.

◦ Reactive Register: see Chapter 12; in white matter this register produces the sclerotic scars that name multiple sclerosis, with glial fibrillary acidic protein (GFAP)-laden Rosenthal fibres in the Alexander-disease extreme.

• THE TRANSITION TABLE.

◦ Sustained tract activity - deepens Background Drain load but not a mode change - seconds.

◦ Demyelination, axon loss, or barrier breach - Background Drain into Reactive Register - days.

• FAULT REGIMES AND WATCHDOGS.

◦ Buffering saturation: if potassium load outruns the syncytium, conduction block follows; the watchdog is the sheer size of the coupled network, and its failure signature is local potassium accumulation.

◦ No distinct healthy fault regime beyond the shared astrocytic ones; stated honestly, this is a one-job cell with an emergency clause.


ENTRY 3. INTERLAMINAR ASTROCYTE (keyed to other-cells catalogue Chapter 3)

MODES AND MODE TRANSITIONS:

The interlaminar astrocyte is a mode-poor cell with one signature working regime, a vertical calcium relay, plus the shared reactive register; its function is still under active investigation and the register is stated at moderate confidence.

• THE MODE REGISTER.

◦ Cross-Layer Relay - the Plumb-Line Pulse - a calcium event ignited in layer-1 processes propagating down the descending fibre to deliver a chemical signal hundreds of micrometres below.

◦ Local Housekeeping - the Quiet Tide - baseline layer-1 calcium activity and ion handling between relays.

◦ Reactive Register - the Emergency Brigade - the Chapter 12 state, into which the palisade disorganises in Alzheimer disease and Down syndrome.

• PER-MODE TRANSFER FUNCTIONS.

◦ Plumb-Line Pulse: a layer-1 calcium ignition travels the interlaminar fibre at 10 to 30 micrometres per second, delivering gliotransmitter release and potassium handling to layer 3 or 4 some 10 to 60 seconds later.

◦ Quiet Tide: local transients of 1 to 5 seconds handle spillover and neuromodulators without launching a travelling wave.

◦ Reactive Register: see Chapter 12; low confidence on the interlaminar-specific reactive profile.

• THE TRANSITION TABLE.

◦ Strong or coordinated layer-1 drive, feedback and neuromodulators (partly thrown from above by cortical state) - Quiet Tide to Plumb-Line Pulse - seconds.

◦ Signal decay along the fibre - Plumb-Line Pulse back to Quiet Tide - tens of seconds.

◦ Meningeal inflammation or superficial injury - into Reactive Register - hours to days.

• FAULT REGIMES AND WATCHDOGS.

◦ Palisade disorganisation is the disease boundary rather than a used mode; the watchdog is the structural integrity of the descending fibre, and its warning is loss of columnar alignment.


ENTRY 4. VARICOSE PROJECTION ASTROCYTE (keyed to other-cells catalogue Chapter 4)

MODES AND MODE TRANSITIONS:

This is the most hypothesis-rich, data-poor cell of the section, so its register is given as a minimal defensible set at low confidence, with no direct electrophysiology yet published.

• THE MODE REGISTER.

◦ Long-Range Address Delivery - the Express Line - a hypothesised calcium export from soma to a varicosity millimetres away in one hop, bypassing the slow domain-to-domain relay.

◦ Local Housekeeping - the Quiet Tide - presumed standard protoplasmic calcium and ion handling in its short spiny local field.

◦ Reactive Register - the Emergency Brigade - the shared Chapter 12 state.

• PER-MODE TRANSFER FUNCTIONS.

◦ Express Line: a somatic calcium event is proposed to deliver gliotransmitter at beaded release sites 1 to 5 millimetres away over tens of seconds to minutes at wave speeds, possibly faster if electrotonically triggered, all unconfirmed and low confidence.

◦ Quiet Tide: presumed local transients over seconds within a 100 to 200 micrometre field; no measurements exist.

◦ Reactive Register: see Chapter 12.

• THE TRANSITION TABLE.

◦ Strong somatic calcium ignition - Quiet Tide to Express Line - seconds to initiate, tens of seconds to deliver; mechanism unproven.

◦ Injury signals - into Reactive Register - hours to days.

• FAULT REGIMES AND WATCHDOGS.

◦ No disease is pinned to this cell specifically; it shares general astrocytic failure modes and stands as a hypothesised suspect in uniquely human cortical disorders, stated as hypothesis not finding.


ENTRY 5. VELATE ASTROCYTE (keyed to other-cells catalogue Chapter 5)

MODES AND MODE TRANSITIONS:

The velate astrocyte is a mode-poor substrate cell built around one high-capacity buffering regime for the densest neurons in the brain, plus the shared reactive register.

• THE MODE REGISTER.

◦ Veil Buffering - the Blotting Sheet - the standing regime in which thin curved lamellae soak potassium and glutamate from granule-cell clusters and glomeruli.

◦ Reactive Register - the Emergency Brigade - the Chapter 12 state, seen in cerebellar gliosis.

• PER-MODE TRANSFER FUNCTIONS.

◦ Blotting Sheet: local potassium spikes above 10 millimolar during granule-cell bursts are absorbed over hundreds of milliseconds to seconds by sub-micrometre veils spanning 10 to 50 micrometres, with glutamate returned as glutamine and lactate delivered; calcium transients ride on activity without a distinct secretory mode.

◦ Reactive Register: see Chapter 12.

• THE TRANSITION TABLE.

◦ Dense granule-layer bursting - deepens Blotting Sheet load, no mode change - seconds.

◦ Cerebellar injury or degeneration - into Reactive Register - days.

• FAULT REGIMES AND WATCHDOGS.

◦ Buffering saturation during extreme granule-layer synchrony is the boundary the cell avoids; membrane surface area is its watchdog. No distinct healthy fault regime otherwise.


ENTRY 6. BERGMANN GLIA (keyed to other-cells catalogue Chapter 6)

MODES AND MODE TRANSITIONS:

Bergmann glia are a genuinely two-phase cell across the lifespan, a developmental migration ladder and an adult microdomain controller, and within the adult phase they run a mode-rich array of thousands of independent per-synapse loops; the reactive register sits above both.

• THE MODE REGISTER.

◦ Migration Ladder - the Climbing Frame - the developmental regime guiding granule neurons down the radial fibre.

◦ Microdomain Tuning - the Spine-by-Spine Governor - the adult regime in which each of thousands of microdomains adjusts ensheathment of one synapse through calcium admitted by calcium-permeable alpha-amino-3-hydroxy-5-methyl-4-isoxazolepropionic acid (AMPA) receptors.

◦ Reactive Register - the Emergency Brigade - the Chapter 12 state, seen in cerebellar ataxias and alcohol-related degeneration.

• PER-MODE TRANSFER FUNCTIONS.

◦ Climbing Frame: axonal and adhesion cues sustain a scaffold over developmental weeks; no fast secretory output.

◦ Spine-by-Spine Governor: glutamate escaping a Purkinje synapse enters through calcium-permeable AMPA receptors (subunits GluA1 and GluA4, lacking calcium-blocking GluA2), driving microdomain calcium transients of 0.1 to 2 seconds that set ensheathment fraction per spine over minutes to days; roughly 8 cells serve each Purkinje cell, 3 to 6 fibres each, glutamate cleared in about 1 millisecond at the transporter GLAST.

◦ Reactive Register: see Chapter 12.

• THE TRANSITION TABLE.

◦ Completion of granule migration (developmental clock, thrown from above by the maturation programme) - Climbing Frame to Spine-by-Spine Governor - postnatal weeks, one-way.

◦ Local glutamate spillover - drives moment-to-moment Governor loops - sub-second, within mode.

◦ Ataxia, autoimmune GLAST attack, or degeneration - into Reactive Register - days.

• FAULT REGIMES AND WATCHDOGS.

◦ Calcium-permeable AMPA receptor overload: excess calcium admission can retract ensheathment; the watchdog is transporter-backed glutamate clearance, and experimentally forcing the receptors calcium-impermeable retracts wrapping and impairs fine motor learning.


ENTRY 7. MÜLLER CELL (keyed to other-cells catalogue Chapter 7)

MODES AND MODE TRANSITIONS:

The Müller cell is a mode-poor multitasker: an always-on regime that simultaneously guides light and runs retinal chemistry, plus a single injury-thrown gliosis register.

• THE MODE REGISTER.

◦ Optical-and-Chemical Service - the Living Light-Pipe - the continuous regime guiding photons to photoreceptors while clearing glutamate, siphoning potassium, and supplying lactate.

◦ Gliotic Register - the Scar Sheet - the reactive state (a retinal parallel of Chapter 12) driving oedema and scarring.

• PER-MODE TRANSFER FUNCTIONS.

◦ Living Light-Pipe: light of 400 to 700 nanometres is guided effectively instantaneously through a refractive index near 1.38 against tissue near 1.36; potassium is siphoned via Kir4.1 over tens of milliseconds to seconds and glutamate cleared in milliseconds, at roughly one Müller cell per sixteen retinal neurons.

◦ Scar Sheet: glial fibrillary acidic protein (GFAP) rises, potassium siphoning degrades, and swelling drives macular oedema over hours to days.

• THE TRANSITION TABLE.

◦ Retinal injury, ischaemia, or diabetic stress - Living Light-Pipe into Scar Sheet - hours to days.

◦ Resolution of mild insult - partial return toward Living Light-Pipe - days.

• FAULT REGIMES AND WATCHDOGS.

◦ Swelling and Kir4.1 loss are the disease boundary, with warning signs of rising GFAP and impaired electroretinogram waves; osmotic and potassium balance is the watchdog.


ENTRY 8. TANYCYTE (keyed to other-cells catalogue Chapter 8)

MODES AND MODE TRANSITIONS:

The tanycyte is a mode-moderate sensor-and-gate cell: it chemosenses with taste hardware and then acts mechanically, so its modes are a sensing regime, a barrier-transport regime, and a slow seasonal or cyclic gating regime thrown largely from the neuroendocrine axis above.

• THE MODE REGISTER.

◦ Chemosensing - the Tasting Sheet - calcium responses to glucose and tastants sweeping through the lining as waves.

◦ Barrier Transcytosis - the Customs Gate - the standing regime shuttling leptin, ghrelin and thyroxine across the median-eminence barrier.

◦ Endfoot Gating - the Seasonal Shutter - the slow advance or withdrawal of endfeet that opens or closes the gonadotropin-releasing hormone gateway.

• PER-MODE TRANSFER FUNCTIONS.

◦ Tasting Sheet: blood glucose of 4 to 8 millimolar and sweet or bitter tastants evoke calcium rises over seconds, propagated through the sheet by adenosine triphosphate (ATP) release.

◦ Customs Gate: hormone transcytosis proceeds over minutes, gated by tight-junction integrity and deiodinase 2 expression.

◦ Seasonal Shutter: beta-2 tanycyte endfeet remodel over hours to days across the ovarian cycle and over weeks for thyroid-hormone season, physically gating neurosecretory terminals.

• THE TRANSITION TABLE.

◦ Rising or falling blood glucose - into and out of Tasting Sheet - seconds.

◦ Hormonal demand - engages Customs Gate - minutes.

◦ Ovarian-cycle and photoperiod signals (thrown from above by the hypothalamic-pituitary axis) - engages Seasonal Shutter - hours to weeks.

• FAULT REGIMES AND WATCHDOGS.

◦ Barrier and transport failure is the disease boundary, implicated in leptin resistance of obesity; the watchdog is tight-junction integrity, and ablation deranges glucose sensing and feeding.


ENTRY 9. PITUICYTE (keyed to other-cells catalogue Chapter 9)

MODES AND MODE TRANSITIONS:

The pituicyte is essentially a two-mode mechanical valve: an engulfing basal mode that throttles hormone release and a retracted stimulated mode that surrenders release sites, switched by demand from above.

• THE MODE REGISTER.

◦ Engulfed Throttle - the Closed Valve - the basal regime in which processes enclose neurosecretory terminals and occupy the basal lamina, limiting hormone access to blood.

◦ Retracted Release - the Open Valve - the demand regime in which the cell withdraws, multiplying terminal contact with the perivascular space.

• PER-MODE TRANSFER FUNCTIONS.

◦ Closed Valve: at rest, pituicyte processes cover much of the basal lamina; calcium responses to adenosine triphosphate (ATP), adenosine, noradrenaline, oxytocin and vasopressin run over seconds while coverage stays high.

◦ Open Valve: sustained adenosine and adrenergic drive retracts the cell over hours, dropping basal-lamina coverage toward minimal and raising oxytocin and vasopressin output to the whole-body circulation.

• THE TRANSITION TABLE.

◦ Sustained secretory demand, osmotic stress, parturition or suckling (thrown from above by the hypothalamic magnocellular system) - Closed Valve to Open Valve - hours.

◦ Demand relief - Open Valve back to Closed Valve, re-engulfing terminals - hours to days.

• FAULT REGIMES AND WATCHDOGS.

◦ Terminal loss from stalk damage yields central diabetes insipidus, a boundary beyond the valve's range; the watchdog is trophic support of terminals. Pituicytoma is its tumour, not a physiological mode.


ENTRY 10. GOMORI-POSITIVE ASTROCYTE (keyed to other-cells catalogue Chapter 10)

MODES AND MODE TRANSITIONS:

The Gomori-positive astrocyte is a mode-poor housekeeping astrocyte with one distinctive slow accumulation regime layered on ordinary astrocytic service; its electrophysiology is undistinguished and the register is low confidence.

• THE MODE REGISTER.

◦ Astrocytic Housekeeping - the Quiet Tide - ordinary transmitter clearance, potassium buffering and metabolic support in the arcuate nucleus and hippocampus.

◦ Metal Sequestration - the Iron Vault - the slow, near-irreversible locking of redox-active iron and copper into cysteine-rich granules.

◦ Reactive Register - the Emergency Brigade - the shared Chapter 12 state.

• PER-MODE TRANSFER FUNCTIONS.

◦ Quiet Tide: presumed family-standard calcium and ion handling over milliseconds to seconds; no dedicated electrophysiology, low confidence.

◦ Iron Vault: transition metals are trapped in 0.5 to 2 micrometre granules that accumulate over years to decades, protecting feeding circuitry at the cost of the cell's own oxidative scarring.

◦ Reactive Register: see Chapter 12.

• THE TRANSITION TABLE.

◦ Ongoing metal and oxidative flux - continuous feed into Iron Vault - years to decades, effectively one-way.

◦ Metabolic insult or injury - into Reactive Register - hours to days.

• FAULT REGIMES AND WATCHDOGS.

◦ Oxidative overload: as granule burden grows the cell becomes a suspected local amplifier of age-related damage; the watchdog is mitochondrial reserve, and experimental gold-thioglucose destruction produces hypothalamic obesity, tying it to metabolic sensing.


ENTRY 11. SURFACE-ASSOCIATED AND GLIA LIMITANS ASTROCYTE (keyed to other-cells catalogue Chapter 11)

MODES AND MODE TRANSITIONS:

This border astrocyte is a mode-moderate plumbing cell whose defining regime, glymphatic water flux, is gated nightly by sleep from above, with a sealing register and the shared reactive register beyond it.

• THE MODE REGISTER.

◦ Barrier Standby - the Border Wall - the standing regime maintaining the limiting membrane, potassium disposal and vessel control.

◦ Glymphatic Flush - the Night Rinse - the sleep-gated regime in which polarised AQP4 endfeet drive cerebrospinal-fluid solute clearance.

◦ Reactive Sealing Register - the Emergency Brigade - the Chapter 12 state, rebuilding a breached glia limitans.

• PER-MODE TRANSFER FUNCTIONS.

◦ Border Wall: near-complete endfoot coverage handles water flux over milliseconds to seconds and dumps siphoned potassium of 3 to 10 millimolar into the perivascular space.

◦ Night Rinse: during deep sleep the interstitial space expands by tens of percent and glymphatic exchange runs over tens of minutes, clearing amyloid-beta through AQP4-lined walls.

◦ Reactive Sealing Register: see Chapter 12; wall repair after breach takes days to weeks.

• THE TRANSITION TABLE.

◦ Sleep onset and slow-wave activity (thrown from above by sleep-wake state) - Border Wall to Night Rinse - minutes at sleep onset.

◦ Waking - Night Rinse back to Border Wall - minutes.

◦ Barrier breach, trauma, or autoantibody attack - into Reactive Sealing Register - hours to weeks.

• FAULT REGIMES AND WATCHDOGS.

◦ AQP4 depolarisation and endfoot detachment degrade glymphatic clearance in ageing and trauma; the watchdog is AQP4 polarisation, and its collapse is the boundary. Neuromyelitis optica attacks AQP4 itself.


ENTRY 12. REACTIVE ASTROCYTE (keyed to other-cells catalogue Chapter 12)

MODES AND MODE TRANSITIONS:

The reactive astrocyte is a state, not a type: it is the injury- and disease-induced defensive mode of the astrocyte family, above all the protoplasmic astrocyte of Chapter 1 and the fibrous astrocyte of Chapter 2, and its entries in those chapters list it as their reactive register. This entry formalises that the reactive astrocyte IS a mode; what follows is the internal mode structure of that single register.

• THE MODE REGISTER.

◦ Mild Hypertrophy - the Raised Guard - a graded, fully reversible swelling with modest gene changes and preserved domains.

◦ Neuroprotective Reactivity - the Repair Crew - a trophic, barrier-rebuilding profile that takes up glutamate and potassium and secretes protective factors.

◦ Neurotoxic Reactivity - the Executioner - the complement C3-positive profile that actively kills neurons and oligodendrocytes.

◦ Scar-Forming Reactivity - the Wall Builder - proliferative border astrocytes laying a permanent scar of chondroitin sulphate proteoglycans.

• PER-MODE TRANSFER FUNCTIONS.

◦ Raised Guard: glial fibrillary acidic protein (GFAP) rises up to order-of-magnitude, hypertrophy is two- to several-fold, potassium buffering degrades as Kir4.1 falls and membrane depolarises modestly from minus 80 to minus 85 millivolts, and calcium signalling becomes exaggerated; resolves over days to weeks.

◦ Repair Crew: uptake and trophic secretion dominate, glia limitans is rebuilt over one to two weeks.

◦ Executioner: cytokine-driven C3-positive cells secrete saturated-lipid candidate toxins that kill neurons and mature oligodendrocytes.

◦ Wall Builder: cells re-enter the cell cycle at the border, re-express developmental programs, and lay a permanent proteoglycan scar that blocks regeneration.

• THE TRANSITION TABLE.

◦ Damage signals (adenosine triphosphate (ATP) from dying cells, microglial interleukin-1 alpha, tumour necrosis factor and C1q, interferons, fibrinogen) - resting astrocyte into Raised Guard - molecular induction hours, hypertrophy days.

◦ Cytokine context steering (microglial A1-inducing signals versus trophic milieu) - Raised Guard toward Executioner or Repair Crew - days.

◦ Severe or persistent injury - toward Wall Builder scar - one to two weeks, then permanent.

◦ Mild transient insult - Raised Guard back to resting astrocyte - days to weeks.

• FAULT REGIMES AND WATCHDOGS.

◦ Alexander disease is the disease of this program's own signature protein, GFAP mutation choking cells with Rosenthal fibres; the watchdog is graded, resolvable induction, and its failure is the locked neurotoxic or scarring state seen across Alzheimer, Parkinson, multiple sclerosis and amyotrophic lateral sclerosis.


ENTRY 13. MYELINATING (INTERFASCICULAR) OLIGODENDROCYTE (keyed to other-cells catalogue Chapter 13)

MODES AND MODE TRANSITIONS:

The mature myelinating oligodendrocyte is a mode-poor but internally parallel cell: it is post-mitotic and holds one lifelong maintenance-and-tuning regime, run semi-independently across up to 60 internodes, with slow activity-driven remodelling within that regime.

• THE MODE REGISTER.

◦ Steady Insulation and Feeding - the Delay-Line Keeper - the standing regime maintaining 20 to 60 myelin internodes and delivering lactate to axons.

◦ Adaptive Remodelling - the Speed Tuner - the activity-driven adjustment of myelin thickness and length to equalise conduction times.

• PER-MODE TRANSFER FUNCTIONS.

◦ Delay-Line Keeper: at a resting potential near minus 80 millivolts it sets conduction velocity from roughly 1 to 100 metres per second by myelin geometry, holding a g-ratio near 0.77, cleaving lactate through MCT1, with per-sheath calcium transients over seconds.

◦ Speed Tuner: axonal activity (glutamate, adenosine, nodal potassium) drives new internode growth over hours to days and remodelling over days, with whole-life turnover over years.

• THE TRANSITION TABLE.

◦ Sustained patterned axonal activity - Delay-Line Keeper into Speed Tuner remodelling - hours to days.

◦ Return to baseline drive - back to steady maintenance - days.

◦ Note that the differentiation event which creates this cell belongs to Chapter 15; this cell does not divide.

• FAULT REGIMES AND WATCHDOGS.

◦ Demyelination is a boundary imposed on it, not a used mode: immune attack in multiple sclerosis kills the cell and strips dozens of internodes at once; the watchdog is trophic and metabolic support of each internode, and hypoxia or PLP1 mutation drives its death.


ENTRY 14. SATELLITE (PERINEURONAL) OLIGODENDROCYTE (keyed to other-cells catalogue Chapter 14)

MODES AND MODE TRANSITIONS:

The satellite oligodendrocyte is a clean two-mode switch: a resting reserve mode holding a silenced myelination programme, and a recruited mode that switches that programme on in place.

• THE MODE REGISTER.

◦ Idle Reserve - the Fire Extinguisher - the healthy resting state that maintains no myelin but buffers the neuron it sits against.

◦ Recruited Myelination - the Broken Glass - the injury- or demand-triggered state that begins wrapping nearby axon segments.

• PER-MODE TRANSFER FUNCTIONS.

◦ Fire Extinguisher: at a resting potential near minus 80 millivolts with strong Kir4.1 conductance it siphons somatic potassium over milliseconds to seconds and supplies lactate, holding a complete but silenced myelin programme.

◦ Broken Glass: injury or demand switches on myelination locally over days, converting a support cell into a working myelinator.

• THE TRANSITION TABLE.

◦ Neuronal stress or injury signals - Fire Extinguisher to Broken Glass - days.

• FAULT REGIMES AND WATCHDOGS.

◦ Rising perineuronal satellitosis is a histological warning of neuronal stress, not a distinct fault mode of the glial cell; as an oligodendrocyte it dies in multiple sclerosis grey-matter lesions.


ENTRY 15. OLIGODENDROCYTE PRECURSOR CELL (NG2 GLIA / POLYDENDROCYTE) (keyed to other-cells catalogue Chapter 15)

MODES AND MODE TRANSITIONS:

The oligodendrocyte precursor cell (OPC) is a mode-rich decision cell, the most electrically neuron-like of all glia, that integrates real synaptic input over hours to days and converts it into one of three cell-fate regimes; it is a slow analogue-to-digital converter of circuit activity.

• THE MODE REGISTER.

◦ Proliferative Self-Renewal - the Dividing Recruit - cell-cycle activity that refills the tiled population and holds vacancies open.

◦ Quiescent Surveillance - the Listening Sentry - a poised, synapse-receiving standby that integrates activity without committing.

◦ Differentiation Commitment - the One-Way Door - the essentially irreversible conversion into a myelinating oligodendrocyte, dismantling its own synapses as it transforms.

• PER-MODE TRANSFER FUNCTIONS.

◦ Dividing Recruit: platelet-derived growth factor and injury signals drive division on a cell-cycle time of weeks in adult grey matter, faster in white matter and after injury, refilling any domain vacancy in days by contact-repulsion sensing.

◦ Listening Sentry: genuine glutamatergic and GABAergic synapses deliver quantal currents of a few picoamperes with fast alpha-amino-3-hydroxy-5-methyl-4-isoxazolepropionic acid (AMPA) and gamma-aminobutyric acid (GABA)-A kinetics at a resting potential of minus 80 to minus 90 millivolts, integrated over hours to days; a subset fires small broad depolarisations under strong drive.

◦ One-Way Door: sustained integrated activity plus thyroid hormone triggers differentiation into a working myelinator over days to weeks, dismantling synapses en route.

• THE TRANSITION TABLE.

◦ Neighbour loss and contact-repulsion release - Listening Sentry to Dividing Recruit - hours to days.

◦ Patterned neuronal activity crossing an integration threshold - Listening Sentry to One-Way Door - days to weeks, effectively irreversible.

◦ Domain refilled - Dividing Recruit back to Listening Sentry - days.

• FAULT REGIMES AND WATCHDOGS.

◦ Differentiation arrest: in progressive multiple sclerosis OPCs stall undifferentiated inside lesions, a failure of the One-Way Door; the watchdog is the activity-and-thyroid drive to differentiate. Malignant escape of the proliferative mode yields oligodendroglioma and diffuse gliomas.


ENTRY 16. MICROGLIA (keyed to other-cells catalogue Chapter 16)

MODES AND MODE TRANSITIONS:

The microglial cell is a mode-rich immune sentinel whose modes span a continuum from restless surveillance through graded activation to amoeboid phagocytosis, readable directly from its morphology and its potassium-current signature; recent single-cell work resolves a stepwise disease-associated microglia (DAM) programme within the activated arc.

• THE MODE REGISTER.

◦ Ramified Surveillance - the Restless Sentinel - the healthy resting-but-motile state constantly sampling the parenchyma and pruning synapses.

◦ Hyper-Ramified Alert - the Raised Antennae - an intermediate primed state with modestly upregulated activation genes and downregulated homeostatic ones.

◦ Amoeboid Phagocytosis - the Clean-Up Crew - the retracted, macrophage-like state clearing debris and pathogens.

◦ Disease-Associated (DAM) Register - the Damage Sensor - a two-step lipid- and lysosome-focused activation programme engaged in neurodegeneration.

• PER-MODE TRANSFER FUNCTIONS.

◦ Restless Sentinel: at a comparatively depolarised minus 20 to minus 40 millivolts, processes extend and retract at about 1.5 micrometres per minute, visiting each synapse about once an hour and surveying the whole parenchyma every few hours; eat-or-spare pruning reads complement tags C1q and C3b against CD47 shields.

◦ Raised Antennae: a transitional state bridging homeostatic and DAM profiles, appearing over hours to days.

◦ Clean-Up Crew: processes retract, the soma migrates, and phagocytosis dominates, guided up adenosine triphosphate (ATP) gradients through the P2Y12 receptor; converging processes wall off a microbleed within minutes.

◦ Damage Sensor: stage 1 DAM transition is TREM2-independent (homeostatic genes down); stage 2 requires TREM2 and upregulates lysosomal, phagocytic and lipid-metabolism pathways, over days.

• THE TRANSITION TABLE.

◦ Local ATP or ADP gradient at injury (P2Y12) - Restless Sentinel to Clean-Up Crew locally - minutes.

◦ Loss of neuronal restraint (fractalkine, CD47) or chronic cytokines from the neuroimmune channel (arousal, systemic inflammation thrown from the body) - Sentinel to Raised Antennae - hours to days.

◦ Neurodegenerative signals then TREM2 engagement - Raised Antennae into stage 1 then stage 2 Damage Sensor - days.

◦ Insult resolved - back toward Surveillance - hours to days, though primed epigenetic states persist months.

• FAULT REGIMES AND WATCHDOGS.

◦ Aberrant complement-driven over-pruning is a used mechanism turned pathological: in Alzheimer disease and, through the C4 gene, in schizophrenia it eats healthy synapses; the watchdog is neuronal spare-me signalling, and TREM2 or cerebrospinal fluid (CSF)1R mutation breaks the register outright in Nasu-Hakola disease.


ENTRY 17. EPENDYMOCYTE (keyed to other-cells catalogue Chapter 17)

MODES AND MODE TRANSITIONS:

The ependymocyte is a mode-poor mechanical cell whose excitement is ciliary, not electrical: essentially one continuous beating regime whose frequency is modulated by neuromodulators from above.

• THE MODE REGISTER.

◦ Coordinated Ciliary Beating - the Rowing Crew - the standing regime generating near-wall cerebrospinal-fluid flow and guiding neuroblast migration.

• PER-MODE TRANSFER FUNCTIONS.

◦ Rowing Crew: at a resting potential near minus 70 to minus 80 millivolts, thousands of cells beat 16 to 60 cilia at about 25 to 40 hertz in planar-polarised phase, driving streaming of tens to hundreds of micrometres per second; one stroke takes tens of milliseconds and frequency is tuned by serotonin, adenosine triphosphate (ATP) and melanin-concentrating hormone over seconds to minutes.

• THE TRANSITION TABLE.

◦ Serotonin, ATP or melanin-concentrating hormone (partly thrown from above by raphe and hypothalamic state) - speeds or slows beat frequency within the single mode - seconds to minutes.

◦ There is no second cellular mode and no renewal on any adult timescale.

• FAULT REGIMES AND WATCHDOGS.

◦ Ciliary failure or denudation is a boundary the cell cannot recover from, since it does not divide; it produces hydrocephalus and, in the ciliopathies, congenital beat failure. The watchdog is planar-polarity maintenance; ependymoma is its tumour, not a mode.


ENTRY 18. CHOROID PLEXUS EPITHELIAL CELL (keyed to other-cells catalogue Chapter 18)

MODES AND MODE TRANSITIONS:

The choroid plexus epithelial cell is a mode-moderate secretory-and-barrier cell running a circadian-scheduled fluid-production regime with a superimposed immune-gating register.

• THE MODE REGISTER.

◦ Continuous CSF Secretion - the Fluid Factory - the always-on regime pumping ions and water to make cerebrospinal fluid.

◦ Circadian Up-Shift - the Night Shift - the clock-driven increase in production toward night that supports the sleep-time glymphatic flush.

◦ Immune-Gating Register - the Border Guard - the inflammation-reading state that licenses immune-cell entry.

• PER-MODE TRANSFER FUNCTIONS.

◦ Fluid Factory: at an unusually depolarised minus 45 to minus 60 millivolts it secretes about 0.3 to 0.4 millilitres per minute (roughly 500 millilitres per day, turning the 150 millilitre pool over three to four times daily) by pumping sodium, chloride and bicarbonate with water following through aquaporin 1; transport cycles run in milliseconds.

◦ Night Shift: an autonomous circadian clock raises secretion toward night over the 24-hour cycle.

◦ Border Guard: inflammatory signals in blood and cerebrospinal fluid reconfigure junctions and adhesion to admit immune cells over hours.

• THE TRANSITION TABLE.

◦ Circadian clock phase (thrown from above by the master clock) - Fluid Factory to Night Shift - across 24 hours.

◦ Systemic or central inflammation - engages Border Guard - hours.

• FAULT REGIMES AND WATCHDOGS.

◦ Overproduction hydrocephalus in papilloma and failure of amyloid clearance with ageing are the boundaries; the watchdog is tight-junction integrity, whose loss opens the plexus as an entry gate in multiple sclerosis.


ENTRY 19. MYELINATING SCHWANN CELL (keyed to other-cells catalogue Chapter 19)

MODES AND MODE TRANSITIONS:

The myelinating Schwann cell is a two-plus-one mode cell: a developmental wrapping mode, a lifelong steady maintenance mode, and an injury-thrown repair register that is itself catalogued as Chapter 21 and cross-referenced here as this cell's repair mode.

• THE MODE REGISTER.

◦ Active Wrapping - the Membrane Bulldozer - the developmental regime crawling an inner tongue up to a hundred times around one axon.

◦ Steady Maintenance - the One-Axon Keeper - the lifelong regime holding a single internode, feeding the axon and maintaining nodal architecture.

◦ Repair Register - the Regrowth-Rail Layer - the c-Jun-driven repair state catalogued as Chapter 21; it is a mode of this cell, cross-referenced there.

• PER-MODE TRANSFER FUNCTIONS.

◦ Membrane Bulldozer: reading axon diameter above about 1 micrometre through axonal neuregulin 1 type III, it lays 20 to 100 wraps over days to weeks to a g-ratio near 0.6.

◦ One-Axon Keeper: at a resting potential of minus 70 to minus 80 millivolts it maintains an internode 200 micrometres to 2 millimetres long supporting conduction up to 80 to 120 metres per second, delivering lactate and pyruvate, sensing axonal potassium and adenosine triphosphate (ATP); internodes elongate over years of body growth.

◦ Repair Register: see Chapter 21; dedifferentiation begins within about 48 hours of axon loss.

• THE TRANSITION TABLE.

◦ Neuregulin 1 type III above threshold on a large axon (developmental signal) - precursor into Membrane Bulldozer, then One-Axon Keeper - days to weeks, then lifelong.

◦ Axon injury and loss of contact - One-Axon Keeper into Repair Register (Chapter 21) - within about 48 hours.

◦ Axon regrowth and re-contact - Repair Register back to One-Axon Keeper - weeks to months.

• FAULT REGIMES AND WATCHDOGS.

◦ Dysmyelination is a boundary from gene dosage rather than a used mode: PMP22 duplication causes Charcot-Marie-Tooth type 1A; immune attack strips myelin in Guillain-Barre syndrome. The watchdog is the neuregulin-set g-ratio, and metabolic injury in diabetes is the world's commonest neuropathy.


ENTRY 20. NON-MYELINATING (REMAK) SCHWANN CELL (keyed to other-cells catalogue Chapter 20)

MODES AND MODE TRANSITIONS:

The Remak Schwann cell is a mode-poor caretaker with one continuous partition-and-support regime, plus the injury-thrown repair register catalogued as Chapter 21 and cross-referenced here as its repair mode.

• THE MODE REGISTER.

◦ Bundle Sleeving - the Fibre Sorter - the standing regime pocketing and separating many small axons so no two touch, with ionic and metabolic support.

◦ Repair Register - the Regrowth-Rail Layer - the c-Jun-driven repair state of Chapter 21; a mode of this cell, cross-referenced there.

• PER-MODE TRANSFER FUNCTIONS.

◦ Fibre Sorter: at a resting potential of minus 70 to minus 80 millivolts it holds 1 to more than 20 axons of 0.2 to 1.5 micrometres in separate pockets over roughly 200 to 500 micrometres of bundle, buffering potassium and supplying glial-cell-line-derived neurotrophic factor, answering C-fibre adenosine triphosphate (ATP) with calcium transients over seconds and continuously rebuilding the wall between every pair of axons.

◦ Repair Register: see Chapter 21; reprogramming within about 48 hours of injury.

• THE TRANSITION TABLE.

◦ Axonal ATP and adhesion cues - moment-to-moment partition maintenance within Fibre Sorter - seconds to days.

◦ Axon injury - Fibre Sorter into Repair Register (Chapter 21) - within about 48 hours.

◦ Reinnervation - Repair Register back to Fibre Sorter - weeks.

• FAULT REGIMES AND WATCHDOGS.

◦ Partition failure is a used capability turned pathological: deleting single Schwann-cell genes such as LRP1 degrades the pocketing and produces neuropathic pain; the watchdog is continuous wall rebuilding. Small-fibre neuropathy degenerates the C fibres and their sleeves together.


ENTRY 21. REPAIR SCHWANN CELL (keyed to other-cells catalogue Chapter 21)

MODES AND MODE TRANSITIONS:

The repair Schwann cell is not a resident type but the injury-induced repair mode into which the myelinating Schwann cell of Chapter 19 and the Remak cell of Chapter 20 reprogram under the master switch c-Jun; this entry formalises that status, and Chapters 19 and 20 list it as their repair register. What follows is the internal phase structure of that one register.

• THE MODE REGISTER.

◦ Demolition - the Wrecking Crew - myelin breakdown and macrophage recruitment for Wallerian degeneration.

◦ Trophic Support and Track-Laying - the Rail Layer - secretion of survival factors and elongation into the bands of Buengner.

◦ Escort and Redifferentiation - the Homecoming - guiding a regrowing axon and then reverting to the myelinating or Remak identity.

• PER-MODE TRANSFER FUNCTIONS.

◦ Wrecking Crew: within about 48 hours of axotomy, resting near minus 70 millivolts, the cell activates c-Jun, digests its own myelin (myelinophagy) and summons macrophages, with injury calcium waves sweeping the network within minutes; demolition runs over 1 to 2 weeks.

◦ Rail Layer: cells 700 to 1,000 micrometres long align into bands of Buengner and secrete trophic factors, maintained over months, migrating as cords along regrown blood vessels.

◦ Homecoming: on axon arrival the cell guides growth cones at roughly 1 to 3 millimetres per day (clinical rule of thumb about 1 millimetre per day) and then redifferentiates.

• THE TRANSITION TABLE.

◦ Loss of axon contact and axonal neuregulin, plus injury adenosine triphosphate (ATP) and calcium - myelinating or Remak cell into Wrecking Crew - within about 48 hours.

◦ Debris cleared - Wrecking Crew to Rail Layer - 1 to 2 weeks.

◦ Regrowing axon re-contact - Rail Layer to Homecoming, then back to Chapter 19 or 20 identity - weeks to months.

• FAULT REGIMES AND WATCHDOGS.

◦ Chronic-denervation atrophy is the central failure: over roughly 6 to 12 months without an axon the repair cells atrophy and regeneration stalls, the unsolved problem of nerve surgery; the watchdog is the time-since-injury clock, and disordered escort yields painful neuromas.


ENTRY 22. TERMINAL (PERISYNAPTIC) SCHWANN CELL / TELOGLIA (keyed to other-cells catalogue Chapter 22)

MODES AND MODE TRANSITIONS:

The terminal Schwann cell is a two-mode junction glia: a resting capping-and-listening mode that modulates transmission, and an injury-thrown sprouting mode that guides reinnervation.

• THE MODE REGISTER.

◦ Capping Modulation - the Junction Caretaker - the healthy state insulating the neuromuscular junction and tuning transmitter release by reading synaptic traffic.

◦ Sprouting Guidance - the Bridge Builder - the denervation state growing processes that lead axons back to vacated endplates.

• PER-MODE TRANSFER FUNCTIONS.

◦ Junction Caretaker: at a resting potential near minus 70 millivolts, three to five cells per endplate over a 20 to 50 micrometre territory read spillover acetylcholine and adenosine triphosphate (ATP) through muscarinic and purinergic receptors, and high-frequency motor bursts evoke calcium transients over seconds that feed back to adjust transmitter release.

◦ Bridge Builder: denervation triggers sprouts extending up to several hundred micrometres over 1 to 3 days, guiding regenerating axons back over weeks.

• THE TRANSITION TABLE.

◦ Loss of nerve-terminal contact (denervation) - Junction Caretaker to Bridge Builder - 1 to 3 days.

◦ Endplate re-occupied - Bridge Builder back to Junction Caretaker - weeks.

• FAULT REGIMES AND WATCHDOGS.

◦ Failed reinnervation is the boundary: in amyotrophic lateral sclerosis teloglial repair cannot keep pace with terminal die-back, and in ageing sarcopenia the endplate fragments; the watchdog is the sprouting programme, which botulinum toxin engages therapeutically.


ENTRY 23. SATELLITE GLIAL CELL (keyed to other-cells catalogue Chapter 23)

MODES AND MODE TRANSITIONS:

The satellite glial cell is a two-mode envelope cell: a resting insulated-housekeeping mode and an injury-thrown coupled-and-activated mode that rewires the ganglion's glial architecture.

• THE MODE REGISTER.

◦ Insulated Housekeeping - the Private Room - the resting state cradling one neuronal soma across a 20-nanometre cleft, buffering potassium and glutamate.

◦ Coupled Activation - the Party Wall Down - the injury state opening gap junctions between previously separate envelopes into coupled assemblies.

• PER-MODE TRANSFER FUNCTIONS.

◦ Private Room: 4 to 10 cells per neuron at a resting potential near minus 80 millivolts, dominated by Kir4.1, clear potassium and glutamate and supply glutamine, answering neuronal adenosine triphosphate (ATP) with calcium transients over seconds; loss of Kir4.1 alone produces pain.

◦ Party Wall Down: injury raises glial fibrillary acidic protein (GFAP) and opens gap junctions several-fold over 1 to 4 days, converting isolated units into coupled networks that persist weeks to months.

• THE TRANSITION TABLE.

◦ Nerve injury or inflammation - Private Room to Party Wall Down - 1 to 4 days.

◦ The activated coupled state outlasts the injury and does not reliably reverse, helping explain chronic pain - weeks to months.

• FAULT REGIMES AND WATCHDOGS.

◦ The coupled-activation mode itself, when it persists, is the pathological boundary, underlying chronic neuropathic pain, postherpetic neuralgia and migraine mechanisms; the watchdog is envelope insulation, and its loss is read as rising GFAP and gap-junction coupling.


ENTRY 24. ENTERIC GLIAL CELL (keyed to other-cells catalogue Chapter 24)

MODES AND MODE TRANSITIONS:

The enteric glial cell is a mode-moderate three-way interpreter: a standing housekeeping-and-signalling regime, a reactive register, and a rare demand-driven neurogenic mode.

• THE MODE REGISTER.

◦ Housekeeping and Modulation - the Gut Interpreter - the standing regime buffering ions, translating microbial, neuronal and immune signals, and modulating motility.

◦ Reactive Register - the Alarmed Housekeeper - the inflammation-induced state altering barrier and signalling.

◦ Neurogenic Conversion - the Reserve Mother - the duress state in which glia become new enteric neurons.

• PER-MODE TRANSFER FUNCTIONS.

◦ Gut Interpreter: at a resting potential near minus 60 to minus 80 millivolts, calcium transients evoked by adenosine triphosphate (ATP), transmitters and stretch propagate through connexin 43 gap junctions as slow waves over tens of seconds, tracking motility cycles.

◦ Alarmed Housekeeper: microbial products and cytokines shift the cell to a reactive phenotype over days, altering barrier support.

◦ Reserve Mother: sustained injury or loss drives conversion to enteric neurons over weeks.

• THE TRANSITION TABLE.

◦ ATP, stretch, microbial and immune signals - moment-to-moment activity within Gut Interpreter - seconds to tens of seconds.

◦ Inflammation (Crohn, colitis) - Gut Interpreter to Alarmed Housekeeper - days.

◦ Neuronal loss under duress - toward Reserve Mother - weeks.

• FAULT REGIMES AND WATCHDOGS.

◦ Persistent reactive signalling underlies irritable bowel syndrome and inflammatory bowel disease; enteric glia also carry early alpha-synuclein pathology in Parkinson disease. The watchdog is balanced three-way signalling, and ganglion destruction in Chagas disease is the boundary yielding megacolon.


ENTRY 25. OLFACTORY ENSHEATHING CELL (keyed to other-cells catalogue Chapter 25)

MODES AND MODE TRANSITIONS:

The olfactory ensheathing cell is a mode-poor perpetual-escort cell that reruns a developmental guidance programme for life; it is best described as one lifelong regime with a phagocytic sub-duty rather than distinct switched modes.

• THE MODE REGISTER.

◦ Perpetual Escort - the Border Pilot - the lifelong regime sorting and targeting newborn olfactory axons across the central-nervous-system frontier.

◦ Debris Clearance - the Quiet Sweeper - the concurrent phagocytic sub-duty removing dying axons without inflammation.

• PER-MODE TRANSFER FUNCTIONS.

◦ Border Pilot: at a resting potential near minus 60 to minus 70 millivolts it gathers axons of about 0.2 micrometres into sleeves, negotiates a standing gate with repellent astrocytes, and guides each new axon to its correct glomerulus zone over the days to weeks a new axon takes to reach the bulb, answering axonal adenosine triphosphate (ATP) with calcium responses.

◦ Quiet Sweeper: it phagocytoses dead-axon debris over hours to days without triggering inflammation.

• THE TRANSITION TABLE.

◦ Arrival of newborn growth cones - engages Border Pilot escort - days to weeks; this is continuous rather than a state switch.

◦ Accumulating debris - engages Quiet Sweeper in parallel - hours to days.

• FAULT REGIMES AND WATCHDOGS.

◦ Corridor failure is the boundary: when regeneration cannot re-thread the cribriform route, post-viral or post-traumatic anosmia follows, made famous by COVID-19; the watchdog is the open gate itself, whose price is a pathogen entry route toward the brain.


ENTRY 26. PERINEURIAL GLIAL CELL (keyed to other-cells catalogue Chapter 26)

MODES AND MODE TRANSITIONS:

The perineurial cell is a mode-poor barrier cell with a single standing regime: maintain a sealed, pressurised diffusion fence. Its electrically relevant property is resistance, not excitability.

• THE MODE REGISTER.

◦ Sealed Barrier Maintenance - the Fascicle Fence - the standing regime holding tight-junction-sealed lamellae as the blood-nerve barrier and bearing the nerve's tensile strength.

• PER-MODE TRANSFER FUNCTIONS.

◦ Fascicle Fence: 3 to 15 concentric layers 0.1 to 1 micrometre thick, sealed by claudin-1 tight junctions at a resting potential near minus 60 millivolts, actively transport glucose inward and contaminants outward while holding a few millimetres of mercury of positive endoneurial pressure; transport across the sheath takes minutes, as regional anaesthesia demonstrates.

• THE TRANSITION TABLE.

◦ There is no second working mode; the only state change is injury-driven barrier breakdown and slow re-formation over weeks, which is a fault, not a used mode.

• FAULT REGIMES AND WATCHDOGS.

◦ Barrier breakdown admits proteins and immune attack in diabetic and inflammatory neuropathies; the watchdog is junctional integrity, near-zero permeability to protein. Leprosy breaches the compartment via Schwann cells behind the fence, and perineurioma is its rare tumour.


ENTRY 27. BOUNDARY CAP CELL (keyed to other-cells catalogue Chapter 27)

MODES AND MODE TRANSITIONS:

The boundary cap cell is a transient developmental cell with a two-phase life: a checkpoint mode policing the root frontier, then a dispersal mode seeding progeny. Its signalling is molecular, not electrical, and membrane properties are uncharacterised.

• THE MODE REGISTER.

◦ Frontier Checkpoint - the Customs House - the regime clustering at root entry and exit points, turning back cell bodies while passing growth cones.

◦ Dispersal and Differentiation - the Handover - the regime dissolving the cap into Schwann cells, satellite glia, sensory neurons and cutaneous glia.

• PER-MODE TRANSFER FUNCTIONS.

◦ Customs House: defined by Krox20 (Egr2), a cap of dozens to hundreds of cells uses plexin-semaphorin repulsion read only by somata to hold roughly 120 crossing points through the second to fourth months of human gestation, inspecting parts of a single crossing cell differently.

◦ Handover: over the following weeks the cap disperses and its daughters commit to root Schwann-cell, satellite-glial, sensory-neuron and cutaneous-glial fates.

• THE TRANSITION TABLE.

◦ Developmental timing signals (thrown from above by the embryonic programme) - Customs House to Handover - across gestational weeks, one-way.

• FAULT REGIMES AND WATCHDOGS.

◦ In animal models, cap ablation or semaphorin-plexin disruption lets motor neurons escape into the roots; corresponding human malformations are presumed but not proven, low confidence. Its lineage is a candidate origin for root neurofibromas.


ENTRY 28. CUTANEOUS (SKIN) GLIA / NOCICEPTIVE SCHWANN CELL (keyed to other-cells catalogue Chapter 28)

MODES AND MODE TRANSITIONS:

The nociceptive Schwann cell is a genuine sensory transducer, so its mode axis is electrical after all: a resting maintenance mode and an active transduction mode, with an inflammation-driven sensitised register.

• THE MODE REGISTER.

◦ Resting Maintenance - the Idle Mesh - the standing state supporting nociceptor terminals in the subepidermal plexus.

◦ Mechanotransduction - the Pain Trigger - the active state converting mechanical force into depolarisation that drives the partner nociceptor.

◦ Sensitised Register - the Sore Mesh - the inflammation-driven lowered-threshold state.

• PER-MODE TRANSFER FUNCTIONS.

◦ Idle Mesh: at a resting potential near minus 70 millivolts, 20 to 50 micrometres below the surface, it maintains terminals and guides reinnervation.

◦ Pain Trigger: mechanical force opens cation channels within milliseconds of indentation, depolarising the cell and, sufficient by itself in mice, firing the nociceptor to evoke pain behaviour; silencing it raises mechanical thresholds while sparing heat pain.

◦ Sore Mesh: keratinocyte adenosine triphosphate (ATP) and inflammatory mediators sensitise the cell over minutes to hours.

• THE TRANSITION TABLE.

◦ Mechanical indentation - Idle Mesh to Pain Trigger - milliseconds.

◦ Skin inflammation - Idle Mesh to Sore Mesh - minutes to hours.

◦ Force removed / inflammation resolved - back toward Idle Mesh - milliseconds to hours.

• FAULT REGIMES AND WATCHDOGS.

◦ Loss or dysfunction causes terminal retraction with paradoxical mechanical hypersensitivity, a candidate mechanism in small-fibre neuropathy; the watchdog is mesh integrity, and persistent sensitisation contributes to tactile allodynia.


ENTRY 29. LAMELLAR CORPUSCLE CELLS OF MEISSNER AND PACINI (keyed to other-cells catalogue Chapter 29)

MODES AND MODE TRANSITIONS:

These lamellar cells compute by mechanics: their primary mode axis is the mechanical filter state of the corpuscle, with a newly recognised active electrical mode in the Pacinian inner core.

• THE MODE REGISTER.

◦ Mechanical Band-Pass Filtering - the Onion Filter - the standing regime in which stacked lamellae absorb static pressure and pass only rapid oscillation to the axon.

◦ Inner-Core Amplification - the Glial Preamp - the Pacinian inner-core mode carrying mechanically gated and voltage-gated sodium currents that boost the vibration response, moderate confidence, established 2025.

• PER-MODE TRANSFER FUNCTIONS.

◦ Onion Filter: sustained pressure is absorbed by lamellar creep over tens to hundreds of milliseconds and never reaches the axon; Meissner corpuscles pass flutter near 10 to 50 hertz, Pacinian corpuscles vibration near 40 to 400 hertz with best sensitivity near 200 to 250 hertz, down to tens of nanometres of displacement, at zero energy cost per event.

◦ Glial Preamp: inner-core lamellar Schwann cells resting near minus 70 millivolts add an active excitatory boost over fractions of a millisecond, potentiating rather than merely cushioning.

• THE TRANSITION TABLE.

◦ Onset of vibration in the tuned band - Onion Filter engages, and in the Pacinian core the Glial Preamp - sub-millisecond.

◦ Static or slowly changing load - filtered out by lamellar creep - tens to hundreds of milliseconds.

• FAULT REGIMES AND WATCHDOGS.
