◦ Age-related and diabetic corpuscle loss degrades fine touch and contributes to falls; vibration white finger damages the apparatus. The watchdog is lamellar mechanical integrity; hyperplasia and pacinioma are rare painful lesions of the organ.


ENTRY 30. COCHLEAR SUPPORTING CELLS (keyed to other-cells catalogue Chapter 30)

MODES AND MODE TRANSITIONS:

The cochlear supporting cells are a mode-poor scaffold family that computes nothing but sets the physical constants of the cochlear amplifier: essentially one standing regime combining ionic recycling and mechanical support, with slow calcium waves as its only dynamic signal.

• THE MODE REGISTER.

◦ Recycling-and-Scaffolding Service - the Harp Frame - the standing regime passing potassium through gap junctions and holding hair-cell geometry with active mechanical stiffness.

• PER-MODE TRANSFER FUNCTIONS.

◦ Harp Frame: at a resting potential near minus 60 to minus 90 millivolts set by potassium permeability, connexin-26 and connexin-30 junctions pass potassium laterally toward the spiral ligament and conduct calcium waves cell to cell over seconds, while pillar and Deiters cells set the stiffness that shapes basilar-membrane amplification, roughly one Deiters cell per outer hair cell across about 12,000 outer hair cells.

• THE TRANSITION TABLE.

◦ Continuous hearing drives ongoing potassium recycling within the single mode; extracellular adenosine triphosphate (ATP) and vibration modulate calcium waves over seconds. There is no second working mode.

• FAULT REGIMES AND WATCHDOGS.

◦ Connexin-26 (GJB2) mutation breaks the potassium recycling loop and is the commonest cause of hereditary non-syndromic deafness; the watchdog is gap-junction continuity. Loss of supporting-cell integrity contributes to permanent noise-induced and age-related hearing loss, since these cells cannot regenerate hair cells.


ENTRY 31. VESTIBULAR SUPPORTING CELLS (keyed to other-cells catalogue Chapter 31)

MODES AND MODE TRANSITIONS:

This is a mode-poor housekeeping cell whose one everyday job is continuous ion recycling and secretion, but which holds in reserve a rare, injury-triggered regenerative mode that its cochlear cousin has lost.

• THE MODE REGISTER.

◦ Steady-State Support - the Quiet Scaffold - continuous potassium recycling and structural and secretory maintenance of the hair cells and their gels.

◦ Secretory Manufacture - the Crystal Foundry - active production and repair of the gelatinous cupula, otolithic membrane, and calcium-carbonate otoconia.

◦ Regenerative Conversion - the Second Chance - injury-triggered re-entry into a proliferative, hair-cell-producing programme, unique among mammalian inner-ear support cells.

• PER-MODE TRANSFER FUNCTIONS.

◦ Steady-State Support: membrane held near minus 60 to minus 80 millivolts, potassium clearance scaled to hair-cell activity, no action potentials; the resting default.

◦ Secretory Manufacture: matrix and otoconial output rising with developmental and repair demand, running continuously at a low background rate in the adult (moderate confidence, rates not well quantified).

◦ Regenerative Conversion: a small fraction of cells re-enter the cell cycle or trans-differentiate over weeks to months, giving partial, incomplete recovery of hair-cell number after damage (low confidence on human rates).

• THE TRANSITION TABLE.

◦ Rising hair-cell activity - Steady-State Support to heightened potassium clearance within the same mode - seconds.

◦ Developmental or repair demand - Steady-State Support to Secretory Manufacture - hours to days.

◦ Hair-cell loss and injury signalling - Steady-State Support to Regenerative Conversion - days to weeks; not thrown from above but triggered locally by damage.

• FAULT REGIMES AND WATCHDOGS.

◦ Otoconial displacement: crystals shed into a semicircular canal, warning signature positional vertigo, watchdog the repositioning reflexes and normal otoconial anchoring; underlies benign paroxysmal positional vertigo.

◦ Ionic overload and hydrops: failure of potassium recycling with endolymph swelling, watchdog the dark-cell potassium loop, contributing to Meniere disease.


ENTRY 32. OLFACTORY SUSTENTACULAR CELL (keyed to other-cells catalogue Chapter 32)

MODES AND MODE TRANSITIONS:

This is a mode-poor guardian cell running one continuous protective-and-detoxifying regime, with a distinct injury regime forced on it by viral infection rather than chosen physiologically.

• THE MODE REGISTER.

◦ Protective Support - the Standing Bodyguard - continuous insulation, ionic buffering, mucus contribution, and detoxification around the exposed olfactory neurons.

◦ Detoxifying Clearance - the Chemical Filter - upregulated drug-metabolising-enzyme activity and phagocytosis of dead cells when the inhaled chemical or debris load rises.

• PER-MODE TRANSFER FUNCTIONS.

◦ Protective Support: membrane near minus 50 to minus 80 millivolts, no action potentials, steady mucus and ionic maintenance; the default.

◦ Detoxifying Clearance: enzyme activity and engulfment scaling up over minutes to hours with toxin and debris load, then relaxing back (moderate confidence).

• THE TRANSITION TABLE.

◦ Rising inhaled toxin or debris load - Protective Support to Detoxifying Clearance - minutes to hours; locally triggered, not thrown from above.

◦ Epithelial turnover signals - renewal from basal stem cells replacing worn cells - weeks.

• FAULT REGIMES AND WATCHDOGS.

◦ Viral infection regime: SARS-CoV-2 (the virus of COVID-19) enters through the cell's abundant ACE2 (angiotensin-converting enzyme 2) and TMPRSS2 (transmembrane serine protease 2), warning signature sudden loss of smell without neuron death, watchdog the basal-stem-cell renewal that usually restores function over weeks.

◦ Toxic and inflammatory injury: chemical exposure or chronic rhinosinusitis degrading the support layer, with age-related decline as the slow boundary; this is a driven regime, not a used mode.


ENTRY 33. OLFACTORY HORIZONTAL AND GLOBOSE BASAL CELLS (keyed to other-cells catalogue Chapter 33)

MODES AND MODE TRANSITIONS:

This is a stem-cell pair defined by its cell-cycle and commitment modes: a quiescent reserve and an active everyday progenitor, formalising the quiescence-activation-division axis that the specification asks stem types to carry.

• THE MODE REGISTER.

◦ Reserve Quiescence - the Sleeping Reserve - horizontal basal cells held out of cycle against the basement membrane, a deep dormant pool.

◦ Routine Amplification - the Daily Line - globose basal cells dividing continuously to replace neurons and support cells during normal turnover.

◦ Emergency Reconstitution - the Rebuild Order - horizontal basal cells activating after severe injury to regenerate the whole epithelium, neurons and support cells alike.

• PER-MODE TRANSFER FUNCTIONS.

◦ Reserve Quiescence: out of cycle, low protein synthesis, high stress resistance, membrane near minus 50 to minus 70 millivolts; can wait indefinitely (moderate confidence).

◦ Routine Amplification: several divisions producing new olfactory sensory neurons over roughly one to a few weeks; the steady default of the globose pool.

◦ Emergency Reconstitution: multipotent divisions rebuilding the epithelium over weeks to months after severe injury.

• THE TRANSITION TABLE.

◦ Continuous turnover demand - Routine Amplification runs by default - days to weeks.

◦ Severe injury and loss of overlying neurons - Reserve Quiescence to Emergency Reconstitution - hours to days to switch on, weeks to months to complete; triggered locally by damage, not from above.

◦ Restoration of normal architecture - Emergency Reconstitution back to Reserve Quiescence - over weeks.

• FAULT REGIMES AND WATCHDOGS.

◦ Stem-cell exhaustion: repeated or severe injury depleting the reserve, warning signature failure of smell to recover, watchdog the finite dormant pool; underlies permanent anosmia after some viral and toxic injuries and part of age-related smell loss.


ENTRY 34. TASTE BUD TYPE I CELL (keyed to other-cells catalogue Chapter 34)

MODES AND MODE TRANSITIONS:

This is a mode-poor glial-like buffering cell running essentially one continuous clearance regime, a peripheral astrocyte in disguise, with a hint of an added immune regime.

• THE MODE REGISTER.

◦ Clearance Buffering - the Bud Janitor - continuous uptake of spent glutamate and adenosine triphosphate and recycling of potassium to keep each taste channel clean and private.

◦ Immune Signalling - the Quiet Sentinel - secretion of immune signalling molecules on challenge, a recently described minor regime.

• PER-MODE TRANSFER FUNCTIONS.

◦ Clearance Buffering: transmitter clearance over milliseconds to seconds via GLAST and ectoATPase, potassium buffering continuous, no informative action potentials; the default.

◦ Immune Signalling: secretion of defensive mediators when challenged (low confidence, newly characterised).

• THE TRANSITION TABLE.

◦ Taste-cell firing spilling transmitter and potassium - clearance activity scales within the same mode - milliseconds to seconds.

◦ Local immune or damage signal - Clearance Buffering to added Immune Signalling - minutes to hours; locally triggered.

◦ Whole-bud renewal from local stem cells - continuous replacement - every 1 to 2 weeks.

• FAULT REGIMES AND WATCHDOGS.

◦ No distinct pathological electrical regime; its dysfunction is a loss-of-function slide contributing to taste disturbance and taste loss in ageing, after radiotherapy and chemotherapy, and in some viral and inflammatory conditions, with the whole-bud renewal cycle as the watchdog.


ENTRY 35. RETINAL PIGMENT EPITHELIAL CELL (keyed to other-cells catalogue Chapter 35)

MODES AND MODE TRANSITIONS:

This is a rhythm-driven service cell whose signature is a circadian two-phase duty cycle, a resting valet mode and a once-a-day phagocytic burst, rather than an electrical mode set.

• THE MODE REGISTER.

◦ Resting Valet - the Round-the-Clock Attendant - continuous retinoid cycling, ion and nutrient transport, light absorption, and barrier maintenance.

◦ Dawn Phagocytic Burst - the Morning Feed - a clock-timed surge of engulfment of shed photoreceptor disc tips one to two hours after light onset.

• PER-MODE TRANSFER FUNCTIONS.

◦ Resting Valet: strong transepithelial polarisation, retinoid recycling and transport continuous, generating the eye's slow standing potential; no action potentials; the baseline.

◦ Dawn Phagocytic Burst: each cell engulfs a burst clearing a few thousand discs per day across the 20 to 30 photoreceptors it serves, digesting in excess of a billion discs over a lifetime (well characterised).

• THE TRANSITION TABLE.

◦ Circadian clock plus light onset - Resting Valet to Dawn Phagocytic Burst - once per day, burst lasting 1 to 2 hours; thrown from above by the circadian broadcast and light-dark cycle.

◦ Burst completion - back to Resting Valet - over hours.

• FAULT REGIMES AND WATCHDOGS.

◦ Lipofuscin and drusen overload: decades-long accumulation of undigested material, warning signature failing phagocytosis and pigment change, watchdog the daily clearance rhythm; drives age-related macular degeneration.

◦ Retinoid-cycle or phagocytic gene defects: driving forms of retinitis pigmentosa and Leber congenital amaurosis; a driven fault, not a used mode.


ENTRY 36. RADIAL GLIAL CELL (keyed to other-cells catalogue Chapter 36)

MODES AND MODE TRANSITIONS:

This is a mode-rich founder cell whose regimes are cell-division modes crossed with a scheduled competence programme, coordinated across the tissue by calcium waves rather than by firing.

• THE MODE REGISTER.

◦ Symmetric Proliferation - the Pool-Builder - two stem daughters per division, expanding the progenitor pool early.

◦ Asymmetric Neurogenesis - the Cortex-Builder - one stem daughter and one neuron or intermediate progenitor per division, building the cortical layers.

◦ Gliogenic Switch - the Late Shift - late-stage production of glia after neurons are made.

◦ Guide-Rail Scaffolding - the Ladder - the basal fibre serving continuously as a migration rail regardless of division mode.

• PER-MODE TRANSFER FUNCTIONS.

◦ Symmetric Proliferation: short cell cycle (roughly 8 hours early), exponential pool growth; dominant early in gestation.

◦ Asymmetric Neurogenesis: lengthening cell cycle (toward more than 24 hours), deep-layer then upper-layer neurons in sequence.

◦ Gliogenic Switch: output shifts to astrocytes and oligodendrocyte precursors late.

◦ Guide-Rail Scaffolding: passive mechanical function, endfoot anchored at the pia; continuous.

• THE TRANSITION TABLE.

◦ Competence-clock progression plus Notch and morphogen signalling - Symmetric Proliferation to Asymmetric Neurogenesis - over developmental days.

◦ Later competence window - Asymmetric Neurogenesis to Gliogenic Switch - over developmental weeks.

◦ Propagating calcium waves through the germinal zone - modulate division rate tissue-wide - seconds to minutes; a collective timing signal, not thrown from a brain state.

• FAULT REGIMES AND WATCHDOGS.

◦ Division-mode imbalance: too few proliferative divisions gives microcephaly (as in ASPM or WDR62 mutations), too many gives megalencephaly; watchdog the competence clock.

◦ Fibre or endfoot failure: migrating neurons stall, producing lissencephaly, periventricular heterotopia, and cobblestone malformations; a driven fault.


ENTRY 37. OUTER (BASAL) RADIAL GLIAL CELL (keyed to other-cells catalogue Chapter 37)

MODES AND MODE TRANSITIONS:

This is an amplifier progenitor whose modes are proliferative self-renewal versus differentiation, punctuated by a distinctive pre-division movement, the mechanism behind human cortical expansion.

• THE MODE REGISTER.

◦ Proliferative Self-Renewal - the Amplifier - repeated divisions that multiply the progenitor pool in the outer subventricular zone.

◦ Neurogenic and Gliogenic Output - the Delivery - differentiative divisions producing upper-layer neurons and, later, glia.

◦ Mitotic Somal Translocation - the Launch - the pre-division leap of the cell body outward along its own fibre.

• PER-MODE TRANSFER FUNCTIONS.

◦ Proliferative Self-Renewal: many-hour cell cycles with self-secreted matrix keeping daughters proliferative, giving exponential expansion; dominant in the second trimester.

◦ Neurogenic and Gliogenic Output: differentiative divisions biased by developmental time.

◦ Mitotic Somal Translocation: a minutes-long outward saltation of the soma immediately before division.

• THE TRANSITION TABLE.

◦ Niche growth-factor and matrix enrichment - toward Proliferative Self-Renewal - over hours to days.

◦ Competence progression - Proliferative Self-Renewal to Neurogenic and Gliogenic Output - over developmental weeks.

◦ Each division cycle - transient Mitotic Somal Translocation then division - minutes; intrinsic, not thrown from above.

• FAULT REGIMES AND WATCHDOGS.

◦ Under-amplification: too few rounds, warning signature reduced cortex and gyrification, watchdog the outer-subventricular-zone niche; contributes to microcephaly.

◦ Disordered proliferation: linked to malformations of cortical development and modelled in cerebral organoids; a driven fault.


ENTRY 38. ADULT NEURAL STEM CELL OF THE VENTRICULAR-SUBVENTRICULAR ZONE, THE B1 CELL (keyed to other-cells catalogue Chapter 38)

MODES AND MODE TRANSITIONS:

This is a two-mode stem switch whose whole biology turns on reversible dormancy: a deep quiescence and a rare activation, with the human cell effectively frozen in the first.

• THE MODE REGISTER.

◦ Deep Quiescence - the Sleeping Reserve - years-long dormancy with full stem competence retained, the near-permanent human default.

◦ Activated Division - the Wake-Up - re-entry into the cell cycle to produce transit-amplifying cells and neuroblasts, robust in other mammals, minimal in adult humans.

• PER-MODE TRANSFER FUNCTIONS.

◦ Deep Quiescence: out of cycle, hyperpolarised near minus 80 millivolts, potassium-dominated, held by gamma-aminobutyric acid (GABA) and Wnt-modulating brakes such as SFRP1; lasts months to years.

◦ Activated Division: a single activation-and-division cycle over days, feeding the rostral migratory stream in non-human mammals; near-zero output in humans after roughly 18 months of age (moderate confidence).

• THE TRANSITION TABLE.

◦ Loss of GABA quiescence signal, injury, or release of Wnt brakes - Deep Quiescence to Activated Division - days; partly locally triggered, partly under vascular and cerebrospinal-fluid control.

◦ Restoration of niche signals - Activated Division back to Deep Quiescence - days.

◦ Human developmental decline - progressive lock into Deep Quiescence - over the first 18 months of life.

• FAULT REGIMES AND WATCHDOGS.

◦ Niche failure: age-related collapse of germinal capacity, watchdog the niche signalling itself.

◦ Aberrant reactivation: uncontrolled division proposed as a cell-of-origin for certain gliomas; so both too little and too much activity are pathological.


ENTRY 39. ADULT HIPPOCAMPAL RADIAL GLIA-LIKE CELL, THE TYPE 1 CELL (keyed to other-cells catalogue Chapter 39)

MODES AND MODE TRANSITIONS:

This is a stem switch whose cognitive weight lies not in the stem cell's own firing but in the transient hyper-excitable mode of the young neurons it makes, and whose human activity is genuinely contested.

• THE MODE REGISTER.

◦ Stem Quiescence - the Waiting Seed - the astrocyte-like Type 1 cell held out of cycle, the resting default.

◦ Stem Activation - the Seeding - re-entry into division to produce transit-amplifying progeny (robust in rodents, uncertain in adult humans).

◦ Young-Neuron Critical Window - the Eager Recruit - the transient high-excitability, high-plasticity mode of adult-born granule neurons, the cell's cognitive payload.

• PER-MODE TRANSFER FUNCTIONS.

◦ Stem Quiescence: non-spiking, near minus 80 millivolts, passive astrocytic currents; the baseline.

◦ Stem Activation: division producing new granule neurons, experience- and activity-regulated in rodents; human rate plausibly near-zero to modest (low confidence, contested).

◦ Young-Neuron Critical Window: low firing threshold, high input resistance, enhanced synaptic plasticity around 4 to 6 weeks of cell age (rodent), sharpening pattern separation.

• THE TRANSITION TABLE.

◦ Exercise, enriched environment, serotonin - Stem Quiescence to Stem Activation - days; upregulated by these, downregulated by stress, thrown partly from systemic and behavioural state.

◦ Neuron maturation - Young-Neuron Critical Window to mature granule cell - over several weeks; intrinsic ageing of the neuron.

• FAULT REGIMES AND WATCHDOGS.

◦ Reduced neurogenesis: associated with impaired pattern separation, major depression, and cognitive ageing; watchdog the exercise- and mood-linked drive.

◦ Aberrant migration: new neurons mis-migrate in temporal-lobe epilepsy, and neurogenesis is disrupted in Alzheimer's disease.

◦ The catalogue's unique honest fault: we do not yet know how much of this cell's function survives into adult human life (Sorrells 2018 versus Boldrini 2018 and Moreno-Jimenez 2019, largely a tissue-fixation dispute).


ENTRY 40. TRANSIT-AMPLIFYING PROGENITOR AND NEUROBLAST POPULATIONS (keyed to other-cells catalogue Chapter 40)

MODES AND MODE TRANSITIONS:

These are process cells whose modes are the stages of an assembly line: fast amplification, then chain migration, then survival-or-death, driven by gamma-aminobutyric acid (GABA) and adhesion rather than by firing.

• THE MODE REGISTER.

◦ Rapid Amplification - the Multiplier - the transit-amplifying cell dividing several times fast to set the batch size of neurons.

◦ Chain Migration - the Train - neuroblasts gliding as coordinated trains along one another through astrocytic tunnels.

◦ Survival Selection - the Audition - the activity-dependent choice between recruitment into the circuit and programmed death.

• PER-MODE TRANSFER FUNCTIONS.

◦ Rapid Amplification: several divisions over hours near minus 80 millivolts, non-spiking; sets neuron number per stem activation.

◦ Chain Migration: GABA-driven (depolarising, from high internal chloride) movement over days along the rostral migratory stream; neuroblasts electrically immature, not yet firing.

◦ Survival Selection: most neuroblasts die before maturing unless recruited by circuit activity.

• THE TRANSITION TABLE.

◦ Stem-cell activation upstream - into Rapid Amplification - hours.

◦ Completion of divisions plus adhesion cues (PSA-NCAM) - Rapid Amplification to Chain Migration - hours to days.

◦ Arrival and circuit activity - Chain Migration to Survival Selection - days; activity-dependent.

• FAULT REGIMES AND WATCHDOGS.

◦ Disordered amplification and migration: contributing to malformations of cortical development, with dentate neuroblasts mis-migrating in temporal-lobe epilepsy.

◦ Dysregulated transit-amplifying-like cells: implicated as a proliferative compartment in gliomas; a driven, oncological fault rather than a used mode.


ENTRY 41. BRAIN MICROVASCULAR ENDOTHELIAL CELL (keyed to other-cells catalogue Chapter 41)

MODES AND MODE TRANSITIONS:

This is a barrier cell with a clear two-regime axis, barrier-intact versus permeabilised, sitting on top of a continuous molecular-sorting function; the register formalises the barrier modes the literature supports.

• THE MODE REGISTER.

◦ Sealed Barrier - the Closed Customs House - continuous tight-junction sealing, near-zero transcytosis, and selective transport, the healthy default.

◦ Regulated Opening - the Controlled Gate - transient, reversible loosening of tight junctions for physiological signalling or deliberate drug delivery.

◦ Molecular Sorting - the Whitelist-Blacklist - the always-on import and efflux computation running underneath both barrier states.

• PER-MODE TRANSFER FUNCTIONS.

◦ Sealed Barrier: transendothelial resistance roughly 1,000 to 8,000 ohm square centimetres, near-zero fenestration, resting potential minus 30 to minus 60 millivolts; the baseline.

◦ Regulated Opening: vascular endothelial growth factor and similar signals loosening junctions over minutes to hours, resistance falling, then recovering.

◦ Molecular Sorting: GLUT1 glucose influx (blood glucose 4 to 6 millimolar), amino-acid carriers, receptor-mediated transcytosis, and roughly ninety efflux substrates via P-glycoprotein and BCRP; continuous.

• THE TRANSITION TABLE.

◦ Developmental Wnt and pericyte and astrocyte contact - induction into Sealed Barrier - developmental days to weeks; thrown by the neurovascular niche.

◦ Vascular endothelial growth factor, focused ultrasound, or mild inflammation - Sealed Barrier to Regulated Opening - minutes to hours, reversible.

◦ Age - slow drift toward looser barrier - decades.

• FAULT REGIMES AND WATCHDOGS.

◦ Barrier breakdown: cytokine-driven (TNF-alpha, IL-6, IL-1-beta) loss of claudin-5 and occludin with reactive-oxygen-species generation, boundary signature paracellular leak and oedema, warning condition sustained rather than transient opening; watchdog the pericyte and astrocyte support and junction repair. Occurs in multiple sclerosis, stroke, traumatic brain injury, and sepsis, with chronic leak in Alzheimer's and small-vessel disease.

◦ GLUT1 deficiency: SLC2A1 loss starving the brain of glucose; a driven genetic fault.


ENTRY 42. PERICYTE (keyed to other-cells catalogue Chapter 42)

MODES AND MODE TRANSITIONS:

This is a contractile mural cell whose everyday modes are graded constriction and relaxation for single-capillary flow control, plus a slow barrier-signalling role, and which can be driven into a lethal locked-shut regime.

• THE MODE REGISTER.

◦ Relaxed Patency - the Open Segment - low contractile tone keeping its capillary segment dilated and flowing.

◦ Active Constriction - the Squeeze - calcium-driven shortening that narrows one capillary segment to throttle local flow.

◦ Barrier and Trophic Support - the Wall-Keeper - continuous signalling to endothelium to induce and maintain the barrier and clear waste.

• PER-MODE TRANSFER FUNCTIONS.

◦ Relaxed Patency: resting near minus 40 to minus 50 millivolts, low intracellular calcium, actin-myosin relaxed; the default.

◦ Active Constriction: depolarisation opens voltage-gated and store-operated calcium channels, calcium rises, the segment constricts over seconds; graded, capillary-by-capillary.

◦ Barrier and Trophic Support: PDGF-BB-linked signalling, matrix secretion, amyloid-beta clearance over hours to days; continuous.

• THE TRANSITION TABLE.

◦ Vasoactive mediators from active neuropil (potassium, prostaglandins, nitric oxide, adenosine, adenosine triphosphate (ATP)) - between Relaxed Patency and Active Constriction - seconds; locally driven by neural activity.

◦ Astrocyte end-foot signals and wall stress - tuning tone within the same axis - seconds.

• FAULT REGIMES AND WATCHDOGS.

◦ Die-in-rigor no-reflow: after stroke, a calcium and oxidative-stress surge clamps the pericyte shut and it dies contracted, keeping the capillary plugged even after the artery reopens, boundary signature persistent constriction, watchdog calcium homeostasis and Rho-kinase control; a case of one cell dooming its patch of tissue.

◦ Progressive dropout: coverage falls roughly 35 to 45 percent in white-matter Alzheimer's, vascular, and post-stroke dementias, driving barrier breakdown; diabetic retinal pericyte loss gives microaneurysms.


ENTRY 43. VASCULAR SMOOTH MUSCLE CELL OF THE BRAIN VESSELS (keyed to other-cells catalogue Chapter 43)

MODES AND MODE TRANSITIONS:

This is a genuinely excitable contractile cell whose modes are graded tone with rhythmic vasomotion, plus a longer-term phenotype switch between contractile and synthetic identities.

• THE MODE REGISTER.

◦ Resting Tone - the Held Ring - partial tonic contraction setting baseline cerebral vascular resistance.

◦ Functional Hyperaemia Dilation - the Blood Summons - rapid relaxation that widens the vessel to feed an active region.

◦ Vasomotion - the Slow Pulse - spontaneous rhythmic calcium-driven oscillation of diameter.

◦ Synthetic Phenotype - the Remodeller - a proliferative, matrix-producing state used in growth and repair.

• PER-MODE TRANSFER FUNCTIONS.

◦ Resting Tone: resting minus 40 to minus 60 millivolts with a fraction of L-type calcium channels always open; the default.

◦ Functional Hyperaemia Dilation: astrocyte potassium and endothelial signals hyperpolarise the cell, close calcium channels, and dilate within 1 to 2 seconds, conducted upstream by gap junctions.

◦ Vasomotion: calcium sparks driving cycles of seconds.

◦ Synthetic Phenotype: shift to proliferation and matrix output over days.

• THE TRANSITION TABLE.

◦ Neural activity plus astrocyte potassium and arachidonic-acid metabolites - Resting Tone to Functional Hyperaemia Dilation - 1 to 2 seconds; locally driven.

◦ Intravascular pressure and shear - modulating Resting Tone and vasomotion - seconds.

◦ Chronic injury or hypertension signals - Contractile to Synthetic Phenotype - days.

• FAULT REGIMES AND WATCHDOGS.

◦ Hypertensive remodelling: wall thickening and stiffening over months to years degrading autoregulation, watchdog normal pressure control.

◦ Amyloid replacement (cerebral amyloid angiopathy), CADASIL (cerebral autosomal dominant arteriopathy with subcortical infarcts and leukoencephalopathy), and post-haemorrhage vasospasm; driven faults, with impaired neurovascular coupling in ageing.


ENTRY 44. PERIVASCULAR FIBROBLAST (keyed to other-cells catalogue Chapter 44)

MODES AND MODE TRANSITIONS:

This is a two-mode connective-tissue cell: a quiescent matrix-maintaining state and an injury-triggered myofibroblast scar state, with mechanosensing running throughout.

• THE MODE REGISTER.

◦ Quiescent Maintenance - the Plumber - steady matrix secretion and mechanically tuned remodelling of the perivascular sleeve.

◦ Myofibroblast Activation - the Scar Architect - injury-triggered proliferation and heavy scar-matrix production.

• PER-MODE TRANSFER FUNCTIONS.

◦ Quiescent Maintenance: non-excitable, variable resting potential (roughly minus 10 to minus 50 millivolts), collagen turnover over days, calcium used for secretion and mechanosensation; the default.

◦ Myofibroblast Activation: proliferation and fibrotic-matrix output over days to weeks, walling off damage but impeding regeneration.

• THE TRANSITION TABLE.

◦ Vessel pulsation and cerebrospinal-fluid flow - tuning matrix output within Quiescent Maintenance - continuous; mechanically driven.

◦ Injury, platelet-derived growth factor, transforming growth factor beta, inflammatory cytokines - Quiescent Maintenance to Myofibroblast Activation - days; locally triggered.

• FAULT REGIMES AND WATCHDOGS.

◦ Fibrotic scarring: after traumatic and ischaemic brain and spinal-cord injury, boundary signature persistent myofibroblast state, watchdog resolution signalling.

◦ Enlarged perivascular spaces and impaired glymphatic clearance in small-vessel disease and ageing; a slow driven fault.


ENTRY 45. MENINGEAL FIBROBLAST POPULATIONS: DURAL, ARACHNOID, AND PIAL CELLS (keyed to other-cells catalogue Chapter 45)

MODES AND MODE TRANSITIONS:

This is a barrier-and-scaffold cell group whose modes are a barrier-intact state (the arachnoid barrier), continuous matrix maintenance, and an injury scar state.

• THE MODE REGISTER.

◦ Barrier Seal - the Second Wall - the arachnoid barrier cells holding a claudin-11 and E-cadherin blood-cerebrospinal-fluid barrier.

◦ Matrix Maintenance - the Cloak-Maker - continuous collagen and matrix production of the three coverings.

◦ Scar Activation - the Wound-Filler - injury-triggered invasion and fibrotic-scar-core production.

• PER-MODE TRANSFER FUNCTIONS.

◦ Barrier Seal: 2 to 3 arachnoid cell layers sealed by claudin-11 and zonula occludens 1, non-excitable; the barrier default.

◦ Matrix Maintenance: dural collagen on the millimetre scale, pia near single-cell thickness, turnover over days.

◦ Scar Activation: proliferation and scar-matrix output over days to weeks after injury.

• THE TRANSITION TABLE.

◦ Developmental signals - mesenchymal fibroblasts switching on epithelial genes to form Barrier Seal - developmental days to weeks.

◦ Injury and inflammatory cytokines - Matrix Maintenance to Scar Activation - days to weeks; locally triggered.

• FAULT REGIMES AND WATCHDOGS.

◦ Barrier breakdown: bacterial meningitis disrupting claudin-11 junctions, boundary signature a leaky arachnoid barrier, watchdog junction integrity.

◦ Meningeal fibrosis and scarring after injury; meningioma arising from arachnoid cap cells (Chapter 46); driven faults.


ENTRY 46. ARACHNOID CAP AND GRANULATION CELLS (keyed to other-cells catalogue Chapter 46)

MODES AND MODE TRANSITIONS:

This is a two-state mechanical valve cell: pressure opens it, reversed pressure closes it, a one-way drain built from living cells with no moving parts of its own.

• THE MODE REGISTER.

◦ Open Valve - the Drain - transcellular vacuoles and channels passing cerebrospinal fluid into venous blood when fluid pressure exceeds venous pressure.

◦ Closed Valve - the Seal - the pathway shut when the gradient reverses, preventing blood reflux.

• PER-MODE TRANSFER FUNCTIONS.

◦ Open Valve: opens at a pressure gradient of roughly 3 to 5 millimetres of mercury, giving bulk one-way outflow; non-excitable, transport-driven.

◦ Closed Valve: sealed below threshold, no backflow; the resting state at low gradient.

• THE TRANSITION TABLE.

◦ Cerebrospinal-fluid pressure exceeding venous pressure by a few millimetres of mercury - Closed Valve to Open Valve - seconds; mechanically driven, not from a brain state.

◦ Gradient reversal - Open Valve to Closed Valve - seconds.

◦ Lifelong demand - granulation growth expanding drainage capacity - years to decades.

• FAULT REGIMES AND WATCHDOGS.

◦ Impaired absorption: obstruction after subarachnoid haemorrhage or meningitis, boundary signature failure to open at normal gradient, watchdog the pressure-sensing valve; causes communicating hydrocephalus and raised intracranial pressure.

◦ Cap-cell proliferation giving meningioma; a driven oncological fault.


ENTRY 47. CHOROID PLEXUS STROMAL CELLS (keyed to other-cells catalogue Chapter 47)

MODES AND MODE TRANSITIONS:

This is a mode-poor support pair (fibroblasts and pericytes) running continuous matrix maintenance and pericyte flow tuning, notable for deliberately holding a leaky vascular bed open.

• THE MODE REGISTER.

◦ Structural Maintenance - the Scaffold-Keeper - continuous collagen-stroma production suspending the fenestrated capillaries.

◦ Pericyte Flow Tuning - the Local Tap - graded pericyte contraction and relaxation regulating plexus blood flow.

• PER-MODE TRANSFER FUNCTIONS.

◦ Structural Maintenance: non-excitable fibroblasts (roughly minus 10 to minus 50 millivolts), matrix turnover over days, supporting capillaries with 60 to 80 nanometre fenestrations; the barrier sits in the epithelium above, not here.

◦ Pericyte Flow Tuning: pericytes near minus 40 to minus 50 millivolts contracting over seconds to adjust local flow.

• THE TRANSITION TABLE.

◦ Mechanical support demand - matrix output within Structural Maintenance - continuous.

◦ Local flow demand - pericyte constriction and relaxation - seconds.

◦ Inflammation - stromal expansion and rising immune traffic - hours to days.

• FAULT REGIMES AND WATCHDOGS.

◦ Age-related fibrosis and calcification: thickened basement membranes and psammoma bodies (the "brain sand"), boundary signature stiffened stroma, watchdog normal matrix turnover.

◦ Stromal inflammation in infection and immune disease; contribution to choroid plexus tumours, cysts, and hydrocephalic oedema; driven faults.


ENTRY 48. BORDER-ASSOCIATED MACROPHAGES (keyed to other-cells catalogue Chapter 48)

MODES AND MODE TRANSITIONS:

This is an immunologically mode-rich cell whose regimes are the surveillance-to-activation spectrum familiar in macrophages, formalising the homeostatic versus activated axis, signalled by calcium and second messengers rather than by firing.

• THE MODE REGISTER.

◦ Quiet Surveillance - the Watchful Guard - continuous low-level sampling of perivascular and cerebrospinal fluid, the homeostatic default.

◦ Pro-Inflammatory Activation - the Alarm - a pathogen-killing state releasing cytokines, reactive oxygen species, and presenting antigen.

◦ Resolving Repair - the Cleaner - a tissue-repairing, debris-clearing state that resolves inflammation.

• PER-MODE TRANSFER FUNCTIONS.

◦ Quiet Surveillance: non-spiking, resting minus 20 to minus 60 millivolts, low phagocytic and cytokine output; the baseline.

◦ Pro-Inflammatory Activation: receptor binding triggers calcium transients within seconds, cytokine production over hours, a shift toward glycolytic metabolism.

◦ Resolving Repair: sustained phagocytosis and pro-repair signalling over hours to days.

• THE TRANSITION TABLE.

◦ Pathogen and damage patterns via toll-like receptors, complement, cytokines - Quiet Surveillance to Pro-Inflammatory Activation - seconds to hours; locally driven, tunable by circulating glucocorticoids from the stress axis (thrown partly from above).

◦ Clearance of the threat - Pro-Inflammatory Activation to Resolving Repair - hours to days.

◦ Population turnover - replaceable subsets exchanged for fresh monocytes - months to years.

• FAULT REGIMES AND WATCHDOGS.

◦ Chronic activation: failure to resolve, boundary signature persistent pro-inflammatory output, watchdog glucocorticoid and resolution signalling; feeds neuroinflammation in multiple sclerosis, Alzheimer's, and small-vessel disease, impairs amyloid clearance, and serves as a human-immunodeficiency-virus reservoir.


ENTRY 49. EPIPLEXUS (KOLMER) CELL (keyed to other-cells catalogue Chapter 49)

MODES AND MODE TRANSITIONS:

This is a two-mode surveillance macrophage uniquely stationed on the open cerebrospinal-fluid surface: quiet sampling versus activated amoeboid phagocytosis.

• THE MODE REGISTER.

◦ Quiet Surveillance - the Fluid Watchman - continuous sampling of the ventricular fluid for danger, the default.

◦ Activated Amoeboid - the First Responder - a mobile, phagocytic, cytokine-releasing state on detecting debris or blood.

• PER-MODE TRANSFER FUNCTIONS.

◦ Quiet Surveillance: non-spiking, resting minus 20 to minus 60 millivolts, low output; baseline.

◦ Activated Amoeboid: calcium-triggered engulfment within minutes to hours, cytokine and reactive-oxygen-species output over hours.

• THE TRANSITION TABLE.

◦ Debris, blood breakdown products, or pathogens in the fluid - Quiet Surveillance to Activated Amoeboid - minutes to hours; locally triggered.

◦ Resolution - back to Quiet Surveillance - hours.

◦ Lifelong self-renewal - local replacement without monocyte input - continuous.

• FAULT REGIMES AND WATCHDOGS.

◦ Maladaptive response to intraventricular haemorrhage: over-activation, boundary signature sustained inflammation, watchdog resolution signalling; contributes to post-haemorrhagic hydrocephalus, and chronic activation feeds neuroinflammation in infection and neurodegeneration.


ENTRY 50. MENINGEAL LYMPHOCYTES, DENDRITIC CELLS, AND MAST CELLS (keyed to other-cells catalogue Chapter 50)

MODES AND MODE TRANSITIONS:

This is an immunologically mode-rich group whose modes are the resting-to-activated states of adaptive and granule cells, all signalled by calcium influx rather than by firing.

• THE MODE REGISTER.

◦ Resting Surveillance - the Frontier Watch - lymphocytes, dendritic cells, and mast cells sampling and patrolling the border, the default.

◦ Lymphocyte Activation - the Clonal Call - antigen-driven proliferation, cytokine output, and memory formation.

◦ Dendritic Maturation - the Messenger - a shift from sampling to antigen-presenting, carrying antigen to cervical lymph nodes.

◦ Mast-Cell Degranulation - the Flare - explosive calcium-triggered histamine and protease release.

• PER-MODE TRANSFER FUNCTIONS.

◦ Resting Surveillance: non-spiking, minus 30 to minus 60 millivolts, low output; baseline.

◦ Lymphocyte Activation: calcium influx driving cytokine genes (interferon-gamma, interleukin-17a, interleukin-4, interleukin-13) over hours, clonal expansion over days, memory over years.

◦ Dendritic Maturation: antigen capture then presentation over hours.

◦ Mast-Cell Degranulation: a sharp calcium rise emptying granules within seconds.

• THE TRANSITION TABLE.

◦ Drained antigen, cytokines, danger signals - Resting Surveillance to Lymphocyte Activation or Dendritic Maturation - hours to days; locally driven, modulated by behavioural state.

◦ Allergens, neuropeptides, complement - Resting Surveillance to Mast-Cell Degranulation - seconds.

• FAULT REGIMES AND WATCHDOGS.

◦ Dysregulation: driving meningitis and neuroinflammation, watchdog immune tolerance.

◦ Mast-cell activation implicated in migraine and barrier breakdown; altered meningeal lymphatic drainage worsening Alzheimer's; autoreactive lymphocytes in multiple sclerosis; driven faults.


ENTRY 51. SOMATOTROPE (keyed to other-cells catalogue Chapter 51)

MODES AND MODE TRANSITIONS:

This is a mode-rich excitable endocrine cell, the growth-hormone pacemaker, whose signature is a synchronised pulsatile burst mode layered on spontaneous plateau bursting.

• THE MODE REGISTER.

◦ Basal Plateau Bursting - the Background Tick - spontaneous calcium plateau bursts giving low resting hormone release.

◦ Stimulated Secretory Burst - the Growth Pulse - growth-hormone-releasing-hormone-driven population-synchronised bursting that emits sharp hormone pulses.

◦ Somatostatin Silence - the Off Switch - hyperpolarised quiet imposed by somatostatin between pulses.

• PER-MODE TRANSFER FUNCTIONS.

◦ Basal Plateau Bursting: resting minus 50 to minus 60 millivolts, tetrodotoxin-resistant calcium spikes, BK channels lengthening the plateau; low steady release.

◦ Stimulated Secretory Burst: growth-hormone-releasing hormone and ghrelin deepen and synchronise bursts, calcium rises, granules (300 to 400 nanometres) released in pulses every 2 to 3 hours, peaking in early deep sleep.

◦ Somatostatin Silence: membrane driven toward the potassium equilibrium near minus 85 millivolts, secretion off.

• THE TRANSITION TABLE.

◦ Growth-hormone-releasing hormone and ghrelin from the portal blood - Basal to Stimulated Secretory Burst - seconds to minutes; thrown from above by the hypothalamus and sleep state.

◦ Somatostatin pulse - Stimulated or Basal to Somatostatin Silence - seconds; hypothalamic, patterning the pulse train.

◦ Insulin-like growth factor 1 feedback - damping output - minutes to hours.

• FAULT REGIMES AND WATCHDOGS.

◦ Autonomous excess: somatotroph adenoma firing free of control, boundary signature loss of pulsatility, watchdog somatostatin and insulin-like-growth-factor-1 feedback; causes acromegaly and gigantism.

◦ Silence from destruction: growth-hormone-deficient short stature in children, reduced vitality and bone loss in adults; the somatopause is the slow physiological decline over decades.


ENTRY 52. LACTOTROPE (keyed to other-cells catalogue Chapter 52)

MODES AND MODE TRANSITIONS:

This is an excitable endocrine cell with inverted control logic: its default is spontaneous secretory activity held down by a dopamine brake, so its modes are brake-on versus brake-off.

• THE MODE REGISTER.

◦ Spontaneous Secretion - the Default On - intrinsic plateau-bursting calcium activity sustaining prolactin release when unopposed.

◦ Dopamine-Braked Silence - the Held Brake - dopamine opening potassium channels to hyperpolarise and silence the cell, the normal resting state.

◦ Suckling Surge - the Milk Command - a stimulated release burst evoked by suckling and thyrotropin-releasing hormone.

• PER-MODE TRANSFER FUNCTIONS.

◦ Spontaneous Secretion: resting minus 50 to minus 60 millivolts, plateau-bursting calcium spikes, self-driven release.

◦ Dopamine-Braked Silence: potassium channels opened, membrane hyperpolarised toward the potassium equilibrium, secretion off; the tonic default.

◦ Suckling Surge: dopamine withdrawal plus thyrotropin-releasing hormone and oestrogen releasing prolactin within minutes.

• THE TRANSITION TABLE.

◦ Loss of dopamine (suckling, stalk section, dopamine-blocking drugs) - Dopamine-Braked Silence to Spontaneous Secretion or Suckling Surge - minutes; thrown from above (removal of hypothalamic brake).

◦ Restored dopamine - back to Dopamine-Braked Silence - minutes.

◦ Oestrogen of pregnancy - population several-fold expansion - weeks, regressing over weeks after weaning.

• FAULT REGIMES AND WATCHDOGS.

◦ Prolactinoma: the commonest hormone-secreting pituitary tumour, boundary signature dopamine-resistant secretion, watchdog the dopamine brake; causes hyperprolactinaemia with galactorrhoea, amenorrhoea, and infertility.

◦ Deficiency: failure of lactation, as in Sheehan syndrome after obstetric haemorrhage; a driven fault.


ENTRY 53. THYROTROPE (keyed to other-cells catalogue Chapter 53)

MODES AND MODE TRANSITIONS:

This is an excitable endocrine cell acting as a feedback comparator, with a basal mode and a thyrotropin-releasing-hormone-stimulated mode, gated slowly by thyroid-hormone feedback.

• THE MODE REGISTER.

◦ Basal Comparator - the Steady Thermostat - low resting electrical activity and secretion setting baseline thyroid-stimulating-hormone output.

◦ Stimulated Secretion - the Metabolic Call - thyrotropin-releasing-hormone-driven increase in electrical activity and calcium-driven release.

• PER-MODE TRANSFER FUNCTIONS.

◦ Basal Comparator: resting minus 50 to minus 60 millivolts, occasional action potentials, steady thyroid-stimulating-hormone secretion; the default.

◦ Stimulated Secretion: thyrotropin-releasing hormone raises firing and calcium, boosting release over minutes; granules about 100 to 150 nanometres.

• THE TRANSITION TABLE.

◦ Thyrotropin-releasing hormone from the hypothalamus - Basal Comparator to Stimulated Secretion - minute-to-minute; thrown from above.

◦ Thyroxine and triiodothyronine feedback - damping both modes - hours to days; the slow negative-feedback gate.

◦ Sustained thyroid failure - hypertrophy and proliferation raising output - weeks.

• FAULT REGIMES AND WATCHDOGS.

◦ Autonomous excess: rare thyrotroph adenoma causing central hyperthyroidism, watchdog thyroid-hormone feedback.

◦ Loss or suppression: central (secondary) hypothyroidism; a driven fault. Blood thyroid-stimulating hormone remains the most sensitive clinical readout of this cell's balance.


ENTRY 54. GONADOTROPE (keyed to other-cells catalogue Chapter 54)

MODES AND MODE TRANSITIONS:

This is an excitable endocrine cell that is a frequency decoder: its output mode depends on the pulse rate of its hypothalamic command, with store-released calcium rather than plateau bursts driving secretion.

• THE MODE REGISTER.

◦ Basal Spiking - the Idle Tick - sharp brief spontaneous action potentials that on their own drive little secretion.

◦ Fast-Pulse Luteinising Mode - the Surge Setting - fast gonadotropin-releasing-hormone pulses biasing output toward luteinising hormone.

◦ Slow-Pulse Follicle-Stimulating Mode - the Maturation Setting - slow pulses biasing output toward follicle-stimulating hormone.

• PER-MODE TRANSFER FUNCTIONS.

◦ Basal Spiking: resting minus 50 to minus 60 millivolts, brief spikes, minimal release.

◦ Fast-Pulse Luteinising Mode: gonadotropin-releasing hormone releases internal-store calcium in large oscillations (seconds), favouring luteinising hormone when pulses are fast.

◦ Slow-Pulse Follicle-Stimulating Mode: the same calcium mechanism favouring follicle-stimulating hormone when pulses are slow; granules about 200 to 250 nanometres.

• THE TRANSITION TABLE.

◦ Gonadotropin-releasing-hormone pulse frequency (roughly every 60 to 120 minutes) - shifting between Fast-Pulse and Slow-Pulse modes - per pulse; thrown from above by the hypothalamic pulse generator.

◦ Puberty - awakening from childhood quiescence - developmental; menopause - disinhibition raising output.

◦ The ovulatory surge - a high-output episode - hours.

• FAULT REGIMES AND WATCHDOGS.

◦ Continuous (non-pulsatile) gonadotropin-releasing hormone: paradoxically shuts reproduction down, boundary signature loss of the pulse code, used medically as chemical castration; watchdog the pulse generator.

◦ Gonadotroph adenoma (usually clinically silent, presenting by mass effect) and gonadotrope failure causing hypogonadotropic hypogonadism; driven faults.


ENTRY 55. CORTICOTROPE (keyed to other-cells catalogue Chapter 55)

MODES AND MODE TRANSITIONS:

This is a mode-rich excitable stress-axis cell whose defining transition is a well-documented shift from basal single spiking to secretagogue-evoked bursting, tuned in real time by cortisol feedback.

• THE MODE REGISTER.

◦ Basal Single Spiking - the Background Tick - predominantly single spikes under resting conditions giving low adrenocorticotropic-hormone output.

◦ Stress Bursting - the Stress Trigger - corticotropin-releasing-hormone and vasopressin-driven complex bursting that efficiently drives hormone release.

◦ Cortisol-Braked Damping - the Feedback Brake - glucocorticoid suppression of spontaneous activity and of the spike-to-burst transition.

• PER-MODE TRANSFER FUNCTIONS.

◦ Basal Single Spiking: resting minus 50 to minus 60 millivolts, single spikes, low secretion; the default (well characterised).

◦ Stress Bursting: corticotropin-releasing hormone triggers BK-channel-dependent bursting (vasopressin adds BK-independent high-frequency spiking), bursting more efficient at secretion than spiking; granules 200 to 400 nanometres; adrenocorticotropic hormone and beta-endorphin released.

◦ Cortisol-Braked Damping: pre-exposure to glucocorticoid reduces spontaneous activity and prevents the spike-to-burst transition.

• THE TRANSITION TABLE.

◦ Corticotropin-releasing hormone and vasopressin from the hypothalamus - Basal Single Spiking to Stress Bursting - seconds; thrown from above by the stress axis, with a daily rhythm peaking near waking.

◦ Rising cortisol - Stress Bursting toward Cortisol-Braked Damping - minutes; the fast feedback brake.

◦ Chronic stress or loss of feedback - hypertrophy and proliferation - weeks.

• FAULT REGIMES AND WATCHDOGS.

◦ Autonomous excess: corticotroph adenoma causing Cushing disease, boundary signature cortisol-resistant bursting, watchdog glucocorticoid feedback; after adrenal removal can become the aggressive Nelson syndrome.

◦ Suppression: chronic steroid treatment silencing the cells, making abrupt withdrawal dangerous, and causing secondary adrenal insufficiency.


ENTRY 56. MELANOTROPE OF THE INTERMEDIATE LOBE (keyed to other-cells catalogue Chapter 56)

MODES AND MODE TRANSITIONS:

This is a mode-poor, largely vestigial excitable cell in the adult human: essentially a spontaneous-firing state held down by a dopamine brake, with its richer regulation lost along with the human intermediate lobe.

• THE MODE REGISTER.

◦ Spontaneous Firing - the Twilight Tick - intrinsic spontaneous action potentials driving melanocyte-stimulating-hormone release when unopposed.

◦ Dopamine-Braked Silence - the Held Brake - direct dopaminergic innervation silencing electrical activity and release, the normal state.

• PER-MODE TRANSFER FUNCTIONS.

◦ Spontaneous Firing: resting minus 50 to minus 60 millivolts, spontaneous spikes, peptide release; active in the fetus and in animals with a working lobe.

◦ Dopamine-Braked Silence: dopamine hyperpolarising and quieting the cell; the adult human default (low confidence given the vestigial lobe).

• THE TRANSITION TABLE.

◦ Dopamine withdrawal - Dopamine-Braked Silence to Spontaneous Firing - seconds; thrown from above by direct innervation.

◦ Background colour and light (in species with an active lobe) - slow modulation of output - seasonal; largely absent in adult humans.

• FAULT REGIMES AND WATCHDOGS.

◦ Little distinct human disease given the vestigial lobe; intermediate-lobe corticotroph tumours occur, and dysregulated pro-opiomelanocortin products can disturb skin pigmentation. Honestly, the mode register here is minimal and low-confidence because the human lobe is rudimentary.


ENTRY 57. FOLLICULOSTELLATE CELL (keyed to other-cells catalogue Chapter 57)

MODES AND MODE TRANSITIONS:

This is a non-spiking glia-like network cell whose modes are those of an astrocyte network: a resting coupled state and a propagating calcium-wave state, cross-referenced to the reactive-astrocyte register of the astrocyte chapters.

• THE MODE REGISTER.

◦ Resting Coupling - the Quiet Network - the gap-junction-coupled network at rest, supporting endocrine neighbours.

◦ Calcium-Wave Relay - the Gland-Wide Wave - a stimulus launching a calcium wave that travels across the whole gland through gap junctions.

◦ Paracrine and Immune Signalling - the Messenger - secretion of interleukin-6, vascular endothelial growth factor, and nitric oxide, rising on immune challenge.

• PER-MODE TRANSFER FUNCTIONS.

◦ Resting Coupling: non-spiking, coupled-glial resting potential, connexin-43 coupling; the default.

◦ Calcium-Wave Relay: a calcium wave propagating at about 17.7 micrometres per second across the gland over seconds, triggered by acetylcholine or interleukin-6.

◦ Paracrine and Immune Signalling: paracrine-factor output over minutes to hours, rising with inflammation.

• THE TRANSITION TABLE.

◦ Acetylcholine, interleukin-6, or local stimulus - Resting Coupling to Calcium-Wave Relay - seconds; locally triggered.

◦ Inflammatory signals - toward heightened Paracrine and Immune Signalling - minutes to hours; a reactive shift analogous to the reactive-astrocyte "state, not a type" (cross-reference the astrocyte chapters).

• FAULT REGIMES AND WATCHDOGS.

◦ Network dysfunction: disturbed paracrine coordination and immune signalling of the gland, watchdog normal gap-junction coupling.

◦ Rare tumour once called folliculostellate-cell tumour, now placed among pituicytomas and spindle-cell oncocytomas; a driven fault.


ENTRY 58. PINEALOCYTE (Melatonin Cell of the Pineal Gland) (keyed to other-cells catalogue Chapter 58)

MODES AND MODE TRANSITIONS:

This is a two-mode circadian switch cell whose modes are secretory rather than electrical, thrown from above by the master clock rather than chosen locally.

Its whole output is a once-daily swing between a silent day and a synthesising night, so its register is short and proud.

• THE MODE REGISTER.

◦ Daytime Baseline - the Daylight Hush - melatonin synthesis switched off while it is light, so the blood carries the chemical meaning of day.

◦ Nighttime Synthesis - the Hormone of Darkness - noradrenaline-driven melatonin manufacture through the biological night, so every organ reads that it is dark.

• PER-MODE TRANSFER FUNCTIONS.

◦ In the Daylight Hush the noradrenergic input is low, the rate-limiting enzyme aralkylamine N-acetyltransferase (the acetylating enzyme, abbreviated AANAT) is near baseline, and melatonin output is minimal.

◦ In the Hormone of Darkness noradrenaline acting on alpha-1 and beta-1 adrenergic receptors raises cyclic adenosine monophosphate (the second messenger, abbreviated cyclic AMP) and calcium, AANAT activity climbs, and melatonin output rises up to roughly a hundredfold, with the peak height and duration tracking night length.

• THE TRANSITION TABLE.

◦ Dusk-to-night: the suprachiasmatic master clock releases its daytime inhibition and sympathetic noradrenaline is delivered, switching Daylight Hush to Hormone of Darkness over tens of minutes - this transition is thrown from above by a Layer 10/11 circadian broadcast.

◦ Dawn or light-at-night: retinal light rapidly cuts the noradrenergic drive and AANAT is degraded within minutes, collapsing the Hormone of Darkness back to Daylight Hush - an acute override that the healthy system uses normally at dawn.

◦ Seasonal: shortening or lengthening nights stretch or compress the night mode over months, a slow re-timing rather than a switch.

• FAULT REGIMES AND WATCHDOGS.

◦ Chronic light at night and shift work pin the cell in Daylight Hush when it should be synthesising, flattening the rhythm - the warning sign is a lost or phase-shifted nocturnal melatonin peak.

◦ Pineal calcification, cysts, and tumours (pineocytoma, pineoblastoma) and the age-related decline of the peak are structural failures rather than mode errors; there is no biological watchdog beyond the clock itself.


ENTRY 59. PINEAL INTERSTITIAL (GLIAL) CELL (keyed to other-cells catalogue Chapter 59)

MODES AND MODE TRANSITIONS:

This is a mode-poor support cell, an astrocyte posted to the pineal, whose regime axis is the same resting-versus-reactive switch shared by astrocytes across the brain.

Its modes are activation states, not firing patterns, and mirror those formalised for the reactive astrocyte at other-cells catalogue Chapter 12.

• THE MODE REGISTER.

◦ Resting Housekeeping - the Clock-Tower Keeper - quiet ionic and metabolic buffering of the melatonin cells under normal conditions.

◦ Reactive State - the Emergency Mason (cross-referenced to Chapter 12) - injury-induced reactive gliosis around damage, tumour, or cyst.

• PER-MODE TRANSFER FUNCTIONS.

◦ In the Clock-Tower Keeper it holds a strongly negative resting potential near minus 80 millivolts (a millivolt being a thousandth of a volt) set by high potassium permeability, buffers potassium and pH, and signals only through slow calcium waves.

◦ In the Emergency Mason it hypertrophies, upregulates glial fibrillary acidic protein (the astrocyte marker protein), and contributes to the gritty calcified brain-sand deposits of the ageing gland.

• THE TRANSITION TABLE.

◦ Injury, tumour, or cyst: local damage signals switch Clock-Tower Keeper to Emergency Mason over hours to days - a locally triggered transition, not one thrown from above.

◦ Ageing: slow accumulation of reactive change and calcification over years, a gradual drift rather than a clean switch.

• FAULT REGIMES AND WATCHDOGS.

◦ Persistent reactive gliosis is itself the fault regime, walling off tissue and seeding calcification; the warning sign is expanding brain sand and glial scarring on imaging, with no fast biological watchdog beyond the general injury response.


ENTRY 60. THYROID FOLLICULAR CELL (Thyrocyte) (keyed to other-cells catalogue Chapter 60)

MODES AND MODE TRANSITIONS:

This is a slow set-point cell, a two-posture secretory switch working over hours to weeks rather than milliseconds, driven by a hormone rather than by voltage.

Treat it as one cell in two postures, flat-and-storing versus tall-and-resorbing, tuned by thyroid-stimulating hormone.

• THE MODE REGISTER.

◦ Quiescent Storage - the Colloid Larder - flat squamous posture banking iodinated thyroglobulin in the colloid lake when demand is low.

◦ Stimulated Resorption - the Thermostat Turned Up - tall columnar posture swallowing colloid back and releasing thyroid hormones when driven.

• PER-MODE TRANSFER FUNCTIONS.

◦ In the Colloid Larder thyroid-stimulating hormone (the pituitary signal, abbreviated TSH) is low, cyclic AMP is near baseline, the cell is flat, and it stockpiles hormone that can supply the body for weeks.

◦ In the Thermostat Turned Up TSH binds its receptor, cyclic AMP rises, the cell grows columnar, and it exports thyroxine (the four-iodine hormone, abbreviated T4) at about 80 to 100 micrograms per day plus a smaller amount of triiodothyronine (the three-iodine hormone, abbreviated T3); the cell rests near minus 50 millivolts throughout and never fires.

• THE TRANSITION TABLE.

◦ Rising TSH: the pituitary raises TSH when circulating hormone falls, switching Colloid Larder to Thermostat Turned Up over hours - set by the hypothalamic-pituitary-thyroid axis above the cell.

◦ Falling TSH or hormone excess: negative feedback suppresses TSH and returns the cell to storage over hours to days.

◦ Chronic iodine lack or sustained TSH: drives lasting hypertrophy and multiplication (goitre) over weeks to months.

• FAULT REGIMES AND WATCHDOGS.

◦ Graves disease is a driven-mode fault in which a TSH-mimicking antibody locks the cell in Thermostat Turned Up regardless of feedback - the warning sign is a suppressed TSH with high free T4.

◦ Hashimoto autoimmune destruction and congenital hypothyroidism are loss regimes; the negative-feedback loop is the natural watchdog, and newborn screening is the clinical one.


ENTRY 61. PARAFOLLICULAR (C) CELL (keyed to other-cells catalogue Chapter 61)

MODES AND MODE TRANSITIONS:

This is a two-mode standby responder, a genuinely excitable neuroendocrine cell that is largely idle and fires only when blood calcium runs high.

Its modes are electrical-secretory and read a single variable, extracellular calcium.

• THE MODE REGISTER.

◦ Standby Idle - the Coast-Guard at Anchor - quiet baseline when calcium is normal.

◦ Calcium-High Secretion - the Coast-Guard Scramble - calcium-triggered calcitonin release when blood calcium climbs.

• PER-MODE TRANSFER FUNCTIONS.

◦ In the Coast-Guard at Anchor it rests near minus 50 to minus 60 millivolts, the calcium-sensing receptor is under-occupied, and calcitonin output is low, under about 10 picograms per millilitre.

◦ In the Coast-Guard Scramble a rise in extracellular calcium activates the calcium-sensing receptor, drives calcium-based action potentials, and triggers release of calcitonin, a 32-amino-acid peptide that restrains bone-dissolving osteoclasts.

• THE TRANSITION TABLE.

◦ Calcium rise: a climb in ionized calcium above about 1.3 millimoles per litre, or a gastrin signal, switches Anchor to Scramble within minutes - a locally sensed transition, not thrown from above.

◦ Calcium fall: return toward normal quiets the cell within minutes, calcitonin having a plasma half-life of about 10 minutes.

• FAULT REGIMES AND WATCHDOGS.

◦ Because adult calcium control is dominated by the parathyroid and vitamin D systems, loss of this cell produces no measurable fault - a mode-poor cell the body can largely do without.

◦ The pathological regime is proliferative, not electrical: C-cell hyperplasia and medullary thyroid carcinoma, watched for by measuring blood calcitonin as a tumour marker.


ENTRY 62. PARATHYROID CHIEF CELL (Principal Cell) (keyed to other-cells catalogue Chapter 62)

MODES AND MODE TRANSITIONS:

This is a fast inverse-control cell, a two-pole secretory switch that runs backwards - it is switched off by the very ion it defends - and holds one of the tightest homeostatic loops in the body.

Its modes are graded secretory states set continuously by blood calcium.

• THE MODE REGISTER.

◦ Low-Calcium Alarm - the Calcium Rescue - brisk parathyroid hormone release when calcium falls.

◦ High-Calcium Silence - the Calcium Stand-Down - suppressed release, and active destruction of stored hormone, when calcium is high.

• PER-MODE TRANSFER FUNCTIONS.

◦ In the Calcium Rescue falling calcium under-occupies the calcium-sensing receptor, and pre-made parathyroid hormone (the 84-amino-acid peptide, abbreviated PTH) is released within seconds, pulling calcium from bone, reclaiming it in the kidney, and activating vitamin D; plasma PTH rises steeply above about 65 picograms per millilitre.

◦ In the Calcium Stand-Down a rise in calcium binds the calcium-sensing receptor and suppresses release, and the cell degrades much of its own PTH intracellularly; the cell is only weakly excitable, resting near minus 50 to minus 70 millivolts.

• THE TRANSITION TABLE.

◦ Calcium fall below the set point (half-maximal secretion near ionized calcium 1.1 to 1.2 millimoles per litre): switches Stand-Down to Rescue within seconds - a locally sensed transition, faster than gene transcription.

◦ Calcium rise: returns Rescue to Stand-Down within seconds to minutes, aided by the short 2-to-4-minute plasma half-life of PTH.

◦ Chronic low calcium or kidney failure: drives cell multiplication and gland enlargement over weeks to months.

• FAULT REGIMES AND WATCHDOGS.

◦ A mutated single cell that escapes calcium control locks into the Calcium Rescue mode regardless of calcium, producing the adenoma of primary hyperparathyroidism - the warning sign is high calcium with an inappropriately high PTH.

◦ Surgical loss of all four glands drops the cell out of service entirely, causing dangerous tetany; the calcium-sensing receptor feedback loop is the natural watchdog.


ENTRY 63. PARATHYROID OXYPHIL CELL (keyed to other-cells catalogue Chapter 63)

MODES AND MODE TRANSITIONS:

This is a genuinely mode-poor cell, and the honest minimal register is a single dormant state, since after a century of study its function remains uncertain.

Its one possible second mode is a low-confidence recruited-secretion regime seen chiefly in chronic kidney disease.

• THE MODE REGISTER.

◦ Idle Dormancy - the Cold Furnace - the everyday state, mitochondria-packed yet apparently secreting little or nothing.

◦ Recruited Secretion - the Furnace Lit (low confidence) - a proposed parathyroid-hormone-secreting state unmasked under chronic drive, especially kidney failure.

• PER-MODE TRANSFER FUNCTIONS.

◦ In the Cold Furnace it rests near minus 50 to minus 70 millivolts, carries the calcium-sensing receptor, and produces little detectable hormone.

◦ In the Furnace Lit it may, on the evidence of recent studies, secrete parathyroid hormone and parathyroid-hormone-related peptide - a claim held with low confidence.

• THE TRANSITION TABLE.

◦ Ageing: the cells appear around puberty and accumulate over years to decades - a slow developmental drift, not a fast switch.

◦ Chronic kidney disease and sustained gland drive: associated with oxyphil-rich glands over months to years, the presumed trigger for any Furnace Lit state.

• FAULT REGIMES AND WATCHDOGS.

◦ No everyday fault regime is known, since removing these cells in a healthy adult changes nothing measurable.

◦ The recognised abnormal regime is proliferative: oxyphil (oncocytic) adenomas and rare carcinoma, watched for by the pathologist rather than by any physiological loop.


ENTRY 64. ZONA GLOMERULOSA CELL (keyed to other-cells catalogue Chapter 64)

MODES AND MODE TRANSITIONS:

This is an electrically tuned steroid factory, unusual among hormone cells in that it computes with slow membrane oscillations, giving it a graded two-mode secretory register.

Its modes are set by blood potassium and angiotensin, not by the pituitary.

• THE MODE REGISTER.

◦ Basal Output - the Salt Idle - low aldosterone production under a replete, normal-potassium state.

◦ Stimulated Output - the Salt Alarm - raised aldosterone when potassium climbs or the renin-angiotensin system calls.

• PER-MODE TRANSFER FUNCTIONS.

◦ In the Salt Idle it sits near minus 80 millivolts, potassium channels dominate, calcium entry is low, and aldosterone output is at the low end of roughly 100 to 400 picomoles per litre.

◦ In the Salt Alarm a rise in blood potassium or angiotensin II depolarises the cell, opens voltage-gated calcium channels in slow oscillations, and drives aldosterone synthase (the final enzyme it alone carries) to raise aldosterone.

• THE TRANSITION TABLE.

◦ Potassium rise or angiotensin II: depolarises the cell and switches Salt Idle to Salt Alarm over minutes - sensed locally from the blood rather than thrown from above.

◦ Volume repletion and falling angiotensin: return the cell to idle over minutes to hours.

◦ Slow conveyor: cells born near the capsule convert into fasciculata cells as they move inward over days, a developmental drift out of the glomerulosa register entirely.

• FAULT REGIMES AND WATCHDOGS.

◦ An autonomous adenoma or aldosterone-producing cell cluster locks the cell in the Salt Alarm mode independent of potassium, producing primary aldosteronism (Conn syndrome) - the warning sign is high aldosterone with suppressed renin.

◦ Loss with the rest of the cortex in Addison disease removes the mode altogether; the renin-angiotensin feedback loop is the natural watchdog.


ENTRY 65. ZONA FASCICULATA CELL (keyed to other-cells catalogue Chapter 65)

MODES AND MODE TRANSITIONS:

This is the mode-rich final effector of the stress axis, a set-point cell whose two secretory modes ride a strong daily rhythm and a fast stress surge.

Its modes are hormonal and thrown from above by the brain's clock and stress circuits.

• THE MODE REGISTER.

◦ Circadian Basal - the Morning Rise - the daily cortisol rhythm peaking before waking and ebbing at night.

◦ Stress Surge - the Alarm Steroid - the acute climb in cortisol on any physical or psychological stressor.

• PER-MODE TRANSFER FUNCTIONS.

◦ In the Morning Rise pulsatile adrenocorticotropic hormone (the pituitary signal, abbreviated ACTH) drives cyclic AMP and steroid enzymes, giving a plasma cortisol high in the early morning (about 140 to 550 nanomoles per litre) and low at night; the cell rests near minus 60 to minus 80 millivolts and never spikes.

◦ In the Alarm Steroid a burst of ACTH mobilises cholesterol via the steroidogenic acute regulatory protein and lifts cortisol within minutes, output reaching the upper end of roughly 10 to 20 milligrams per day.

• THE TRANSITION TABLE.

◦ Dawn clock signal: the brain's circadian and stress circuits raise ACTH to switch the cell into the Morning Rise - thrown from above by a Layer 10/11 broadcast.

◦ Stressor: hypothalamic-pituitary drive throws the Alarm Steroid within minutes and feedback returns it over the 60-to-90-minute cortisol half-life.

◦ Loss of ACTH drive: the zone atrophies over days to weeks, so abrupt withdrawal of long-term steroid drugs leaves it unable to respond.

• FAULT REGIMES AND WATCHDOGS.

◦ An ACTH-secreting tumour or autonomous adenoma pins the cell in a high-output mode, producing Cushing syndrome - the warning sign is loss of the daily rhythm with a high late-night cortisol.

◦ Adrenal crisis on sudden steroid withdrawal is the collapse regime; negative feedback to the pituitary is the natural watchdog.


ENTRY 66. ZONA RETICULARIS CELL (keyed to other-cells catalogue Chapter 66)

MODES AND MODE TRANSITIONS:

This is essentially a one-mode secretory cell whose true variable is not a fast switch but a decades-long developmental clock.

The honest register is a single adrenocorticotropic hormone (ACTH)-driven androgen-output mode set within a slow life-stage envelope.

• THE MODE REGISTER.

◦ Androgen Output - the Slow Clock - steady, ACTH-tuned production of the androgen precursors that other tissues finish.

• PER-MODE TRANSFER FUNCTIONS.

◦ In the Slow Clock ACTH raises cyclic AMP, and high cytochrome b5 with the enzyme CYP17A1 favours the androgen branch, releasing dehydroepiandrosterone (the precursor androgen, abbreviated DHEA), its sulphate, and androstenedione; DHEA-sulphate circulates at higher concentration than any other steroid; the cell rests near minus 60 to minus 80 millivolts and does not spike.

• THE TRANSITION TABLE.

◦ Adrenarche: developmental signals awaken the zone at around age 6 to 8, switching it from near-silent to full output over months to years - a slow programme, not a fast switch.

◦ ACTH pulses: tune the amplitude of the Slow Clock within minutes, without changing its identity.

◦ Adrenopause: output fades steadily from the twenties onward across decades.

• FAULT REGIMES AND WATCHDOGS.

◦ In congenital adrenal hyperplasia a blocked cortisol path diverts precursors down this androgen branch, driving androgen excess - the boundary sign is virilisation with abnormal steroid ratios.

◦ Premature adrenarche and androgen-secreting tumours are the other abnormal regimes; loss in Addison disease removes the mode; there is no fast feedback watchdog on this slow output.


ENTRY 67. ADRENAL CHROMAFFIN CELL (keyed to other-cells catalogue Chapter 67)

MODES AND MODE TRANSITIONS:

This is a mode-rich excitable secretory cell, a sympathetic neuron that kept its incoming synapse but threw away its axon; its full treatment lives at neuron catalogue Chapter 261 and this register is condensed.

Its modes are firing-and-release states plus a release-mode switch between economical and all-out granule discharge.

• THE MODE REGISTER.

◦ Basal Tone - the Quiet Sentry - low resting catecholamine output at rest.

◦ Alarm Discharge - the Wireless Alarm - the fight-or-flight surge of adrenaline and noradrenaline.

◦ Kiss-and-Run versus Full Fusion - the Sip or the Flood - a release-mode setting that tunes how much each granule dumps according to drive.

• PER-MODE TRANSFER FUNCTIONS.

◦ In the Quiet Sentry it rests near minus 50 to minus 60 millivolts, fires sparsely, and plasma adrenaline stays low, in the tens of picograms per millilitre.

◦ In the Wireless Alarm splanchnic cholinergic input drives calcium-dependent action potentials, catecholamine release rises many-fold within seconds, and output runs about 80 percent adrenaline to 20 percent noradrenaline.

◦ In the Sip or the Flood low firing favours brief kiss-and-run partial release, while high firing favours full granule collapse, seen directly as quantal current spikes by amperometry.

• THE TRANSITION TABLE.

◦ Sympathetic activation: acetylcholine from splanchnic preganglionic fibres switches Quiet Sentry to Wireless Alarm within milliseconds to seconds - thrown from above by the brainstem stress axis.

◦ Firing rate: rising drive shifts the Sip toward the Flood as calcium entry grows.

◦ Recovery: circulating catecholamine half-life of only 1 to 3 minutes returns the cell to basal tone quickly.

• FAULT REGIMES AND WATCHDOGS.

◦ Phaeochromocytoma (a catecholamine-secreting tumour) locks the cell into runaway discharge, causing dangerous blood-pressure surges - the warning sign is paroxysmal hypertension with high plasma metanephrines.

◦ In children the related tumour is neuroblastoma; full fault treatment is at neuron catalogue Chapter 261.


ENTRY 68. ADRENAL MEDULLARY SUSTENTACULAR CELL (keyed to other-cells catalogue Chapter 68)

MODES AND MODE TRANSITIONS:

This is a mode-poor glial support cell whose regime axis is support-versus-regeneration rather than firing.

Its two modes mirror the resting-versus-recruited pattern of glia and stem cells across this catalogue.

• THE MODE REGISTER.

◦ Support Rest - the Glial Nursemaid - quiet buffering and cradling of the chromaffin cells.

◦ Stem Recruitment - the Waking Reserve - proliferation and differentiation into new chromaffin cells on demand.

• PER-MODE TRANSFER FUNCTIONS.

◦ In the Glial Nursemaid it is non-excitable, resting around minus 70 to minus 80 millivolts, and provides ionic and trophic support without secreting hormone.

◦ In the Waking Reserve it reawakens its neural-crest potential, divides, and generates daughter chromaffin cells, a source of medullary renewal.

• THE TRANSITION TABLE.

◦ Growth or stress signals and cell loss: switch Glial Nursemaid to Waking Reserve over days - a locally triggered regenerative response.

◦ Restored population: returns the cell to quiet support.

• FAULT REGIMES AND WATCHDOGS.

◦ It has no primary disease of its own; its main abnormal significance is as a diagnostic sustentacular rim around cell nests in phaeochromocytoma and paraganglioma.

◦ There is no notable driven fault regime, which is the honest statement for this quiet reserve cell.


ENTRY 69. BETA CELL (Insulin-Secreting B Cell of the Pancreatic Islet) (keyed to other-cells catalogue Chapter 69)

MODES AND MODE TRANSITIONS:

This is one of the most mode-rich cells in the body, a metabolic-to-electrical analog converter that behaves like a neuron, and it deserves the full treatment.

Its modes are true electrical regimes tuned continuously by blood glucose, and the whole islet burst as one gap-junction-coupled oscillator, so glucose is encoded not as on-off but as burst duty cycle.

• THE MODE REGISTER.

◦ Sub-threshold Silence - the Fasting Silence - electrically quiet at low glucose, with insulin release at basal only.

◦ Glucose-Tuned Bursting - the Feeding Pulse - rhythmic calcium bursts whose duty cycle scales with glucose, driving pulsatile insulin.

◦ Continuous Spiking - the Full Feast - tonic firing at very high glucose when bursts fuse into steady activity.

◦ Pacemaker Hub versus Follower - the Conductor and the Chorus - a division of labour in which a minority of hub cells entrain the bursting of the islet.

• PER-MODE TRANSFER FUNCTIONS.

◦ In the Fasting Silence the ATP-sensitive potassium channel (the metabolism-gated channel, abbreviated K-ATP) is open and holds the membrane near minus 70 millivolts, so no calcium action potentials fire and insulin output is low.

◦ In the Feeding Pulse glucose above about 7 millimoles per litre raises the ratio of adenosine triphosphate to adenosine diphosphate (the cell's energy-charge ratio), closes K-ATP channels, depolarises to a threshold near minus 50 millivolts, and produces plateaux of several seconds carrying calcium spikes separated by silent intervals, the whole pattern oscillating with a period of tens of seconds to a few minutes; rising glucose lengthens the active fraction (the plateau fraction) so more insulin is released per cycle.

◦ In the Full Feast, at very high glucose, the plateau fraction approaches one and bursting becomes continuous spiking with maximal calcium-driven release.

◦ In the Conductor and the Chorus, connexin-36 gap junctions synchronise neighbours and hub cells pace the collective rhythm, so the islet emits coherent glucose-scaled pulses into the portal blood.

• THE TRANSITION TABLE.

◦ Glucose step up: K-ATP closure within seconds drives Fasting Silence to Feeding Pulse, with first-phase insulin within 1 to 2 minutes - a locally sensed metabolic transition.

◦ Glucose rising further: increasing plateau fraction slides Feeding Pulse toward the Full Feast over seconds.

◦ Incretin hormones and autonomic input: glucagon-like peptide-1 (a gut incretin, abbreviated GLP-1) and vagal acetylcholine raise the duty cycle and lower the effective threshold, while sympathetic noradrenaline and delta-cell somatostatin suppress it - a partial mode-setting thrown from above by the autonomic system.

◦ Glucose fall: returns the cell to Fasting Silence within seconds; beta-cell mass itself adapts over days to months with demand.

• FAULT REGIMES AND WATCHDOGS.

◦ Chronic hyperglycaemia (glucotoxicity) can drive the cell into a depolarised, calcium-overloaded, dedifferentiated regime with loss of first-phase and pulsatile secretion - a boundary this cell does not normally use, seen in progressing type 2 diabetes.

◦ Autoimmune destruction abolishes the whole register in type 1 diabetes; K-ATP and glucokinase mutations shift the glucose threshold up or down, causing neonatal diabetes or congenital hyperinsulinism - the natural watchdog is the tight metabolism-to-calcium coupling that keeps signal locked to fuel.


ENTRY 70. ALPHA CELL (Glucagon-Secreting A Cell of the Pancreatic Islet) (keyed to other-cells catalogue Chapter 70)

MODES AND MODE TRANSITIONS:

This is an excitable secretory cell with inverted glucose logic, the beta cell's mirror image, most active when fuel is low.

Its two electrical modes read the same glucose signal as the beta cell but with opposite sign.

• THE MODE REGISTER.

◦ Low-Glucose Firing - the Fasting Alarm - vigorous action potentials and glucagon release when glucose is low.

◦ High-Glucose Suppression - the Fed Hush - firing shut down when glucose is high.

• PER-MODE TRANSFER FUNCTIONS.

◦ In the Fasting Alarm a small K-ATP conductance holds the membrane in a window near minus 60 to minus 70 millivolts where voltage-gated sodium and calcium channels can fire, driving glucagon release that tells the liver to raise blood sugar.

◦ In the Fed Hush rising glucose depolarises the cell too far, inactivates its sodium and calcium channels, and suppresses secretion above roughly 7 millimoles per litre.

• THE TRANSITION TABLE.

◦ Glucose fall: switches Fed Hush to Fasting Alarm within minutes - a locally sensed transition amplified during hypoglycaemia, fasting, and exercise.

◦ Adrenaline and sympathetic drive: strongly reinforce the Fasting Alarm during stress - thrown from above by the sympathetic system.

◦ Paracrine brakes: insulin, zinc, gamma-aminobutyric acid (an inhibitory messenger, abbreviated GABA), and somatostatin from neighbours push the cell toward the Fed Hush within seconds.

• FAULT REGIMES AND WATCHDOGS.

◦ In type 1 and advanced type 2 diabetes the Fasting Alarm fails to fire on cue, producing dangerous hypoglycaemia unawareness - the warning sign is an absent glucagon rise during low glucose.

◦ Inappropriate high-glucose firing (hyperglucagonaemia) worsens fasting hyperglycaemia; a glucagonoma is the tumour regime; the islet's paracrine crosstalk is the natural watchdog.


ENTRY 71. DELTA CELL (Somatostatin-Secreting D Cell of the Pancreatic Islet) (keyed to other-cells catalogue Chapter 71)

MODES AND MODE TRANSITIONS:

This is a two-mode paracrine referee, an excitable cell that, like the beta cell, is switched on by glucose, so it provides built-in negative feedback.

Its wide reach comes from motile filopodia rather than any change in firing pattern.

• THE MODE REGISTER.

◦ Basal Quiet - the Referee at Rest - low somatostatin tone at low glucose.

◦ Glucose-Stimulated Braking - the Whistle Blown - calcium-driven somatostatin release that damps both insulin and glucagon.

• PER-MODE TRANSFER FUNCTIONS.

◦ In the Referee at Rest it sits near minus 60 to minus 70 millivolts with low somatostatin output.
