◦ In the Whistle Blown rising glucose, incretins, and beta-cell urocortin-3 trigger calcium action potentials and somatostatin (the SST-14 form) release, delivered through filopodial processes onto neighbouring alpha and beta cells.

• THE TRANSITION TABLE.

◦ Glucose rise: switches Referee at Rest to Whistle Blown within minutes, in parallel with insulin - a locally sensed feedback transition.

◦ Ghrelin and beta-cell signals: tune the strength of braking over seconds to minutes.

◦ Glucose fall: quiets the cell within minutes.

• FAULT REGIMES AND WATCHDOGS.

◦ A somatostatinoma locks the cell into excess braking, causing diabetes, gallstones, and diarrhoea - the abnormal regime rather than one the cell normally uses.

◦ Weakened delta-cell tone leaves insulin and glucagon under-restrained and volatile; the glucose-driven feedback loop is itself the watchdog.


ENTRY 72. GAMMA (PP) CELL (Pancreatic-Polypeptide-Secreting F Cell) (keyed to other-cells catalogue Chapter 72)

MODES AND MODE TRANSITIONS:

This is a two-mode secretory cell whose defining feature is an almost hard-wired dependence on the vagus nerve.

Its modes are basal versus postprandial release, set largely by parasympathetic drive.

• THE MODE REGISTER.

◦ Basal Quiet - the Idle Accountant - low pancreatic-polypeptide output between meals.

◦ Postprandial Release - the After-Dinner Report - the fed-state rise that signals satiety and dampens exocrine secretion.

• PER-MODE TRANSFER FUNCTIONS.

◦ In the Idle Accountant it rests near minus 60 to minus 70 millivolts with low output.

◦ In the After-Dinner Report vagal acetylcholine and a protein meal drive calcium-dependent release of pancreatic polypeptide (a neuropeptide-Y-family hormone), peaking within about 1 to 2 hours of eating and lasting hours.

• THE TRANSITION TABLE.

◦ Vagal activation after a meal: switches Idle Accountant to After-Dinner Report within minutes - thrown from above by the parasympathetic system.

◦ Low blood glucose: also raises output.

◦ Meal clearance or vagotomy: returns the cell to idle over hours, or abolishes the response entirely.

• FAULT REGIMES AND WATCHDOGS.

◦ A PPoma is the rare tumour regime; loss of the postprandial rise serves as a clinical marker of autonomic neuropathy in long-standing diabetes rather than a disease of its own.

◦ There is no notable driven electrical fault; the vagal loop is the natural watchdog.


ENTRY 73. EPSILON CELL (Ghrelin-Secreting Islet Cell) (keyed to other-cells catalogue Chapter 73)

MODES AND MODE TRANSITIONS:

This is a mode-poor cell whose electrophysiology is barely characterised, so the honest minimal register is a single tonic secretory mode held with low confidence.

Its clearest real variable is developmental abundance rather than any fast electrical switch.

• THE MODE REGISTER.

◦ Tonic Ghrelin Output - the Small Voice of Appetite (low confidence) - presumed steady, fasting-tuned ghrelin release that restrains insulin.

• PER-MODE TRANSFER FUNCTIONS.

◦ In the Small Voice of Appetite it is presumed excitable, resting near minus 60 to minus 70 millivolts with calcium-dependent release of acyl-ghrelin (the active hunger hormone) onto the growth-hormone-secretagogue receptor; all figures are low confidence because the cells are so rare.

• THE TRANSITION TABLE.

◦ Fasting and nutrient signals: tune ghrelin output over minutes - a locally sensed modulation.

◦ Development: abundance is high before birth and dwindles after, a slow change over months to years.

• FAULT REGIMES AND WATCHDOGS.

◦ No well-defined isolated disease exists, given the cell's scarcity; rare ghrelinomas are the tumour regime.

◦ This is honestly a cell too rare to have a documented fault register, and its numbers are its main variable.


ENTRY 74. LEYDIG CELL (Interstitial Cell of the Testis) (keyed to other-cells catalogue Chapter 74)

MODES AND MODE TRANSITIONS:

This is a non-excitable steroid factory with a simple two-mode register, basal versus luteinising-hormone-driven output, riding a daily rhythm.

Its modes are secretory, not electrical, and set by a single pituitary command.

• THE MODE REGISTER.

◦ Basal Output - the Banked Furnace - low tonic testosterone production.

◦ Stimulated Output - the Furnace Stoked - luteinising-hormone-driven surge in testosterone.

• PER-MODE TRANSFER FUNCTIONS.

◦ In the Banked Furnace it holds a resting potential near minus 30 to minus 60 millivolts and produces testosterone at a low tonic rate.

◦ In the Furnace Stoked luteinising hormone (the pituitary signal, abbreviated LH) raises cyclic AMP and steroidogenic acute regulatory protein activity, moving cholesterol into mitochondria and lifting output toward the daily total of about 5 to 7 milligrams.

• THE TRANSITION TABLE.

◦ LH pulse: switches Banked to Stoked over minutes to hours - thrown from above by the hypothalamic-pituitary-gonadal axis.

◦ Diurnal rhythm: gives a morning testosterone peak.

◦ Suppression: exogenous testosterone or opioids shut off LH drive and drop the cell to basal or below over days.

• FAULT REGIMES AND WATCHDOGS.

◦ Primary hypogonadism (as in Klinefelter syndrome or after damage) is the loss regime, with low testosterone and high LH as the warning sign.

◦ Leydig-cell tumours are the autonomous hormone-secreting regime; feedback through LH is the natural watchdog.


ENTRY 75. SERTOLI CELL (Sustentacular Cell of the Seminiferous Tubule) (keyed to other-cells catalogue Chapter 75)

MODES AND MODE TRANSITIONS:

This is a mode-poor support cell, the gonadal analogue of an astrocyte, whose regime axis is a once-in-life developmental switch from dividing to nursing.

Its modes are life-stage states, not firing patterns.

• THE MODE REGISTER.

◦ Proliferative Immature - the Growing Scaffold - the pre-pubertal dividing state that sets final cell number.

◦ Mature Nursing - the Sperm-Nurse Barrier - the adult non-dividing state that nurses germ cells and maintains the blood-testis barrier.

• PER-MODE TRANSFER FUNCTIONS.

◦ In the Growing Scaffold the cell divides during childhood, and its final count caps lifelong sperm output.

◦ In the Sperm-Nurse Barrier it rests near minus 40 to minus 70 millivolts, responds to follicle-stimulating hormone (the pituitary signal, abbreviated FSH) and testosterone through second messengers, supplies lactate to germ cells, and secretes inhibin B and anti-Mullerian hormone; it never fires action potentials.

• THE TRANSITION TABLE.

◦ Puberty: hormonal maturation switches Growing Scaffold to Sperm-Nurse Barrier once, fixing cell number - a developmental transition thrown from above by the gonadal axis.

◦ FSH and testosterone: tune secretory and nursing activity over minutes to hours within the mature mode.

• FAULT REGIMES AND WATCHDOGS.

◦ Sertoli-cell-only syndrome is the loss regime, tubules with no germ cells causing infertility.

◦ Breakdown of the blood-testis barrier by heat, toxins, or trauma exposes germ cells to autoimmune attack (orchitis); the barrier itself is the natural watchdog, and Sertoli-cell tumour is the rare neoplastic regime.


ENTRY 76. GRANULOSA CELL (Follicular Cell of the Ovary) (keyed to other-cells catalogue Chapter 76)

MODES AND MODE TRANSITIONS:

This is a dramatically plastic cell whose central feature is a genuine once-per-cycle mode transition, the luteinisation that turns an oestrogen-making egg-nurse into a progesterone-making pregnancy gland - a transformation worth naming and giving fuller treatment.

Its modes are secretory identities rather than electrical states, thrown by the mid-cycle luteinising-hormone surge.

• THE MODE REGISTER.

◦ Follicular Oestrogen Mode - the Oestrogen Finishing - the follicle-stimulating-hormone-driven state that aromatises androgen into oestrogen and nurses the egg.

◦ Luteal Progesterone Mode - the Luteal Turn - the post-ovulation state in which the same cell makes progesterone to support a possible pregnancy.

◦ Cumulus versus Mural - the Nurse and the Wall - a position-defined split between egg-hugging cumulus cells that nurse directly and steroidogenic mural cells lining the follicle.

• PER-MODE TRANSFER FUNCTIONS.

◦ In the Oestrogen Finishing, follicle-stimulating hormone (FSH) raises cyclic AMP and aromatase, converting theca-supplied androgen into oestradiol that rises to a pre-ovulatory peak; the cell rests near minus 30 to minus 70 millivolts, is coupled to the egg by connexin-43 gap junctions, and does not spike.

◦ In the Luteal Turn the cell enlarges, upregulates low-density-lipoprotein receptors and the cholesterol-to-progestin enzymes (STARD1, CYP11A1, HSD3B2), and secretes progesterone over the roughly two-week corpus-luteum lifespan.

◦ In the Nurse and the Wall, cumulus cells feed the egg through gap junctions while mural cells carry the bulk of steroid output.

• THE TRANSITION TABLE.

◦ Mid-cycle luteinising hormone (LH) surge: switches the Oestrogen Finishing to the Luteal Turn within roughly 30 to 40 hours, the once-per-cycle luteinisation, as oestrogen output gives way to progesterone - thrown from above by the pituitary gonadotropin surge.

◦ Follicle growth: builds the follicular mode by proliferation over about two weeks.

◦ No pregnancy: the luteal cells regress and die back over about two weeks; a pregnancy signal instead sustains them.

• FAULT REGIMES AND WATCHDOGS.

◦ In polycystic ovary syndrome follicle maturation and aromatisation stall, so the Oestrogen Finishing mode is disturbed and ovulation fails - the warning sign is arrested follicles with abnormal hormone ratios.

◦ Premature ovarian insufficiency is the loss regime and granulosa-cell tumour the oestrogen-secreting neoplastic one; the gonadotropin feedback loop is the natural watchdog.


ENTRY 77. THECA CELL (Theca Interna Cell of the Ovarian Follicle) (keyed to other-cells catalogue Chapter 77)

MODES AND MODE TRANSITIONS:

This is a non-excitable steroid supplier, the ovary's Leydig-cell twin, whose two-mode register tracks the ovarian cycle in step with its granulosa partner.

Its modes are secretory, set by luteinising hormone, and it too undergoes a luteal transformation after ovulation.

• THE MODE REGISTER.

◦ Follicular Androgen Supply - the Precursor Handoff - LH-driven androgen production handed across the basement membrane to the granulosa cell.

◦ Theca-Lutein Mode - the Luteal Partner - the post-ovulation state contributing to corpus-luteum steroid output.

• PER-MODE TRANSFER FUNCTIONS.

◦ In the Precursor Handoff, LH raises cyclic AMP and drives cholesterol to androstenedione, which the theca cell passes to the granulosa cell for aromatisation; it rests near minus 30 to minus 60 millivolts and does not spike.

◦ In the Luteal Partner the cell becomes a theca-lutein cell within the corpus luteum, supporting progesterone-dominated output.

• THE TRANSITION TABLE.

◦ Mid-cycle LH surge and ovulation: switches Precursor Handoff to Luteal Partner over hours to a day - thrown from above by the gonadotropin surge.

◦ LH pulses: tune androgen output over minutes to hours within the follicular mode.

◦ Luteal regression: the cell winds down with the corpus luteum over about two weeks if no pregnancy follows.

• FAULT REGIMES AND WATCHDOGS.

◦ In polycystic ovary syndrome and ovarian hyperthecosis the theca cell over-produces androgen, pinning it in an excess-supply regime that drives hyperandrogenism - the warning sign is high androgens with disrupted ovulation.

◦ Thecoma tumours are the neoplastic regime; the LH feedback loop and the obligatory granulosa partnership are the natural watchdogs.


ENTRY 78. THE ENTEROENDOCRINE CELL FAMILY (keyed to other-cells catalogue Chapter 78)

MODES AND MODE TRANSITIONS:

This is a family of excitable chemosensory secretory cells, so its register is a shared two-mode secretory pattern crossed with an architectural split between luminal-tasting and blood-listening cells.

Its modes are basal versus stimulated release, sensed at the gut's vast chemical interface.

• THE MODE REGISTER.

◦ Basal Surveillance - the Quiet Watch - low resting hormone output between stimuli.

◦ Stimulated Secretion - the Meal Report - luminal-nutrient-triggered hormone release.

◦ Open-Type versus Closed-Type - the Taster and the Listener - open cells sampling the lumen directly, closed cells responding to stretch, nerves, and blood.

• PER-MODE TRANSFER FUNCTIONS.

◦ In the Quiet Watch the cell rests near minus 40 to minus 60 millivolts with granules banked at the base.

◦ In the Meal Report a luminal stimulus depolarises the cell, opens voltage-gated calcium channels, and fires calcium-based action potentials that release the cell's chosen hormone - among them gastrin, cholecystokinin, glucose-dependent insulinotropic polypeptide, glucagon-like peptide-1, secretin, motilin, ghrelin, and the serotonin of the enterochromaffin cell, which makes about nine-tenths of the body's serotonin.

◦ In the Taster and the Listener, open-type cells use tongue-like sweet, bitter, umami, and fatty-acid receptors at the apex, while closed-type cells read stretch and blood-borne cues.

• THE TRANSITION TABLE.

◦ Luminal nutrients or microbial metabolites: switch Quiet Watch to Meal Report within seconds - a locally sensed transition.

◦ Neural and hormonal cues from the gut wall: also drive secretion, especially in closed-type cells.

◦ Population re-tuning: diet, microbiome, and position along the villus shift which hormone a maturing cell makes over its few-day lifespan, and the gut renews on the order of a hundred million such cells per day.

• FAULT REGIMES AND WATCHDOGS.

◦ Gastrinoma (Zollinger-Ellison syndrome) locks acid command on and causes intractable ulcers; carcinoid tumours of the serotonin-making cell cause flushing, diarrhoea, and valve disease - boundary regimes signalled by hormone excess.

◦ Loss or dysregulation of GLP-1 and glucose-dependent insulinotropic polypeptide signalling contributes to type 2 diabetes and obesity; rapid cell turnover is the natural safeguard against a stuck single cell.


ENTRY 79. NEUROPOD CELL (Synapse-Forming Enteroendocrine Cell) (keyed to other-cells catalogue Chapter 79)

MODES AND MODE TRANSITIONS:

This is a dual-channel signalling cell, an enteroendocrine cell that can speak in two languages at once, so its register is a two-mode split between slow hormonal and fast synaptic output.

Its fast mode is the defining novelty, a true millisecond synapse onto the vagus.

• THE MODE REGISTER.

◦ Hormonal Broadcast - the Slow Whisper - diffuse peptide release into blood and tissue, as for the wider enteroendocrine family.

◦ Synaptic Transduction - the Fast Telegraph - a genuine glutamate synapse onto a vagal sensory fibre for sub-second gut-to-brain signalling.

• PER-MODE TRANSFER FUNCTIONS.

◦ In the Slow Whisper it releases hormones such as glucagon-like peptide-1 or serotonin over seconds to minutes into the circulation.

◦ In the Fast Telegraph, resting near minus 40 to minus 60 millivolts, a luminal sugar stimulus depolarises the cell, drives calcium entry, and releases glutamate (the fast excitatory transmitter) across a synaptic cleft onto a vagal afferent, evoking a postsynaptic response within milliseconds.

• THE TRANSITION TABLE.

◦ Luminal sugar or nutrient at the apex: engages the Fast Telegraph within milliseconds while also feeding the Slow Whisper - a locally sensed transition, with the two modes running in parallel rather than exclusively.

◦ Slower nutrient and microbial cues: favour the hormonal channel over seconds to minutes.

• FAULT REGIMES AND WATCHDOGS.

◦ No distinct disease is yet assigned to this recently discovered cell; its faults are presumed to overlap those of its parent enteroendocrine types (Chapter 78).

◦ The honest statement is that its fault register is not yet characterised, given how new the synaptic mode is.


ENTRY 80. PULMONARY NEUROENDOCRINE CELL (keyed to other-cells catalogue Chapter 80)

MODES AND MODE TRANSITIONS:

This is a two-mode excitable oxygen sentinel whose secretory switch is thrown by the oxygen content of each breath.

Its modes are normoxic quiet versus hypoxic firing, crossed with a solitary-versus-clustered arrangement.

• THE MODE REGISTER.

◦ Normoxic Quiet - the Sentinel at Ease - low output when inhaled oxygen is adequate.

◦ Hypoxic Secretion - the Airway Alarm - low-oxygen-triggered release of serotonin and peptides with sensory signalling.

◦ Solitary versus Neuroepithelial Body - the Lone Watch and the Watch-Post - single scattered cells versus innervated clusters at airway branch points.

• PER-MODE TRANSFER FUNCTIONS.

◦ In the Sentinel at Ease oxygen-sensitive potassium channels are open, the cell is polarised in the negative tens of millivolts, and secretion is low.

◦ In the Airway Alarm low oxygen inhibits those potassium channels, depolarises the cell, opens voltage-gated calcium channels, and releases serotonin (a pulmonary vasoconstrictor) and calcitonin gene-related peptide (a vasodilator) plus bombesin-like peptides, tuning local blood flow and signalling vagal afferents.

◦ In the Watch-Post, clustered cells in a neuroepithelial body pool their output under dense innervation for a stronger reflex.

• THE TRANSITION TABLE.

◦ Fall in airway oxygen: switches Sentinel at Ease to Airway Alarm within seconds - a locally sensed transition, using the same logic as the carotid glomus cell.

◦ Carbon dioxide, acid, stretch, and irritants: also drive secretion.

◦ Chronic hypoxia or disease: enlarges and multiplies neuroepithelial bodies over days to weeks.

• FAULT REGIMES AND WATCHDOGS.

◦ Neuroendocrine cell hyperplasia of infancy causes childhood rapid breathing; overactivity is implicated in asthma and pulmonary hypertension - boundary regimes of excess.

◦ These cells are the presumed origin of small-cell lung carcinoma and pulmonary carcinoid; their innervation provides the reflex check on runaway signalling.


ENTRY 81. MERKEL CELL (Merkel-Ranvier Tactile Cell) (keyed to other-cells catalogue Chapter 81)

MODES AND MODE TRANSITIONS:

This is a two-mode mechanotransducer, a hormone-lineage cell drafted into touch; its full treatment lives at neuron catalogue Chapter 282 and this register is condensed.

Its modes are a resting state and a sustained-pressure firing state, the signature of a slowly-adapting receptor.

• THE MODE REGISTER.

◦ Resting Quiescence - the Waiting Reader - the unstimulated state at rest.

◦ Sustained-Pressure Firing - the Fine-Print Reader - the slowly-adapting response that holds during steady skin indentation.

• PER-MODE TRANSFER FUNCTIONS.

◦ In the Waiting Reader the mechanically gated channel Piezo2 is shut, the cell is polarised, and its nerve ending is quiet.

◦ In the Fine-Print Reader skin indentation opens Piezo2, depolarises the cell, and releases serotonin and peptides onto a slowly-adapting type I afferent fibre, sustaining its firing throughout steady pressure and encoding fine static form, edges, and texture.

• THE TRANSITION TABLE.

◦ Skin indentation: opens Piezo2 and switches Waiting Reader to Fine-Print Reader within milliseconds - a locally sensed mechanical transition.

◦ Release of pressure: returns the cell to quiescence, the slowly-adapting decay outlasting the fast-adapting receptors.

• FAULT REGIMES AND WATCHDOGS.

◦ Loss of these cells impairs fine tactile discrimination while cruder touch persists through other receptors - a graceful degradation rather than a hard failure.

◦ The neoplastic regime is Merkel cell carcinoma, an aggressive polyomavirus-linked skin cancer; full fault treatment is at neuron catalogue Chapter 282.


ENTRY 82. GLOMUS (TYPE I) CELL OF THE CAROTID AND AORTIC BODIES (keyed to other-cells catalogue Chapter 82)

MODES AND MODE TRANSITIONS:

This is a two-mode excitable chemoreceptor, the body's primary arterial oxygen sensor; context is given at neuron catalogue Chapter 281 and this register is condensed.

Its modes are normoxic silence versus hypoxic firing, read directly from flowing arterial blood.

• THE MODE REGISTER.

◦ Normoxic Silence - the Taster at Rest - quiet baseline when arterial oxygen is adequate.

◦ Hypoxic Firing - the Oxygen Alarm - low-oxygen-triggered transmitter release that drives the reflex surge in breathing and heart rate.

• PER-MODE TRANSFER FUNCTIONS.

◦ In the Taster at Rest the background TASK-type potassium leak current holds the cell near its resting potential in the negative tens of millivolts and transmitter release is low.

◦ In the Oxygen Alarm low oxygen inhibits the TASK potassium current, depolarises the cell, opens voltage-gated calcium channels, and releases adenosine triphosphate (the fast transmitter, abbreviated ATP) onto P2X receptors of the carotid sinus nerve, with acetylcholine and a dopamine brake tuning the response.

• THE TRANSITION TABLE.

◦ Fall in arterial oxygen: switches Taster at Rest to Oxygen Alarm within seconds - a locally sensed transition, reinforced by high carbon dioxide and low pH.

◦ Restored oxygen: quiets the cell within seconds.

◦ Chronic hypoxia (as at altitude): enlarges and sensitises the organ over days to weeks.

• FAULT REGIMES AND WATCHDOGS.

◦ Chronic overactivity drives hypertension and sympathetic overdrive in sleep apnoea and heart failure - a maladaptive sensitised regime rather than one the cell normally holds.

◦ The neoplastic regime is paraganglioma (carotid body tumour); dopamine autoinhibition is the local brake, with fuller context at neuron catalogue Chapter 281.


ENTRY 83. GLOMUS TYPE II (SUSTENTACULAR) CELL (keyed to other-cells catalogue Chapter 83)

MODES AND MODE TRANSITIONS:

This is a mode-poor glial nurse whose regime axis is support-versus-regeneration, matching the sustentacular and stem cells elsewhere in this catalogue.

Its modes are a resting support state and a hypoxia-driven stem-cell state.

• THE MODE REGISTER.

◦ Support Rest - the Oxygen Sensor's Nurse - quiet glial housekeeping around the sensor clusters.

◦ Stem Recruitment - the Dormant Regenerator - hypoxia-triggered proliferation into new type I sensor cells.

• PER-MODE TRANSFER FUNCTIONS.

◦ In the Oxygen Sensor's Nurse it is largely non-excitable at a negative resting potential, buffers the sensor environment, and modulates activity through purinergic (adenosine triphosphate (ATP)-sensing) receptors and calcium changes rather than spikes.

◦ In the Dormant Regenerator it reawakens its neural-crest identity, divides, and differentiates into new type I cells, enlarging the organ.

• THE TRANSITION TABLE.

◦ Chronic low oxygen: switches Nurse to Dormant Regenerator over days to weeks - a locally triggered regenerative response driving acclimatisation.

◦ Restored oxygen and adequate sensor numbers: return the cell to quiet support.

• FAULT REGIMES AND WATCHDOGS.

◦ Dysregulated proliferation contributes to carotid body enlargement in chronic disease and to paraganglioma - the boundary regime of unchecked stem activation.

◦ Loss of its support would impair chemoreceptor function and renewal; there is no fast electrical watchdog, only the hypoxic drive that gates its proliferation.


ENTRY 84. PARAGANGLION CHIEF CELLS, SYMPATHETIC AND PARASYMPATHETIC (keyed to other-cells catalogue Chapter 84)

MODES AND MODE TRANSITIONS:

This is an excitable secretory paraneuron split into two families, so its register carries a lineage-defined mode pair crossed with a strong fetal-versus-involuting developmental axis.

Its modes reflect whether a cluster settled as an adrenaline factory or an oxygen sensor.

• THE MODE REGISTER.

◦ Chromaffin Secretion - the Scattered Adrenal Outpost - the sympathetic (chromaffin) mode releasing catecholamines into the blood.

◦ Chemoreceptor Signalling - the Scattered Oxygen Post - the parasympathetic mode signalling sensory nerves like the carotid body.

◦ Fetal-Active versus Involuting - the Prenatal Prime and the Fading Colony - a developmental state axis, prominent before birth and regressing after.

• PER-MODE TRANSFER FUNCTIONS.

◦ In the Scattered Adrenal Outpost, preganglionic sympathetic input depolarises the cell, opens calcium channels, and releases noradrenaline and adrenaline into the blood; the cell holds a negative resting potential and fires calcium-based responses.

◦ In the Scattered Oxygen Post, arterial chemistry drives release of adenosine triphosphate (ATP) and dopamine onto afferent nerves, mirroring the glomus cell.

◦ In the Prenatal Prime the para-aortic outposts (chiefly the organ of Zuckerkandl) are metabolically busiest before birth, then quiet in the Fading Colony.

• THE TRANSITION TABLE.

◦ Sympathetic drive or arterial chemistry: engages the secretory or chemosensory mode within seconds - the former thrown from above by the autonomic system, the latter locally sensed.

◦ Postnatal development: the sympathetic outposts involute over months to a few years as the adrenal medulla takes over, largely gone by 2 to 3 years of age.

• FAULT REGIMES AND WATCHDOGS.

◦ These cells give rise to paraganglioma and extra-adrenal phaeochromocytoma, catecholamine-secreting tumours causing dangerous blood-pressure surges, the organ of Zuckerkandl being a classic site.

◦ Normal postnatal regression is the expected fate rather than a fault; persistent or neoplastic clusters are the abnormal regime, watched for by plasma metanephrines.


ENTRY 85. JUXTAGLOMERULAR CELL OF THE KIDNEY (keyed to other-cells catalogue Chapter 85)

MODES AND MODE TRANSITIONS:

This is a two-mode inverse-control cell, a modified smooth muscle cell that secretes when hyperpolarised and hushes when depolarised - the opposite of most excitable cells - and is the opening move of the renin-angiotensin cascade.

Its modes are set by arteriolar pressure and tubular salt, with a striking structural recruitment plasticity.

• THE MODE REGISTER.

◦ Basal Restraint - the Pressure Sentry at Ease - low renin release when pressure and salt are adequate.

◦ Low-Pressure Release - the Pressure Alarm - renin secretion when arteriolar pressure or salt falls.

• PER-MODE TRANSFER FUNCTIONS.

◦ In the Pressure Sentry at Ease a normal, somewhat depolarised, higher-calcium state suppresses renin (the so-called calcium paradox of renin secretion), and output is low.

◦ In the Pressure Alarm a fall in arteriolar pressure hyperpolarises the cell and lowers intracellular calcium, favouring release of renin into the blood, which generates angiotensin II to raise blood pressure and, at the brain's circumventricular organs, command thirst.

• THE TRANSITION TABLE.

◦ Falling arteriolar pressure, low tubular sodium chloride sensed by the macula densa, or sympathetic noradrenaline at beta-1 adrenergic receptors: switch Sentry to Pressure Alarm within minutes - part locally sensed, part thrown from above by the sympathetic system.

◦ Restored pressure and volume: returns the cell to restraint over minutes.

◦ Chronic low pressure or salt depletion: recruits previously quiet smooth muscle cells up the arteriole into renin-secreting cells over days, reverting when demand falls.

• FAULT REGIMES AND WATCHDOGS.

◦ Overactivity drives renin-dependent hypertension, and a reninoma locks the cell into excess release, causing severe hypertension with low potassium - the boundary regime of unchecked secretion.

◦ Underactivity contributes to salt-wasting and low blood pressure; the pressure-and-salt feedback of the juxtaglomerular apparatus is the natural watchdog, and this whole axis is the target of the angiotensin-converting enzyme inhibitor and angiotensin-receptor-blocker drugs.
